# The Series 65 Exam — Complete Study Textbook

**Uniform Investment Adviser Law Examination (NASAA)**

Organized section-by-section to the official **NASAA Series 65 test specifications, effective June 12, 2023**.

> **About this book.** This is a study companion generated and organized to the official NASAA Series 65 outline. Every section was drafted and then passed through an independent fact-check and editing pass. It is not legal advice or an official NASAA/FINRA publication — always confirm specific rules, thresholds, and dollar figures against the primary sources listed at the end.

## The exam at a glance

| Item | Detail |
|---|---|
| Full name | Uniform Investment Adviser Law Examination (Series 65) |
| Owner / author | NASAA (North American Securities Administrators Association) |
| Administered by | FINRA |
| Questions | 140 total — 130 scored + 10 unscored pretest |
| Time limit | 180 minutes |
| Passing score | At least 92 of the 130 scored questions (~70.8%) |
| Format | Multiple choice, closed book |
| Fee | $187 |
| Specifications | Effective June 12, 2023 |

### Content areas and weighting

| Part | Content area | Weight | Approx. questions |
|---|---|---|---|
| I | Economic Factors and Business Information | 15% | 20 |
| II | Investment Vehicle Characteristics | 25% | 32 |
| III | Client Investment Recommendations and Strategies | 30% | 39 |
| IV | Laws, Regulations, and Guidelines Including Prohibition on Unethical Business Practices | 30% | 39 |

## How to use this book

Work through the four Parts in order — they mirror the exam blueprint, and earlier concepts (economics, then vehicles) support the later, heavily weighted sections (recommendations and law, 60% of the exam combined). For each section: read the explanation, work every example by hand, and memorize the **Exam tip** and **Common trap** callouts. Use *Appendix A — Key Formulas* for calculation drills and *Appendix B — Glossary* to review terminology.

## Part I — Economic Factors and Business Information

*15% of the exam · approximately 20 questions.*

### A. Basic Economic Concepts

The Series 65 opens with economics because an investment adviser must be able to read the economy the way a doctor reads vital signs. You are not expected to be a professional economist. You are expected to know the vocabulary, understand cause and effect (especially how the Federal Reserve and Congress push the economy around), and answer a set of questions that reward clear thinking over memorization. This subsection builds that foundation from the ground up.

#### The Business Cycle

An economy does not grow in a straight line. It moves through a repeating, wave-like pattern called the **business cycle**, measured primarily by changes in real Gross Domestic Product (GDP). The cycle has four phases:

| Phase | What is happening | Typical signs |
|---|---|---|
| **Expansion** | The economy grows; real GDP rises | Rising output, falling unemployment, rising consumer spending, often rising inflation |
| **Peak** | Growth tops out; the high point of the cycle | Full employment, capacity strained, inflation pressure highest |
| **Contraction (recession)** | The economy shrinks; real GDP falls | Falling output, rising unemployment, weak spending and investment |
| **Trough** | The low point; the economy bottoms out | Unemployment high but stabilizing, ready to recover into the next expansion |

The order flows in a loop: **expansion → peak → contraction → trough → expansion** again. The phases go by other names — expansion may be called "recovery" or "boom," and contraction may be called "recession," "downturn," or "slowdown."

Two definitions are heavily tested:

- **Recession:** the standard exam definition is **two consecutive quarters (6 months) of declining real GDP.**
- **Depression:** a prolonged, severe contraction. The commonly tested benchmark is **six or more consecutive quarters (18 months) of declining real GDP**, often accompanied by a decline in real GDP of 10% or more.

**Exam tip:** If a question describes falling GDP for exactly two quarters, the answer is *recession*, not depression. A depression requires the longer, deeper decline.

**Common trap:** Candidates confuse the peak (top, best conditions, highest inflation risk) with the trough (bottom, worst conditions). Remember: a *peak* is the top of a mountain; a *trough* is the low point where water collects.

#### Economic Indicators: Measuring the Cycle

Economists track the cycle using **economic indicators** — published statistics that describe the health of the economy. Know the following core indicators cold.

**Gross Domestic Product (GDP)** is the total market value of all final goods and services produced within a country's borders in a given period (usually a year or a quarter). It is *the* broadest measure of economic output. GDP is built from four categories of spending:

> GDP = C + I + G + (X − M)

where **C** = consumer spending, **I** = business investment, **G** = government spending, **X** = exports, and **M** = imports.

*Worked example:* Suppose a country reports consumer spending of $14 trillion, business investment of $4 trillion, government spending of $6 trillion, exports of $3 trillion, and imports of $4 trillion.

> GDP = 14 + 4 + 6 + (3 − 4) = 14 + 4 + 6 − 1 = **$23 trillion**

Notice that imports *subtract* from GDP because they represent output produced elsewhere.

- **Nominal GDP** is measured in current dollars and is not adjusted for inflation.
- **Real GDP** is adjusted for inflation, so it reflects actual changes in output. Real GDP is the number used to date the business cycle.

> Real GDP = Nominal GDP ÷ (GDP Deflator ÷ 100)

*Worked example:* If nominal GDP is $23 trillion and the GDP deflator is 115 (meaning prices are 15% higher than the base year), then:

> Real GDP = 23 ÷ (115 ÷ 100) = 23 ÷ 1.15 = **$20 trillion**

**Exam tip:** Growth is always judged by *real* GDP. Nominal GDP can rise purely because prices went up, even if the economy produced nothing more.

**GDP vs. GNP:** GDP counts output produced *within a country's borders* regardless of who owns the resources. GNP (Gross National Product) counts output produced *by a country's residents and companies* regardless of where it occurs. GDP is the modern standard.

**The Consumer Price Index (CPI)** measures the average change over time in the prices paid by urban consumers for a fixed "basket" of goods and services (food, housing, transportation, medical care, etc.). CPI is the most-watched gauge of **inflation**. When CPI rises, the cost of living is rising.

> Inflation rate = (CPI this year − CPI last year) ÷ CPI last year × 100

*Worked example:* If CPI moved from 300 to 309 over one year:

> Inflation rate = (309 − 300) ÷ 300 × 100 = 9 ÷ 300 × 100 = **3%**

**Employment indicators** describe the labor market:

- **Unemployment rate** — the percentage of the labor force actively seeking work but unable to find it. (Note: it is a *lagging* indicator; more on that below.)
- **Nonfarm payrolls** — the monthly count of jobs added or lost.
- **Initial jobless claims** — new filings for unemployment benefits; a timely, forward-looking signal.
- **Average weekly hours in manufacturing** — employers cut hours before laying off, so this turns early.

**The trade deficit (balance of trade)** compares the value of a country's exports to its imports:

> Balance of Trade = Exports − Imports

A **trade deficit** exists when a country imports more than it exports (a negative number); a **trade surplus** exists when it exports more than it imports. The U.S. typically runs a trade deficit. A persistent deficit tends to put downward pressure on the country's currency.

*Worked example:* Exports of $3 trillion and imports of $4 trillion produce a balance of trade of 3 − 4 = **−$1 trillion**, a $1 trillion trade deficit.

#### Leading, Coincident, and Lagging Indicators

Indicators are also classified by their *timing* relative to the business cycle. This is one of the most frequently tested economics concepts on the exam.

| Class | Definition | Commonly tested examples |
|---|---|---|
| **Leading** | Change *before* the economy changes; used to *predict* turning points | Stock market prices (S&P 500), building permits and housing starts, new orders for durable goods, average weekly manufacturing hours, initial unemployment claims, money supply (M2), the yield-curve spread, consumer expectations |
| **Coincident** | Change *at the same time* as the economy; confirm the *current* phase | Nonfarm payroll employment, industrial production, personal income, manufacturing and trade sales, GDP |
| **Lagging** | Change *after* the economy has already turned; *confirm* trends | Unemployment rate and average duration of unemployment, corporate profits, the prime rate, CPI/inflation, business inventories, consumer installment debt |

**Exam tip:** The stock market is the classic *leading* indicator — investors buy in anticipation of a recovery before it shows up in the data. Memorize this.

**Common trap:** The **CPI/inflation** and the **unemployment rate** are *lagging* indicators, not leading. Inflation keeps rising after a peak and unemployment keeps rising after a trough, because employers are slow to rehire. Test writers love to see whether you will wrongly call unemployment a leading indicator.

#### Inflation and Deflation

**Inflation** is a sustained increase in the general price level, which means each dollar buys less — a loss of purchasing power. It is measured mainly by the CPI. Two causes are tested:

- **Demand-pull inflation** — too much money chasing too few goods; demand outruns supply.
- **Cost-push inflation** — rising input costs (wages, raw materials, energy) push producers to raise prices.

Related terms you must distinguish:

- **Hyperinflation** — extremely rapid, out-of-control inflation.
- **Disinflation** — a *slowing of the rate* of inflation (prices still rise, just more slowly). This is not the same as deflation.
- **Deflation** — a sustained *decrease* in the general price level (a negative inflation rate). Purchasing power rises, but deflation is dangerous: consumers delay purchases expecting lower prices, demand falls, and the economy can spiral downward.
- **Stagflation** — the unusual combination of stagnant growth (high unemployment) *plus* high inflation at the same time. It is difficult for policymakers because the usual cure for one problem worsens the other.

**Exam tip:** Inflation hurts lenders and bondholders (they are repaid in cheaper dollars) and helps borrowers (they repay with cheaper dollars). Deflation does the reverse.

The **real interest rate** strips inflation out of a stated (nominal) rate:

> Real Interest Rate = Nominal Interest Rate − Inflation Rate

*Worked example:* A bond yields a nominal 5% while inflation runs at 3%. The real return is 5% − 3% = **2%.** If inflation rose to 6%, the real return would be 5% − 6% = **−1%**, an actual loss of purchasing power despite a positive stated yield.

#### Interest Rates, Yield Curves, and Credit Spreads

**Interest rates** are the price of borrowing money. The single most important relationship in fixed income — and one the exam tests relentlessly — is the **inverse relationship between interest rates and bond prices:** when rates rise, existing bond prices fall; when rates fall, existing bond prices rise. Longer-maturity and lower-coupon bonds are the most sensitive to rate changes.

**Common trap:** Rising rates sound "good" but they *lower* the market value of bonds an investor already owns. Do not equate higher yields with higher prices.

Key benchmark rates to know:

- **Federal funds rate** — the rate banks charge *each other* for overnight loans of reserves. This is the most volatile short-term rate and the Fed's primary policy target.
- **Discount rate** — the rate the Federal Reserve charges banks that borrow reserves directly from it.
- **Prime rate** — the rate banks charge their most creditworthy corporate customers; a *lagging* indicator.
- **Broker call (call money) rate** — what banks charge broker-dealers on loans used to finance customer margin accounts.

**The yield curve** is a graph plotting the yields of bonds of the same credit quality (usually U.S. Treasuries) across different maturities, from short-term to long-term. Its shape signals expectations about growth and interest rates.

| Shape | Description | What it usually signals |
|---|---|---|
| **Normal (positive/upward-sloping)** | Long-term yields higher than short-term yields | Healthy expansion; investors demand more yield to lend longer |
| **Inverted (negative/downward-sloping)** | Short-term yields higher than long-term yields | Widely watched *recession warning*; markets expect the Fed to cut rates as growth slows |
| **Flat** | Short- and long-term yields nearly equal | Transition; often between expansion and contraction, or the reverse |
| **Humped** | Intermediate yields higher than both short- and long-term | Uncertainty; relatively rare |

**Exam tip:** A *normal* curve slopes up because investors require extra compensation (for inflation and uncertainty) to tie up money longer. An *inverted* curve is the notable one — it is the classic leading indicator of a coming recession.

**Credit spreads** measure the extra yield investors demand to hold a riskier bond instead of a "risk-free" Treasury of the same maturity:

> Credit Spread = Yield on Corporate (or lower-quality) Bond − Yield on Comparable Treasury

*Worked example:* A 10-year corporate bond yields 6.2% while the 10-year Treasury yields 4.2%.

> Credit Spread = 6.2% − 4.2% = 2.0%, or **200 basis points** (1% = 100 basis points)

Spreads *widen* when investors grow fearful (recessions, financial stress) and demand more compensation for default risk; spreads *narrow* when confidence and risk appetite return. Widening credit spreads are therefore a sign of economic anxiety, and lower-rated ("high-yield" or "junk") bonds show the widest spreads.

#### Monetary Policy — The Federal Reserve

**Monetary policy** is the management of the money supply and interest rates to promote stable prices and maximum employment. It is conducted by the **Federal Reserve (the Fed)** — the U.S. central bank — with open market operations directed by its **Federal Open Market Committee (FOMC)**. The Fed has three classic tools:

| Tool | What it is | To *loosen* (stimulate) | To *tighten* (restrain) |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Open market operations** | Buying/selling U.S. government securities in the open market | **Buy** securities → adds reserves to banks → lowers rates | **Sell** securities → drains reserves → raises rates |
| **Discount rate** | Rate the Fed charges banks that borrow reserves from it | **Lower** the rate | **Raise** the rate |
| **Reserve requirement** | Percentage of deposits banks must hold in reserve | **Lower** it → banks can lend more | **Raise** it → banks can lend less |

**Open market operations are the Fed's most frequently used and most flexible tool.** When the Fed *buys* securities, it injects money into the banking system, pushing interest rates (including the fed funds rate) down — an **easy-money / accommodative** stance meant to stimulate growth. When the Fed *sells* securities, it pulls money out, pushing rates up — a **tight-money** stance meant to cool inflation.

**Exam tip:** Trace the chain of cause and effect. Fed *buys* securities → money supply *up* → interest rates *down* → borrowing and spending *up* → economy stimulated (and bond prices *up*). Reverse every arrow for a Fed *sale*.

**Common trap:** The **reserve requirement** is considered the Fed's *bluntest* tool because a small change affects every bank's lending capacity dramatically. Also note the **discount rate** is charged by the Fed to banks, whereas the **fed funds rate** is charged *between* banks — do not swap those definitions. (The reserve requirement was reduced to 0% in 2020, but the Series 65 still tests it as a conceptual tool.)

#### Fiscal Policy — Congress and the President

**Fiscal policy** is the use of **government spending and taxation** to influence the economy. Unlike monetary policy, fiscal policy is set by the **President and Congress** (the executive and legislative branches), not the Fed. This approach is rooted in **Keynesian economics**, which holds that government can smooth the business cycle by managing aggregate demand.

- **Expansionary (stimulative) fiscal policy:** *increase* government spending and/or *cut* taxes. This puts more money in the hands of businesses and consumers to boost demand — used to fight recessions. It tends to enlarge the budget deficit.
- **Contractionary (restrictive) fiscal policy:** *decrease* government spending and/or *raise* taxes. This removes money from the economy to cool inflation.

Two terms to distinguish:

- **Budget deficit** — the shortfall in a single year when the government spends more than it collects in revenue.
- **National debt** — the accumulated total of all past deficits (minus surpluses); the cumulative amount owed.

**Exam tip:** Match the branch to the policy. *Monetary* = the Fed (money supply, interest rates). *Fiscal* = Congress and the President (spending, taxes). Questions often test whether you can attribute a given action to the correct body.

Also know the competing schools test writers reference:

- **Keynesian theory** — emphasizes *demand-side* management; government spending and taxation are the key levers to steer the economy.
- **Monetarist theory** (associated with Milton Friedman) — emphasizes controlling the *money supply*; argues that steady, predictable money growth matters most and that fiscal fine-tuning is less effective.
- **Supply-side theory** — argues that lower taxes and lighter regulation stimulate production, investment, and ultimately growth.

#### Global Factors

The U.S. economy is part of a connected world, and the exam expects you to understand several international influences.

**Currency valuation and exchange rates.** The value of the U.S. dollar relative to other currencies drives trade and investment flows. The key idea is the effect of a **strong** versus a **weak** dollar:

| | Strong (appreciating) U.S. dollar | Weak (depreciating) U.S. dollar |
|---|---|---|
| U.S. exports | More expensive abroad → **hurts** U.S. exporters | Cheaper abroad → **helps** U.S. exporters |
| Imports into the U.S. | Cheaper → **helps** importers and U.S. consumers | More expensive → **hurts** importers, raises import prices |
| U.S. tourists abroad | Their dollars buy more → **benefit** | Their dollars buy less → **disadvantage** |

*Worked example:* Suppose 1 euro = $1.10. A U.S. importer buying a €50,000 machine pays 50,000 × 1.10 = **$55,000**. If the dollar *weakens* so that 1 euro = $1.25, the same machine now costs 50,000 × 1.25 = **$62,500** — the weaker dollar made the import more expensive. Meanwhile, a European buyer finds U.S. goods cheaper, helping American exporters.

The **effective exchange rate** is a *trade-weighted* measure of a currency's value against a basket of the currencies of its trading partners, rather than against a single currency. It gives a more complete picture of whether a currency is broadly strengthening or weakening.

**Exam tip:** A weak domestic currency *helps exporters* and *hurts importers*; a strong currency does the reverse. Reason it through from "which direction makes our goods cheaper for foreigners."

**Sovereign debt** is debt issued by national governments (for the U.S., Treasury securities). U.S. Treasuries are treated as the global benchmark for a "risk-free" rate because of the government's taxing power and its ability to print its own currency. Debt issued by other nations — especially emerging markets or countries borrowing in a foreign currency — carries **sovereign (default) risk**: the risk that a government cannot or will not repay. A **sovereign debt crisis** can trigger currency collapse, capital flight, and financial contagion that spreads across borders.

**Geopolitical factors** — wars, trade disputes and tariffs, sanctions, elections, regime changes, oil-supply shocks, and pandemics — create uncertainty that ripples through currencies, commodity prices, interest rates, and stock markets. Heightened geopolitical risk typically drives a **"flight to quality"**: investors sell riskier assets and move into perceived safe havens such as U.S. Treasuries and gold, pushing Treasury prices up and their yields down.

**Exam tip:** When a question describes fear, crisis, or global instability, expect money to flow *toward* safety (Treasuries, gold) and *away* from risk (stocks, high-yield bonds). That flight-to-quality reflex ties together yields, credit spreads, and currency movements into one coherent story — exactly the kind of integrated reasoning the Series 65 rewards.

### B. Financial Reporting

Financial reporting is the language companies use to tell investors how they are doing. On the Series 65, this material shows up in two ways: (1) direct questions asking you to identify a financial statement, a line item, an SEC filing, or an accounting method, and (2) as the raw material for the ratio and analysis questions covered elsewhere in the exam. You do not need to be an accountant, but you must recognize each of the three core financial statements, know what each one measures, understand the accounting methods and audit levels behind them, and match the major SEC filings to their purpose and frequency. This subsection builds those skills in order: first the accounting fundamentals that underlie every statement, then the three statements themselves, then the auditor's role, and finally the public filings that package it all for investors.

#### Accounting Fundamentals: Cash vs. Accrual Accounting

Before you can read a financial statement, you have to know *when* a company records its revenues and expenses. There are two methods.

- **Cash-basis accounting** records revenue when cash is actually **received** and records expenses when cash is actually **paid**. It is simple and tracks the checkbook, but it can distort the picture of a business. A company could deliver $1 million of product in December, collect nothing until January, and show zero revenue for the year it did the work.
- **Accrual accounting** records revenue when it is **earned** and expenses when they are **incurred**, regardless of when cash changes hands. Under the "matching principle," expenses are matched to the revenues they help produce in the same period. If the company delivers that $1 million of product in December, it books $1 million of revenue in December even though the cash arrives later (the amount owed sits on the balance sheet as **accounts receivable**).

**Exam tip:** Accrual accounting is required under **GAAP** (Generally Accepted Accounting Principles) and is the method used in the audited financial statements of public companies. When a question asks which method gives the most accurate picture of a company's true economic performance for a period, the answer is **accrual**. Cash basis is associated with very small businesses and simple tax situations.

**Common trap:** Watch for questions that pair a timing fact with a method. "A firm earns revenue in Q4 but is not paid until Q1 of the next year; under accrual accounting, when is the revenue recognized?" The answer is Q4 (when earned), not Q1 (when collected).

#### Accounting Fundamentals: Audited vs. Unaudited

The second fundamental distinction is the level of independent verification behind the numbers.

- **Audited** financial statements have been examined by an **independent public accounting firm** (a CPA firm) that tests the records and issues a formal opinion on whether the statements fairly present the company's financial position in conformity with GAAP. An audit provides the highest level of assurance.
- **Unaudited** financial statements are prepared by the company's own management and have **not** been independently verified. They may still be accurate, but they carry no outside opinion. Interim (quarterly) figures are typically unaudited; they may be "reviewed" by accountants, which is a lower level of assurance than a full audit.

**Exam tip:** Tie this to the filings. The annual **10-K** contains **audited** statements; the quarterly **10-Q** contains **unaudited** (reviewed) statements. This audited/unaudited split is one of the most testable facts in this subsection.

#### The Balance Sheet

The **balance sheet** is a snapshot of what a company **owns and owes at one specific point in time** (for example, "as of December 31"). It answers the question, "What is the company's financial position on this date?" It is built on the fundamental accounting equation:

Assets = Liabilities + Shareholders' Equity

Because it must always balance, shareholders' equity (also called **net worth** or **book value**) is simply what is left for owners after subtracting what the company owes from what it owns:

Shareholders' Equity = Total Assets − Total Liabilities

**Major components:**

| Section | Contains |
|---|---|
| **Current assets** | Cash, marketable securities, accounts receivable, inventory — items expected to be converted to cash within 12 months |
| **Fixed / non-current assets** | Property, plant & equipment (PP&E), shown net of accumulated depreciation |
| **Intangible assets** | Goodwill, patents, trademarks |
| **Current liabilities** | Accounts payable, accrued expenses, and the portion of long-term debt due within 12 months |
| **Long-term liabilities** | Bonds and notes payable due beyond 12 months |
| **Shareholders' equity** | Par value of stock, capital in excess of par (paid-in surplus), and retained earnings; treasury stock is subtracted |

**Key balance-sheet calculations.** These formulas frequently appear on the exam:

- **Working Capital = Current Assets − Current Liabilities.** This measures short-term liquidity — the cushion available to meet obligations coming due within a year.
  - *Worked example:* Current assets = $600,000; current liabilities = $350,000. Working Capital = $600,000 − $350,000 = **$250,000**.
- **Current Ratio = Current Assets / Current Liabilities.** A ratio above 1.0 means current assets exceed current liabilities.
  - *Worked example:* $600,000 / $350,000 = **1.71**.
- **Quick Ratio (Acid-Test) = (Current Assets − Inventory) / Current Liabilities.** This is a stricter test because inventory is the hardest current asset to convert to cash quickly, so it is removed.
  - *Worked example:* If inventory is $150,000, then ($600,000 − $150,000) / $350,000 = $450,000 / $350,000 = **1.29**.

**Common trap:** The quick ratio removes **inventory** (and sometimes prepaid expenses) from current assets — it does **not** remove cash or receivables. A distractor that subtracts receivables is wrong.

**Exam tip:** The balance sheet is a "point in time" statement, while the income statement and statement of cash flows cover a "period of time." Questions test that distinction directly.

#### The Income Statement

The **income statement** (also called the profit-and-loss or P&L statement) reports a company's **revenues and expenses over a period of time** (a quarter or a year) and shows whether the company earned a profit or a loss. It answers, "Was the company profitable during this period?" Its top-to-bottom structure builds down to the "bottom line":

Revenue (Net Sales)
− Cost of Goods Sold (COGS)
= Gross Profit
− Operating Expenses (including depreciation)
= Operating Income (EBIT — earnings before interest and taxes)
− Interest Expense
= Pre-Tax Income (EBT)
− Taxes
= **Net Income**

**Key income-statement calculation — Earnings Per Share (EPS):**

EPS = (Net Income − Preferred Dividends) / Number of Common Shares Outstanding

- *Worked example:* Net income = $5,000,000; preferred dividends = $500,000; common shares outstanding = 2,000,000. EPS = ($5,000,000 − $500,000) / 2,000,000 = $4,500,000 / 2,000,000 = **$2.25 per share**.

**Common trap:** Preferred dividends are subtracted **before** dividing by common shares, because EPS measures earnings available to **common** stockholders. Forgetting to remove preferred dividends is the classic wrong answer.

**Exam tip:** Know the vocabulary layers — gross profit is after COGS only; operating income (EBIT) is after operating expenses but before interest and taxes; net income is the final figure after everything. Interest paid to bondholders is deducted **before** taxes (making debt financing tax-advantaged), whereas dividends to shareholders are paid **after** taxes from net income.

#### The Statement of Cash Flows

The **statement of cash flows** reconciles the accrual-based income statement back to actual cash. Because accrual accounting records revenues and expenses that have not yet been settled in cash, a company can report positive net income yet still run short of cash — this statement reveals where cash actually came from and went during the period. It is divided into three sections:

| Section | What it captures | Examples |
|---|---|---|
| **Operating activities** | Cash generated by the core business | Cash from customers, cash paid to suppliers and employees; starts from net income and adds back non-cash charges like depreciation |
| **Investing activities** | Cash used for or from long-term assets | Buying or selling PP&E; buying or selling securities or other companies |
| **Financing activities** | Cash from or to capital providers | Issuing or repaying debt, issuing or repurchasing stock, paying cash dividends |

**Exam tip:** The most testable idea is *why* this statement exists: because accrual accounting means reported earnings are not the same as cash. A profitable company can still fail if it runs out of cash. Also remember that **depreciation** is a non-cash expense that reduces net income on the income statement but is **added back** in the operating section, because no cash actually left the company.

**Common trap:** Paying a cash dividend is a **financing** activity, not an operating one. Purchasing new equipment is an **investing** activity. Do not confuse the three buckets.

#### Auditor Disclosures: Qualified vs. Unqualified Opinions

When an independent CPA firm audits a company, it attaches an **auditor's report (opinion)** to the financial statements. The type of opinion tells investors how much to trust the numbers. There are four levels; the exam focuses heavily on the first two.

| Opinion | Meaning |
|---|---|
| **Unqualified opinion** | The "clean" opinion — the **best** outcome. The auditor states that the financial statements fairly present the company's position **in conformity with GAAP**, with no reservations. |
| **Qualified opinion** | The statements are fairly presented **"except for"** a specific issue — a particular item the auditor could not verify or that departs from GAAP. It is a caution flag, not an outright rejection. |
| **Adverse opinion** | The statements do **not** fairly present the company's position and do not conform to GAAP. This is the most negative opinion. |
| **Disclaimer of opinion** | The auditor cannot form an opinion at all (for example, due to insufficient information or a scope limitation). |

**Exam tip:** Do not let the words mislead you. An **un**qualified opinion is the **good** one (nothing was disqualified — clean), while a **qualified** opinion signals a problem ("except for..."). This counterintuitive naming is a favorite trap. If a question asks which opinion an investor most wants to see, the answer is **unqualified**.

**Common trap:** An audit opinion — even a clean, unqualified one — is **not** a guarantee against fraud, a prediction of future performance, or a statement that the company is a good investment. It only attests that the statements fairly present the financials in accordance with GAAP.

#### Corporate SEC Filings: 10-K, 10-Q, and 8-K

Public reporting companies must file periodic reports with the **SEC**, which makes them available to the public through the **EDGAR** system. You must match each filing to its purpose, frequency, and audit level.

| Filing | Frequency / Trigger | Content | Audited? |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Form 10-K** | **Annually** (after fiscal year end) | Comprehensive annual report: full audited financial statements, MD&A (Management's Discussion and Analysis), business and risk-factor disclosures | **Yes — audited** |
| **Form 10-Q** | **Quarterly** (for the first three fiscal quarters) | Condensed interim financial statements and MD&A | **No — unaudited** (reviewed) |
| **Form 8-K** | **As needed**, upon a material event | "Current report" announcing significant events — e.g., a merger or acquisition, bankruptcy, change of auditors, resignation of a director, or a change in control | N/A |

**Filing timelines.** The 10-Q covers only the first three quarters because the fourth quarter is folded into the annual 10-K. Filing deadlines vary by company size: a smaller (non-accelerated) filer's **10-K** is generally due within **90 days** of fiscal year-end and its **10-Q** within **45 days** of quarter-end, while large accelerated filers face shorter windows (60 and 40 days). An **8-K** must generally be filed within **4 business days** of the triggering event. For the Series 65, prioritize the concepts — which filing, how often, audited or not, and that material events go on the 8-K — over memorizing every deadline, but know that the **8-K = 4 business days** figure and the **annual/quarterly** cadence are commonly tested.

**Exam tip:** The cleanest distinctions to lock in: **10-K = annual and audited; 10-Q = quarterly and unaudited; 8-K = material current events, filed promptly (within 4 business days).** If a question describes a company announcing that it just fired its auditor or agreed to be acquired, the correct filing is the **8-K**.

#### Annual Reports

The **annual report** is the document a company sends to its **shareholders** each year. Do not confuse it with the 10-K. The annual report is typically a polished, narrative document — it includes the audited financial statements (income statement, balance sheet, statement of cash flows), the auditor's opinion, a letter from the CEO or chairman, and a discussion of the year's performance and outlook. The **10-K** is the more detailed, standardized, regulatory filing submitted directly to the SEC. Their financial statements overlap and are drawn from the same audited data, but the audiences and formats differ: the annual report speaks to **investors**, and the 10-K satisfies the **SEC**.

**Exam tip:** If asked where a retail investor would find a plain-language discussion of the company's year along with its audited financials, the answer is the **annual report**; if asked for the comprehensive statutory disclosure filed with the SEC, the answer is the **10-K**. Both contain audited financial statements, which is exactly why the exam likes to contrast them.

### C. Analytical Methods

Analytical methods are the quantitative tools an investment adviser uses to value cash flows, measure risk and return, and judge the financial health of a company. On the Series 65 you will see a handful of calculation questions and a larger number of "what does this number mean" interpretation questions. You do not need a financial calculator's advanced functions, but you must know each formula, be able to plug in numbers, and — most importantly — know what a rising or falling result signals. This subsection walks through the four required tool sets: the time value of money, descriptive statistics, liquidity/leverage ratios, and valuation ratios.

#### Time Value of Money (TVM)

The core idea is that a dollar today is worth more than a dollar in the future, because a dollar today can be invested to earn a return. All four TVM concepts below are just different ways of moving money forward (compounding) or backward (discounting) through time.

**Future Value (FV)** answers: "What will a sum today grow into?" It moves money *forward* by compounding.

Formula (plain text): FV = PV x (1 + r)^n

- PV = amount invested today
- r = periodic interest/growth rate (as a decimal)
- n = number of periods

Worked example: You invest $10,000 today at 6% compounded annually for 3 years.
FV = 10,000 x (1.06)^3 = 10,000 x 1.191016 = **$11,910.16**

**Present Value (PV)** answers: "What is a future sum worth in today's dollars?" It moves money *backward* by discounting — the reverse of future value.

Formula: PV = FV / (1 + r)^n

Worked example: You need $11,910.16 in 3 years and can earn 6%.
PV = 11,910.16 / (1.06)^3 = 11,910.16 / 1.191016 = **$10,000**

**Exam tip:** The higher the discount rate (r) or the longer the time horizon (n), the *lower* the present value. This inverse relationship is why rising interest rates push bond prices down — future coupons and principal are discounted more heavily.

A useful shortcut the exam sometimes rewards is the **Rule of 72**: the approximate number of years to double your money = 72 / rate. At 6%, money doubles in about 72 / 6 = 12 years.

**Net Present Value (NPV)** extends present value to an entire stream of cash flows. It is the sum of the present values of all expected inflows minus the initial cost (outflow), discounted at the investor's required rate of return.

Formula: NPV = (Sum of the PVs of all future cash inflows) - Initial Investment

Decision rule:
- NPV > 0 -> the investment earns more than the required return -> accept
- NPV = 0 -> earns exactly the required return
- NPV < 0 -> earns less than the required return -> reject

Worked example: An investment costs $1,000 today and is expected to pay $600 at the end of year 1 and $600 at the end of year 2. Required return = 10%.
- PV of year-1 cash flow = 600 / 1.10 = 545.45
- PV of year-2 cash flow = 600 / (1.10)^2 = 600 / 1.21 = 495.87
- Total PV of inflows = 545.45 + 495.87 = 1,041.32
- NPV = 1,041.32 - 1,000 = **+$41.32** (positive, so accept)

**Internal Rate of Return (IRR)** is the discount rate that makes NPV equal exactly zero. In plain English, IRR is the *actual* compound annual return the investment is expected to deliver, taking the timing of every cash flow into account.

Key relationship the exam loves:

| If... | Then... | Action |
|---|---|---|
| IRR > required return | NPV is positive | Accept |
| IRR = required return | NPV is zero | Indifferent |
| IRR < required return | NPV is negative | Reject |

**Exam tip:** For a bond, IRR is the same thing as **yield to maturity (YTM)** — it is the single rate that discounts all coupons plus the return of principal back to the bond's current price. If a bond is bought at par, its IRR/YTM equals the coupon rate (which at par also equals the current yield). IRR is superior to a simple average return because it accounts for the *time value* and *timing* of each cash flow.

**Common trap:** IRR assumes interim cash flows are reinvested at the IRR itself. Do not confuse IRR (a percentage rate) with NPV (a dollar amount). A question that gives you a discount rate and asks for a dollar figure wants NPV; a question asking for the rate that zeroes out NPV wants IRR.

#### Descriptive Statistics

Descriptive statistics summarize a data set (usually a series of investment returns) so the adviser can describe its central tendency, its dispersion, and its risk-adjusted quality.

**Mean, Median, and Mode** describe the "center" of the data.

- **Mean** = arithmetic average = sum of all values / number of values
- **Median** = the middle value when the data is sorted (with an even count, average the two middle values)
- **Mode** = the value that appears most frequently

Worked example using annual returns of 3%, 5%, 5%, 7%, and 30%:
- Mean = (3 + 5 + 5 + 7 + 30) / 5 = 50 / 5 = **10%**
- Median = middle of the sorted list = **5%**
- Mode = most frequent value = **5%**

**Exam tip:** Notice how one outlier (the 30% year) pulls the *mean* up to 10% while the *median* stays at 5%. The exam tests this: the median is more representative of a "typical" outcome when data is skewed by extreme values, whereas the mean is distorted by outliers.

**Range** is the simplest measure of dispersion: Range = Highest value - Lowest value. In the example above, 30% - 3% = **27%**. Range is crude because it ignores everything between the two extremes.

**Standard Deviation** is the exam's primary measure of an investment's **total risk** (also called total volatility). It quantifies how far, on average, returns deviate from their mean — a higher standard deviation means a wider spread of possible outcomes and thus greater risk.

Its power comes from the **normal (bell-curve) distribution**, where returns cluster around the mean in a predictable way:
- About **68%** of outcomes fall within ±1 standard deviation of the mean
- About **95%** fall within ±2 standard deviations
- About **99.7%** fall within ±3 standard deviations

Worked example: A fund has a mean annual return of 10% and a standard deviation of 15%. Then about 68% of the time its annual return should land between 10% - 15% = **-5%** and 10% + 15% = **+25%**. About 95% of the time it lands between -20% and +40%.

**Exam tip:** Standard deviation measures *total* risk — both systematic (market) and unsystematic (company-specific) risk. It is most meaningful when comparing two similar investments; the one with the higher standard deviation is the more volatile.

**Beta** measures **systematic risk** only — an investment's volatility *relative to the overall market*. The market (e.g., the S&P 500) has a beta of exactly 1.0.

- Beta > 1.0 = more volatile than the market (aggressive)
- Beta < 1.0 = less volatile than the market (defensive)
- Beta = 1.0 = moves in line with the market
- Negative beta = tends to move opposite the market

Worked example: A stock with a beta of 1.5 is expected to rise about 15% when the market rises 10%, and fall about 15% when the market falls 10%.

**Common trap:** Standard deviation vs. beta. Standard deviation captures *total* risk and can compare any two investments; beta captures only *market-related* (systematic) risk relative to a benchmark. A well-diversified portfolio can drive unsystematic risk toward zero, but beta/systematic risk cannot be diversified away.

**Alpha** measures **risk-adjusted excess return** — the amount by which an investment beat (or lagged) the return that would be expected given its beta. Conceptually, alpha = actual return - expected return (where the expected return is derived from the market's return and the investment's beta via CAPM).

- Positive alpha = the portfolio outperformed what its risk level predicted (often attributed to manager skill)
- Zero alpha = performed exactly as expected
- Negative alpha = underperformed for the risk taken

Worked example: If a stock's expected return based on its beta is 9%, and it actually returned 12%, its alpha is 12% - 9% = **+3%**.

**Sharpe Ratio** measures **risk-adjusted return using total risk (standard deviation)**. It tells you how much *excess* return an investor earned per unit of total risk.

Formula: Sharpe Ratio = (Portfolio Return - Risk-Free Rate) / Standard Deviation of the Portfolio

Worked example: A portfolio returned 12%, the risk-free rate (e.g., a T-bill) is 2%, and the portfolio's standard deviation is 8%.
Sharpe = (12 - 2) / 8 = 10 / 8 = **1.25**

A higher Sharpe ratio is better — it means more return per unit of risk. When comparing two portfolios, the one with the higher Sharpe ratio delivered the superior risk-adjusted performance.

**Common trap:** The Sharpe ratio uses **standard deviation** (total risk) in the denominator, and it subtracts the **risk-free rate** in the numerator (not the market return, and not zero). Do not confuse it with alpha or beta. If a question asks for return per unit of *total* risk, it wants the Sharpe ratio.

**Correlation and Covariance** describe how two investments move in relation to each other — the foundation of diversification.

- **Covariance** measures the *direction* of the relationship between two assets' returns. A positive covariance means they tend to move together; a negative covariance means they tend to move in opposite directions. Its size is unbounded and hard to interpret on its own.
- **Correlation** standardizes covariance onto a fixed scale from **-1.0 to +1.0**, making it directly interpretable.

Formula: Correlation (A,B) = Covariance (A,B) / (Standard Deviation of A x Standard Deviation of B)

Reading the correlation coefficient:

| Correlation | Meaning | Diversification benefit |
|---|---|---|
| +1.0 | Perfectly positive — move in lockstep | None |
| 0 | No linear relationship | Substantial |
| -1.0 | Perfectly negative — mirror opposites | Maximum (risk can be greatly reduced) |

**Exam tip:** Diversification reduces portfolio risk most when assets have **low or negative correlation**. Combining two assets with a correlation below +1.0 lowers the portfolio's overall standard deviation. This is the mathematical heart of Modern Portfolio Theory: you can reduce risk without necessarily sacrificing return by mixing assets that do not move together.

Quick reference for the risk/return statistics:

| Measure | What it captures | Key point |
|---|---|---|
| Standard deviation | Total risk (all volatility) | Higher = riskier; basis of the 68-95-99.7 rule |
| Beta | Systematic (market) risk | Market = 1.0 |
| Alpha | Risk-adjusted excess return vs. expectation | Positive = outperformance/skill |
| Sharpe ratio | Return per unit of total risk | Uses (return - risk-free) / standard deviation; higher is better |
| Correlation | How two assets move together | Ranges -1 to +1; low = better diversification |

#### Financial Ratios: Liquidity and Leverage

These ratios are drawn from a company's balance sheet and tell an analyst whether a firm can pay its short-term bills (liquidity) and how heavily it relies on borrowed money (leverage).

**Current Ratio** measures short-term **liquidity** — whether current assets are sufficient to cover current liabilities (obligations due within one year).

Formula: Current Ratio = Current Assets / Current Liabilities

Worked example: Current assets = $600,000; current liabilities = $300,000.
Current Ratio = 600,000 / 300,000 = **2.0 (or 2:1)**

A current ratio of 2:1 is often cited as healthy — the company has twice the current assets needed to cover its near-term debts. A ratio below 1.0 signals potential liquidity problems.

**Quick (Acid-Test) Ratio** is a stricter liquidity test. It removes **inventory** from current assets, because inventory is the current asset that is hardest to convert quickly into cash (and may have to be sold at a discount in a pinch).

Formula: Quick Ratio = (Current Assets - Inventory) / Current Liabilities

Worked example: Current assets = $600,000; inventory = $200,000; current liabilities = $300,000.
Quick Ratio = (600,000 - 200,000) / 300,000 = 400,000 / 300,000 = **1.33 (or 1.33:1)**

**Exam tip:** The only difference between the current ratio and the quick ratio is that the quick ratio subtracts inventory. If a company's current ratio looks strong but its quick ratio is weak, the firm is heavily dependent on selling inventory to meet its obligations.

**Debt-to-Equity Ratio** measures **leverage** — how much of the company's capital structure is financed by debt (creditors) versus equity (owners). It signals financial risk: the more debt, the greater the fixed interest burden and the more vulnerable the company is in a downturn.

Formula: Debt-to-Equity = Total Debt / Total Shareholders' Equity

Worked example: Total debt = $4,000,000; total shareholders' equity = $8,000,000.
Debt-to-Equity = 4,000,000 / 8,000,000 = **0.5 (or 0.5:1)**

A higher debt-to-equity ratio means more leverage and more financial risk; a lower ratio means the firm is more conservatively financed. (Some analyses use only long-term debt in the numerator; the exam focuses on the interpretation — more debt relative to equity equals more risk.)

#### Valuation Ratios

Valuation ratios compare a company's *market price* to a fundamental measure of value, helping the adviser judge whether a stock is relatively cheap or expensive.

**Price-to-Earnings (P/E) Ratio** is the single most-cited valuation metric. It tells you how many dollars investors are willing to pay for each dollar of a company's annual earnings.

Formula: P/E Ratio = Market Price per Share / Earnings per Share (EPS)

Where EPS = (Net Income - Preferred Dividends) / Number of Common Shares Outstanding.

Worked example: A stock trades at $40 and has EPS of $2.
P/E = 40 / 2 = **20** (investors pay $20 for each $1 of current earnings)

Interpretation:
- A **high P/E** typically reflects a growth stock — the market expects rapid future earnings growth and is willing to pay a premium.
- A **low P/E** typically reflects a value stock — priced modestly relative to current earnings.

**Exam tip:** A "trailing" P/E uses the past 12 months of actual earnings; a "forward" P/E uses projected earnings. A high P/E is not automatically "bad" and a low P/E is not automatically "good" — the ratio must be compared to industry peers and the company's own growth prospects.

**Price-to-Book (P/B) Ratio** compares the market price to the company's **book value** (net worth) per share. Book value is essentially what common shareholders would theoretically receive if the company were liquidated at balance-sheet values.

Formula: P/B Ratio = Market Price per Share / Book Value per Share

Where Book Value per Share = (Total Shareholders' Equity - Preferred Equity) / Common Shares Outstanding.

Worked example: A stock trades at $30 and has a book value per share of $15.
P/B = 30 / 15 = **2.0** (the market values the company at twice its accounting net worth)

Interpretation:
- **P/B < 1.0** means the stock trades below its book value, which value investors may view as potentially undervalued (though it can also signal trouble).
- **P/B > 1.0** means the market prices the company above its accounting net worth, reflecting expected future profitability or intangible value.

**Common trap:** Do not mix up the two valuation ratios. P/E is priced against *earnings* (from the income statement); P/B is priced against *book value/net worth* (from the balance sheet). Book value relies on historical accounting figures and often understates firms whose value lies in intangibles (brands, patents, software), which is why high-growth technology companies frequently carry high P/B ratios.

Summary of the ratios and what a higher number signals:

| Ratio | Formula | Higher number signals |
|---|---|---|
| Current ratio | Current Assets / Current Liabilities | Stronger short-term liquidity |
| Quick (acid-test) ratio | (Current Assets - Inventory) / Current Liabilities | Stronger liquidity excluding inventory |
| Debt-to-equity | Total Debt / Shareholders' Equity | More leverage / higher financial risk |
| Price-to-earnings (P/E) | Price per Share / EPS | More expensive per $1 of earnings (growth expectations) |
| Price-to-book (P/B) | Price per Share / Book Value per Share | More expensive relative to net worth |

### D. Types of Risk

Every investment carries risk — the chance that its actual return differs from what the investor expected, including the chance of losing money. For the Series 65, you must be able to name a risk from a short scenario, know which securities each risk hits hardest, and understand which risks can be reduced through diversification and which cannot.

The single most important framework in this subsection is the split between **systematic** and **unsystematic** risk:

> **Total risk = Systematic risk + Unsystematic risk**

- **Systematic risk** affects the entire market (or a broad segment of it) and **cannot be diversified away**. It is also called *market risk* or *non-diversifiable risk*.
- **Unsystematic risk** is unique to a single company or industry and **can be reduced or eliminated through diversification**. It is also called *diversifiable*, *specific*, or *non-systematic risk*.

**Exam tip:** Standard deviation measures an investment's *total* risk (both types combined). *Beta* measures only *systematic* (market) risk. A well-diversified portfolio can drive unsystematic risk toward zero, but its systematic risk (beta) remains.

| | Systematic risk | Unsystematic risk |
|---|---|---|
| Also called | Market / non-diversifiable | Specific / diversifiable |
| Source | The whole market or economy | One company or industry |
| Reduced by diversification? | **No** | **Yes** |
| Measured by | Beta | (Removed by diversification) |
| Examples | Market, interest rate, inflation, sector, geopolitical | Business/financial, credit/default, legal/regulatory, issuer-specific |

#### Systematic (Non-Diversifiable) Risks

These risks move whole markets at once, so holding more securities does not protect you.

**Market risk / interest rate risk.** *Market risk* is the risk that a broad decline in the overall market drags down nearly all securities regardless of their individual merits; it is the systematic risk of equities and is measured by beta (beta of 1.0 moves with the market, above 1.0 is more volatile than the market, below 1.0 is less).

*Interest rate risk* is the risk that changing interest rates change a security's value. It is felt most sharply in **bonds and other fixed-income securities**, which have an **inverse relationship with interest rates**: when market rates rise, the prices of existing bonds fall (their fixed coupons become less attractive than newly issued bonds), and when rates fall, existing bond prices rise. The longer a bond's maturity (and duration) and the lower its coupon, the greater its price swing for a given change in rates. Fixed-rate **preferred stock** and rate-sensitive equities (utilities, REITs) also carry meaningful interest rate risk.

**Exam tip:** "Interest rates up, bond prices down." The bond with the **longest maturity and lowest coupon** has the **greatest** interest rate (price) risk; a short-term, high-coupon bond has the least. A closely related idea is *reinvestment risk* — the risk that coupon payments must be reinvested at lower rates when rates fall (zero-coupon bonds have no reinvestment risk because they pay no coupons to reinvest).

**Purchasing-power (inflation) risk.** This is the risk that inflation erodes the real value — the buying power — of an investment's future dollars. It is the classic enemy of **fixed-income investments and cash**: a bond's fixed coupon and a fixed annuity's level payment buy less and less as prices rise. Assets whose values tend to rise with inflation — **common stock, real estate, commodities, and TIPS** — are considered better inflation hedges.

To see how much inflation costs an investor, convert a *nominal* return into a *real* (inflation-adjusted) return:

> **Real Return ≈ Nominal Return − Inflation Rate**

*Worked example:* A bond yields a nominal 5% while inflation runs at 3%. The approximate real return is 5% − 3% = **2%**. (The precise Fisher calculation is (1.05 ÷ 1.03) − 1 = 1.94%, but the exam accepts the simple subtraction.)

**Exam tip:** When a question asks which security has the **greatest purchasing-power risk**, the answer is almost always a **long-term fixed-rate bond or a fixed annuity**. **Common trap:** Do not confuse inflation risk with interest rate risk. Interest rate risk is about a bond's *price* changing when *rates* move; inflation risk is about the *real value* of the returns being eroded even if the price never changes.

**Sector risk.** This is the risk that a specific industry or sector — technology, energy, financials, biotech — declines as a group because of forces affecting the whole industry (regulation, commodity prices, technological shifts). The outline classifies it among the systematic risks because a portfolio concentrated in one sector bears the full brunt of an industry-wide downturn no matter which particular companies it holds; concentration in a single sector cannot be diversified away by adding more names within that same sector. Spreading holdings across *different* sectors is what reduces exposure to any one industry.

**Geopolitical risk.** The risk that political or global events — wars, elections, changes in government, sanctions, trade policy, terrorism, or international instability — move markets broadly. Its close relatives include *political risk* (especially acute in emerging or foreign markets) and, for overseas investing, *currency/exchange-rate risk*.

#### Unsystematic (Diversifiable) Risks

These risks strike individual companies or industries, so an investor can wash them out by holding many different, unrelated securities.

**Business risk and financial risk.** These are two distinct company-level risks:
- **Business risk** is the operating risk inherent to what the company does — competition, poor management, weak product demand, rising costs. Every operating company has business risk.
- **Financial risk** is the risk created by the company's use of **debt (leverage)** in its capital structure. The more a firm borrows, the larger its fixed interest obligations, and the greater the chance it cannot meet them in a downturn. A highly leveraged company (high debt-to-equity) has high financial risk; an all-equity firm has **no financial risk** (though it still has business risk).

**Exam tip:** If a question ties the risk to the *amount of borrowing or leverage*, the answer is **financial risk**.

**Credit (default) risk.** The risk that a bond issuer fails to make timely interest or principal payments (defaults), or that its creditworthiness deteriorates and the bond is **downgraded** (downgrade risk). Credit quality is graded by the rating agencies — **Moody's, Standard & Poor's, and Fitch**. Bonds rated **BBB−/Baa3 and above are "investment grade"**; below that they are **high-yield or "junk" bonds** carrying high default risk and paying higher yields to compensate. **U.S. Treasury securities are considered free of credit/default risk** because they are backed by the full faith and credit of the U.S. government.

**Common trap:** Treasuries have essentially **no** credit risk, but they still carry **full interest rate risk and purchasing-power risk** — a long Treasury bond can still lose value when rates rise or fail to keep pace with inflation.

**Legal/regulatory risk.** The risk that a change in laws, regulations, or tax policy — or an adverse court or regulatory action — hurts an investment or an entire industry. Also called *legislative risk*. Example: new environmental rules that raise costs across a mining sector, or a tax-law change that removes a benefit an investment relied on.

**Issuer-specific risk.** The risk unique to a single issuer — a product recall, accounting scandal, lawsuit, fraud, or loss of a key executive. This is the textbook example of diversifiable risk: spreading money across many unrelated issuers means one company's bad news barely dents the portfolio.

#### Opportunity Cost

**Opportunity cost** is the return an investor gives up by choosing one investment (or holding cash) instead of the next-best alternative. It is not money paid out of pocket — it is a *forgone* return, which is exactly why it is easy to overlook.

> **Opportunity Cost = Return on Best Forgone Alternative − Return on Chosen Investment**

*Worked example:* An investor keeps cash in a savings account paying 1% when a comparable, essentially risk-free 91-day Treasury bill yields 4.5%. The opportunity cost of staying in savings is 4.5% − 1% = **3.5% per year** in forgone return.

The **risk-free rate**, typically proxied by the **91-day (13-week) U.S. Treasury bill**, is the baseline opportunity cost of investing. Because investors could always earn that rate with virtually no risk, they demand a **risk premium** above it before accepting a riskier investment.

**Exam tip:** Opportunity cost underlies required-return and discount-rate questions — the rate used to discount future cash flows in a present-value calculation reflects the opportunity cost of capital. **Common trap:** Opportunity cost is a *forgone* return, not a cash expense; a "free" decision to sit in cash still has a real cost.

#### Capital Structure and Liquidation Priority

A firm's **capital structure** is the mix of debt and equity it uses to finance itself. That mix determines the **liquidation priority** — the order in which claimants are paid if the company is dissolved or goes bankrupt. Claims are settled from most senior (paid first, lowest risk) to most junior (paid last, highest risk):

| Priority | Claimant | What it is |
|---|---|---|
| 1 (paid first) | **Secured creditors** | Bondholders backed by specific collateral — mortgage bonds, equipment trust certificates, collateral trust bonds |
| 2 | **Unsecured creditors / general creditors** | Debenture holders (backed only by the issuer's full faith and credit) and trade/general creditors |
| 3 | **Subordinated debt holders** | Junior debt that ranks behind general creditors |
| 4 | **Preferred stockholders** | Equity, but paid ahead of common |
| 5 (paid last) | **Common stockholders** | Residual claim — whatever is left, if anything |

The rule of thumb: **all debt is paid before any equity, secured debt before unsecured debt, and preferred stock before common stock. Common stock is always last.**

Because junior claimants absorb losses first, they demand higher expected returns — which is why **common stock has the highest risk (and highest potential return), preferred stock is in between, and secured bonds have the lowest**. This links back to *financial risk* (heavy debt increases the fixed claims ahead of stockholders) and to *capital risk* (the risk of losing invested principal, greatest for common shareholders).

**Common trap:** Preferred stock is **equity**, not debt. Despite its fixed dividend and "senior" name, it ranks **below every bond and general creditor** — it is senior only to common stock. **Exam tip:** For the Series 65, apply the securities-holder order shown above — secured debt → unsecured/general creditors → subordinated debt → preferred → common.

---

## Part II — Investment Vehicle Characteristics

*25% of the exam · approximately 32 questions.*

### A. Cash and Cash Equivalents

"Cash and cash equivalents" is the most conservative rung of the asset ladder. It covers the actual money in a bank account plus a family of very short-term, very safe debt instruments that can be converted into cash quickly and with little or no loss of principal. On the Series 65 these instruments matter for two reasons: first, they are the parking place investors use for money they cannot afford to put at risk (emergency reserves, funds needed soon, the "safe" sleeve of an allocation), and second, they are the classic answer whenever a question asks which security carries the **least** market risk, the **most** liquidity, or the **shortest** maturity. Master the definitions and the comparisons and this subsection becomes easy points.

#### General Characteristics of Cash Equivalents

Every cash equivalent shares three defining traits. Memorize these as a package, because exam questions frequently describe an instrument by these characteristics and ask you to identify it as a cash equivalent (or vice versa).

- **High liquidity.** The instrument can be turned into cash quickly at a price at or very close to its full value. There is either an active secondary market (as with Treasury bills) or an on-demand claim against a bank (as with a checking account).
- **Short maturity.** Cash equivalents are short-term debt. The general benchmark is a maturity of **one year or less**; for accounting ("cash equivalent") purposes the stricter definition is an **original maturity of 90 days (three months) or less**. Short maturity is the key to safety: because the money comes back so soon, the price barely moves when interest rates change.
- **Low risk.** Two kinds of risk are minimal. **Default (credit) risk** is low because issuers are the U.S. government, insured banks, or the strongest corporations. **Interest-rate (market) risk** is low because of the short maturity — there is very little duration for rising rates to erode.

**Exam tip:** The reason cash equivalents have almost no interest-rate risk is their short maturity. A longer-maturity bond has more interest-rate risk; a 90-day T-bill has almost none. If a question links "long maturity" to "cash equivalent," that answer is wrong.

**Common trap:** Low risk is not **no** risk. The biggest genuine risks for cash equivalents are **purchasing-power (inflation) risk** and **reinvestment risk** — the yields are so low that inflation can erode real value, and when the instrument matures the investor may only be able to reinvest at a lower rate. Watch for questions where the "conservative" money-market investor is actually most exposed to inflation risk.

#### Insured Deposits: Demand Deposits

A **demand deposit** is money held at a bank that the depositor can withdraw "on demand" — at any time, without notice and without penalty. The classic example is a **checking account**; savings and NOW accounts offer similarly ready access to funds. Because the balance is payable immediately, a demand deposit is among the most liquid assets there is. In exchange for that instant access, demand deposits pay little or no interest.

Key points for the exam:

- No maturity date — the funds are available at all times.
- Highest liquidity, lowest (often zero) yield.
- Covered by **FDIC insurance** when held at an FDIC-member bank (see below).

#### Insured Deposits: Certificates of Deposit (CDs)

A **certificate of deposit** is a **time deposit**: the depositor agrees to leave a fixed sum with the bank for a fixed term (for example, 6 months, 1 year, 5 years) in return for a stated interest rate. Because the bank has use of the money for a set period, a CD generally pays more than a demand deposit. The critical distinction on the Series 65 is between **non-negotiable** and **negotiable** CDs.

##### Non-negotiable CDs

This is the ordinary retail bank CD. Its defining feature is that it **cannot be sold or transferred** to another investor — there is no secondary market. If the depositor needs the money before maturity, the only way out is to redeem it early with the issuing bank and pay an **early-withdrawal penalty** (typically a forfeiture of some months of interest). Non-negotiable CDs are usually smaller retail amounts and are **FDIC-insured up to the coverage limit**.

##### Negotiable CDs (Jumbo CDs)

A **negotiable CD** is a large-denomination CD that **can be traded in the secondary market** before maturity. Because it is negotiable, a holder who needs cash early does not pay an early-withdrawal penalty — instead, the holder **sells** the CD to another investor at the prevailing market price. Key features:

- Large minimum denomination — commonly **$100,000 or more**, frequently issued in $1,000,000 amounts (hence "jumbo").
- Actively traded, so it is a **money market instrument**.
- Maturities are usually one year or less (though they can be longer).
- Because it trades, its market value fluctuates with interest rates, so a seller before maturity bears **interest-rate/market risk** and may receive more or less than face value.

The table below summarizes the distinction — a favorite exam comparison.

| Feature | Non-negotiable CD | Negotiable (jumbo) CD |
|---|---|---|
| Secondary market? | No | Yes — can be traded |
| Typical size | Retail (small) | $100,000+, often $1 million |
| Early access to cash | Redeem early with penalty | Sell in the market (no penalty) |
| Price risk before maturity | None (fixed redemption) | Yes — value moves with rates |
| Money market instrument? | No | Yes |
| FDIC insured | Yes, up to the limit | Yes, up to the limit; excess uninsured |

**Common trap:** Investors sometimes assume a jumbo negotiable CD is fully insured. FDIC coverage is capped (currently $250,000 per depositor, per bank, per ownership category), so on a $1,000,000 negotiable CD only $250,000 is FDIC-insured — the remaining $750,000 is exposed to the bank's credit. Its safety beyond the cap depends on the issuing bank, not the FDIC.

#### FDIC Insurance

The **Federal Deposit Insurance Corporation (FDIC)** insures **deposits** at member banks against bank failure. The standard, commonly tested coverage limit is **$250,000 per depositor, per insured bank, per ownership category**. Because the limit is applied per ownership category (individual, joint, certain retirement, revocable trust, etc.), a household can lawfully obtain coverage above $250,000 at a single bank by titling accounts in different categories.

What FDIC covers vs. what it does not is a frequent exam point:

- **Covered:** demand deposits (checking), savings accounts, and CDs (both negotiable and non-negotiable) held at an FDIC-member bank — up to the limit.
- **NOT covered:** securities of any kind, including **stocks, bonds, mutual funds, and money market mutual funds**, even when purchased through a bank. Commercial paper and Treasury bills are also **not** FDIC-insured (T-bills instead carry the direct guarantee of the U.S. government).

**Exam tip:** FDIC insures **deposits**, not **investments**. If an item is a security, FDIC does not stand behind it. A money market **mutual fund** (an investment company product) is not the same as a money market **deposit account** — the fund is not FDIC-insured.

#### Money Market Instruments: Commercial Paper

**Commercial paper** is short-term, **unsecured** debt issued by large, creditworthy **corporations** (and some other institutions) to finance short-term needs such as payroll, inventory, and accounts receivable. Because it is unsecured, it is backed only by the general credit and reputation of the issuer, so only financially strong companies can issue it cheaply. Key features tested on the Series 65:

- **Maximum maturity of 270 days (nine months).** Issuers keep it at or under 270 days so it qualifies for an exemption from registration under the Securities Act of 1933; most commercial paper matures in a matter of days to a few months.
- Issued at a **discount** to face value (like a zero-coupon instrument): the investor pays less than par and receives par at maturity, and the difference is the interest earned. (Some paper is interest-bearing, but the discount form is standard.)
- Large denominations, typically **$100,000 or more**, so it is largely an institutional instrument.
- Rated by the rating agencies; the highest short-term tiers (e.g., A-1/P-1) signal top quality.

**Exam tip — the Uniform Securities Act exemption.** Commercial paper is an **exempt security** under the USA when it meets all three commonly tested conditions: a maturity of **nine months (270 days) or less**, issued in denominations of **$50,000 or more**, and **rated in one of the top three rating categories** by a nationally recognized rating agency. Being "exempt" means it is exempt from state **registration and advertising-filing** requirements — it is **not** exempt from the antifraud provisions.

#### Money Market Instruments: Treasury Bills (T-bills)

**Treasury bills** are short-term debt obligations of the **U.S. federal government**. They are the benchmark "risk-free" asset because they are backed by the **full faith and credit of the United States**, so their default risk is treated as effectively zero. Key features:

- **Maturities of one year or less** — commonly issued in terms of 4, 13, 26, and 52 weeks (the Treasury also issues certain other short terms).
- Sold at a **discount** and mature at **par (face value)**; T-bills pay **no periodic coupon**. The investor's return is the difference between the discounted purchase price and the face value received at maturity. This is why T-bills are quoted on a **discount basis**.
- Purchased at auction; highly liquid with a deep, active secondary market.
- **Taxation:** the interest (the discount earned) is **subject to federal income tax but exempt from state and local income tax** — the standard treatment for all direct U.S. Treasury obligations. This state-tax exemption is a frequent exam point.

**Common trap:** T-bills are essentially free of **default** risk, but they are **not** free of all risk — they still carry **inflation/purchasing-power risk** and **reinvestment risk**. "Risk-free" on the exam specifically means free of credit/default risk, not free of every risk.

#### Calculating the Yield on a Discount Instrument

Because T-bills (and discount commercial paper) have no coupon, their yield comes entirely from buying below par and being redeemed at par. Two calculations appear in test-prep material.

**1. Bank discount yield** — the conventional quoting method, which uses face value and a 360-day year:

Bank Discount Yield = (Discount / Face Value) x (360 / Days to Maturity)

where Discount = Face Value − Purchase Price.

Worked example: A $10,000 face-value T-bill is purchased for $9,800 with 180 days to maturity.
- Discount = $10,000 − $9,800 = $200
- Bank Discount Yield = ($200 / $10,000) x (360 / 180) = 0.02 x 2 = **4.00%**

**2. Investment (bond-equivalent) yield** — a truer measure of the investor's return, because it uses the actual amount invested (the purchase price) and a 365-day year:

Investment Yield = (Discount / Purchase Price) x (365 / Days to Maturity)

Using the same numbers:
- Investment Yield = ($200 / $9,800) x (365 / 180) = 0.020408 x 2.0278 = **4.14%**

Notice the investment yield (4.14%) is higher than the quoted bank discount yield (4.00%). That is always the case, for two reasons: the investment yield divides the gain by the **smaller** amount actually paid ($9,800, not $10,000), and it uses a **365-day** year instead of 360.

**Exam tip:** You are far more likely to be tested on the **concepts** — that T-bills are sold at a discount, pay no coupon, are quoted on a discount basis, and that their price rises toward par as maturity approaches — than to grind through a full bank-discount calculation. Know that a discount instrument's price is **below par** and that its return is the discount, and you will handle most questions.

#### Putting It Together: Cash Equivalents Compared

| Instrument | Issuer | Maturity | Return form | Insured/Guaranteed | Tradable (money market)? |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Demand deposit (checking) | Bank | None (on demand) | Little/no interest | FDIC to limit | No |
| Non-negotiable CD | Bank | Fixed term | Stated interest | FDIC to limit | No |
| Negotiable (jumbo) CD | Bank | Usually ≤ 1 yr | Stated interest | FDIC to limit; excess uninsured | Yes |
| Commercial paper | Corporation | ≤ 270 days | Discount (usually) | None — issuer credit | Yes |
| Treasury bill | U.S. government | ≤ 1 year | Discount | U.S. full faith & credit | Yes |

**Exam tip:** When a question asks for the **safest** or **most liquid** security overall, the answer is almost always the **Treasury bill** — it combines a short maturity, the deepest market, and the direct backing of the U.S. government. When it asks which cash instrument lets an investor sell before maturity without a penalty, look for the **negotiable CD** or a **Treasury bill**, not the ordinary bank CD.

### B. Types of Fixed Income Securities

Fixed income securities are debt instruments. When you buy a bond, you are lending money to the issuer in exchange for a promise to pay periodic interest (the coupon) and to return the face amount (par, typically $1,000) at maturity. This subsection covers the major issuer categories the Series 65 tests: U.S. government securities, asset-backed securities, corporate bonds, municipal bonds, and foreign-issued bonds. Because every bond type shares the same pricing and yield mechanics, we start with the fundamentals that recur throughout the exam.

#### Fixed Income Basics: Price, Yield, and Quotes

A bond's **par value** (face value) is usually $1,000 and is the amount repaid at maturity. The **coupon rate** (also called the nominal yield) is the fixed annual interest as a percentage of par. A 5% bond pays $50 per year per $1,000 bond, almost always in two semiannual installments of $25.

Bond prices move inversely to interest rates: when market rates rise, existing bond prices fall, and vice versa. A bond trading below par is at a **discount**; above par, a **premium**.

**Current Yield** measures annual coupon income relative to what you pay for the bond:

- Current Yield = Annual Coupon Income / Current Market Price

Worked example: A bond has a 5% coupon ($50 annual income) and trades at $950.
Current Yield = 50 / 950 = 0.0526 = **5.26%**.

The current yield (5.26%) exceeds the coupon (5%) because the bond was bought at a discount. This produces one of the most heavily tested relationships on the exam:

| Bond trading at | Order of yields (lowest to highest) |
|---|---|
| Discount (below par) | Nominal (coupon) < Current Yield < Yield to Maturity < Yield to Call |
| Premium (above par) | Yield to Call < Yield to Maturity < Current Yield < Nominal (coupon) |

**Exam tip:** Yield to maturity (YTM) accounts for the coupon *plus* the gain or loss from buying below or above par and holding to maturity. Yield to call (YTC) assumes the issuer redeems the bond early at the call date. For a premium bond, YTC is the *lowest* yield an investor should expect ("yield to worst").

Quote conventions differ by issuer and are frequently tested:

- **Treasury notes and bonds** are quoted as a percentage of par in **32nds**. A quote of "99-16" (sometimes written 99:16) means 99 and 16/32 = 99.50% of par = **$995.00** per $1,000 bond.
- **Treasury bills** are quoted on a **discount yield** basis, not as a price.
- **Corporate bonds** are quoted as a percentage of par; **municipal bonds** are often quoted on a yield (basis) basis.

Accrued interest day-count conventions also appear: **Treasuries accrue on an actual/actual basis; corporate and municipal bonds use 30/360.**

#### U.S. Government Securities: Treasury Bills, Notes, and Bonds

Marketable U.S. Treasury securities are direct obligations backed by the **full faith and credit of the U.S. government**. They are considered effectively free of **credit (default) risk**, which makes them the benchmark "risk-free" investment. They are *not* free of all risk—longer maturities still carry **interest rate risk** and **purchasing power (inflation) risk**.

| Security | Maturity at issue | Pays a coupon? | How sold |
|---|---|---|---|
| Treasury bill (T-bill) | 1 year or less (4, 13, 26, 52 weeks) | No — issued at a discount | Auctioned at a discount to par; return = par minus purchase price |
| Treasury note (T-note) | 2 to 10 years | Yes — semiannual | At or near par |
| Treasury bond (T-bond) | More than 10 years (20 and 30 years) | Yes — semiannual | At or near par |

A **T-bill** pays no coupon. You buy it below par and receive full par at maturity; the difference is your interest. **T-notes and T-bonds** pay semiannual interest and return par at maturity.

**STRIPS** (Separate Trading of Registered Interest and Principal Securities) are zero-coupon Treasuries created by "stripping" the coupon and principal payments and selling them separately. Like all zero-coupon bonds, they are bought at a deep discount and generate **imputed ("phantom") interest**—the annual accretion is taxed each year even though no cash is received until maturity.
