Taxation is a critical, high-yield exam point: **interest on all U.S. Treasury securities is subject to federal income tax but is exempt from state and local income tax.**

**Common trap:** Students reverse the tax treatment of Treasuries and municipals. Treasury interest is **federally taxable, state/local exempt**. Municipal interest is the mirror image—**federally exempt, potentially state taxable**. Memorize the two as opposites.

#### Treasury Inflation-Protected Securities (TIPS)

TIPS are Treasury securities designed to protect investors from **inflation (purchasing power) risk**. The coupon *rate* is fixed, but the **principal is adjusted for inflation** based on changes in the Consumer Price Index (CPI). Because the fixed rate is applied to a rising principal balance, the *dollar amount* of each semiannual interest payment rises with inflation (and can fall during deflation).

Worked example: An investor buys $1,000 of TIPS with a 4% coupon. Over the first year, inflation is 5%.

- Adjusted principal = $1,000 × 1.05 = **$1,050**.
- Annual interest = 4% × $1,050 = **$42** (versus $40 on the un-adjusted principal).

In practice the CPI adjustment and the 2% (half of 4%) semiannual coupon are applied every six months, but the concept is the same: principal grows with inflation, and interest is calculated on the higher balance.

At maturity, the investor receives the **greater of the inflation-adjusted principal or the original par**—a built-in **deflation floor** guaranteeing you never receive less than your original principal.

**Common trap:** The annual upward adjustment to principal is **taxable in the year it occurs**, even though the investor does not receive that principal until maturity. This is another form of **phantom income**, which is why TIPS are often held in tax-deferred accounts.

**Exam tip:** If a question asks which security best protects a client worried that rising prices will erode their fixed income, the answer is **TIPS**. If the concern is a bond with *no* reinvestment risk and a locked-in return, that points to zero-coupon bonds/STRIPS—but those offer no inflation protection.

#### Asset-Backed Securities (ABS)

An asset-backed security is created by pooling many individual income-producing assets and selling investors a claim on the cash flows those assets generate. The pooled collateral can be auto loans, credit card receivables, student loans, equipment leases, or—most commonly tested—home mortgages. Turning illiquid loans into tradable securities is called **securitization**.

**Mortgage-backed securities (MBS)** are the classic ABS. In a **pass-through** security, homeowners' monthly payments of principal and interest flow through to investors monthly. The main agency issuers are frequently tested on their differing government backing:

| Issuer | Nickname | Government backing |
|---|---|---|
| GNMA (Government National Mortgage Association) | Ginnie Mae | Directly backed by the **full faith and credit of the U.S. government** |
| FNMA (Federal National Mortgage Association) | Fannie Mae | Government-sponsored enterprise (GSE); **not** directly backed by the U.S. government |
| FHLMC (Federal Home Loan Mortgage Corp.) | Freddie Mac | Government-sponsored enterprise (GSE); **not** directly backed by the U.S. government |

**Collateralized mortgage obligations (CMOs)** repackage mortgage cash flows into different maturity classes called **tranches**, allowing investors to choose their preferred risk and maturity profile.

Two risks unique to mortgage-backed products are essential exam knowledge:

- **Prepayment risk:** When interest rates *fall*, homeowners refinance and pay off their mortgages early. Investors get their principal back sooner and must reinvest it at *lower* prevailing rates.
- **Extension risk:** When interest rates *rise*, homeowners pay off mortgages more slowly, so principal is returned later than expected—leaving the investor locked into a below-market yield for longer.

**Exam tip:** Only **GNMA** carries the direct full-faith-and-credit guarantee of the U.S. government. A common distractor treats Fannie Mae and Freddie Mac as government-guaranteed—they are not. Also remember that CMO cash flows are paid **monthly**, and that interest on agency MBS is generally **fully taxable at both the federal and state levels**, unlike direct Treasuries.

#### Corporate Bonds

Corporate bonds are debt obligations issued by companies to raise capital. They carry higher yields than Treasuries because they expose investors to **credit (default) risk**—the risk that the issuer cannot make interest or principal payments. Corporate bond interest is **fully taxable** at the federal, state, and local levels.

Corporate debt is classified by whether it is backed by specific collateral:

- **Secured bonds** are backed by specific assets. Examples: **mortgage bonds** (backed by real property), **equipment trust certificates** (backed by equipment such as railcars or aircraft), and **collateral trust bonds** (backed by securities the issuer owns).
- **Unsecured bonds** are backed only by the issuer's general creditworthiness. A **debenture** is an unsecured corporate bond; a **subordinated debenture** ranks *below* other debt in a liquidation and therefore pays a higher yield.

Bonds may also be **convertible** (exchangeable for the issuer's common stock), **callable** (the issuer can redeem them early, usually when rates fall), or **puttable** (the investor can require early redemption).

Creditworthiness is measured by ratings agencies (Moody's, Standard & Poor's, Fitch). The dividing line the exam tests is **investment grade versus high-yield ("junk")**:

| Category | Standard & Poor's / Fitch | Moody's |
|---|---|---|
| Investment grade (top four tiers) | AAA, AA, A, BBB | Aaa, Aa, A, Baa |
| Below investment grade / high-yield | BB and below | Ba and below |

**Exam tip:** The lowest investment-grade rating is **BBB (S&P/Fitch) / Baa (Moody's)**. Anything below that is speculative "junk," offering higher yield to compensate for higher default risk.

**Common trap:** In a corporate liquidation, the priority of claims is tested: secured creditors, then unsecured creditors (general creditors, including debenture holders), then subordinated debt holders, then preferred stockholders, and finally common stockholders. Bondholders always rank ahead of stockholders.

#### Municipal Bonds: General Obligation, Revenue, and Insured

Municipal bonds ("munis") are issued by states, cities, counties, and other local government entities to fund public projects. Their defining feature is **federal tax-exempt interest** (covered in detail below). Munis fall into three exam-critical categories.

**General Obligation (GO) bonds** are backed by the **full faith, credit, and taxing power** of the issuer. Repayment comes from the issuer's general tax revenues—**ad valorem (property) taxes** for local issuers, and income or sales taxes for states. Because GO bonds obligate the taxpayers, they typically require **voter approval** and are subject to statutory **debt limits**. They are generally considered safer than revenue bonds of the same issuer.

**Revenue bonds** are backed **only by the revenue generated by a specific project or facility**—a toll road, airport, hospital, water/sewer system, or stadium. They are **not** backed by taxing power, do **not** require voter approval, and are **not** subject to debt limits. Before issuance, a **feasibility study** estimates whether the project's revenue will be sufficient. Because repayment depends entirely on the project's success, revenue bonds generally carry **more credit risk and higher yields** than GO bonds.

| Feature | General Obligation (GO) | Revenue |
|---|---|---|
| Backed by | Full faith, credit, and taxing power | Revenue from a specific project |
| Repayment source | General taxes (e.g., property taxes) | User fees/tolls from the facility |
| Voter approval | Usually required | Not required |
| Subject to debt limits | Yes | No |
| Analysis focuses on | Issuer's tax base and finances | Project feasibility/revenue |
| Relative risk | Generally lower | Generally higher |

**Insured municipal bonds** carry a guarantee from a private bond insurer (historically firms such as MBIA and AMBAC) that interest and principal will be paid even if the issuer defaults. Insurance typically **raises the bond's credit rating** (often to the insurer's higher rating) and therefore **lowers its yield**—investors accept less income in exchange for the added safety.

Short-term municipal financing is done with **municipal notes** such as TANs, RANs, BANs, and TRANs (Tax, Revenue, Bond, and Tax & Revenue Anticipation Notes), which are repaid from expected future taxes, revenues, or a permanent bond issue.

#### Municipal Bond Tax Implications and Tax-Equivalent Yield

The central appeal of municipal bonds is that their **interest is exempt from federal income tax**. If an investor buys a bond issued *within their own state of residence*, the interest is usually also exempt from that **state's** income tax ("double tax-exempt"). Bonds from a different state are federally exempt but generally **state-taxable**.

**Common trap:** Only the *interest* is tax-exempt. Any **capital gain** from selling a municipal bond at a profit (or having it redeemed above your cost) **is taxable**. A question describing an investor who buys a muni at a discount and sells at a gain is testing whether you know the gain is taxable even though the coupon interest is not.

**Common trap:** Interest on certain **private activity bonds** (municipal bonds financing private projects) may be a preference item subject to the **alternative minimum tax (AMT)**. Not all municipal interest is entirely tax-free for every investor.

Because their interest escapes federal tax, munis pay lower nominal yields than comparable taxable bonds. To compare a tax-free muni against a taxable bond, use the **tax-equivalent yield (TEY)**:

- Tax-Equivalent Yield = Municipal (Tax-Free) Yield / (1 − Investor's Marginal Tax Rate)

Worked example: A municipal bond yields 4%. The investor is in the 32% federal marginal tax bracket.
TEY = 0.04 / (1 − 0.32) = 0.04 / 0.68 = 0.0588 = **5.88%**.

Interpretation: This investor would need a **fully taxable** bond yielding 5.88% to match the after-tax return of the 4% muni. If a comparable corporate bond yields only 5.5%, the muni is the better choice for this investor.

The reverse calculation converts a taxable yield into its after-tax equivalent:

- After-Tax Yield = Taxable Yield × (1 − Marginal Tax Rate)

Worked example: A corporate bond yields 5.5% for an investor in the 32% bracket.
After-Tax Yield = 0.055 × (1 − 0.32) = 0.055 × 0.68 = 0.0374 = **3.74%**, which is *less* than the muni's 4% tax-free yield—confirming the muni wins for this high-bracket investor.

**Exam tip:** The higher an investor's tax bracket, the more valuable tax-free municipal interest becomes. Municipal bonds are most suitable for **high-tax-bracket investors** and are generally **inappropriate inside tax-deferred accounts** (like IRAs), where the tax exemption is wasted and the investor gives up higher taxable yields for no benefit.

#### Foreign-Issued Bonds: Sovereign and Corporate Debt

Foreign bonds are debt securities issued by governments or corporations outside the United States. They can enhance yield and provide diversification, but they add risks not present in domestic bonds—most importantly **currency (exchange rate) risk** (the value of foreign-currency interest and principal changes as exchange rates move) and **political risk** (instability, expropriation, or changes in law in the issuer's country).

**Sovereign (government) debt** is issued by national governments. Examples include UK **gilts**, German **bunds**, and Japanese government bonds (**JGBs**). Credit quality varies enormously: debt of stable, developed economies may be very safe, while **emerging-market** sovereign debt can carry substantial default risk. A distinctive feature is that there is **no bankruptcy court for a nation**—if a sovereign defaults or restructures its debt, investors have limited legal recourse to compel repayment.

**Foreign corporate debt** is issued by companies domiciled outside the U.S. It carries the same credit risk as domestic corporate debt *plus* currency and political risk, and issuers may report under **different accounting standards** and lighter regulation than U.S. companies.

Two related terms are commonly tested for how bonds are denominated and where they are sold:

- **Yankee bonds:** issued by a **foreign** entity but denominated in **U.S. dollars**, registered with the SEC, and sold in the U.S. market. A U.S. investor buying a Yankee bond bears the *issuer's* credit risk but **no direct currency risk**, because payments are in dollars.
- **Eurodollar bonds:** denominated in **U.S. dollars** but issued and sold **outside** the United States. More broadly, a **Eurobond** is denominated in a currency different from that of the country where it is sold.

**Exam tip:** The single most-tested risk for foreign bonds (other than Yankee bonds) is **currency/exchange rate risk**. If the foreign currency weakens against the dollar, a U.S. investor's interest and principal decline in dollar terms even if the issuer pays in full. The key exception is the Yankee bond, which removes currency risk for the U.S. investor by paying in dollars.

### C. Characteristics and Valuation Factors of Fixed Income Securities

Fixed income securities (bonds) are debt instruments: the investor lends money to an issuer, and the issuer promises to pay periodic interest and to return the principal at maturity. On the Series 65, this topic is tested less through complicated math and more through your command of vocabulary, relationships, and a handful of formulas. If you understand how price, yield, coupon, and maturity fit together, most questions answer themselves. This subsection builds that framework piece by piece.

#### The Building Blocks: Par, Coupon, and Maturity

Every bond has three defining features:

- **Par value (face value):** the amount repaid at maturity, standardized at **$1,000** for most corporate and municipal bonds (Treasuries are also based on $1,000 par per bond). Par is the reference point for everything else.
- **Coupon rate (nominal yield):** the fixed annual interest rate stated on the bond, expressed as a percentage of par. A 5% coupon on a $1,000 bond pays **$50 per year**, almost always split into two semiannual payments of $25.
- **Maturity:** the date the issuer repays par and the bond ceases to exist.

Bonds are quoted as a **percentage of par**, where each "point" equals $10 (1% of $1,000). A bond quoted at 98 is priced at $980; a bond at 101.5 is priced at $1,015.

**Exam tip:** The coupon rate never changes over the life of a standard bond. What changes is the bond's market price, and therefore its yield. Keep "coupon" (fixed) and "yield" (moves with price) mentally separated.

#### Coupon Bonds vs. Zero-Coupon Bonds

A **coupon bond** makes periodic interest payments (typically semiannual) and returns par at maturity.

A **zero-coupon bond** makes no periodic payments. It is issued at a deep **discount** to par and "accretes" up to par at maturity; the investor's entire return comes from the difference between the discounted purchase price and the $1,000 received at maturity. Treasury STRIPS are the classic example.

Key features of zeros the exam loves:

- **No reinvestment risk on the coupons** (there are no coupons to reinvest), which makes them useful for funding a known future liability like a college tuition payment.
- **Highest volatility for a given maturity.** Because all the cash flow arrives at the end, a zero has the **longest duration** of any bond of the same maturity, so its price swings the most when interest rates change.
- **Phantom income.** The annual accretion (the imputed interest) on a taxable zero is taxed each year as ordinary income even though the investor receives no cash. This is a heavily tested trap (see Tax Implications below).

#### Pricing: Par, Premium, and Discount, and the Price–Yield Seesaw

The single most important relationship in fixed income: **bond prices and yields move in opposite directions.** When market interest rates rise, existing bond prices fall; when rates fall, existing bond prices rise. Picture a seesaw with price on one end and yield on the other.

Where a bond trades relative to par is determined entirely by comparing its fixed coupon to current market yields:

| Situation | Bond trades at | Why |
|---|---|---|
| Coupon = current market yield | **Par** ($1,000) | The bond is competitive as-is |
| Coupon < current market yield | **Discount** (below $1,000) | A below-market coupon must be offset by a lower price |
| Coupon > current market yield | **Premium** (above $1,000) | An above-market coupon is worth paying extra for |

**Common trap:** Students assume a "good" bond trades at a premium because something is wrong with a discount bond. Not so. A bond trades at a discount simply because its coupon is lower than what newly issued bonds offer. The premium/discount is a pricing adjustment, not a quality judgment.

#### Yield: The Four Yields and the Yield Hierarchy

There are four yields tested on the Series 65. Learning them in order is the whole game.

**1. Nominal yield (coupon yield).** Just the coupon rate — annual coupon divided by par. A 5% bond has a 5% nominal yield, always.

**2. Current yield.** Annual coupon income as a percentage of the bond's current market price. This reflects the cash return you actually earn at today's price.

> **Current Yield = Annual Coupon Income / Current Market Price**

*Worked example:* A bond has a 6% coupon (so it pays $60 per year) and is currently trading at $800 (a discount).

Current Yield = $60 / $800 = **0.075 = 7.5%**

Notice the current yield (7.5%) is higher than the nominal yield (6%) because the bond is bought at a discount. If the same bond instead traded at a premium of $1,200: Current Yield = $60 / $1,200 = 5.0%, below the 6% coupon.

**3. Yield to maturity (YTM).** The total annualized return if the bond is held to maturity, accounting for coupon income *plus* the gain (for a discount bond) or loss (for a premium bond) equal to the difference between purchase price and par, and assuming coupons are reinvested at the YTM. YTM is the bond's true "market rate of return" and is what people mean when they quote "the yield" on a bond. A discount bond has a built-in gain that pulls YTM above the current yield; a premium bond has a built-in loss that pulls YTM below the current yield.

**4. Yield to call (YTC).** The annualized return if the bond is called (redeemed early) at the first call date rather than held to maturity. Calculated the same way as YTM but using the call date and the call price in place of the maturity date and par.

**The yield hierarchy.** For a bond bought at a **discount**, the four yields line up in ascending order; for a bond bought at a **premium**, the exact reverse:

| Bond bought at a... | Yield ordering (lowest to highest) |
|---|---|
| **Discount** | Nominal < Current < YTM < YTC |
| **Premium** | YTC < YTM < Current < Nominal |
| **Par** | Nominal = Current = YTM (≈ YTC) |

**Exam tip — how to remember it:** List the four yields in this fixed order — Nominal, Current, YTM, YTC. For a **discount** bond they rank from lowest to highest in exactly that order; for a **premium** bond the ranking is the mirror image (Nominal highest, YTC lowest). The logic behind the endpoints: for a discount bond, a call lets you capture the gain up to par *sooner*, which raises the annualized return, so **YTC is the highest**. For a premium bond, a call forces you to absorb the loss down to par sooner, which lowers the return, so **YTC is the lowest** — this is why YTC is the **worst-case yield** on a premium callable bond, disclosed as the "yield to worst."

**Common trap:** Expect a question asking "which yield is highest (or lowest) on a premium bond bought at 105?" The answer for *highest* is nominal yield; for *lowest* it is yield to call.

#### Call Features

A **callable bond** gives the issuer the right to redeem the bond before maturity, usually at par or at a small **call premium** (e.g., 102). Issuers call bonds when interest rates have fallen, so they can refinance at a lower coupon — which is exactly when the bondholder least wants to give the bond up.

Consequences for the investor:

- **Call risk / reinvestment risk:** the bondholder gets cash back early and must reinvest at lower prevailing rates.
- **Capped upside:** a callable bond's price appreciation is limited near the call price, because buyers know it may be redeemed.
- **Call protection:** many bonds have a period (e.g., the first 5–10 years) during which they cannot be called; this is valuable to investors.
- Because of call risk, **yield to call** becomes the relevant valuation yield, especially for premium bonds.

**Exam tip:** Callable bonds carry a higher coupon than otherwise-identical non-callable bonds to compensate investors for accepting call risk. Contrast with a **put feature**, which benefits the investor (the holder can force early redemption) and therefore carries a *lower* coupon.

#### Duration

**Duration** is the weighted-average time (in years) to receive a bond's cash flows, and, more usefully, it is the standard measure of a bond's **interest rate sensitivity** — how much its price will move when yields change. Higher duration means greater price volatility.

The drivers of duration:

- **Longer maturity → higher duration** (more sensitivity to rate changes).
- **Lower coupon → higher duration** (less cash arrives early, so more of the value sits far in the future). A **zero-coupon bond's duration equals its maturity** — the maximum for any bond of that length.
- **Higher market yields → slightly lower duration.**

**Modified duration** translates this into an estimate: a bond with a modified duration of 7 will fall roughly **7% in price for a 1% (100 basis point) rise in yields**, and rise about 7% for a 1% drop.

*Quick illustration:* If interest rates rise 1%, a bond with a duration of 4 loses about 4% of its value, while a bond with a duration of 9 loses about 9%. Same rate move, very different pain.

**Exam tip:** If a client fears rising interest rates, recommend **short-duration** (short-maturity, higher-coupon) bonds to reduce price risk. If a client wants to maximize price gains from an expected *drop* in rates, favor **long-duration** bonds (long maturity, low or zero coupon).

#### Bond Ratings: Investment Grade vs. High Yield

Independent agencies — **Moody's, Standard & Poor's, and Fitch** — assess an issuer's creditworthiness (its ability to pay interest and principal). Ratings sort bonds into two broad camps:

| Grade | Moody's | S&P / Fitch |
|---|---|---|
| **Investment grade** (highest to lowest) | Aaa, Aa, A, **Baa** | AAA, AA, A, **BBB** |
| **High yield / "junk"** (speculative) | Ba, B, Caa, Ca, C | BB, B, CCC, CC, C, D |

The dividing line is critical: the **lowest investment-grade rating is Baa3 (Moody's) / BBB– (S&P and Fitch)**. Anything below that is **high yield** (also called junk or speculative-grade), which pays a higher yield to compensate for higher default risk.

**Exam tip:** Memorize the cutoff. A bond rated **BB** or **Ba** is *below* investment grade. Many fiduciaries and regulated institutions (pension funds, insurers) are restricted to investment-grade holdings, so a downgrade from BBB– to BB+ ("falling out of investment grade," a "fallen angel") can force selling and is a commonly tested consequence.

#### Credit Spread

The **credit spread** is the difference in yield between a bond and a comparable-maturity U.S. Treasury (the risk-free benchmark). It isolates the extra yield investors demand for taking on credit (default) risk.

> **Credit Spread = Yield on the bond − Yield on a same-maturity Treasury**

If a 10-year corporate bond yields 6.0% and the 10-year Treasury yields 4.0%, the credit spread is **2.0% (200 basis points)**. **Widening spreads** signal rising perceived risk or economic stress (a "flight to quality" into Treasuries); **narrowing spreads** signal improving confidence. Lower-rated (high-yield) bonds carry wider spreads than investment-grade bonds.

#### Market Liquidity

**Liquidity** is how quickly and cheaply a bond can be sold near its fair value. **U.S. Treasuries are the most liquid** securities in the world; large, highly rated corporate issues are reasonably liquid; small municipal issues and lower-rated corporates can be **thinly traded**, forcing a seller to accept a lower price (a wider bid-ask spread). This is **liquidity risk**, and it is one reason less-liquid bonds must offer higher yields to attract buyers.

#### Liquidation Preference

If an issuer goes bankrupt, claims are paid in a fixed priority order. Bondholders (creditors) always rank **ahead of stockholders (owners)**. From highest to lowest priority:

1. **Secured creditors** (e.g., mortgage bonds, equipment trust certificates — backed by specific collateral)
2. **Unsecured creditors** — **debentures** (backed only by the issuer's general credit) and general creditors
3. **Subordinated debentures** (junior debt, paid after senior debt)
4. **Preferred stockholders**
5. **Common stockholders** (last, residual claim)

**Exam tip:** A **debenture** is an *unsecured* bond backed only by the issuer's full faith and credit — a frequent definitional question. Within debt, "senior" and "subordinated" describe rank; secured bonds outrank all unsecured debt.

#### Conversion Valuation (Convertible Bonds)

A **convertible bond** can be exchanged by the holder for a fixed number of shares of the issuer's common stock. This gives the investor upside participation in the stock while retaining a bond's interest and priority — so convertibles pay a **lower coupon** than comparable non-convertible bonds.

Two formulas drive the math:

> **Conversion Ratio = Par Value ($1,000) / Conversion Price**
>
> **Parity (Conversion Value) = Conversion Ratio × Current Stock Price**

*Worked example:* A $1,000 par convertible bond has a conversion price of $40.

- Conversion Ratio = $1,000 / $40 = **25 shares** per bond.
- If the common stock trades at **$45**, the parity (conversion) value = 25 × $45 = **$1,125**. Converting yields $1,125 of stock, so the bond should trade at *at least* $1,125.
- Working backward, if the bond trades at **$1,080**, the **parity price of the stock** = $1,080 / 25 = **$43.20**. The stock must reach $43.20 for the stock and bond to be at parity.

**Exam tip:** The bond and stock are "at parity" when converting produces neither a gain nor a loss. If the bond's market price is *above* parity, it is trading at a **conversion premium**. Watch for questions that give you the conversion price and stock price and ask for shares, parity value, or parity price.

#### Tax Implications

Taxation of interest income is one of the most tested fixed-income concepts:

| Issuer | Federal tax on interest | State/local tax on interest |
|---|---|---|
| **U.S. Treasuries** | Taxable | **Exempt** |
| **Corporate bonds** | Taxable | Taxable |
| **Municipal bonds** | **Exempt** | Exempt in the issuer's home state; taxable if out-of-state |

An in-state municipal bond can be **"triple tax-free"** (exempt from federal, state, and local tax) for a resident. Because muni interest is federally tax-exempt, it appeals most to investors in **high tax brackets**, and munis carry lower stated yields than comparable taxable bonds. To compare fairly, use the **taxable-equivalent yield (TEY)**:

> **Taxable-Equivalent Yield = Municipal Yield / (1 − Investor's Marginal Tax Rate)**

*Worked example:* A muni yields **4%** and the investor is in the **32%** federal bracket.

TEY = 0.04 / (1 − 0.32) = 0.04 / 0.68 = **0.0588 = 5.88%**

The investor would need a **5.88%** yield on a taxable corporate bond to net the same after-tax return as the 4% muni. If comparable corporates yield less than 5.88%, the muni is the better after-tax choice.

**Common trap:** Municipal bond *interest* is federally tax-exempt, but a **capital gain** from selling a muni above your cost basis is **still taxable**. Tax-exempt applies to the interest, not to price appreciation.

**Phantom income on zeros:** The annual accretion on a **taxable** zero-coupon bond (e.g., a corporate zero or Treasury STRIP) is taxed each year as it accrues, even though no cash is received — hence "phantom income." A **municipal** zero, by contrast, accretes tax-free at the federal level. This makes tax-deferred accounts (IRAs) a natural home for taxable zeros.

#### Discounted Cash Flow: How a Bond Is Actually Valued

At its core, a bond's fair price is the **present value of all its future cash flows** — every coupon plus the return of par at maturity — discounted at the investor's required rate of return (the market yield):

> **Bond Price = Sum of the present values of each future coupon + present value of par at maturity**

When the required yield (discount rate) **rises**, each future cash flow is discounted more heavily, so the present value — the price — **falls**; when the required yield falls, the price rises. This is the mathematical engine behind the price–yield seesaw, and it explains why longer-maturity and lower-coupon bonds (whose cash flows sit further in the future) are more sensitive to yield changes.

#### Summary of Fixed-Income Risks

Pull the risk vocabulary together, because questions often ask you to match a client's concern to a risk or a solution:

| Risk | What it means | Who it hurts most |
|---|---|---|
| **Interest rate (market) risk** | Prices fall when rates rise | Long-duration bonds |
| **Reinvestment risk** | Coupons/principal must be reinvested at lower rates | High-coupon and callable bonds |
| **Credit/default risk** | Issuer fails to pay interest or principal | Low-rated / high-yield bonds |
| **Call risk** | Bond redeemed early, capping gains and forcing reinvestment | Callable premium bonds |
| **Liquidity risk** | Hard to sell near fair value | Small/thinly traded issues |
| **Purchasing power (inflation) risk** | Fixed payments lose real value | Long-term, fixed-rate bonds |

**Exam tip:** Note that duration/interest-rate risk and reinvestment risk pull in **opposite directions** — a bond held to maturity trades off one against the other. Zero-coupon bonds eliminate reinvestment risk on coupons but maximize interest-rate risk, a favorite contrast on the exam.

### D. Types of Equity Securities

Equity securities represent **ownership** in a corporation. Unlike a bond, which is a loan to the issuer, a share of stock makes the holder a part-owner with a claim on the company's earnings and, ultimately, its assets. The two broad classes of equity are **common stock** and **preferred stock**. On the Series 65 you must know the rights attached to each, how foreign ownership is packaged for U.S. investors through ADRs, and the several varieties of preferred stock — especially convertible and floating-rate preferred. This subsection walks through each in turn and then contrasts common and preferred directly.

#### Common Stock: The Basics of Equity Ownership

Common stock is the most basic ownership unit of a corporation. A common shareholder participates in the company's success primarily two ways: through **dividends** (a share of profits, paid only when the board of directors declares them) and through **capital appreciation** (a rise in the market price of the shares). Together these make up an investor's **total return**.

Common stock is attractive because it offers the greatest potential for long-term growth and historically serves as a **hedge against inflation** — as prices and corporate earnings rise, share values tend to rise as well. The trade-off is that common stock carries the most **market risk** and **business risk** of any equity position and sits **last in line** if the company fails.

Key features every candidate must know:

- **Limited liability.** A shareholder can lose no more than the amount invested; personal assets are not exposed to the corporation's debts.
- **Residual claim.** In a liquidation, common stockholders are paid **last**, after all creditors and preferred stockholders. The liquidation priority ladder is: (1) secured creditors, (2) unsecured (general) creditors, including debenture holders and other unsecured bondholders, (3) subordinated (junior) debt holders, (4) preferred stockholders, and (5) common stockholders.
- **Transferability.** Shares can generally be bought and sold freely.
- **Right of inspection.** Shareholders may review the corporate books and the list of shareholders.

**Exam tip:** Remember the phrase "common is *last* but has the *most* upside." Bondholders and preferred holders have priority in bad times, but common shareholders capture unlimited growth in good times.

#### Common Stockholder Rights: Voting and Preemptive Rights

Common shareholders have two rights that preferred shareholders usually lack: the right to vote and (sometimes) the preemptive right.

**Voting rights.** Common shareholders elect the board of directors and vote on major corporate matters (mergers, issuance of additional shares, and other fundamental changes). There are two voting methods:

| Method | How votes are allocated | Who it favors |
|---|---|---|
| **Statutory voting** | One vote per share *for each* open board seat, cast separately | Large/majority shareholders |
| **Cumulative voting** | Shares × open seats = total votes, which may be **concentrated** on one candidate | Small/minority shareholders |

Example: an investor owns 100 shares, and 3 board seats are open. Under **statutory** voting she casts up to 100 votes for each seat (100/100/100). Under **cumulative** voting she has 300 total votes and may pile all 300 on a single candidate.

**Exam tip:** Cumulative voting **benefits the small (minority) shareholder** because it lets them concentrate their voting power to elect at least one favorable director. This is a classic tested distinction.

**Preemptive rights.** A preemptive right lets existing shareholders maintain their proportional ownership by subscribing to newly issued shares *before* they are offered to the public. If a company issues new stock, a shareholder who owns 2% of the company can buy enough new shares to stay at 2%, avoiding **dilution** of their ownership percentage.

#### Domestic vs. Foreign Stock

**Domestic stock** is issued by a company organized and headquartered in the United States. **Foreign stock** is issued by a company domiciled outside the U.S. Investing directly in foreign shares gives U.S. investors diversification and access to growth abroad, but it introduces two risks the exam emphasizes:

- **Currency (exchange-rate) risk** — the value of the investment and its dividends, when converted back to dollars, changes as exchange rates move.
- **Political risk** — instability, expropriation, or changes in law in the foreign country can impair the investment.

Buying foreign shares directly can also be cumbersome: the shares trade on a foreign exchange, in a foreign currency, during foreign market hours, and dividends arrive in foreign currency. To solve this, U.S. investors typically use **American Depositary Receipts**.

#### American Depositary Receipts (ADRs)

An **American Depositary Receipt (ADR)** is a negotiable certificate issued by a **U.S. depositary bank** that represents a specific number of shares (or a fraction of a share) of a **foreign** company. The actual foreign shares are held in custody by a bank in the issuer's home country; the U.S. bank issues receipts against them. This lets Americans invest in foreign companies conveniently — the ADR **trades on U.S. exchanges (or OTC), in U.S. dollars, during U.S. market hours**, and settles like any domestic security.

How ADRs work in practice:

- **Dividends** are paid by the foreign company in its local currency, then **converted into U.S. dollars** by the depositary bank and passed through to the ADR holder.
- ADRs are a tool to facilitate **foreign investment**, so they still carry **currency risk** and **political risk** — the ADR's price reflects both the underlying stock's price and the exchange rate.
- ADR holders generally have **limited or no voting rights** and typically **no preemptive rights**. The depositary bank, as record holder of the foreign shares, usually votes on the holder's behalf or forwards proxy materials.

**Common trap:** Candidates sometimes think an ADR eliminates currency risk because it trades in dollars. It does **not**. The dollar price of an ADR still rises and falls with the foreign exchange rate, and dividends shrink when the foreign currency weakens against the dollar.

**Exam tip:** Know the one-line definition cold — "an ADR is a receipt issued by a U.S. bank representing shares of a foreign company, trading in U.S. dollars on U.S. markets, subject to currency and political risk."

#### Preferred Stock: Equity That Behaves Like a Bond

**Preferred stock** is an equity security, but it behaves in many ways like a fixed-income (debt) security. Its defining feature is a **fixed dividend**, stated either as a percentage of par or as a fixed dollar amount. Par value for preferred stock is conventionally **$100** (unlike the largely nominal $0.01 par often assigned to common).

Because the dividend is fixed, preferred stock is prized mainly for **income and safety**, not growth. Its two priorities over common stock are:

1. **Dividend priority** — preferred dividends must be paid before any common dividend.
2. **Liquidation priority** — in a bankruptcy, preferred is paid *before* common (but still *after* all creditors/bondholders).

In exchange for that priority and steadier income, preferred shareholders typically **give up voting rights and preemptive rights**, and they usually **do not participate** in the company's growth — the fixed dividend caps their income.

**Interest-rate sensitivity.** Because the dividend is fixed, straight preferred stock prices move **inversely to interest rates**, just like bonds. When market interest rates rise, existing preferred (with its now-below-market fixed rate) falls in price; when rates fall, preferred rises. This makes **interest-rate risk** the primary risk of most preferred stock.

**Common trap:** Preferred stock is often labeled a "fixed-income" or "senior" security, which can trick students into calling it debt. It is still **equity** — dividends can be *skipped* (they are not a legal obligation the way bond interest is), and preferred holders rank *below* every creditor in liquidation.

#### Calculating Preferred Dividends and Current Yield

**Stated (fixed) dividend.** The annual dividend equals the stated rate times par:

Annual Dividend = Stated Rate × Par Value

Worked example: A **5% preferred** stock with a **$100 par** value pays:

0.05 × $100 = **$5.00 per share per year.**

A preferred stock may instead be quoted directly in dollars — e.g., a "$6 preferred" simply pays $6 per year.

**Current yield.** Because preferred trades in the market at prices above or below par, its actual yield to a buyer is the **current yield**:

Current Yield = Annual Dividend / Current Market Price

Worked example: The $5 preferred above is trading at **$80** (below par because rates rose):

$5.00 / $80 = **6.25%.**

If instead it traded at **$125** (rates fell), the current yield would be $5 / $125 = **4.0%.** Notice the inverse relationship: as the price rises, the yield falls.

#### Types of Preferred Stock

Preferred stock comes in several varieties, and a single issue can combine features (e.g., "cumulative convertible preferred"). The commonly tested types:

- **Straight (noncumulative) preferred.** Pays the fixed dividend when declared. If the board **skips** a dividend, it is gone forever — the holder has no claim to the missed payment.
- **Cumulative preferred.** If the issuer skips (omits) a dividend, it accumulates as **dividends in arrears**. All arrears *plus* the current dividend must be paid to cumulative preferred holders **before any common dividend** can be paid. This makes cumulative preferred **safer** than straight preferred, so it typically carries a **lower** stated rate.
- **Participating preferred.** In addition to its fixed dividend, it may receive **extra** dividends when the company has strong earnings, effectively sharing in growth alongside common.
- **Callable (redeemable) preferred.** The **issuer** can buy the shares back at a preset **call price** (often at or slightly above par), usually after a set date. Callability benefits the issuer — companies call when rates fall so they can reissue at a lower rate — so callable preferred pays a **higher** stated rate to compensate the holder for that call risk.
- **Convertible preferred** and **floating-rate preferred** — covered in detail below.

**Cumulative preferred worked example.** A **6% cumulative preferred** ($100 par, so $6/year) paid no dividend for **2 years**. Before common shareholders may receive anything, the company must pay the two years of arrears plus the current year:

($6 × 2 years in arrears) + $6 current = **$18 per share** to cumulative preferred holders first.

**Exam tip:** With cumulative preferred, arrears do **not** earn interest — you simply add up the missed fixed dividends plus the current one. And skipped preferred dividends never trigger default the way skipped bond interest would.

#### Convertible Preferred Stock

**Convertible preferred** gives the holder the right to exchange each preferred share for a fixed number of **common shares**. This lets the investor collect a steady preferred dividend *and* participate in the upside of the common stock. Because that conversion feature is valuable, convertible preferred pays a **lower** stated dividend than comparable nonconvertible preferred.

Two numbers drive the math:

**Conversion ratio** = number of common shares received per preferred share. It is set by a **conversion price**:

Conversion Ratio = Par Value / Conversion Price

Worked example: A $100 par convertible preferred with a **conversion price of $25**:

$100 / $25 = **4 common shares** per preferred share.

**Conversion (parity) value** = what the preferred is worth if converted right now:

Conversion Value = Conversion Ratio × Current Common Price

If the common trades at **$30**:

4 × $30 = **$120.** The preferred is worth at least $120 in conversion value, so it should trade at or above $120.

**Parity price of the common** answers the reverse question — at what common price does the common equal the preferred's market price:

Parity Price of Common = Market Price of Preferred / Conversion Ratio

If the preferred trades at **$110**:

$110 / 4 = **$27.50.** The two are at parity when the common is at $27.50.

**Common trap:** If the common price is above parity (e.g., common at $30 while the preferred still trades at $110), an **arbitrage** opportunity exists — buy the underpriced preferred, convert, and sell the common. Exam questions test whether you can compare conversion value to the preferred's market price and spot which is cheaper.

#### Floating-Rate (Adjustable-Rate) Preferred Stock

**Floating-rate preferred** (also called **adjustable-rate preferred**) does **not** pay a fixed dividend. Instead, its dividend **resets periodically** based on a benchmark interest rate (for example, a reference short-term rate). When market rates rise, the dividend rises; when rates fall, the dividend falls.

The key consequence — and the whole reason it's tested — is **price stability**. Because the dividend adjusts toward market rates, the security stays attractive at roughly its par value regardless of rate moves, so its **market price is far more stable** than that of straight (fixed-rate) preferred. In other words, floating-rate preferred has **much lower interest-rate (price) risk**.

The trade-off: the investor gives up **income predictability**. The dollar amount of the dividend is uncertain and will fall if rates decline.

**Exam tip:** Contrast the two directly. **Fixed-rate preferred → stable income, volatile price.** **Floating-rate preferred → volatile income, stable price.** If a question asks which preferred best protects *principal value* when interest rates are expected to change, the answer is **floating-rate (adjustable-rate) preferred**.

#### Key Differences Between Common and Preferred Stock

The exam frequently asks candidates to distinguish the two directly. Use this comparison:

| Feature | Common Stock | Preferred Stock |
|---|---|---|
| **Ownership** | Yes — full equity owner | Yes — equity, but with debt-like traits |
| **Dividend** | Variable; only if board declares | **Fixed** (stated % of par or $ amount) |
| **Dividend priority** | Paid **last**, after preferred | Paid **before** common |
| **Growth potential** | High — participates in appreciation | Limited — capped by fixed dividend |
| **Voting rights** | **Yes** | Usually **no** |
| **Preemptive rights** | Often yes | Usually **no** |
| **Liquidation priority** | **Last** (residual claim) | Ahead of common, **behind all creditors** |
| **Primary risk** | Market/business risk | **Interest-rate risk** (like a bond) |
| **Primary appeal** | Growth + inflation hedge | Income + relative safety |
| **Par value** | Nominal (often $0.01) | Conventionally **$100** |

**Exam tip:** A reliable memory anchor: **preferred = safer, steadier income, priority, but no vote and no growth; common = riskier, no priority, but votes and unlimited growth.** Most Series 65 equity questions turn on one of these contrasts, on the ADR definition and its currency risk, or on the preferred-stock calculations shown above.

### E. Characteristics of Equity Securities

Equity securities represent **ownership** in a corporation. The two broad classes are **common stock** (the residual ownership interest, carrying voting control and the greatest upside) and **preferred stock** (a senior equity that trades more like a fixed-income instrument). This subsection covers the rights that attach to equity ownership, the special rules governing restricted and control stock, how dividends are paid and measured, and the two types of employee stock options. On the Series 65 these topics are tested mostly conceptually — you must know *who has which right*, *the order of priority*, and *how each item is treated* — with a few quick calculations (dividend yield and cumulative voting) mixed in.

#### Shareholder Voting Rights: Statutory vs. Cumulative

Common shareholders elect the board of directors and vote on major corporate matters (mergers, charter amendments, authorization of additional shares, etc.). Preferred shareholders normally do **not** vote. There are two systems for counting votes in a board election, and the difference determines whether a small shareholder can influence the outcome.

- **Statutory (regular) voting.** A shareholder may cast up to the number of shares owned **for each open seat**, but votes cannot be combined across seats. This favors **large/majority** shareholders, because a holder controlling a majority can fill every seat.
- **Cumulative voting.** A shareholder's total votes equal **shares owned × number of open seats**, and those votes may be concentrated on one or a few candidates. This favors **small/minority** shareholders, because it lets them pool votes behind a single director.

**Worked example — same investor, both systems.** An investor owns **200 shares** and **4 board seats** are up for election.

- *Statutory:* the maximum for any one candidate = **200** (200 per seat, not transferable). The investor could vote 200-200-200-200 across the four seats but no more than 200 for any single candidate.
- *Cumulative:* total votes = 200 × 4 = **800**, all of which may be placed on one candidate (e.g., 800-0-0-0).

Formula to remember: **Cumulative votes = Shares Owned × Number of Directors Being Elected.**

**Exam tip:** If a question stresses protecting the "little guy" or a minority shareholder's ability to win a board seat, the answer is **cumulative voting**. Statutory voting benefits those who already hold the most shares.

#### Preemptive Rights and Antidilution Protection

A **preemptive right** gives existing common shareholders the right — but not the obligation — to buy newly issued shares **before** they are offered to the public, in an amount sufficient to **maintain their proportional (percentage) ownership**. This protects them against **dilution** of both their ownership percentage and their voting power when the company raises capital by selling more stock.

- Preemptive rights are exercised through a **rights offering**. Each shareholder receives one **right** per share held; a stated number of rights plus a **subscription price** (usually below the current market price) buys one new share.
- Rights are short-term (typically a few weeks) and transferable — a shareholder who does not wish to subscribe may sell the rights in the market.
- "Antidilution provisions" is the broader term for contractual protections (common in preferred stock, convertible securities, and warrants) that adjust conversion terms or grant additional shares when new stock is issued, again to preserve value/percentage.

**Common trap:** Preemptive rights protect *percentage ownership*, not a guaranteed price. Also, a **right** (short-term, issued to existing holders, usually below market) differs from a **warrant** (long-term, a "sweetener" attached to a bond or preferred offering, usually with an exercise price *above* market at issuance). The exam frequently contrasts the two.

#### Liquidation Preferences and Priority of Claims

If a corporation is liquidated (dissolved or in bankruptcy), its assets are distributed in a fixed **order of priority**. Equity holders are last in line because they are **owners**, not creditors. The commonly tested ladder, from first paid to last paid, is:

| Priority | Claimant |
|---|---|
| 1 (paid first) | **Secured creditors** (e.g., mortgage bondholders, equipment trust certificate holders) |
| 2 | Unpaid **wages** and **taxes** owed |
| 3 | **General/unsecured creditors** (debenture holders, trade creditors) |
| 4 | **Subordinated debt** holders |
| 5 | **Preferred stockholders** |
| 6 (paid last) | **Common stockholders** |

The key concept is that **debt is always senior to equity**, and within equity, **preferred is senior to common**. A "liquidation preference" is exactly this: preferred stock has a stated par value (often $100) that must be satisfied ahead of any distribution to common shareholders.

**Exam tip:** Two memory anchors. (1) "**DEBT before EQUITY, PREFERRED before COMMON.**" (2) Common stockholders have **limited liability** — the most they can lose is their investment; they are never personally liable for the corporation's debts.

#### Restricted and Control Stock; Resale Restrictions (Rule 144)

Not all shares can be freely resold. Two categories are restricted, and **SEC Rule 144** governs how they may re-enter the public market.

- **Restricted (unregistered) stock** is acquired in a transaction that was *not* registered — most often a **private placement** under Regulation D. It bears a legend and cannot be sold publicly until Rule 144 conditions are met.
- **Control stock** is stock held by an **affiliate** — an officer, director, or large shareholder (the figure usually tested is a holder of **more than 10%**), i.e., someone in a control relationship with the issuer. Control stock may have been bought in the open market (so it is "registered"), but the *affiliate's* resale is still restricted because of the insider's influence.

**Rule 144 conditions (the commonly tested points):**

1. **Holding period (applies to restricted stock).** The securities must be held, fully paid, for at least **6 months** if the issuer is a **reporting company** (files with the SEC), or **1 year** if it is a **non-reporting** company. The holding period does **not** apply to control stock purchased in the open market.
2. **Volume limits (apply to affiliates).** In any **90-day** period, an affiliate may sell no more than the **greater of**:
   - **1% of the total outstanding shares**, or
   - the **average weekly trading volume** over the **preceding 4 weeks**.
3. **Form 144 (notice of sale).** An affiliate must file **Form 144** with the SEC at the time the sell order is placed if the sale within a 90-day period will exceed **5,000 shares OR $50,000**. Form 144 is effective for **90 days**.
4. **Non-affiliates get relief.** A non-affiliate who has satisfied the holding period may sell **without volume limits**; once the securities have been held for the full period (and public-information requirements are met), the shares essentially become freely tradable.

**Exam tip:** Remember the "**greater of 1% or average weekly volume**" test and the "**5,000 shares or $50,000**" Form 144 threshold — both are frequent exam facts. **Common trap:** Volume limits and Form 144 apply to **affiliates (control persons)**; a long-term non-affiliate holder is largely freed from these restrictions.

#### Dividends: Key Dates and Dividend Yield

A **dividend** is a distribution of a portion of a corporation's earnings to shareholders, declared at the discretion of the **board of directors** (there is no legal obligation to pay a common dividend). Four dates define the process:

| Date | What happens |
|---|---|
| **Declaration date** | The board announces (declares) the dividend, its amount, the record date, and the payment date. |
| **Ex-dividend date** | The first day the stock trades **without** the right to the upcoming dividend. A buyer on or after this date does **not** receive it; the seller keeps it. The stock price typically **drops by roughly the dividend amount** on this day. |
| **Record date** | The date on which you must be the **holder of record** to receive the dividend. |
| **Payment date** | The date the dividend is actually paid to shareholders of record. |

The ex-dividend date is tied to the securities **settlement cycle**. Under current **T+1** settlement (effective May 2024), a trade must settle by the record date to make the buyer a holder of record, so the **ex-dividend date falls on the same business day as the record date**.

**Exam tip:** Many older question banks were written under **T+2** settlement, where the ex-date was **one business day before** the record date. If a question clearly assumes T+2, apply "one business day before record"; otherwise, under current T+1 rules, ex-date = record date. The tested *concept* — that you must own the stock **before** the ex-dividend date to receive the dividend — never changes.

**Dividend Yield.** This measures the income return a stock pays relative to its price. The formula (plain text) is:

**Dividend Yield = Annual Dividends per Share / Current Market Price per Share**

**Worked example.** A stock pays a quarterly dividend of **$0.50** and currently trades at **$40**.

- Annualize the dividend: $0.50 × 4 = **$2.00 per share** per year.
- Dividend Yield = $2.00 / $40.00 = **0.05 = 5%**.

**Common trap:** Use the **annual** dividend, not the quarterly figure — a very common miss. Also note the **inverse relationship**: if the dividend is unchanged but the **share price falls**, the dividend yield **rises** (and vice versa), exactly parallel to the bond price/yield relationship.

#### Employee Stock Options: ISOs vs. NQSOs

Companies grant employees the right to buy company stock at a fixed **exercise (strike) price**. The two types differ almost entirely in **taxation**, which is what the Series 65 tests.

- **Incentive Stock Options (ISOs)** — also called *statutory* options.
  - **No regular income tax at grant or at exercise.** However, the **bargain element** (the spread between market value and the strike price at exercise) is an **AMT preference item**, so exercising can trigger **Alternative Minimum Tax**.
  - **Qualifying disposition:** if the shares are held **at least 2 years from the grant date AND at least 1 year from the exercise date**, the **entire gain** (sale price minus strike price) is taxed as **long-term capital gain** — the most favorable outcome.
  - If those holding periods are **not** met (a **disqualifying disposition**), the spread is taxed as **ordinary income**, like an NQSO.
  - ISOs can be granted **only to employees**, are subject to a **$100,000** annual limit on the value first exercisable, and generally must be exercised within **10 years** of grant.

- **Nonqualified Stock Options (NQSOs / NSOs).**
  - **Taxed as ordinary income at exercise** on the spread (market value at exercise minus strike price); this amount is also subject to payroll (FICA) taxes, and the **employer receives a tax deduction** for it.
  - Any further gain or loss from exercise to sale is a **capital gain or loss**.
  - NQSOs are **flexible** — they may be granted to employees, directors, consultants, and others.

| Feature | ISO (Incentive) | NQSO (Nonqualified) |
|---|---|---|
| Tax at grant | None | None |
| Tax at exercise | No regular tax; **spread is an AMT item** | **Ordinary income** on the spread |
| Best-case tax on sale | **Long-term capital gain** on entire gain (if holding periods met) | Capital gain only on post-exercise appreciation |
| Required holding for favorable tax | ≥2 yrs from grant **and** ≥1 yr from exercise | None (already taxed at exercise) |
| Who may receive | **Employees only** | Employees and non-employees |
| Employer deduction | Generally none (if qualifying) | **Yes**, at exercise |

**Exam tip:** The single most tested fact is *when the spread is taxed*: an **NQSO is taxed as ordinary income at exercise**, while an **ISO is not taxed for regular purposes at exercise but can create AMT** and, if the holding periods are satisfied, produces **all long-term capital gain**. **Common trap:** Watch for a "disqualifying disposition" — selling ISO shares too soon converts the favorable capital-gain treatment into **ordinary income**, erasing the ISO's main advantage.

### F. Methods Used to Determine the Value of Equity Securities

Analysts use two broad approaches to judge what a stock is worth and when to buy or sell it: **fundamental analysis**, which asks *"What is this company actually worth?"*, and **technical analysis**, which asks *"What are supply and demand doing to the price right now?"* Within fundamental analysis sit two specific valuation models the exam tests directly: the **dividend discount model (DDM)** and **discounted cash flow (DCF)**. This section walks through each, shows the math you must be able to do by hand, and then contrasts the two schools of thought.

#### Fundamental Analysis: Valuing the Business

**What it is.** Fundamental analysis estimates a security's **intrinsic value** — what the stock is really worth — by studying the company itself and the economy around it. A fundamentalist reads the firm's **financial statements**, examines its industry, and looks at macroeconomic conditions (interest rates, GDP, inflation). If the intrinsic value they calculate is *higher* than the current market price, the stock is considered **undervalued** (a buy); if intrinsic value is *lower* than market price, it is **overvalued** (a sell or avoid).

**Top-down vs. bottom-up.** A *top-down* analyst starts with the broad economy, narrows to promising industries, and finally picks companies. A *bottom-up* analyst starts with an individual company's financials regardless of the macro picture. Both are forms of fundamental analysis.

**The three core financial statements:**

| Statement | What it shows | Key idea |
|---|---|---|
| **Balance sheet** | Assets, liabilities, and shareholders' equity at a single point in time | Assets = Liabilities + Shareholders' Equity |
| **Income statement** | Revenues, expenses, and net income over a period | Shows profitability |
| **Cash flow statement** | Cash generated and used in operating, investing, and financing activities | Cash is not the same as reported earnings |

**Key financial ratios.** The exam expects you to recognize what each ratio measures and to compute the simple ones.

- **Working capital = Current Assets − Current Liabilities.** A dollar measure of short-term liquidity.
- **Current ratio = Current Assets / Current Liabilities.** Measures liquidity. A ratio of 2:1 means the firm has twice the current assets it needs to cover current liabilities.
- **Quick ratio (acid-test) = (Current Assets − Inventory) / Current Liabilities.** A stricter liquidity test because inventory is the current asset hardest to convert to cash.
- **Debt-to-equity ratio = Total Long-Term Debt / Total Shareholders' Equity.** Measures leverage; higher means more financial risk.
- **Earnings per share (EPS) = (Net Income − Preferred Dividends) / Common Shares Outstanding.**
- **Price-to-earnings (P/E) ratio = Market Price per Share / EPS.** How much investors pay per dollar of earnings; a "growth" stock typically carries a high P/E.
- **Dividend payout ratio = Common Dividends per Share / EPS.** The percentage of earnings paid out to common shareholders.
- **Book value per share = (Tangible Assets − Liabilities − Preferred Stock) / Common Shares Outstanding.** A rough "liquidation" value per share.
- **Return on equity (ROE) = (Net Income − Preferred Dividends) / Common Shareholders' Equity.** Profitability relative to owners' capital.

**Worked example — EPS and P/E.** A company reports net income of $10,000,000, pays $1,000,000 in preferred dividends, and has 3,000,000 common shares outstanding, trading at $60.

- EPS = ($10,000,000 − $1,000,000) / 3,000,000 = $9,000,000 / 3,000,000 = **$3.00**
- P/E = $60 / $3.00 = **20** (investors pay $20 for each $1 of earnings)

If it also pays a $1.20 common dividend, the payout ratio = $1.20 / $3.00 = **40%**.

**Worked example — current and quick ratios.** Current assets = $2,000,000 (of which inventory = $500,000); current liabilities = $1,000,000.

- Current ratio = $2,000,000 / $1,000,000 = **2:1**
- Quick ratio = ($2,000,000 − $500,000) / $1,000,000 = **1.5:1**

**Exam tip:** Remember which asset the quick ratio strips out — **inventory** (and any prepaid expenses). If a question hands you inventory, it usually wants the quick ratio, not the current ratio.

**Common trap:** EPS subtracts the **preferred** dividend from net income (preferred holders get paid before common), but it divides by **common** shares only. Don't subtract common dividends when computing EPS — common dividends are paid *out of* the earnings that EPS measures.

**How it's tested.** Expect questions that: (1) identify fundamental analysis as the study of a company's financial statements, industry, and the economy; (2) ask you to label a stock over- or undervalued by comparing intrinsic value to market price; (3) require a one-step ratio calculation; and (4) match a ratio to what it measures (liquidity, leverage, profitability, valuation).

#### The Dividend Discount Model (DDM)

**What it is.** The DDM is a fundamental-analysis tool that says a share of stock is worth the **present value of all the future dividends** it will pay. Because a dollar received years from now is worth less than a dollar today, each expected dividend is discounted back to the present using the investor's required rate of return.

**Constant-growth DDM (Gordon Growth Model).** When dividends are assumed to grow at a steady rate forever, the formula collapses to:

    Price = D1 / (r − g)

where:
- **D1** = the dividend expected next year = D0 × (1 + g)
- **D0** = the most recent dividend just paid
- **r** = required rate of return (the discount rate)
- **g** = constant annual dividend growth rate

**Worked example — constant growth.** A stock just paid a dividend (D0) of $2.00. Dividends are expected to grow 5% per year forever, and an investor requires a 10% return.

- D1 = $2.00 × (1 + 0.05) = $2.10
- Price = $2.10 / (0.10 − 0.05) = $2.10 / 0.05 = **$42.00**

If the stock currently trades below $42, a fundamentalist calls it undervalued.

**Zero-growth DDM.** If dividends never change (the classic case is **fixed-rate preferred stock**), growth g = 0 and the formula simplifies to:

    Price = D / r

**Worked example — zero growth.** A preferred stock pays a fixed $5.00 annual dividend and investors require an 8% return.

- Price = $5.00 / 0.08 = **$62.50**

**Exam tip:** In the constant-growth formula you must "grow" the dividend by one year first (use **D1**, not D0). Multiply the given dividend by (1 + g) before dividing.

**Common trap:** The denominator is **r − g**. The model only works when the required return exceeds the growth rate (r > g). It also cannot value a company that pays **no dividends** — a frequently tested limitation. For non-dividend payers, analysts turn to discounted cash flow instead.

**How it's tested.** Questions supply D0 (or D1), g, and r and ask for the theoretical price, or they describe the model conceptually ("the value of a stock equals the present value of its expected future dividends"). Know that DDM is a fundamental method and that its weaknesses are its dependence on dividends and its sensitivity to the growth and discount-rate assumptions.

#### Discounted Cash Flow (DCF)

**What it is.** DCF is the broader present-value technique behind the DDM. Instead of discounting only dividends, DCF discounts a stream of expected **cash flows** (or free cash flows) back to today at a chosen discount rate. It rests entirely on the **time value of money**: money available sooner is worth more than the same amount later.

**Core present-value formula:**

    Present Value = Cash Flow / (1 + r)^n

where r = the discount rate and n = the number of years until the cash flow is received. For a multi-year stream you discount each year's cash flow separately and add them up.

**Worked example — single cash flow.** An investment is expected to return $110 one year from now, and the appropriate discount rate is 10%.

- PV = $110 / (1 + 0.10)^1 = $110 / 1.10 = **$100.00**

So $100 today is a fair price for that future $110. If the asset were offered for $95, its cash flows are worth more than its price (attractive); at $105 it is overpriced.

**Worked example — a multi-year cash-flow stream.** Suppose a stock is expected to generate cash flows of $50 in year 1 and $50 in year 2, discounted at 10%.

- Year 1 PV = $50 / 1.10 = $45.45
- Year 2 PV = $50 / (1.10)^2 = $50 / 1.21 = $41.32
- Total intrinsic value = $45.45 + $41.32 = **$86.77**

If the market price is below $86.77, DCF flags the stock as undervalued.

**Discount rate.** The discount rate reflects the investor's **required return**, which rises with risk and with prevailing interest rates. Note the inverse relationship the exam likes: **as the discount rate rises, present value falls** (and vice versa) — the same reason bond prices fall when rates rise.

**Exam tip:** DDM is simply a *special case* of DCF where the cash flows being discounted are dividends. If a question contrasts them, DCF is the more general model and can value firms that pay no dividends by discounting free cash flow.

**Common trap:** Present value moves **inversely** to the discount rate. A higher required return does not make a security worth more — it makes its discounted value **smaller**.

**How it's tested.** Look for one-step present-value calculations (PV = future amount ÷ (1 + r)^n), the inverse relationship between discount rate and present value, and the conceptual point that DCF/DDM value a security as the present value of its expected future cash flows.

#### Technical Analysis: Reading the Market

**What it is.** Technical analysis ignores the company's fundamentals and instead studies **market data — price and trading volume — plotted on charts** to forecast future price movements. The technician assumes that all known information is already reflected in the price, that prices move in **trends**, and that historical patterns tend to repeat because investor psychology repeats. Technicians are concerned with *timing* (when to buy or sell), while fundamentalists are concerned with *selection* (what to buy).

**Trends.** A **trendline** connects a series of price points to reveal direction: an **uptrend** shows successively higher highs and higher lows; a **downtrend** shows lower highs and lower lows; a **sideways (horizontal) trend** stays within a range.

**Support and resistance.** These are the two most-tested technical terms:

- **Support** is a price level (a "floor") where buying interest has historically been strong enough to stop a price decline. Demand emerges here.
- **Resistance** is a price level (a "ceiling") where selling pressure has historically stopped a price advance. Supply emerges here.

A **breakout** occurs when the price pushes decisively **above resistance** (bullish signal) or **below support** (bearish signal). Technicians often act on breakouts. When a stock breaks above former resistance, that old resistance level frequently becomes new support.

**Other tools technicians use** (recognize, don't memorize deeply):

- **Moving averages** smooth out day-to-day noise to reveal the underlying trend.
- **Chart patterns** such as the head-and-shoulders (a reversal pattern) or consolidations.
- **Market sentiment / contrarian indicators:** the **short interest theory** (high short interest is bullish because those shorts must eventually buy to cover), the **odd-lot theory** (small investors are presumed to be wrong at market turning points), and put/call ratios.
- **Breadth indicators** such as the advance/decline line.

**Exam tip:** Fix the direction in your mind — **support is below** the current price (buyers step in), **resistance is above** (sellers step in). A break *below support* is bearish; a break *above resistance* is bullish.

**Common trap:** Technical analysis uses **charts of price and volume**, not financial statements, earnings, or economic data. Any answer choice that has a "technician" studying P/E ratios, balance sheets, or GDP is describing a *fundamentalist* and is wrong.

**How it's tested.** Expect definitions of support, resistance, trendlines, and breakouts; recognition that technicians chart price and volume to time trades; and identification of tools (moving averages, short interest, odd-lot theory) as technical rather than fundamental.

#### Contrasting Technical vs. Fundamental Analysis

The single most important distinction: **fundamental analysis tells you *what* to buy; technical analysis tells you *when* to buy.** Use this comparison to answer the "which analyst would…" questions that appear on the exam.

| Feature | Fundamental Analysis | Technical Analysis |
|---|---|---|
| **Central question** | What is the stock really worth? | Which way is the price headed, and when? |
| **Data used** | Financial statements, ratios, industry data, the economy (interest rates, GDP, inflation) | Charts of price and trading volume |
| **Goal** | Estimate **intrinsic value**; find over-/undervalued stocks | Identify **trends** and time entry/exit points |
| **Key tools** | P/E and other ratios, EPS, DDM, DCF | Support/resistance, trendlines, breakouts, moving averages, sentiment indicators |
| **Primary use** | Security **selection** (what to buy) | Market **timing** (when to buy or sell) |
| **View of price** | Price may differ from true value — that's the opportunity | Price already reflects all information; study its behavior |
| **Typical practitioner** | Long-term investor, portfolio manager | Trader, market timer |

**Exam tip:** Many practitioners use these two approaches together — pick a fundamentally sound company (fundamental), then use support/resistance to time the purchase (technical). The exam may reward recognizing that the methods are complementary, not mutually exclusive.

**Common trap:** Don't let a question blur the two. "An analyst reviewing a company's cash flow statement to estimate intrinsic value" is **fundamental**. "An analyst noting the stock bounced off its $40 support level three times" is **technical**. Match the *data source* — statements/economy versus charts — and you will place the analyst correctly every time.

### G. Equity Public Offering

When a corporation raises money by selling ownership (equity) to the public, it conducts an **equity public offering**. This subsection covers how new stock reaches investors, the difference between a company raising capital and an insider cashing out, and the special-purpose vehicles (SPACs) that have become a heavily tested topic. The organizing idea that ties everything together is the distinction between the **primary market** and the **secondary market** — master that first and the rest falls into place.

#### Primary vs. Secondary Market

The two "markets" describe *who receives the money* when a security changes hands.

- **Primary market** — where an **issuer** sells **newly created** securities to investors for the first time. The cash flows *into* the company (the issuer). This is capital formation. Every IPO and every follow-on issuance of new shares is a primary-market transaction. Sales in the primary market are made by **prospectus** and are regulated under the **Securities Act of 1933** (the "paper act" governing new issues).
- **Secondary market** — where investors trade **already-issued** securities among themselves. The issuer is not a party and receives none of the proceeds; money flows from one investor to another. Trading on the NYSE, Nasdaq, and the OTC markets is secondary-market activity, regulated primarily under the **Securities Exchange Act of 1934**.

| Feature | Primary Market | Secondary Market |
|---|---|---|
| Securities sold | Newly issued | Already outstanding |
| Seller | The issuer (corporation) | An existing investor |
| Who gets the proceeds | The issuer | The selling investor |
| Purpose | Raise capital for the company | Provide liquidity / price discovery |
| Governing act | Securities Act of 1933 | Securities Exchange Act of 1934 |
| Document delivered | Prospectus | Trade confirmation |

**Exam tip:** Ask "Does the issuing company receive the cash?" If yes, it is a **primary-market** transaction. If the money goes to another investor, it is **secondary-market** activity.

**Common trap:** Do not confuse the **secondary market** (any trading of outstanding shares) with a **secondary offering** (defined below — a specific type of registered distribution by insiders). They share a word but are different concepts, and the exam deliberately plays on the overlap.

#### Initial Public Offering (IPO)

An **initial public offering** is the **first** time a company sells its stock to the general public — the event where a private company "goes public." Because brand-new shares are being issued and the company receives the proceeds, an IPO is a **primary-market, primary offering**.

How an IPO works, in the sequence tested:

1. **Underwriting.** The issuer hires an **investment bank (underwriter)**, often forming a **syndicate** of several firms to spread risk and distribution. Under a **firm-commitment** underwriting the syndicate buys the entire issue and resells it, bearing the risk of unsold shares; under a **best-efforts** underwriting it merely acts as agent and returns unsold shares to the issuer.
2. **Registration.** The issuer files a **registration statement** with the SEC under the Securities Act of 1933, followed by a **cooling-off period** of at least **20 days**.
3. **Preliminary prospectus (red herring).** During the cooling-off period the underwriter may circulate a **preliminary prospectus** to gauge interest and take *indications of interest* — but may **not** sell shares or accept money yet.
4. **Effective date and final prospectus.** Once the SEC clears the registration, sales occur at a **fixed public offering price (POP)**, and every buyer must receive a **final (statutory) prospectus**.

**Exam tip:** SEC clearance ("effectiveness") is **never** an endorsement of the security's merit or accuracy. The SEC only reviews for **full and fair disclosure**; claiming otherwise is a prohibited representation.

**Common trap:** IPO shares are frequently **speculative and illiquid at first**, and allocations are often limited. On the Series 65, an adviser recommending an IPO must still apply a **suitability/fiduciary** analysis — "it's a hot IPO" is never a substitute for matching the security to the client's objectives and risk tolerance.

#### Secondary Offering (and Follow-On Offerings)

After a company is public, it can bring more shares to market. Here the terminology is precise and heavily tested.

- **Primary (follow-on / subsequent) offering:** The already-public company issues **additional new shares**. This raises new capital, so proceeds go to the **issuer**. It is still a *primary* offering — just not the *initial* one. A downside for existing owners is **dilution**: more shares outstanding shrinks each holder's percentage ownership and can reduce earnings per share.
- **Secondary offering:** A registered sale of **large blocks of already-outstanding shares by existing shareholders** — typically founders, early venture investors, or other insiders cashing out. The shares are not new; the company issues nothing and receives **no proceeds**. All cash goes to the **selling shareholders**.
- **Combined (split) offering:** Some registrations include **both** new shares (primary) and insider shares (secondary) in the same deal.

| | Primary Offering | Secondary Offering |
|---|---|---|
| Source of shares | Newly issued by the company | Previously issued, held by insiders |
| Who sells | The issuer | Existing large shareholders |
| Who receives proceeds | The issuer | The selling shareholders |
| Effect on shares outstanding | Increases (dilution) | No change |
| Examples | IPO, follow-on issuance | Founder/insider block sale |

**Worked example — following the money.** A public company files a combined offering: it issues **5,000,000 new shares** (primary) at a **$20** public offering price with a **$1.20** per-share underwriting spread, while insiders sell **2,000,000 existing shares** (secondary) at the same price and spread.

- Net proceeds to the **issuer** (primary portion) = new shares × (offer price − spread)
  = 5,000,000 × ($20.00 − $1.20) = 5,000,000 × $18.80 = **$94,000,000**.
- Proceeds to the **issuer from the secondary portion** = **$0**. The secondary shares gross 2,000,000 × $20.00 = $40,000,000, and after the spread the selling insiders net 2,000,000 × $18.80 = **$37,600,000** — none of which reaches the company.

**Exam tip:** The defining test question is *who gets the proceeds*. In a **secondary offering the issuer receives nothing** — the money goes to the selling shareholders even though the sale is registered and made by prospectus.

**Common trap:** "Secondary offering" does **not** mean "the second time a company sells stock." A company's *second* sale of **new** shares is a **follow-on primary** offering. A **secondary** offering specifically means **outstanding shares being sold by insiders**.

#### Special Purpose Acquisition Companies (SPACs) / Blind Pools / Blank-Check Companies
