A **special purpose acquisition company (SPAC)** is a shell company with **no commercial operations** that conducts an **IPO for the sole purpose of raising cash to later acquire or merge with an existing private company**, thereby taking that target public. Because investors buy in *before* any target is identified, a SPAC is the classic example of two overlapping exam terms:

- **Blank-check company** — a development-stage company with **no specific business plan**, or whose only plan is to merge with or acquire an unspecified company.
- **Blind pool** — an offering in which investors commit money **without knowing the specific investments** the sponsor will make; they are trusting the sponsor's judgment and track record.

Every SPAC is both a blank-check company and a blind pool. The typical lifecycle:

1. **IPO.** The SPAC sells **units** to the public, commonly one common share plus a fraction of a **warrant**, usually priced around **$10 per unit**. The offering is registered under the Securities Act of 1933.
2. **Trust account.** IPO proceeds are placed in a **trust**, generally invested in short-term U.S. **government securities**, and held while the sponsor searches for a target.
3. **Search window.** The sponsor typically has a **set deadline (commonly about 18–24 months)** to identify a target and complete a business combination (the **"de-SPAC"** transaction).
4. **Vote and redemption.** Shareholders vote on the proposed merger and generally have the right to **redeem** their shares for their pro-rata share of the trust (roughly the IPO price plus interest) if they disapprove.
5. **Liquidation if no deal.** If no acquisition is completed by the deadline, the SPAC is **dissolved and the trust is returned** to shareholders.

Key risks the Series 65 emphasizes:

- **Speculative / blind-pool uncertainty** — investors cannot analyze a target that does not yet exist; they rely entirely on the **sponsor**.
- **Conflicts of interest** — the sponsor's **"promote"** (often about **20%** in founder shares) rewards *completing a deal*, creating pressure to close even a mediocre acquisition.
- **Dilution** from founder shares and warrants.
- **Illiquidity and volatility**, especially around the de-SPAC vote.

**Exam tip:** When a question describes a company that "raises money through an IPO before identifying what it will buy," that is a **SPAC = blank-check company = blind pool** — all three labels point to the same speculative structure.

**Common trap:** Do not treat the trust account as a guarantee of profit. It protects the **return of principal** to investors who redeem or if the SPAC liquidates, **not** the success of any eventual acquisition. Recommending a SPAC still demands a full **suitability/fiduciary** analysis, because the underlying investment is unknown and high-risk.

### H. Types of Pooled Investments

A **pooled investment** collects money from many investors into a single portfolio managed toward a stated objective. Pooling gives the small investor three things they could not get alone: professional management, diversification, and economies of scale. The Series 65 expects you to know how each pooled vehicle is structured, how it is priced, how it is bought and sold, whether it is redeemable or traded, and — critically — which are registered "investment companies" and which are exempt private funds.

Start with the legal backbone. The **Investment Company Act of 1940** defines and regulates *investment companies* and sorts them into three classifications:

| 1940 Act classification | Examples | Managed? |
|---|---|---|
| **Face-amount certificate company** | Issues certificates promising a fixed sum at maturity | N/A (nearly obsolete) |
| **Unit investment trust (UIT)** | Fixed portfolio sold in "units" | No — supervised, not managed |
| **Management company** | **Open-end** (mutual fund) and **closed-end** funds | Yes — has an adviser and board |

Everything in this subsection is either one of these registered types (mutual funds, closed-end funds, UITs, most ETFs) or a vehicle deliberately structured to *avoid* investment-company registration (private funds; REITs are exempt on different grounds).

**Exam tip:** Memorize the three 1940 Act classifications and that "management company" splits into open-end and closed-end. A very common question simply asks you to identify which of these categories a UIT, mutual fund, or hedge fund falls into (a hedge fund falls into *none* — it is exempt).

#### 1. Mutual Funds: Open-End vs. Closed-End

Both open-end and closed-end funds are **management companies**: each has a board of directors, a registered investment adviser, and an actively (or index-) managed portfolio. What separates them is capitalization — how shares are created and priced.

##### Open-End Funds (the traditional "mutual fund")

An open-end fund has a *continuous* offering. It stands ready to **issue new shares** to any buyer and to **redeem** existing shares from any seller, every business day. Because supply is elastic, an open-end share never trades in a secondary market; you buy from and sell back to the fund itself.

Key structural features tested on the exam:

- **Redeemable securities.** Investors sell shares back to the fund at net asset value (NAV). The fund must pay redemption proceeds within **seven days**.
- **One class of security — common stock only.** An open-end fund may *not* issue senior securities (no bonds, no preferred stock) and is sharply limited in borrowing. It may, however, offer multiple *share classes* (A, B, C) that differ only in how sales charges are levied.
- **Forward pricing (Rule 22c-1).** Orders are executed at the **next** computed NAV, not the last one. NAV is calculated at least once per business day (typically 4:00 p.m. ET market close). You cannot know your exact price at the moment you place the order.

**Net asset value (NAV) per share.** This is the fund's "wholesale" value.

```
NAV per share = (Total Assets − Total Liabilities) / Shares Outstanding
```

*Worked example:* A fund holds $500,000,000 in assets and owes $20,000,000 in liabilities, with 24,000,000 shares outstanding.

NAV = ($500,000,000 − $20,000,000) / 24,000,000 = $480,000,000 / 24,000,000 = **$20.00 per share**.

**Sales charges (loads) and the Public Offering Price (POP).** Many mutual funds add a front-end sales charge. The price the investor pays is the POP:

```
POP = NAV + Sales Charge
POP = NAV / (100% − Sales Charge %)
Sales Charge % = (POP − NAV) / POP
```

*Worked example:* NAV is $20.00 and the fund carries a 5% front-end load.

POP = $20.00 / (1 − 0.05) = $20.00 / 0.95 = **$21.05**.
Dollar sales charge = $21.05 − $20.00 = $1.05.
Sales charge % = ($21.05 − $20.00) / $21.05 ≈ **5%** — note that the load is always figured as a percentage of the **POP**, not of NAV.

**The 8.5% ceiling.** FINRA caps the maximum sales charge on a mutual fund at **8.5% of the POP**, and a fund may charge the full 8.5% only if it offers investors all three of: breakpoints, rights of accumulation, and reinvestment of dividends at NAV. Otherwise the maximum is lower.

**Breakpoints and related concepts** (frequent suitability questions):
- **Breakpoints** — discounts on the sales charge at stated investment levels (e.g., a lower load at $25,000, $50,000, $100,000).
- **Letter of Intent (LOI)** — a non-binding letter to invest enough to reach a breakpoint within **13 months**; it may be **backdated up to 90 days**.
- **Rights of accumulation (ROA)** — lets prior holdings count toward reaching a breakpoint on new purchases.
- **Breakpoint selling** — deliberately keeping an order just *below* a breakpoint (e.g., recommending $24,000 instead of $25,000) to earn a higher commission. This is a **prohibited practice** and a favorite exam trap.

**12b-1 fees.** An annual, asset-based fee charged out of fund assets to cover distribution and marketing. It is reviewed by the board (at least quarterly). A fund may not call itself **"no-load"** if its 12b-1 fee exceeds **0.25%**.

**Share classes** (know the suitability logic):

| Class | Charge structure | Best suited for |
|---|---|---|
| **A** | Front-end load; lowest 12b-1; gets breakpoints | Large and/or long-term investors |
| **B** | Back-end load (CDSC) that declines to zero over years; higher 12b-1; converts to A | Smaller long-term investors (largely phased out) |
| **C** | Level load (~1% 12b-1); little/no back-end charge after ~1 year | Shorter horizons; **expensive if held long term** |

**Common trap:** Because Class C has no front-end load, students assume it is "cheapest." For a long holding period, the perpetual ~1% 12b-1 makes Class C the *most* expensive. Match the share class to the time horizon and amount invested.

##### Closed-End Funds

A closed-end fund raises a **fixed** amount of capital in a one-time IPO, then its shares **trade on an exchange or OTC** like any stock. The fund does **not** continuously issue or redeem shares.

- **Not redeemable.** To exit, you sell your shares to another investor in the secondary market through a broker (paying a commission or spread), not back to the fund.
- **Priced by supply and demand.** The market price can be **above NAV (a premium)** or **below NAV (a discount)** — it is not tied to NAV.
- **Priced continuously** throughout the trading day, not once at 4:00 p.m.
- **May issue senior securities.** Closed-end funds can issue preferred stock and bonds and use **leverage**, which open-end funds cannot.

**Premium/discount to NAV:**

```
Premium/Discount % = (Market Price − NAV) / NAV
```

*Worked example:* A closed-end fund has an NAV of $20.00 and trades at $18.00.
(18 − 20) / 20 = −0.10 = **trading at a 10% discount**. If instead it traded at $22.00: (22 − 20) / 20 = +10% **premium**.

**Open-end vs. closed-end — the master comparison (memorize this):**

| Feature | Open-end (mutual fund) | Closed-end fund |
|---|---|---|
| Shares outstanding | Variable (continuous issue/redeem) | Fixed (IPO) |
| How you exit | **Redeem** with the fund at NAV | **Sell** in the secondary market |
| Pricing | NAV-based (POP), forward priced daily | Market price; continuous; premium/discount to NAV |
| Secondary trading | No | Yes (exchange/OTC) |
| Senior securities / leverage | Not permitted | Permitted |
| Cost to buy | Sales charge/load | Commission/markup |

**Common trap:** "Closed-end fund shares are redeemable" is **false** — that is an open-end feature. Conversely, "a mutual fund can trade at a discount to NAV" is **false**; only closed-end (and other exchange-traded) vehicles do.

#### 2. Unit Investment Trusts (UITs)

A **UIT** is the second 1940 Act classification. It buys a **fixed, unmanaged portfolio** of securities and holds it, then sells redeemable **"units"** (units of beneficial interest) to investors.

Defining characteristics:
- **No active management.** There is **no board of directors and no investment adviser**, so there are **no management fees**. The portfolio is *supervised* (may be swapped only under narrow rules) but not traded around. A trustee holds the assets.
- **Fixed portfolio with a termination date.** A UIT has a defined maturity/termination point — for example, a bond UIT holds a set of bonds until they mature, then dissolves and returns principal.
- **Redeemable units.** Like a mutual fund, units are **redeemable** with the trust at NAV; the sponsor often maintains a secondary market as a convenience.
- Two broad forms: a **fixed (debt) trust** holding bonds to maturity, and an **equity trust** holding a fixed basket of stocks for a set term.

**Exam tip:** The single most-tested UIT fact is that it is **not managed** — no adviser, no board, no ongoing management fee. If an answer choice says a UIT is "actively managed" or "has a portfolio manager who trades to beat an index," it is wrong.

**Common trap:** Do not confuse a UIT with a *closed-end* fund. Both may be traded through a sponsor's secondary market, but UIT units are **redeemable** and the portfolio is **fixed/unmanaged**, whereas closed-end fund shares are **not redeemable** and the portfolio **is actively managed**. Note also that many **ETFs and insurance-company separate accounts** are legally organized as UITs.

#### 3. Exchange-Traded Funds (ETFs)

An **ETF** is a registered investment company — usually an **open-end management company**, sometimes a **UIT** — whose shares **trade on an exchange throughout the day** like a stock. Most ETFs are **passively managed to track an index** (e.g., the S&P 500), though actively managed ETFs exist.

What to know:
- **Intraday trading and pricing.** Unlike a mutual fund (one daily NAV), an ETF trades continuously at a **market price** set by supply and demand, and can trade at a small premium or discount to NAV.
- **Arbitrage keeps price near NAV.** Large institutions called **authorized participants** create and redeem shares in giant "creation units" (in kind) whenever price diverges from NAV, arbitraging the gap away. Retail investors **cannot redeem** individual shares with the fund — they must **sell in the market**.
- **Trades like a stock.** ETFs can be **bought on margin, sold short, and traded with limit/stop orders and options**. Investors pay a **commission/spread**, not a traditional sales load.
- **Low expense ratios and tax efficiency.** The in-kind creation/redemption mechanism minimizes taxable capital-gain distributions, and index ETFs have low costs.

**ETF vs. open-end mutual fund (frequent comparison):**

| Feature | Mutual fund (open-end) | ETF |
|---|---|---|
| Pricing frequency | Once/day at NAV (forward priced) | Continuous, intraday market price |
| How bought/sold | With the fund; sales charge | On an exchange; commission/spread |
| Margin / short selling / options | No | Yes |
| Retail redemption | Yes, at NAV | No (only APs redeem creation units) |
| Typical management style | Active or index | Mostly index (some active) |

**Exam tip:** The distinguishing ETF features are **intraday exchange trading, marginability/shortability, and low cost/tax efficiency**. **Common trap:** an ETF is *not* "redeemable" for an ordinary investor the way a mutual fund is; the retail investor **sells** the ETF in the market.

#### 4. Real Estate Investment Trusts (REITs)

A **REIT** is a company that **owns, operates, or finances income-producing real estate** and pools investor capital to do so. A REIT is **not** an investment company under the 1940 Act — it is a distinct tax entity — but the exam treats it as a pooled real-estate vehicle.

**Types by what they hold:**
- **Equity REIT** — owns and operates properties (apartments, malls, offices); income is mostly rent. Most common.
- **Mortgage REIT** — holds real-estate debt (mortgages/mortgage-backed securities); income is mostly interest; sensitive to interest rates.
- **Hybrid REIT** — combination of both.

**Tax structure (heavily tested):** To qualify for special "conduit" tax treatment, a REIT must generally distribute **at least 90% of its taxable income** to shareholders; it then avoids corporate tax on the distributed portion. Qualification also requires roughly **75% of assets** in real estate and about **75% of gross income** from real estate. REIT dividends are generally taxed to the investor as **ordinary income** (they are usually *not* "qualified dividends"), though a portion may be eligible for the 20% qualified business income deduction.

**Common trap — the DPP distinction:** A REIT **passes through income but NOT losses**. It is *not* a direct participation program (limited partnership). If a question says a REIT lets investors "deduct the property's operating losses/depreciation on their own returns," that describes a real estate **limited partnership/DPP**, not a REIT.

**Liquid (listed) vs. non-liquid (non-traded) REITs** — an explicit outline point:

| | **Listed (liquid) REIT** | **Non-traded (non-liquid) REIT** |
|---|---|---|
| SEC registration | Registered | **Registered** (SEC-registered, but not listed) |
| Trading venue | Trades on a national exchange | **No public exchange** listing |
| Liquidity | High — sell any trading day | **Low** — redemption programs are limited, suspendable, and often penalized |
| Pricing | Transparent market price | Opaque; NAV estimated periodically |
| Fees | Modest | **High upfront** commissions/offering costs |
| Suitability concern | Standard | Illiquidity, valuation uncertainty, high costs |

**Exam tip:** Distinguish these three: **listed/public** REITs (registered + exchange-traded = liquid), **non-traded/public** REITs (registered but *not* listed = illiquid, high fees, valuation risk), and **private** REITs (not registered, sold to accredited investors only). The exam's biggest concern with **non-traded REITs is illiquidity and lack of transparent pricing**, which drive suitability problems.

#### 5. Private Funds: Hedge Funds, Private Equity, and Venture Capital

**Private funds** are pooled vehicles deliberately structured to **avoid registration as investment companies** under the 1940 Act. They rely on one of two exclusions:

- **Section 3(c)(1)** — no more than **100 investors**.
- **Section 3(c)(7)** — an unlimited number of investors, but **all must be "qualified purchasers"** (generally individuals with **$5 million** in investments).

Because they are private, their interests are sold by **private placement (Regulation D)** to **accredited investors** — generally income over **$200,000** (individual) / **$300,000** (joint) for two years, **or** net worth over **$1 million excluding the primary residence**. They are illiquid, lightly regulated, and carry high minimums.

**Performance-based (incentive) fees.** Private funds famously charge **"2 and 20"** — about a **2% management fee** plus a **20% performance fee (carried interest)** on profits. Under **Investment Advisers Act Rule 205-3**, an adviser may charge such performance fees only to a **"qualified client,"** defined (current thresholds) as a client with at least **$1,100,000** managed by the adviser **or** a net worth over **$2,200,000**.

**The three private-fund types:**

- **Hedge funds** — aggressively managed pools that pursue absolute returns using **leverage, short selling, derivatives, and concentrated bets**. They often impose **lock-up periods** and redemption gates, so liquidity is limited. Investments in the underlying securities are generally liquid, but *investor access* is restricted.
- **Private equity (PE) funds** — invest in the **equity of private (or taken-private) operating companies**, often via **buyouts**, aiming to improve and later sell them. Structured as **limited partnerships** (investors are LPs; the fund manager is the GP), with **capital calls** over a multi-year horizon and long lock-ups (often 7–10+ years). Highly illiquid.
- **Venture capital (VC) funds** — a form of private equity focused on **early-stage, high-growth startups**. Very high risk, long horizons, and expected high failure rates offset by occasional large winners.

**Private funds vs. registered funds (the key contrasts):**

| | Registered fund (mutual/closed-end) | Private fund (hedge/PE/VC) |
|---|---|---|
| 1940 Act registration | Registered | **Exempt** — 3(c)(1) or 3(c)(7) |
| Who may invest | General public | **Accredited investors / qualified purchasers** |
| Offering | Public offering (prospectus) | **Private placement (Reg D)** |
| Liquidity | High (daily) | **Low** (lock-ups, gates, capital calls) |
| Performance fees | Restricted | **Common (2 and 20)** — only to qualified clients |

**Exam tip:** The defining traits of a private fund are that it is **unregistered under the 1940 Act, sold privately to accredited/qualified investors, illiquid, and charges performance fees**. **Common trap:** hedge funds are *not* registered investment companies and are *not* liquid to the investor — an answer describing a hedge fund as "SEC-registered and redeemable daily like a mutual fund" is wrong. Also remember the distinction in emphasis: **VC = startups/early stage; PE (buyouts) = mature private companies; hedge funds = tradable securities with aggressive strategies.**

### I. Characteristics of Pooled Investments

A **pooled investment** collects money from many investors and buys a single, professionally managed portfolio of securities. Each investor owns a proportional share of that portfolio. The four types you must know are:

| Vehicle | Legal form | How shares are priced | Where you buy/sell |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Open-end fund** (mutual fund) | Continuously issues/redeems shares | At NAV (forward priced) once per day | Bought from and redeemed by the fund itself |
| **Closed-end fund (CEF)** | Fixed number of shares, one-time IPO | Market price set by supply/demand | Traded on an exchange all day |
| **Exchange-traded fund (ETF)** | Usually open-end structure with a creation/redemption mechanism | Market price, kept near NAV by arbitrage | Traded on an exchange all day |
| **Unit investment trust (UIT)** | Fixed, unmanaged portfolio, set termination date | At NAV | Redeemable with the trust |

Most Series 65 questions center on **open-end mutual funds**, so start there and treat CEFs, ETFs, and UITs as contrasts.

**Exam tip:** The single most important structural fact is *redeemability*. Open-end fund shares and UIT units are **redeemable securities** — you sell them back to the issuer at NAV. Closed-end fund shares are **not** redeemable at all; ETF shares are redeemable only by authorized participants in large blocks. An ordinary investor exits a CEF or ETF by selling to another investor on an exchange at the prevailing market price.

#### Pricing: Net Asset Value (NAV) and Forward Pricing

**NAV** is the per-share value of the fund's portfolio. It is the foundation for everything else in this subsection.

> **NAV = (Total Assets − Total Liabilities) / Number of Shares Outstanding**

**Worked example.** A fund holds securities and cash worth $500,000,000, owes $20,000,000 in liabilities, and has 24,000,000 shares outstanding.

- NAV = ($500,000,000 − $20,000,000) / 24,000,000
- NAV = $480,000,000 / 24,000,000 = **$20.00 per share**

**Forward pricing** is the rule (Rule 22c-1 under the Investment Company Act of 1940) that mutual fund orders are executed at the **next** NAV calculated after the order is received — not the last posted price. NAV must be computed at least once every business day, and in practice this happens at the close of the market (4:00 p.m. ET). An order to buy or redeem placed at 2:00 p.m. gets that day's 4:00 p.m. NAV; an order placed at 4:30 p.m. gets the *next* business day's NAV.

**Common trap:** Forward pricing exists to prevent **late trading** and stale-price arbitrage. Answer choices suggesting an investor can lock in the "current" or "known" price of a mutual fund are wrong — the exact NAV is unknown at the moment the order is entered.

#### Pricing: Discount and Premium (ETFs and Closed-End Funds)

Because closed-end funds and ETFs trade on an exchange, their **market price is set by supply and demand** and can differ from NAV.

- **Premium:** market price is *above* NAV.
- **Discount:** market price is *below* NAV.

> **Premium/Discount % = (Market Price − NAV) / NAV**

**Worked example (closed-end fund).** A CEF has an NAV of $22.00 but trades at $19.80.

- (19.80 − 22.00) / 22.00 = −2.20 / 22.00 = −0.10 = **trading at a 10% discount**

**Why CEFs can drift from NAV:** A closed-end fund has a fixed share count and no redemption mechanism, so nothing forces price toward NAV. Many CEFs trade at persistent discounts (sometimes premiums), driven purely by market demand.

**Why ETFs stay close to NAV:** ETFs have a **creation/redemption** mechanism. Large institutions ("authorized participants") can exchange baskets of the underlying securities for ETF shares and vice versa. If the ETF trades above NAV, arbitrageurs create new shares and sell them (pushing price down); if below NAV, they buy shares and redeem them (pushing price up). This **arbitrage** keeps the ETF's market price tightly aligned with NAV.

**Exam tip:** Only exchange-traded pooled products (CEFs and ETFs) can trade at a premium or discount. An **open-end mutual fund never trades at a discount or premium** — it is always transacted at NAV (plus any sales charge on the buy side).

#### Fee Structures and Other Costs

There are two very different kinds of cost, and the exam tests whether you can tell them apart:

1. **Sales charges (loads)** — one-time charges tied to buying or selling, paid to compensate the selling broker-dealer.
2. **Ongoing operating expenses (the expense ratio)** — annual costs deducted from fund assets every year you hold the fund.

**Loads and the Public Offering Price (POP).** For a front-end-load mutual fund, the price you pay (the POP) equals NAV plus the sales charge.

> **POP = NAV + Sales Charge**, and equivalently **POP = NAV / (1 − Sales Charge %)**
> **Sales Charge % = (POP − NAV) / POP**

**Worked example.** A Class A fund has an NAV of $20.00 and a 5% sales charge.

- POP = $20.00 / (1 − 0.05) = $20.00 / 0.95 = **$21.05**
- Dollar sales charge = $21.05 − $20.00 = $1.05
- Check: $1.05 / $21.05 = 4.99% ≈ 5%

**Common trap:** The sales charge percentage is figured **on the POP, not the NAV**. Do *not* compute POP as $20.00 × 1.05 = $21.00 — that understates the load. Always divide NAV by (1 − load %).

**Maximum sales charge:** Under FINRA rules, a mutual fund's total sales charge may not exceed **8.5% of the POP**. To charge the full 8.5%, the fund must offer investors **breakpoints, rights of accumulation, and reinvestment of dividends at NAV**; missing any of these lowers the cap.

**Breakpoints** are volume discounts — the sales charge percentage drops at stated investment thresholds. Two ways to reach a breakpoint:
- **Letter of Intent (LOI):** the investor pledges to invest enough to reach a breakpoint within **13 months**; it may be **backdated up to 90 days**. Shares are held in escrow to cover the difference if the investor falls short.
- **Rights of accumulation (ROA):** existing holdings count toward the next breakpoint on new purchases (no time limit).

**Common trap: breakpoint sales.** It is a prohibited practice to recommend a purchase just *below* a breakpoint (e.g., $24,000 when the discount begins at $25,000) so as to earn a higher commission.

**Expense ratio.** This is the fund's **annual operating cost** as a percentage of average net assets. It captures the management (advisory) fee, 12b-1 fees, and other administrative costs.

> **Expense Ratio = Annual Fund Operating Expenses / Average Net Assets**

**Worked example.** A fund incurs $15,000,000 of operating expenses on average net assets of $1,000,000,000.

- Expense Ratio = $15,000,000 / $1,000,000,000 = 0.015 = **1.5%**

**Exam tip:** The expense ratio **includes** the management fee and 12b-1 fees but **excludes sales loads** (loads are a one-time charge, not an annual operating expense) and **excludes portfolio brokerage/transaction costs**.

**12b-1 fees.** Named after the 1940 Act rule that permits them, these are annual fees charged against fund assets to pay for **distribution and marketing** (advertising, selling, and shareholder-service costs). Because they are deducted every year, they raise the expense ratio and are a form of ongoing cost that ultimately compensates for distribution.

- The maximum 12b-1 fee is **0.75%** for distribution/marketing, plus up to **0.25%** for shareholder services — **1.00% total**.
- A fund may call itself **"no-load"** only if it charges no sales load and its 12b-1/service fee is **0.25% or less**.

**Exam tip:** A "no-load" fund still has an expense ratio — it just has no sales charge and a low (≤0.25%) 12b-1 fee. "No-load" does **not** mean "no cost."

#### Share Classes (A, B, and C)

Many funds offer the *same* portfolio through different share classes that repackage *when and how* the investor pays. The portfolio is identical; only the cost structure differs.

| Feature | **Class A** | **Class B** | **Class C** |
|---|---|---|---|
| Front-end load | Yes (paid at purchase) | No | No |
| Back-end load (CDSC) | Usually none | Yes, **declining** over time | Small (often 1% for the first year), then none |
| 12b-1 / ongoing expenses | **Lowest** | High | High (level ~1%) |
| Breakpoints | Yes | No | No |
| Converts to Class A? | — | Yes, after several years | Historically no (level load continues) |
| Best suited for | **Large amounts / long horizons** | Rarely offered today | **Smaller amounts / shorter-to-intermediate horizons** |

- **Class A** — pay a front-end load up front, but enjoy the lowest annual expenses and qualify for breakpoints. Cheapest for a large investment held a long time.
- **Class B** — no front-end load, but a **contingent deferred sales charge (CDSC)** applies if you redeem early; the CDSC declines each year to zero (e.g., 5% year 1 down to 0% by year 6), after which the shares typically convert to lower-cost Class A. B shares carry high 12b-1 fees while held and have largely been phased out in the industry.
- **Class C** — no front-end load and usually a small 1% CDSC only in the first year, but a **level load** — persistently high (~1%) annual 12b-1 expenses that never step down. Cheapest for short holding periods; most expensive over long periods.

**Exam tip:** Match the class to the **investment amount and time horizon**. Large + long-term → **Class A** (breakpoints + low ongoing cost). Short-term → **Class C** (no front load, small CDSC). Recommending **Class B or C for a very large, long-term purchase** is a classic *unsuitable* recommendation because the investor loses A-share breakpoints and pays higher annual fees.

#### Liquidity

- **Open-end mutual funds:** Highly liquid — shares are redeemable with the fund at NAV. By law the fund must send redemption proceeds within **7 days**. The limitation is that you can transact **only once per day** at the forward-priced NAV; you cannot trade intraday.
- **Closed-end funds and ETFs:** Trade **intraday** on an exchange, so you can buy or sell any time the market is open at the prevailing market price. ETFs are generally very liquid; a thinly traded closed-end fund may have less trading volume and wider spreads.
- **UITs:** Units are redeemable with the trust, and many sponsors maintain a secondary market.

**Exam tip:** Both structures are "liquid," but in different ways — mutual funds offer **once-daily redemption at NAV**, while exchange-traded products offer **continuous intraday trading at market price**.

#### Tax Implications

Under **Subchapter M** of the Internal Revenue Code, a fund that distributes at least **90% of its net investment income** to shareholders qualifies as a **regulated investment company (RIC)** and is taxed only on the amount it *retains*. This is the **conduit (pipeline) theory** — income "flows through" to shareholders, so distributed income is taxed once, at the shareholder level, rather than twice.

Key rules the exam tests:

- **Distributions are taxable to the shareholder even if reinvested.** Automatic reinvestment does not defer the tax.
- **Reinvested distributions increase the shareholder's cost basis**, which reduces the taxable gain when the shares are eventually sold.
- **Dividend distributions:** taxed as ordinary income unless they are **qualified dividends** (taxed at lower long-term capital-gains rates when holding-period requirements are met).
- **Capital gains distributions:** when the fund sells appreciated holdings, it passes the gain to shareholders. A capital gains **distribution** is treated as **long-term** to the shareholder if the *fund* held the asset more than a year — regardless of how long the shareholder has owned the fund.
- **Selling your fund shares:** any gain or loss is the shareholder's own capital gain/loss, and *its* long- vs. short-term character depends on how long the **shareholder** held the shares.

**Common trap — "buying a dividend":** Purchasing fund shares just before a distribution's record date is generally unwise. The NAV drops by the distribution amount, and the investor simply receives back part of their own principal as a **taxable** distribution.

**ETF tax efficiency:** Because ETFs use an **in-kind creation/redemption** process, they can dispose of appreciated securities without triggering taxable capital gains inside the fund. This typically makes ETFs **more tax-efficient** than comparable open-end mutual funds, which must sell securities for cash to meet redemptions.

#### Benefits and Risks

**Benefits of pooled investing:**
- **Diversification** — spread risk across many securities that a small investor could not buy individually.
- **Professional management** — a dedicated adviser selects and monitors the portfolio.
- **Economies of scale** — lower per-share transaction costs and access to institutional pricing.
- **Liquidity** — daily (mutual funds) or intraday (ETFs/CEFs) access to your money.
- **Convenience** — automatic dividend reinvestment, systematic investing, recordkeeping, and a range of objectives to choose from.

**Risks and drawbacks:**
- **Market risk** — diversification reduces company-specific (unsystematic) risk but **cannot eliminate systematic (market) risk**; the fund still falls when its market falls.
- **Costs** — loads and annual expenses drag on returns; high-fee funds must outperform just to keep pace.
- **No control over the timing of capital gains** — a shareholder can receive (and owe tax on) a capital gains distribution even in a year the fund's price *declined*.
- **Manager / strategy risk** — performance depends on the manager's skill, and the strategy may underperform its benchmark.
- **Closed-end/ETF-specific** — a CEF can trade at a persistent discount; thinly traded products carry liquidity and pricing risk.

**Exam tip:** A favorite point is that a mutual fund **cannot diversify away market (systematic) risk**. Answer choices claiming a diversified fund is "safe" or "removes all risk" are wrong.

#### Relative Comparisons: Evaluating and Comparing Funds

When an adviser compares funds (a suitability skill the exam rewards), several relative factors matter beyond raw past return:

- **Benchmarks.** Compare a fund's performance to an **appropriate index** and to peer funds with the same objective. A large-cap U.S. stock fund should be measured against the **S&P 500**; a small-cap fund against a small-cap index; a bond fund against a bond index. Beating the wrong benchmark tells you nothing. Also weigh return **relative to the risk** taken, not return alone.
- **Manager tenure.** A track record is only meaningful if the **same manager** produced it. If the star manager who generated the fund's history recently left, the historical numbers may not reflect what the new manager will do. Always ask *how long the current manager has run the fund.*
- **Change in investment policy.** A fund's **fundamental investment objective or policy cannot be changed without a majority vote of the outstanding shares** (Investment Company Act of 1940). A change signals that past results were achieved under a different mandate and may not carry forward — evaluate the fund under its current policy.
- **Investment style.** Classify the fund by **style** — e.g., **value vs. growth** and **large-, mid-, or small-cap** (the familiar "style box"). Comparisons are only fair between funds of the same style. Watch for **style drift** — a manager straying from the stated style (a growth manager buying value names), which can throw off diversification and benchmark comparisons and make the fund behave differently than the client expects.

**Exam tip:** Past performance is disclosed with the reminder that it **does not guarantee future results**, and it is most relevant only when the **same manager, same policy, and same style** remain in place. Comparing a fund only to itself, to an inappropriate benchmark, or ignoring risk and expenses are all flawed approaches the exam wants you to reject.

### J. Types of Derivative Securities

A **derivative** is a financial instrument whose value is *derived from* — that is, depends on — the value of some other asset. That other asset is called the **underlying**. The underlying can be a stock, a bond, a stock index, a commodity (oil, wheat, gold), a currency, or an interest rate. A derivative is not the underlying asset itself; it is a separate contract whose price moves in response to changes in the underlying's price.

On the Series 65, derivatives are tested at a **conceptual level**, not at the level of complex trading strategies found on a securities-representative exam. You are expected to know the **definitions**, who is obligated versus who has a choice, the direction of the bet (bullish or bearish), where each instrument comes from (issuer versus exchange), and the standard vocabulary. Heavy multi-leg options math is generally *not* tested, though a small amount of intrinsic-value or breakeven arithmetic can appear.

**Exam tip:** The single most useful mental model is *right versus obligation*. **Options** give the buyer a **right** (a choice); **futures** and **forwards** create an **obligation** (a binding commitment) for both parties. Nail this distinction and most derivative questions become easy.

#### Basic Terminology (underlying, strike, expiration)

Learn this vocabulary first, because every other topic in this subsection uses it.

- **Underlying (underlying asset):** The asset the derivative is based on. If you own a call option on ABC stock, ABC stock is the underlying. A standard listed equity option contract covers **100 shares** of the underlying stock.
- **Strike price (exercise price):** The fixed, predetermined price at which the holder of an option (or warrant/right) may buy or sell the underlying if the contract is exercised. It does **not** change as the market price moves.
- **Expiration date:** The date on which the contract ends. After expiration, the right is gone and the contract is worthless if not exercised. Options have relatively short lives (traditionally up to about nine months for standard listed options; **LEAPS** are longer-dated options that can run a year or more). Rights are very short-lived (weeks); warrants are long-lived (often years).
- **Premium:** The market price a buyer pays to acquire an option — the cost of obtaining the right. The option *seller* (writer) receives the premium.
- **Holder (long) vs. writer (short):** The **holder** buys and owns the option and has the right to exercise. The **writer** sells (creates) the option and takes on the obligation to perform if the holder exercises.
- **Exercise:** Invoking the contractual right — e.g., a call holder buying the stock at the strike.
- **In-the-money / at-the-money / out-of-the-money:** Describes whether exercising the option would currently produce value (defined precisely below).

**Intrinsic value** is the amount by which an option is in-the-money — the value it would have if exercised right now.

- For a **call**: Intrinsic Value = Market Price of Underlying − Strike Price (never less than 0)
- For a **put**: Intrinsic Value = Strike Price − Market Price of Underlying (never less than 0)

*Worked example (call):* An investor holds a call with a **$50 strike** while the stock trades at **$58**. Intrinsic Value = $58 − $50 = **$8 per share**. Because a standard contract covers 100 shares, that is $800 of intrinsic value for one contract. If the stock instead traded at $47, the call would be out-of-the-money and its intrinsic value would be **$0** (never negative).

Any premium above intrinsic value is **time value**. If that $50 call trades at a $10 premium while intrinsic value is $8, the extra **$2** is time value. At expiration, time value is gone and an option is worth only its intrinsic value.

#### Options: Calls and Puts

An **option** is a contract that gives its **buyer/holder the right, but not the obligation**, to buy or sell the underlying at the strike price on or before expiration. The **seller/writer** has the *obligation* to perform if the holder exercises. Listed options trade on exchanges and are standardized; the **Options Clearing Corporation (OCC)** issues and guarantees listed options, which is why an option holder does not rely on the specific writer's creditworthiness.

**Call option — the right to BUY.**
A call gives the holder the right to *buy* the underlying at the strike price.

- **Call buyer (long call):** **Bullish** — profits if the underlying rises. Maximum loss is the premium paid; potential gain is theoretically unlimited (the stock can keep rising). Motivation: to profit from a price increase with limited capital at risk, or to lock in a future purchase price.
- **Call writer (short call):** **Bearish to neutral** — wants the stock to stay flat or fall so the option expires worthless and the writer keeps the premium. A writer who does not own the underlying (a **naked/uncovered call**) faces theoretically unlimited loss.

**Put option — the right to SELL.**
A put gives the holder the right to *sell* the underlying at the strike price.

- **Put buyer (long put):** **Bearish** — profits if the underlying falls. Maximum loss is the premium paid; maximum gain is large but limited — at most the strike price minus the premium, reached if the stock falls to $0. A put is also commonly bought as **portfolio protection** (a "protective put"): an investor who owns a stock buys a put to guarantee a minimum selling price, similar to insurance.
- **Put writer (short put):** **Bullish to neutral** — wants the stock to stay flat or rise so the option expires worthless and the writer keeps the premium. Maximum loss (strike price minus premium) occurs if the stock falls to $0.

The table below summarizes the four basic positions (max loss and max gain are stated per share, as profit/loss figures).

| Position | Right or Obligation | Market View | Max Loss | Max Gain |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Long call (buy call) | Right to buy | Bullish | Premium paid | Unlimited |
| Short call (write call) | Obligation to sell | Bearish/neutral | Unlimited (if naked) | Premium received |
| Long put (buy put) | Right to sell | Bearish | Premium paid | Strike − Premium |
| Short put (write put) | Obligation to buy | Bullish/neutral | Strike − Premium | Premium received |

**Breakeven** (a commonly tested calculation): the underlying price at which the option buyer neither makes nor loses money at expiration.

- Call breakeven = Strike Price + Premium
- Put breakeven = Strike Price − Premium

*Worked example (put):* An investor buys one put with a **$40 strike** for a **$3 premium**. Put breakeven = $40 − $3 = **$37**. The put buyer starts to profit only if the stock falls below $37. If the stock is at $35 at expiration, intrinsic value is $40 − $35 = $5; subtract the $3 premium for a **net profit of $2 per share** ($200 per contract).

**Exam tip:** Remember the mnemonic — **calls give the right to *call away* (buy)**; **puts give the right to *put to* (sell)** the underlying. Buyers of options are optimistic about their chosen direction (call buyers bullish, put buyers bearish); writers take the opposite view.

**Common trap:** Do not confuse the *writer's* obligation with the *buyer's* right. The exam loves to ask what happens if the option is exercised: the **buyer chooses** whether to exercise; the **writer must perform** and has no choice. Also, the *maximum loss for any option buyer is the premium paid* — a frequent, easy-to-miss answer.

#### Warrants

A **warrant** is a certificate, **issued by a corporation**, that gives the holder the right to buy the issuer's stock at a fixed price for a **long period of time** — often **several years, and occasionally with no expiration (perpetual)**. Functionally it behaves like a long-term call option, but it is issued by the company itself rather than created by market participants, and exercising a warrant causes the company to **issue new shares** (diluting existing shareholders).

Key features:

- **Long-lived:** Warrants may run for years, far longer than typical options.
- **Issued at a premium:** When first issued, the exercise price is typically set *above* the current market price of the stock, so warrants are usually out-of-the-money at issuance and have value only if the stock rises over time.
- **A "sweetener":** Warrants are frequently attached to a bond or preferred stock offering to make it more attractive, allowing the issuer to offer a lower coupon. They are often **detachable** and can then trade separately.
- **No dividends, no voting rights:** A warrant holder is not yet a shareholder.

#### Rights (Subscription Rights / Preemptive Rights)

A **right** (subscription right) is also **issued by the corporation**, but it exists to protect existing shareholders from dilution when the company issues new shares. Under a **preemptive right**, existing common shareholders are given the opportunity to buy newly issued shares **before** they are offered to the public, so they can maintain their proportionate ownership.

Key features:

- **Short-lived:** Rights expire quickly — typically a few **weeks** (often 30–45 days).
- **Issued below market:** The subscription price is set *below* the current market price, giving existing holders an incentive to subscribe. This makes rights immediately valuable (in-the-money) when issued.
- **One right per share:** Shareholders receive one right for each share they own; it usually takes a specified number of rights (plus cash) to buy one new share.
- **Tradable:** Rights can typically be exercised, sold, or allowed to expire.

The following table contrasts the three instruments most often confused on the exam.

| Feature | Right (subscription) | Warrant | Listed Call Option |
|---|---|---|---|
| Issued by | The corporation | The corporation | Created by investors; guaranteed by the OCC |
| Gives right to | Buy new shares | Buy shares from issuer | Buy shares from the writer |
| Life span | Very short (weeks) | Long (years / sometimes perpetual) | Short (traditionally ≤ ~9 months; LEAPS longer) |
| Price vs. market at issue | Below current market | Above current market | Varies |
| Primary purpose | Prevent dilution of current holders | "Sweetener" to make an offering attractive | Speculation / hedging |
| New shares created on exercise? | Yes | Yes | No |

**Exam tip:** The cleanest way to separate **rights** from **warrants**: **R**ights are **R**apid/short-term and priced **below** market; **W**arrants are **W**ay long-term and priced **above** market at issue. Both are issued *by the company* and both are ways to buy the issuer's stock, which is why they are contrasted with options (created by third parties).

**Common trap:** A question may describe "a long-term instrument, issued by the company, letting the holder buy stock, sometimes attached to a bond to lower its interest cost." That is a **warrant**, not a right and not an option. Conversely, "a short-term instrument protecting existing shareholders' proportionate ownership" is a **right**.

#### Futures

A **futures contract** is a standardized, **exchange-traded, legally binding agreement to buy or sell a specific quantity of an underlying asset at a set price on a set future date**. The crucial difference from an option is obligation: **both parties are obligated** to perform. There is no premium and no "choice" to walk away — the buyer must buy and the seller must sell (unless the position is closed out before delivery, which is what happens the vast majority of the time).

Terminology mirrors options in some ways but differs in spirit:

- **Long position (buyer):** Obligated to *take delivery* (buy) at the agreed price. Bullish — profits if prices rise.
- **Short position (seller):** Obligated to *make delivery* (sell) at the agreed price. Bearish — profits if prices fall.
- **Underlying:** Historically agricultural and other **commodities** (grain, livestock, metals, energy), and now also **financial futures** on interest rates, currencies, and stock indexes.
- **Settlement:** Most contracts are closed out before expiration by taking an offsetting position; a minority go to actual physical delivery or cash settlement.

Two common uses on the exam:

- **Hedging:** A farmer worried that wheat prices will fall before harvest sells wheat futures to lock in a selling price; a manufacturer worried about rising input costs buys futures to lock in a purchase price. Hedgers use futures to *reduce* risk.
- **Speculation:** Traders take futures positions purely to profit from expected price moves, accepting risk in pursuit of gain.

A closely related instrument is a **forward contract**: like a futures contract, it obligates both parties to a future transaction at a set price, but a **forward is a private, customized, non-standardized agreement** (negotiated directly between two parties and traded over-the-counter) rather than a standardized exchange-listed product. Forwards carry more **counterparty (default) risk** because there is no exchange clearinghouse guaranteeing performance, and they are less liquid because their custom terms make them hard to trade to a third party.

| Feature | Futures | Forward | Option |
|---|---|---|---|
| Obligation | Both parties obligated | Both parties obligated | Only the *writer* is obligated; buyer has a *choice* |
| Standardized / where traded | Standardized; exchange-traded | Customized; over-the-counter (private) | Standardized (listed); exchange-traded |
| Counterparty risk | Low (clearinghouse guarantees) | Higher (no clearinghouse) | Low for listed (OCC guarantees) |
| Upfront cost | No premium (margin/good-faith deposit) | No premium | Buyer pays a premium |
| Bullish party | Long (buyer) | Long (buyer) | Call buyer / put writer |

**Exam tip:** If a question hinges on "who *must* perform," think **futures/forwards = obligation for both sides; options = right for the buyer, obligation only for the writer.** If it hinges on "standardized and exchange-traded versus customized and private," that is **futures versus forwards.**

**Common trap:** Students assume "buying" a futures contract works like buying an option — that the buyer can simply choose not to complete the trade and lose only a premium. That is wrong. A futures **buyer is obligated** and has real exposure well beyond any small good-faith margin deposit; there is no premium and no walk-away right. This obligation-versus-right contrast is the most frequently tested idea in this entire subsection.

### K. Characteristics of Derivative Securities

A **derivative** is a security or contract whose value is *derived from*—that is, based on—the price of some other underlying asset. That underlying asset can be a stock, a stock index, a bond, a commodity, a currency, or an interest rate. The derivative has no independent value of its own; it is simply a claim tied to, or a bet on, the movement of the underlying.

The main derivatives tested on the Series 65 are **options** (calls and puts), **forwards and futures**, and the two equity derivatives issued by corporations, **rights** and **warrants**. You are not expected to trade complex multi-leg options strategies the way a Series 7 candidate is. Instead, the exam focuses on the *characteristics*: why investors use derivatives, how leverage magnifies both gains and losses, the difference between hedging and speculating, who the counterparty is and whether that party can fail to pay, and which positions expose an investor to potentially unlimited loss.

#### Overview of the Main Derivative Types

Before analyzing the risks, know the vehicles at a high level.

| Derivative | What it is | Underlying | Where it trades |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Call option** | Right to *buy* 100 shares at a set price (strike) before expiration | Stock/index | Listed exchange (standardized) |
| **Put option** | Right to *sell* 100 shares at a set price before expiration | Stock/index | Listed exchange (standardized) |
| **Forward** | Private contract to buy/sell an asset at a set price on a future date | Commodity, currency, etc. | Over-the-counter (customized) |
| **Future** | Standardized, exchange-traded version of a forward | Commodity, index, rate | Futures exchange |
| **Right** | Short-term privilege to buy new shares at a discount (from a rights offering) | Issuer's own stock | Issued to existing shareholders; short-lived (weeks) |
| **Warrant** | Long-term option to buy the issuer's stock at a fixed price, often above market at issuance | Issuer's own stock | Often attached to bonds/stock as a "sweetener"; lasts years |

**Exam tip:** The classic rights-versus-warrants distinction is *time and price*. **Rights** are short-term (weeks) and let existing shareholders buy new shares *below* the current market price. **Warrants** are long-term (often years) and are typically issued with a subscription price *above* the market price at the time of issue.

#### Options Vocabulary You Must Know

Every option has two sides:

- The **buyer (long/holder)** pays the **premium** and owns the *right* to exercise. The buyer's maximum loss is limited to the premium paid.
- The **seller (short/writer)** receives the premium and takes on the *obligation* to perform if exercised.

For a **call**, the buyer profits when the underlying rises; for a **put**, the buyer profits when the underlying falls. One listed equity option contract covers **100 shares**, so a premium quoted as "$3" costs $3 x 100 = **$300**.

**Breakeven formulas (plain text):**

- **Call** breakeven = Strike Price + Premium
- **Put** breakeven = Strike Price − Premium

*Worked example:* An investor buys 1 XYZ Oct 50 call at a premium of 3.
Breakeven = 50 + 3 = **$53**. The stock must climb above $53 by expiration for the buyer to profit. Between $50 and $53 the call has intrinsic value, so the holder recovers part—but not all—of the premium and still shows a net loss.

#### Benefit and Risk #1: Leverage

**Leverage** is the single most important characteristic of derivatives. It means controlling a large amount of the underlying asset with a small amount of money. A $300 call can control $5,000 worth of stock (100 shares at $50). This magnifies **percentage** gains and losses far beyond what owning the stock outright would produce.

*Worked leverage example:*
Stock trades at $50/share. An investor has $300 to commit.

- **Option route:** Buy 1 call with a $50 strike for a $3 premium ($300 total). This controls 100 shares.
- **Stock route:** $300 buys 6 shares outright.

Now assume the stock rises to $55.

- Option intrinsic value = 55 − 50 = $5, so the contract is worth $5 x 100 = $500.
  Gain = $500 − $300 = $200. Return = 200 / 300 = **+66.7%**.
- Stock: gain = ($55 − $50) x 6 shares = $30. Return = 30 / 300 = **+10%**.

The same $5 move produces a 66.7% return with the option versus 10% with the stock—that is leverage. But leverage cuts both ways. If instead the stock closes at $50 or below at expiration, the option expires worthless and the buyer loses **100%** of the $300, while the stock owner is only down (or flat) a few dollars.

**Exam tip:** When a question asks *why* an investor would buy an option instead of the stock, the answer is usually **leverage**—more exposure per dollar and a defined, limited maximum loss (the premium) for the buyer.

**Common trap:** Leverage magnifies **percentage** gains and losses, not just the dollars at risk. A leveraged position can lose 100% of the premium quickly, whereas a diversified stock position rarely goes to zero overnight.

#### Benefit #2: Hedging vs. Speculation

Derivatives are used for two fundamentally different purposes, and the exam wants you to tell them apart.

- **Hedging** = using a derivative to *reduce* risk in an existing position, like insurance. The investor already owns (or is short) the underlying and wants protection.
- **Speculation** = using a derivative to *seek profit* from a price move, taking on risk in hopes of a leveraged gain. The speculator often has no underlying position at all.

The two most-tested hedges:

| Strategy | Position | Purpose |
|---|---|---|
| **Protective put** | Own the stock **+** buy a put | Insures a *long* stock position against a price drop; the put sets a floor selling price. |
| **Covered call** | Own the stock **+** sell a call | Generates premium income on a long position; the premium cushions a modest decline but caps the upside. |

*Protective put logic:* An investor owns 100 shares of a stock bought at $50 and fears a decline but doesn't want to sell. She buys a 45 put for $1. No matter how far the stock falls, she can exercise the put and sell at $45. Her maximum loss is limited to ($50 − $45) + $1 premium = **$6 per share**, in exchange for giving up $1 of return in every scenario. This is textbook risk reduction.

**Exam tip:** A **protective put** is frequently described as "buying insurance" on a stock you own. The put's strike is the floor price; the premium is the insurance cost.

**Common trap:** A **covered** call is a conservative income strategy—the risk is limited because you own the stock you might have to deliver. An **uncovered (naked)** call is the opposite: high-risk with unlimited loss potential (see below). Watch for questions that swap "covered" and "uncovered."

#### Risk #3: Unlimited Loss Potential on Some Positions

The most heavily tested risk concept for derivatives is that **certain positions carry unlimited loss potential, while others have loss capped at the premium.** The key is whether the position is "naked" (uncovered) and whether the underlying can move against it without limit.

| Position | Maximum Gain | Maximum Loss | Why |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Long call** (buyer) | Unlimited | Premium paid | Stock can rise without limit; buyer only risks premium. |
| **Long put** (buyer) | Strike − Premium (stock to $0) | Premium paid | Stock can only fall to $0; buyer only risks premium. |
| **Short (naked) call** (writer) | Premium received | **Unlimited** | Stock can rise infinitely; writer must deliver at strike. |
| **Short put** (writer) | Premium received | Strike − Premium | Stock can fall only to $0; large but *limited* loss. |
| **Short stock** | Short-sale price (stock to $0) | **Unlimited** | Must buy back; price can rise without limit. |

*Worked max-loss example (naked call):* A speculator writes (sells) 1 uncovered call with a $50 strike and collects a $3 premium. If the stock rises to $90, the writer must deliver 100 shares he does not own—buying them at $90 and selling at $50, a $40 loss, offset by the $3 premium = **$37 loss per share ($3,700)**. Because there is no ceiling on the stock price, the theoretical loss is unlimited.

**Exam tip:** Memorize this rule of thumb—**the seller of a naked call and the short seller of stock both face unlimited loss** because the underlying price has no upper bound. Everyone else's downside is finite (buyers lose only the premium; put writers lose down to a zero underlying).

**Common trap:** A long put's maximum gain is *not* unlimited. Because the stock can fall no lower than $0, the put buyer's max gain = Strike − Premium (times 100 shares). Candidates often mislabel a long put as having "unlimited" profit.

#### Risk #4: Counterparty Risk

**Counterparty risk** (also called default or credit risk) is the danger that the *other side of the contract fails to honor its obligation*. This risk depends almost entirely on **where** the derivative trades.

- **Exchange-traded/standardized derivatives** (listed options, futures) carry **little counterparty risk**. A central clearinghouse stands between buyer and seller and guarantees performance. For listed options that clearinghouse is the **Options Clearing Corporation (OCC)**; for futures it is the exchange's clearinghouse, supported by daily **margin** and **mark-to-market** settlement. You are effectively facing the clearinghouse, not an individual trader.
- **Over-the-counter (OTC) derivatives** (forwards, swaps, and other privately negotiated contracts) carry **significant counterparty risk** because there is no central guarantor. If the party owing you money defaults, you may collect nothing. This is exactly the risk that surfaced with OTC derivatives in the 2008 financial crisis.

**Exam tip:** Remember the trade-off. **Forwards** are customized and private, so they carry counterparty risk but offer flexibility. **Futures** are standardized and cleared, so they sacrifice customization but nearly eliminate counterparty risk. If a question emphasizes "customized" or "private negotiation," think forward/OTC and heightened counterparty risk.

**Common trap:** Do not assume "derivative = high counterparty risk." Listed, exchange-cleared options and futures actually have *low* counterparty risk thanks to the clearinghouse. The high counterparty risk lives in the **OTC/forward/swap** world.

#### Costs of Owning Derivatives

Beyond the market risks, derivatives carry specific *costs* the exam may reference:

- **Premium (options):** The non-refundable price the buyer pays; it is a real cost that must be recovered before the position is profitable (hence the breakeven formulas above).
- **Time decay (theta):** An option is a *wasting asset*. All else equal, its value erodes as expiration approaches, and it becomes **worthless** if it expires out-of-the-money. The buyer can lose 100% simply by the passage of time even if the stock never moves against them.
- **No income or ownership rights:** Option holders and warrant/right holders receive **no dividends** and have **no voting rights** on the underlying shares unless and until they exercise. Derivatives give price exposure only.
- **Margin and mark-to-market (futures/short options):** Writers and futures traders must post margin and meet daily variation margin calls as positions are marked to market, which ties up capital and can force liquidation at a loss.
- **Commissions and bid-ask spreads:** Transaction costs, plus the frictional cost of illiquid or wide-spread contracts.

#### Putting It Together: Benefits vs. Risks Summary

| Benefits of derivatives | Risks of derivatives |
|---|---|
| **Leverage**—large exposure for a small outlay | Leverage magnifies losses; buyers can lose 100% of premium |
| **Hedging**—transfer or reduce risk (protective put, covered call) | **Unlimited loss** on naked calls and short stock |
| **Income**—premium collection (covered calls) | **Time decay**—options are wasting assets |
| **Flexibility**—tailor exposure to a view | **Counterparty risk** on OTC forwards/swaps |
| Low counterparty risk on **listed/cleared** contracts | No dividends or voting rights; margin and transaction costs |

**Exam tip:** For a suitability question, derivatives are generally appropriate only for investors with the **sophistication, risk tolerance, and financial capacity** to absorb potentially rapid, total, or (in the case of naked writing) unlimited losses. A protective put or covered call can be *conservative*, but naked option writing and speculative futures trading are aggressive strategies suitable only for a small, knowledgeable subset of investors.

### L. Alternative Investments

"Alternative investments" is the umbrella term for products that fall outside the traditional trio of stocks, bonds, and plain mutual funds. On the Series 65 they are grouped together because they share three recurring themes the exam loves to test: **complexity**, **illiquidity or hidden risk**, and **narrow suitability**. For almost every product below, the correct answer to a suitability question is some version of "only for a sophisticated investor who understands the structure, can tolerate the risk, and does not need liquidity." Learn the mechanics, but also learn to recognize when a product is simply inappropriate.

#### Limited Partnerships and Direct Participation Programs (DPPs)

A **Direct Participation Program (DPP)** is a pooled investment that lets income, gains, losses, deductions, and credits flow **directly through** to the investors, who are taxed as if they owned the underlying assets personally. The most common legal form is the **limited partnership (LP)**. Common DPP categories tested on the exam are **real estate**, **oil and gas** (exploratory/wildcatting, developmental, and income programs), and **equipment leasing**.

**Two classes of participant:**

| Role | Management | Liability | Tax treatment |
|---|---|---|---|
| **General Partner (GP)** | Runs day-to-day operations; makes all decisions | **Unlimited** personal liability | Flow-through |
| **Limited Partner (LP)** | **Passive**; may NOT manage | Limited to amount invested (plus any signed obligations) | Flow-through |

Key features the exam expects you to know:

- **Flow-through (pass-through) taxation.** The partnership itself pays no tax; there is **no double taxation** as there is with a C corporation. Each partner reports their share of income and loss on their own return.
- **Passive income and passive losses.** DPP losses are **passive losses**, and passive losses may only offset **passive income** — not earned income (salary) and not portfolio income (interest, dividends, capital gains).
- **Illiquidity.** There is little or no active secondary market, and interests are not freely transferable. This is a defining suitability concern.
- **Limited liability is conditional.** A limited partner who begins to actively manage the business can lose LP status and be treated as a general partner with unlimited liability.
- **Documents.** A **certificate of limited partnership** is filed with the state; the investor completes a **subscription agreement** (the application), and the interest is not effective until the **GP signs and accepts** it. The subscription agreement is where the investor attests to meeting net-worth/income suitability standards.

**Dissolution priority (order in which claims are paid if the partnership liquidates):** secured lenders → general (unsecured) creditors → limited partners → general partners.

**Exam tip:** The single most important suitability principle for DPPs is that the investment must make **economic sense on its own**. Tax benefits should be a secondary consideration, not the primary reason to invest. An answer choice that recommends a DPP mainly to generate a tax write-off is a trap.

**Common trap:** Confusing which losses can be used. A DPP passive loss **cannot** shelter an investor's wages or their dividend/interest income — only passive income from that or another passive activity.

#### Exchange-Traded Notes (ETNs)

An **Exchange-Traded Note (ETN)** is a **senior unsecured debt obligation**, usually issued by a bank, that promises to pay a return tied to a specified index or benchmark — an equity index, a commodity, a currency, or a volatility measure — minus fees, at maturity. ETNs trade on an exchange throughout the day like a stock.

The critical distinction the exam draws is **ETN versus ETF**:

| Feature | Exchange-Traded Note (ETN) | Exchange-Traded Fund (ETF) |
|---|---|---|
| Legal nature | Unsecured **debt** of the issuer | **Equity/ownership** in a portfolio |
| Underlying holdings | **None** — just a promise to pay | Owns actual securities/assets |
| Primary risk | **Issuer credit (default) risk** | Market risk of the holdings |
| Tracking | Promises index return (minus fee) | Approximates index; may have tracking error |
| Maturity | Yes (often 10–30 years) | No maturity |

Because an ETN holder owns **only the issuer's promise to pay**, the dominant risk is **credit risk**: if the issuing bank becomes insolvent, the investor can lose most or all of their money **even if the tracked index performed well**. Additional risks include **liquidity/market-price risk** (the issuer can stop issuing new notes, causing the market price to trade at a premium or discount to indicative value) and **call risk** (many ETNs can be called by the issuer).

**Exam tip:** If a question asks for the feature that distinguishes an ETN from an ETF, the answer is that the ETN is **unsecured debt carrying issuer credit risk with no underlying portfolio**.

#### Leveraged Funds

A **leveraged fund** (typically a leveraged ETF) uses derivatives — swaps, futures, and options — to seek a **multiple** of the **daily** return of an index, commonly **2x** or **3x**. If the index rises 1% on a given day, a 3x fund seeks +3% **that day**.

The concept the exam insists on is **daily reset**. The fund rebalances its exposure at the end of **each trading day** so the multiple applies to a single day's return. Because returns **compound**, the fund's performance over any period longer than one day will **not** equal the multiple times the index's cumulative return. In volatile, choppy (up-and-down) markets this drift is generally negative — a phenomenon called **volatility decay** (or "beta slippage").

**Worked example (daily-reset compounding):**

Formula for a leveraged fund's cumulative value: **Ending Value = Start × (1 + m×r₁) × (1 + m×r₂) × …**, where *m* is the leverage multiple and *rₜ* is the index's return on day *t*.

Assume a 3x leveraged fund (m = 3) and an index that starts at 100.

- **Day 1:** index +10% → index goes 100 → 110. Fund: $100 × (1 + 3×0.10) = $100 × 1.30 = **$130**.
- **Day 2:** index −10% (from 110) → index goes 110 → 99. Fund: $130 × (1 + 3×(−0.10)) = $130 × 0.70 = **$91**.

Over the two days the **index fell only about 1%** (100 → 99). A naive investor would expect a 3x fund to be down about 3% (to ~$97). Instead the fund is down **9%** (to $91). The daily reset, combined with volatility, produced a loss far worse than 3× the index's cumulative move.

**Exam tip:** Leveraged funds are designed as **short-term (often single-day) trading tools**, not buy-and-hold investments. Recommending a leveraged ETF as a long-term core holding for a retiree is a classic **wrong answer**.

#### Inverse Funds

An **inverse fund** seeks to deliver the **opposite** of the daily return of an index — **−1x**, or in leveraged-inverse form **−2x** or **−3x**. They let an investor profit from, or hedge against, a market decline **without** selling short or buying puts. If the index falls 1% on a day, a −1x fund seeks +1% that day.

Inverse funds have the **same daily-reset and compounding problem** as leveraged funds, and the drift can be even more pronounced with leveraged-inverse products. Held over long or volatile periods, an inverse fund can lose money **even if the index ends lower over that period**, because each day's move is compounded off a reset base.

**Common trap:** Assuming that holding a −1x inverse fund for a year gives you the exact opposite of the index's yearly return. It does not — only the **daily** return is inverted, and compounding causes multi-day results to diverge, usually to the investor's disadvantage in choppy markets.

**Exam tip:** For **both** leveraged and inverse funds, remember the tested phrase: because of **daily reset and compounding**, performance over periods **longer than one day** can differ **substantially** from the stated multiple of the index. These funds require **daily monitoring** and are unsuitable for unsophisticated, long-term investors.

#### Structured Products

A **structured product** is a security whose payoff is **derived from** an underlying asset — a single stock, a basket, an index, a commodity, or a currency — by combining a **debt instrument** with an embedded **derivative**. Common examples include **structured notes**, **principal-protected notes (PPNs)**, **market-linked (equity-linked) CDs**, and **reverse convertible notes**.

Most structured products are issued by a bank or brokerage firm as its own **unsecured debt**, so — like an ETN — they carry the **credit risk of the issuer**. (Market-linked CDs are an exception on principal: the CD itself is a bank deposit that is FDIC-insured up to applicable limits, though the market-linked return is not guaranteed.) Issuer credit risk is the heart of the "principal protection" trap:

- A **principal-protected note** promises return of principal at maturity **plus** upside linked to an index. But that protection is only as good as the **issuer's solvency**. If the issuer defaults, the "guarantee" is worthless. (The historical teaching example is investors in Lehman Brothers structured notes, whose "protected" principal was lost when Lehman failed in 2008.)
- A **reverse convertible** pays an **above-market coupon**, but if the linked stock falls below a set level, the investor can be repaid in **shares of the depreciated stock** rather than cash — exposing them to the full downside.

Other suitability concerns the exam raises about structured products: **complexity and opacity** (hard for a retail investor to value or understand), **illiquidity** (thin or nonexistent secondary market — often designed to be held to maturity), **high embedded costs/fees**, and frequently **capped upside** in exchange for only partial downside protection.

**Exam tip:** For structured products the recurring correct themes are (1) **issuer credit risk** — "principal protection" is a promise, not a guarantee; (2) **complexity** demanding a sophisticated investor; and (3) **illiquidity**. If a question offers a structured product to an unsophisticated client seeking safety and liquidity, it is unsuitable.

#### Putting It Together: Suitability Summary

| Product | What it really is | Signature risk | Suitable for |
|---|---|---|---|
| Limited partnership / DPP | Pass-through pooled venture | Illiquidity; passive-loss limits; GP unlimited liability | Sophisticated investors who don't need liquidity and can use passive losses |
| ETN | Unsecured debt tracking an index | **Issuer credit risk** (no underlying holdings) | Investors who understand it is a bank's promise, not a portfolio |
| Leveraged fund | Daily multiple (2x/3x) of an index | **Daily reset / volatility decay** | Short-term traders who monitor actively |
| Inverse fund | Daily opposite (−1x/−2x/−3x) of an index | **Daily reset / compounding** | Short-term hedgers/traders, not buy-and-hold |
| Structured product | Bond + embedded derivative | Issuer credit risk; complexity; illiquidity | Sophisticated investors who understand the payoff |

**Exam tip:** Across this entire category, when in doubt the exam rewards caution: these are **complex, higher-risk, often illiquid** products, and the adviser's duty is to confirm the client truly understands the structure and that the risk and liquidity profile fits the client's objectives before recommending any of them.

### M. Insurance-Based Products

Insurance-based products are contracts issued by insurance companies, but some of them are also **securities**. That dual nature is the single most tested idea in this subsection. A product becomes a security when the *purchaser* takes on investment risk through a **separate account** whose value rises and falls with the markets. When the *insurer* guarantees the outcome and invests in its own **general account**, the product is pure insurance and is regulated only by the state insurance commissioner.

Keep this master distinction in mind as you read every product below:

| Product | Security? | Who bears investment risk | Investment held in |
|---|---|---|---|
| Fixed annuity | No | Insurance company | General account |
| Indexed annuity | No (federally exempt) | Shared — insurer guarantees a floor; owner gives up full upside | General account |
| **Variable annuity** | **Yes** | **Contract owner** | **Separate account** |
| Term life | No | N/A (no cash value) | General account |
| Whole life | No | Insurance company | General account |
| Universal life | No | Insurance company (guaranteed minimum rate) | General account |
| **Variable life / VUL** | **Yes** | **Policyholder** | **Separate account** |

**Exam tip:** To sell any *variable* product (variable annuity, variable life, or variable universal life) an agent needs **both** a securities registration (Series 6 or 7, plus state securities registration) **and** a life insurance license. Fixed and indexed products require only the insurance license.

#### Annuities: Purpose and the Two Phases

An **annuity** is a contract between an individual and an insurance company designed to provide income, most often for retirement. Its defining feature is that it can pay income **for the life of the annuitant** — it insures against *outliving your money* (longevity risk). Non-qualified annuities have **no contribution limits** and no required starting age for contributions.

Every annuity has two phases:

- **Accumulation phase** — the period when money is paid in and grows **tax-deferred**. Contributions can be a single lump sum (single-premium) or a series of payments (periodic-premium). No taxes are due while the money grows.
- **Annuitization phase (payout phase)** — the contract is converted into a stream of income payments. This is a **one-way, irrevocable** decision once elected in a traditional annuity.

**Common trap:** *Annuitizing* is not the same as simply *withdrawing*. You can take withdrawals without annuitizing, but true annuitization converts the accumulated value into a guaranteed income stream and is generally irreversible.

Common payout options (tested by their trade-off between payment size and survivor protection):

| Payout option | Description | Relative payment |
|---|---|---|
| Straight life (life only) | Pays for the annuitant's life; **stops at death**, nothing to heirs | **Largest** payment |
| Life with period certain | Pays for life, but guarantees a minimum number of years (e.g., 10) to a beneficiary if the annuitant dies early | Smaller |
| Joint and last survivor | Pays until the **second** of two people dies | Smallest |
| Unit/cash refund | Guarantees the remaining principal is refunded to a beneficiary | Smaller |

**Exam tip:** Straight life pays the most because the insurer keeps the balance if the annuitant dies early; it delivers the **largest lifetime income** but **no protection for heirs**.

#### Fixed Annuities

A **fixed annuity** guarantees a fixed rate of return during accumulation and a fixed dollar payment during payout. Because the insurer promises the outcome, the **insurance company bears the investment risk**, and the money is held in the insurer's **general account**. A fixed annuity is an **insurance product, not a security**, and is regulated by the state insurance department.

- Guaranteed minimum interest rate during accumulation.
- Predictable, level payments in retirement.
- The annuitant's main danger is **purchasing-power (inflation) risk** — fixed dollars buy less over time.

**Common trap:** Because a fixed annuity is not a security, it may be sold by someone holding **only** an insurance license. Don't require a securities registration for it.

#### Variable Annuities

A **variable annuity (VA)** lets the owner allocate premiums among **subaccounts** (similar to mutual funds — stock, bond, money-market portfolios) held in the insurer's **separate account**. Values fluctuate with the markets, so the **contract owner bears the investment risk**. There is **no guaranteed return and no guaranteed principal**. A VA is a **security**: the separate account is registered as an investment company under the Investment Company Act of 1940, the contract is registered under the Securities Act of 1933, and sales are supervised by the SEC/FINRA and state securities regulators. (This flows from *SEC v. VALIC*, the case establishing that variable annuities are securities.)

**Units — the classic exam distinction:**
