- During the **accumulation phase**, premiums buy **accumulation units**. The **number of units you own varies** (you buy more or fewer each period depending on price), and the value per unit fluctuates.
- At **annuitization**, accumulation units are converted into a **fixed number of annuity units**. From then on the **number of annuity units is fixed**, but the **value of each annuity unit still fluctuates**, which is why the monthly income varies.

**Assumed Interest Rate (AIR):** The AIR is a benchmark used to calculate variable annuity payments during the payout phase. Each period the **actual** separate-account performance is compared to the AIR:

- Actual return **greater than** AIR → next payment **rises**.
- Actual return **equal to** AIR → next payment **unchanged**.
- Actual return **less than** AIR → next payment **falls**.

**Worked AIR example:** A variable annuity has an AIR of **4%**.
- If the separate account earns **6%** this period, it beat the AIR, so the next payment is **larger** than the previous one.
- If it earns exactly **4%**, the payment stays the **same**.
- If it earns **2%**, the payment **decreases**, even though the account still made money — because 2% is below the 4% AIR.

**Common trap:** The comparison is *actual performance vs. the AIR*, not actual performance vs. the prior period. A positive return can still produce a *smaller* payment if it falls short of the AIR.

Variable annuities also carry **mortality and expense (M&E) risk charges**: the insurer charges a fee to cover its guarantee to pay for the annuitant's lifetime (mortality guarantee) and to cap administrative expenses (expense guarantee). Many VAs add optional **living benefit** or **death benefit** riders (e.g., a guaranteed minimum death benefit returning at least premiums paid).

#### Indexed Annuities

An **indexed annuity** (also called an equity-indexed or fixed-indexed annuity) credits interest based on the performance of a securities index such as the **S&P 500**, subject to limits, while **guaranteeing a minimum return** so the owner cannot lose principal to market declines. Because principal is protected by the insurer, an indexed annuity is treated as an **insurance product, not a security**, under federal law (the SEC's attempt to regulate them via Rule 151A was vacated). Risk is **shared**: the insurer bears downside risk, and the owner gives up part of the upside.

Three features control how much index gain the owner actually receives:

- **Participation rate** — the percentage of the index's gain that is credited (e.g., 80%).
- **Cap rate** — a ceiling on the credited return in a period (e.g., 6%).
- **Floor / guaranteed minimum** — the least you can be credited, typically **0%** (no loss) or a small positive rate.

**Worked example:** An indexed annuity has an **80% participation rate**, a **6% cap**, and a **0% floor**. The S&P 500 index gains **10%** this year.
- Apply the participation rate: 10% × 80% = **8%**.
- Apply the cap: 8% exceeds the 6% cap, so the credited rate is capped at **6%**.
- Result: **6% is credited** for the year.

Now suppose the following year the index **falls 12%**. Because of the **0% floor**, the owner is credited **0%** — no gain, but **no loss of principal**.

**Common trap:** Owners of indexed annuities do **not** receive index **dividends**, and their gains are limited by caps and participation rates. In strong bull markets an indexed annuity typically underperforms a direct index investment — that limited upside is the price of downside protection.

#### Taxation of Annuities

These rules apply to **non-qualified** annuities (bought with after-tax dollars):

- **No deduction** for contributions; earnings grow **tax-deferred**.
- Withdrawn earnings are taxed as **ordinary income** — **never** as capital gains, even in a variable annuity holding stock subaccounts.
- Non-qualified annuity withdrawals are **LIFO** (last-in, first-out): the **taxable earnings** are deemed withdrawn **first**, then the tax-free return of your cost basis.
- Withdrawals of the taxable portion before age **59½** incur a **10% federal penalty** (on top of ordinary income tax).
- **No step-up in basis at death** — heirs owe ordinary income tax on the deferred gain.

**Exclusion ratio (during annuitization):** Once annuitized, each payment is part tax-free return of principal and part taxable earnings.

> Exclusion Ratio = Investment in the Contract / Expected Return

**Worked example:** An investor contributed **$100,000** (the investment in the contract). Based on the payout option and life expectancy, the **expected return** is **$200,000**. The exclusion ratio = 100,000 / 200,000 = **50%**. If the annuitant receives **$10,000** per year, then **$5,000 (50%) is tax-free** return of principal and **$5,000 is taxable** as ordinary income. Once the entire $100,000 basis has been recovered (i.e., if the annuitant lives beyond life expectancy), **all** subsequent payments become **fully taxable**.

**1035 exchange:** Section 1035 permits a **tax-free exchange** of one annuity for another, or of a **life insurance policy into an annuity**. You **cannot** exchange an annuity **into** a life insurance policy tax-free.

**Exam tip:** Placing an annuity **inside an IRA or other qualified plan is usually a red flag for unsuitability** — you are paying extra annuity fees (like M&E charges) for tax deferral the IRA **already** provides. Expect a question where the "wrong" recommendation is a variable annuity in an IRA sold to a young or low-tax client, or one who needs liquidity.

#### Surrender Charges

A **surrender charge** is a fee the insurer deducts if the owner withdraws money (beyond a small free-withdrawal amount) during the early years of the contract. Charges typically **decline each year over a surrender period** (often 6–8 years) until they disappear.

**Example schedule:** 7% in year 1, 6% in year 2, 5% in year 3, and so on to 0% after year 7. A $50,000 withdrawal in year 2 (6% charge) would cost **$3,000** in surrender charges.

**Common trap:** Surrender charges make annuities **illiquid** early on. Recommending an annuity to a client with a short time horizon or an anticipated near-term need for the funds is a classic **suitability failure**.

#### Life Insurance: Overview

Life insurance pays a **death benefit** to beneficiaries. **Permanent** policies (whole, universal, variable) also build **cash value** that grows tax-deferred and can be borrowed against. Death benefits are generally **income-tax-free** to beneficiaries. Only **variable** life products are securities.

#### Term Life

**Term life** is **pure insurance**: a death benefit only, for a specified period (the "term"), with **no cash value**. It is the **cheapest** way to buy the largest death benefit and provides only **temporary** protection. Common forms include **level term** (constant premium and death benefit), **annual renewable term** (renews yearly at rising premiums), and **decreasing term** (declining death benefit, often used to cover a mortgage). Term life is **not a security**.

#### Whole Life

**Whole life** (also called *ordinary* or *straight life*) is **permanent** insurance with three guarantees: a **fixed, level premium**, a **guaranteed death benefit**, and **guaranteed cash value** that grows on a set schedule. The **insurer bears all investment risk** and holds premiums in its **general account**. Cash value grows tax-deferred. Whole life is **not a security**. Its trade-off is higher, less flexible premiums in exchange for guarantees.

#### Universal Life

**Universal life (UL)** is **permanent** insurance built for **flexibility**: the policyholder can adjust the **premium amount and timing** and the **death benefit** (subject to limits and evidence of insurability). Cash value earns a **current interest rate with a guaranteed minimum floor**, so the **insurer still bears the core investment risk**. UL is "unbundled" — the cost of insurance and the savings element are shown separately. UL offers two death-benefit designs: **Option A** (level death benefit) and **Option B** (death benefit increases with cash value). Universal life is **not a security**.

**Common trap:** *Flexible premiums* in UL can backfire — if the owner underfunds the policy and the cash value can't cover the cost of insurance, the policy can **lapse**. Flexibility is not the same as a guarantee.

#### Variable Life (and Variable Universal Life)

**Variable life (VLI)** is **permanent** insurance whose **cash value and (in part) death benefit vary** with the performance of investments the policyholder selects in a **separate account**. Key features:

- The **policyholder bears the investment risk**.
- Premiums are typically **fixed/scheduled**, but the **cash value is not guaranteed**.
- There is usually a **guaranteed minimum death benefit**, while amounts above it depend on investment performance.
- Because it uses a separate account, **variable life IS a security** — the agent needs a securities registration plus an insurance license.

**Variable universal life (VUL)** combines the **separate-account investing of variable life** with the **flexible premiums and adjustable death benefit of universal life**. It offers the most flexibility and the most investment risk to the owner, and it is likewise a **security**. VUL often has **no guaranteed minimum cash value**, and depending on how the death benefit is structured, may offer weaker death-benefit guarantees than traditional variable life.

**Life insurance comparison — memorize this grid:**

| Feature | Term | Whole Life | Universal Life | Variable Life | VUL |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Cash value | None | Guaranteed | Current rate w/ min. floor | Varies (separate acct.) | Varies (separate acct.) |
| Premium | Level for term | Fixed & level | Flexible | Usually fixed | Flexible |
| Death benefit | Level (temporary) | Guaranteed | Adjustable | Min. guaranteed + variable | Adjustable + variable |
| Who bears investment risk | N/A | Insurer | Insurer (guar. min.) | **Policyholder** | **Policyholder** |
| Security? | No | No | No | **Yes** | **Yes** |

**Exam tip:** The fastest way to answer most insurance questions on the Series 65 is to ask two things: (1) *Is there a separate account / does the customer bear investment risk?* — if yes, it's a **variable product and a security** requiring a securities registration; and (2) *Who is taxed and how?* — annuity and permanent-policy growth is **tax-deferred**, and annuity earnings come out as **ordinary income under LIFO** with a **10% penalty before 59½**.

### N. Other Assets

Beyond stocks, bonds, packaged products, and derivatives, the Series 65 expects you to understand a small set of "other" assets that clients increasingly ask about: physical **commodities**, **precious metals**, and **digital assets** (cryptocurrencies and related tokens). They are grouped together because they share key traits — most are *not* traditional securities, they generally produce *no* cash flow, and their return depends almost entirely on price movement. The exam tests whether you understand their characteristics, how they are regulated, and how an investment adviser applies suitability and fiduciary judgment to them.

#### Commodities

A **commodity** is a raw physical good that is interchangeable with any other unit of the same grade — one producer's bushel of wheat or barrel of oil is essentially identical to another's. This interchangeability is called **fungibility**. Commodities fall into a few broad groups:

| Category | Examples |
|---|---|
| Agricultural ("softs" and grains) | Wheat, corn, soybeans, coffee, sugar, cotton |
| Livestock | Cattle, hogs |
| Energy | Crude oil, natural gas, heating oil |
| Industrial metals | Copper, aluminum, zinc |
| Precious metals | Gold, silver, platinum, palladium |

**How investors gain exposure.** A client rarely buys and stores physical barrels of oil. Instead, commodity exposure typically comes through:

- **Spot market** — buying the physical good for immediate delivery.
- **Futures and forwards** — contracts to buy or sell a set quantity at a set price on a future date (covered in the derivatives section).
- **Options on futures.**
- **Commodity ETFs / exchange-traded products (ETPs)** — funds that hold either the physical commodity (common for metals) or a portfolio of futures contracts.
- **Commodity pools / managed futures funds** — pooled vehicles run by professional managers.

**Key characteristics to remember:**

- **No income.** A commodity pays no dividend or interest. The *only* source of return is price appreciation, and the investor also bears **carrying costs** (storage, insurance, and financing). This is a frequently tested distinction.
- **Inflation hedge.** Commodity prices tend to rise with inflation, so commodities are often used to protect purchasing power.
- **Low correlation** with stocks and bonds, which makes them useful for **diversification**.
- **High volatility**, driven by weather, geopolitics, supply/demand shocks, and currency moves.
- **Leverage.** Futures require only a small margin deposit relative to contract value, magnifying both gains and losses.

**Regulation — heavily tested.** Physical commodities and commodity futures are **not securities**. They are regulated by the **Commodity Futures Trading Commission (CFTC)** under the Commodity Exchange Act, with the **National Futures Association (NFA)** acting as the industry self-regulatory organization. Professionals in this space register differently from securities professionals:

- A **Commodity Pool Operator (CPO)** operates a pooled commodity/futures fund.
- A **Commodity Trading Advisor (CTA)** advises others on futures.

Both register with the **CFTC/NFA**, not the SEC or the states, when they deal purely in commodities and futures.

**Common trap:** A candidate sees "commodity" and assumes SEC/state securities regulation. The commodity or futures contract itself is *not* a security and is regulated by the CFTC. However, an *investment vehicle* that packages commodities — such as a **commodity ETF or a commodity pool sold as a fund interest** — can itself be a security, and advice about that packaged product can fall under securities law.

**Worked example — leverage in a futures position.** Suppose crude oil futures trade at $80 per barrel and one contract is 1,000 barrels.

- Contract value = 1,000 barrels × $80 = **$80,000**.
- Assume the exchange requires initial margin of $5,000 (6.25% of contract value).
- If oil rises to $85, the gain = 1,000 × $5 = **$5,000**.

Return on margin = Gain / Margin Deposited = $5,000 / $5,000 = **100%**.

A 6.25% move in the underlying produced a 100% return on the amount posted — and a 6.25% move the other way would wipe out the entire deposit. This is why the exam labels commodity/futures strategies as high-risk and suitable only for investors who understand and can absorb leveraged losses.

#### Precious Metals

Precious metals — chiefly **gold, silver, platinum, and palladium** — are a specialized commodity category tested on their own, with gold the classic example.

**Ways to own precious metals:**

| Method | Notes |
|---|---|
| Physical bullion and coins | Direct ownership; investor bears **storage, insurance, and security** costs and faces dealer markups/spreads. |
| Precious-metal ETFs | Fund holds physical metal in a vault; investor holds a security representing that metal. Convenient, liquid. |
| Mining company stocks / mining funds | Equity in producers — these *are* securities and behave partly like stocks (operational risk, leverage to the metal price). |
| Futures and options | Leveraged, exchange-traded, CFTC-regulated. |

**Key characteristics:**

- **Store of value / safe haven.** Investors move into gold during economic stress, geopolitical crisis, or currency debasement.
- **Inflation hedge**, similar to broader commodities.
- **No income.** Like other commodities, bullion pays nothing; all return is price change, minus carrying costs.
- **Inverse tendencies.** Gold often moves inversely to the **U.S. dollar** and to **real (inflation-adjusted) interest rates** — when rates rise, non-yielding gold becomes relatively less attractive.
- **Volatility** and wide dealer spreads on physical metal.

**Worked example — total return on physical gold.** Use:

Total Return = (Ending Value − Beginning Value − Holding Costs) / Beginning Value

An investor buys gold at $1,800 per ounce, sells at $2,070, and pays $30 per ounce in combined storage and insurance over the holding period.

Total Return = ($2,070 − $1,800 − $30) / $1,800 = $240 / $1,800 = **13.3%**.

Notice that the carrying cost *reduces* the return — a point the exam uses to contrast metals with income-producing assets.

**Exam tip:** If a question stresses "protection against inflation and currency risk, but no current income and carrying costs," precious metals (and commodities generally) are the intended answer, not bonds or dividend stocks.

#### Digital Assets — Definition and Distinctions

A **digital asset** is a representation of value or rights that exists in purely electronic form, typically recorded on a **distributed ledger** (a **blockchain**) and secured by **cryptography**. There is no paper certificate and, for most, no central issuer or intermediary — ownership is verified across a decentralized network rather than by a single institution.

The NASAA outline asks you to distinguish digital assets **across securities, currencies, and assets**. The crucial idea is that "digital asset" is an umbrella term, and *what a particular digital asset legally is* depends on how it functions and how it is offered:

| Functional type | Description | Likely classification |
|---|---|---|
| **Cryptocurrency** (e.g., Bitcoin) | Designed as a medium of exchange / store of value; decentralized. | Often treated as a **commodity/currency** — the CFTC has called Bitcoin and Ether commodities. |
| **Security token** | A token representing an investment stake, sold to raise capital with profit expectations (e.g., many ICO tokens). | A **security** if it meets the Howey test. |
| **Utility token** | Grants access to a product or service on a network. | Depends on the facts; may still be a security if sold as an investment. |
| **Stablecoin** | Pegged to a fiat currency or asset to reduce volatility. | Regulatory status still evolving; may implicate securities, money-transmission, or banking law. |

**The Howey test determines whether a digital asset is a security.** Under *SEC v. W.J. Howey Co.*, an arrangement is an **investment contract** (and therefore a security) when there is:

1. an **investment of money**,
2. in a **common enterprise**,
3. with a **reasonable expectation of profits**,
4. derived **from the efforts of others** (a promoter or third party).

If all four prongs are met, the digital asset is a security subject to the Securities Act, the Uniform Securities Act, and state registration and anti-fraud rules — even though it is "digital." A token marketed to investors as a way to profit from a promoter's development efforts (a typical **ICO — initial coin offering**) generally satisfies Howey and is treated as a security. Bitcoin, by contrast, has no central promoter whose efforts drive an investor's profit and is generally *not* treated as a security.

**Exam tip:** The single most testable digital-asset concept is that **the Howey test — not the label "coin" or "token" — decides whether it is a security.** Do not assume all crypto is a security, and do not assume none of it is.

#### Characteristics of Digital Assets

- **Decentralization.** Many operate peer-to-peer with no central bank or clearing authority; transactions are validated by the network.
- **Blockchain ledger.** Transactions are recorded on a shared, tamper-resistant, generally public ledger.
- **Cryptographic security and self-custody.** Ownership is controlled by a **private key**. Whoever holds the key controls the asset — and losing the key means losing the asset permanently.
- **Divisibility and portability.** Assets can be split into tiny fractions and transferred globally, quickly, at any hour (**24/7/365** trading).
- **Pseudonymity.** Addresses are not overtly tied to names, though the ledger itself is transparent.
- **No inherent cash flow.** Like commodities, most digital assets pay no dividend or interest; value rests on supply/demand and adoption expectations.
- **No standard investor protection.** Digital assets are **not FDIC-insured** (they are not bank deposits) and generally **not SIPC-protected**.

#### Regulatory Uncertainty

The Series 65 explicitly wants you to acknowledge **regulatory uncertainty**. There is no single, comprehensive federal framework for digital assets; instead, overlapping regulators each claim a slice:

| Regulator | Angle |
|---|---|
| **SEC** | Digital assets that are securities (applies Howey; polices ICOs and exchanges). |
| **CFTC** | Digital assets deemed **commodities** (e.g., Bitcoin, Ether) and crypto derivatives. |
| **FinCEN** | Anti-money-laundering and money-transmission rules. |
| **IRS** | Treats virtual currency as **property**, so sales trigger capital-gain/loss reporting — *not* like spending cash. |
| **State securities regulators (NASAA members)** | Enforce state registration and **anti-fraud** provisions and issue investor alerts. Digital-asset fraud is a recurring NASAA enforcement priority. |

Because classification can shift with the facts and the rules are still developing, an adviser cannot assume a stable legal treatment. **NASAA has repeatedly flagged digital assets among its top investor-threat concerns**, emphasizing fraud, unregistered offerings, and unsuitable recommendations.

**Common trap:** Thinking a token is "unregulated." Even where no tailored digital-asset statute applies, the **anti-fraud provisions of state and federal securities law still reach any digital asset that is a security**, and an IA's fiduciary duty always applies to the advice given.

#### Risks of Digital Assets (Including Volatility)

The outline calls out **volatility** and **regulatory uncertainty** by name, but the full risk picture is testable:

- **Extreme volatility.** Prices can swing double-digit percentages in a single day. On the exam, digital assets are the archetype of a **speculative, high-risk** holding — inappropriate for conservative, income-oriented, or short-horizon investors.
- **Regulatory uncertainty.** Changing rules or enforcement can suddenly impair value or access.
- **Custody and security risk.** Exchange hacks, failures, and **lost private keys** can cause total, irreversible loss. There is no bank to reverse a fraudulent or mistaken transfer.
- **No safety net.** No FDIC or SIPC coverage, and limited recourse if a platform collapses.
- **Fraud and manipulation.** Pump-and-dump schemes, fake ICOs, and Ponzi structures are common — a core NASAA enforcement theme.
- **Liquidity risk.** Major coins are liquid, but thinly traded tokens can be difficult to sell at a fair price.
- **Valuation difficulty.** With no cash flows, there is no cash-flow-based intrinsic value; pricing rests on sentiment and adoption.

**How this ties to the adviser's duties (the exam's real target).** An investment adviser recommending or holding digital assets must still meet the **suitability** and **fiduciary** standards:

- Recommend digital assets only where consistent with the client's **risk tolerance, time horizon, objectives, and financial capacity** — typically a small, "loss-you-can-afford" allocation, if any.
- Provide **full and fair disclosure** of the volatility, custody, and regulatory risks.
- Recognize that **holding client crypto keys may constitute custody**, triggering the adviser's custody safeguards.

**Exam tip:** When a question describes a risk-averse retiree or someone needing stable income and asks whether digital assets are appropriate, the answer is generally **no** — the recommendation would violate suitability and fiduciary duty. Conversely, for a wealthy, risk-tolerant investor, a *small* speculative allocation with clear risk disclosure can be defensible.

#### How "Other Assets" Is Tested on the Series 65

Focus your recall on these high-yield points:

- **Commodities and precious metals pay no income**; their return is price change minus carrying costs, and they serve as **inflation hedges and diversifiers**.
- **Commodities and futures are regulated by the CFTC/NFA, not the SEC** — the commodity itself is *not* a security, though a commodity ETF or pool can be.
- **Precious metals** (gold especially) act as a **safe haven** and tend to move **inversely to the dollar and real interest rates**.
- For **digital assets**, apply the **Howey test** to decide whether the asset is a security; know that Bitcoin and Ether are generally treated as **commodities**, and the IRS treats crypto as **property**.
- Emphasize **regulatory uncertainty**, **extreme volatility**, the lack of **FDIC/SIPC** protection, and **custody/fraud** risk.
- Frame every digital-asset recommendation through the adviser's **suitability and fiduciary** obligations — speculative, disclose fully, size appropriately.

---

## Part III — Client Investment Recommendations and Strategies

*30% of the exam · approximately 39 questions.*

### A. Type of Client

Before you can recommend a single security, you must know *who* the client is. On the Series 65, "type of client" is not trivia — the legal form of the account controls three things the exam tests over and over:

- **Taxation** — who pays tax on the account's income and gains (the entity itself, or the people behind it).
- **Liability** — whether the owners' personal assets are exposed to the entity's debts and lawsuits.
- **Account authority** — who is legally allowed to give instructions on the account, and what document proves it.

Keep those three lenses — **tax, liability, authority** — in mind for every entity below. Most "type of client" questions are really asking about one of the three.

A useful starting distinction: a **natural person** is a living human being; a **legal person** (also called an artificial or juridical person) is an entity — a corporation, LLC, partnership, trust, or estate — that the law treats as capable of owning property and entering contracts. Some entities (a C- or S-corporation, an LLC, an irrevocable trust, an estate) are **separate legal persons** from their owners; others (a sole proprietorship, a revocable trust) are legally *the same* as the individual behind them. That separateness is the root of almost every taxation, liability, and authority answer.

#### Individual / Natural Person

An **individual account** is owned by one natural person who has full control and is the sole beneficial owner. It is the simplest client type.

- **Taxation:** All income, dividends, interest, and capital gains are reported on the individual's own Form 1040 and taxed at personal rates.
- **Liability:** Not applicable in the entity sense — the person simply owns the assets directly.
- **Authority:** Only the account owner may transact, unless the owner grants **trading authorization**. A **limited power of attorney** lets a third party trade but not withdraw funds; a **full power of attorney** also permits withdrawals. Authority ends at the owner's death, at which point the assets pass to the estate.

**Exam tip:** Watch for **legal capacity**. To open an account in one's own name, a person must be of legal age (the age of majority, generally 18) and mentally competent. Accounts for minors are handled under **UTMA/UGMA** custodial rules, not as individual accounts.

#### Sole Proprietorship

A **sole proprietorship** is an unincorporated business owned by one individual. The single most important idea to lock in: **a sole proprietorship is not a separate legal entity from its owner.** The business and the person are one and the same in the eyes of the law.

- **Taxation:** Fully "pass-through." Business income is reported on **Schedule C** and flows onto the owner's personal Form 1040. There is no entity-level tax.
- **Liability:** **Unlimited personal liability.** Because there is no legal separation, business creditors can reach the owner's personal assets (home, savings, personal investments). This is the proprietorship's biggest weakness.
- **Authority:** The proprietor has complete authority over the account, exactly like an individual.

**Common trap:** A question may describe "a business owner" and tempt you toward corporate rules. If the business is a sole proprietorship, treat it like the individual — one taxpayer, unlimited liability, one decision-maker. The business assets and personal assets are legally indistinguishable.

#### Business Entities Overview

Once more than one owner is involved, or the owners want liability protection, a formal entity is used. The exam expects you to distinguish five forms: **general partnership, limited partnership, LLC, C-corporation, and S-corporation.** The table below is the high-yield summary; the sections that follow explain each.

| Entity | Liability of owners | Taxation | Who has account authority |
|---|---|---|---|
| General partnership | Unlimited, joint & several — all partners | Pass-through (Form 1065 → K-1s) | Any general partner (per partnership agreement) |
| Limited partnership | GP: unlimited; LPs: limited to investment | Pass-through (Form 1065 → K-1s) | The general partner(s) only |
| LLC | Limited to investment (members) | Flexible — "check-the-box" (default pass-through) | Member(s) or manager per operating agreement |
| C-corporation | Limited to investment (shareholders) | **Double taxation** (Form 1120) | Officers named in a corporate resolution |
| S-corporation | Limited to investment (shareholders) | Pass-through (Form 1120-S → K-1s) | Officers named in a corporate resolution |

#### General Partnership

A **general partnership (GP)** is an association of two or more people carrying on a business for profit. It is easy to form (it can arise without any filing) but offers no liability shield.

- **Taxation:** Pass-through. The partnership files an **informational return, Form 1065**, and issues each partner a **Schedule K-1** reporting their share of income, deductions, and credits. The partnership itself pays no income tax; partners report their shares on their personal returns.
- **Liability:** Each general partner has **unlimited personal liability**, and it is **joint and several** — a creditor can pursue any one partner for the *entire* obligation, not just that partner's fractional share. Partners are also generally liable for acts of the other partners taken in the ordinary course of business.
- **Authority:** Any general partner can typically bind the partnership. To open a securities account, the firm requires a **partnership agreement** (and often a **partnership resolution**) identifying who is authorized to transact.

**Common trap:** "Joint and several liability" means a single partner can be made to pay 100% of a claim even if they own only, say, 25% of the partnership. Don't limit a partner's exposure to their ownership percentage.

#### Limited Partnership

A **limited partnership (LP)** has two classes of owner and must have at least one of each:

- **General partner (GP):** Manages the business and carries **unlimited liability**.
- **Limited partner (LP):** A passive investor whose liability is **limited to the amount invested**. In exchange for that protection, limited partners generally may **not participate in management**.

- **Taxation:** Pass-through, with mechanics identical to a general partnership — Form 1065 and K-1s to all partners.
- **Liability:** As above. The critical rule: a **limited partner who takes an active role in management can lose limited-liability protection** and be treated like a general partner.
- **Authority:** Only the **general partner** has authority to open and direct the account. A limited partner cannot bind the partnership.

**Exam tip:** LPs are the classic structure for pooled real-estate and oil-and-gas programs. Remember the trade-off: limited partners get liability protection *because* they give up control. Every LP must have at least one general partner who bears unlimited liability.

#### Limited Liability Company (LLC)

An **LLC** is a hybrid that combines the **limited liability of a corporation** with the **tax flexibility of a partnership.** Owners are called **members**, and the entity is governed by an **operating agreement.**

- **Taxation — the "check-the-box" rule:** An LLC can elect how it is taxed.
  - A **single-member LLC** is by default a "disregarded entity," taxed like a sole proprietorship.
  - A **multi-member LLC** is by default taxed as a partnership (pass-through).
  - Any LLC may instead **elect** to be taxed as a **C-corporation or S-corporation.**
- **Liability:** Members enjoy **limited liability** — personal assets are protected from business debts (barring fraud or personal guarantees).
- **Authority:** Governed by the operating agreement. An LLC may be **member-managed** (members transact directly) or **manager-managed** (a designated manager transacts). The custodian requires the **operating agreement** to confirm who is authorized.

**Exam tip:** If a question stresses that an entity wants *both* liability protection *and* pass-through taxation with maximum flexibility, the answer is an **LLC.** Its defining feature is flexibility of tax treatment.

#### C-Corporation

A **C-corporation** is a corporation taxed under Subchapter C — the default corporate form. It is a fully **separate legal person** from its owners (shareholders).

- **Taxation — double taxation:** This is the C-corp's signature exam point. Corporate profits are taxed **once at the corporate level** (Form 1120), and then again **at the shareholder level** when after-tax profits are distributed as **dividends.** The same dollars are taxed twice.
- **Liability:** Shareholders have **limited liability** — the most they can lose is their investment. Personal assets are shielded.
- **Authority:** A **corporate resolution** (board authorization) must name the officers empowered to open and transact in the account.

**Worked example — the cost of double taxation:**

> A C-corporation earns **$1,000,000** in pre-tax profit and distributes all after-tax profit as dividends.
>
> Formula: Total tax = Corporate tax + Shareholder tax on the distributed amount
>
> - Corporate tax at 21% = 0.21 × $1,000,000 = **$210,000**
> - After-tax profit available to distribute = $1,000,000 − $210,000 = **$790,000**
> - Shareholder tax on qualified dividends at 15% = 0.15 × $790,000 = **$118,500**
> - **Total tax paid = $210,000 + $118,500 = $328,500**
> - Effective combined rate = $328,500 / $1,000,000 = **32.85%**
>
> (The 21% federal corporate rate and 15% qualified-dividend rate are used here as standard illustrative figures; the exam tests the *concept* of two layers of tax, not a memorized combined rate.)

**Common trap:** Double taxation applies to **distributed** earnings. Profits the corporation retains and reinvests are taxed at the corporate level but not (yet) at the shareholder level — the second layer hits only when a dividend is paid.

#### S-Corporation

An **S-corporation** is a corporation that has **elected** Subchapter S status to avoid double taxation while keeping the corporate liability shield. It behaves like a corporation for liability and like a partnership for taxes.

- **Taxation:** **Pass-through.** The S-corp files **Form 1120-S** (informational) and issues **K-1s**; income is taxed once, on the shareholders' personal returns. This eliminates the corporate-level layer — in the example above, the $210,000 corporate tax would never be assessed.
- **Liability:** **Limited liability**, same as a C-corporation.
- **Authority:** Same as any corporation — a **corporate resolution** names authorized officers.
- **Eligibility restrictions (frequently tested):** To qualify for S status, a corporation must:
  - Have **no more than 100 shareholders**;
  - Have **only one class of stock**;
  - Have shareholders who are generally **individuals** who are **U.S. citizens or resident aliens** (certain estates and specific trusts also qualify); and
  - **Not** have shareholders that are **corporations, partnerships, or non-resident aliens.**

**Exam tip:** The clean way to remember it — an **S-corp = corporate liability protection + partnership-style single taxation.** If a question wants to avoid double taxation *and* keep the corporate shield, the answer is S-corporation (or an LLC electing S status).

**Common trap:** Don't confuse *number of shareholders* with *classes of stock.* An S-corp may have up to **100 shareholders** but only **one class of stock** (differences in voting rights are allowed; differences in distribution or liquidation rights are not).

#### Trusts

A **trust** is a legal arrangement in which one party (the **grantor**, also called settlor or trustor) transfers assets to a **trustee**, who manages them for the benefit of one or more **beneficiaries.** The document that creates and governs it is the **trust agreement (trust instrument).**

Two key classifications the exam tests:

**1. Revocable vs. irrevocable**

| Feature | Revocable (living/inter vivos) trust | Irrevocable trust |
|---|---|---|
| Can grantor change/cancel? | Yes | No (generally) |
| Separate taxpayer? | No — grantor is taxed on the income | Yes — the trust is its own taxpayer |
| Assets in grantor's estate? | Yes | Generally removed from estate |
| Creditor / liability protection | Little | Stronger |

- A **revocable trust** is a "grantor trust": the grantor keeps control, so the trust's income is taxed to the **grantor** personally. Its main benefit is **avoiding probate**, not saving tax.
- An **irrevocable trust** is a separate legal and taxable entity. It files **Form 1041** and can shift assets out of the grantor's taxable estate, offering estate-tax and asset-protection advantages — at the cost of the grantor giving up control.

**2. Living vs. testamentary**

- A **living (inter vivos) trust** is created during the grantor's lifetime.
- A **testamentary trust** is created by the grantor's **will** and comes into existence only **at death.**

Applying the three lenses:

- **Taxation:** Depends on type (see table). Irrevocable and testamentary trusts are separate taxpayers. **Exam-relevant point:** trusts reach the **highest marginal income-tax bracket at a very low income threshold** (the brackets are "compressed"), which pushes trustees toward distributing income to beneficiaries or investing for tax efficiency.
- **Liability:** Trust assets are generally distinct from both the trustee's and the beneficiaries' personal assets.
- **Authority:** The **trustee** — not the beneficiary — has authority to transact. The custodian requires the **trust agreement** or a **certificate of trust** identifying the trustee and their powers. The trustee is a **fiduciary** and, when investing, is bound by the **prudent investor rule** (the Uniform Prudent Investor Act): invest prudently, diversify, and act in the beneficiaries' best interest, judging the portfolio as a whole.

**Common trap:** The **beneficiary cannot direct the account** — only the trustee can. And in a **revocable** trust, remember the income is still taxed to the **grantor**, not the trust.

#### Estates

An **estate** is the legal entity that holds a deceased person's assets from the moment of death until those assets are distributed to heirs. It is a **temporary, separate taxable entity.**

- **Who runs it:** The **executor** (named in a valid will) or the **administrator** (appointed by the court when there is no will) — collectively the **personal representative.**
- **Taxation — two different taxes, don't confuse them:**
  - **Estate tax:** A transfer tax on the value of the estate itself, owed before assets pass to heirs (only very large estates exceeding the federal exemption are affected).
  - **Estate income tax:** Income the estate *earns during administration* (interest, dividends, gains) is reported on **Form 1041**, just like a trust.
- **Liability:** The estate's debts are paid from estate assets; heirs are not personally liable for the decedent's debts beyond what they inherit.
- **Authority:** Only the personal representative may transact. The custodian requires **letters testamentary** (for an executor) or **letters of administration** (for a court-appointed administrator) as proof of authority.

**Exam tip:** Distinguish **estate tax** (a tax on the *transfer* of wealth at death) from **income tax on the estate** (a tax on money the estate *earns* while being settled). A question mentioning Form 1041 is pointing at the income-tax side.

#### Foundations and Charities

**Foundations and charities** are typically organized as **tax-exempt organizations under IRC Section 501(c)(3).** They exist for charitable, educational, religious, or scientific purposes rather than for private profit.

- **Two broad categories:**
  - **Public charities** receive broad public support (churches, hospitals, universities, community organizations).
  - **Private foundations** are usually funded by a single source — an individual, family, or corporation — and often make grants to other charities (e.g., a family foundation with an endowment).
- **Taxation:** Generally **tax-exempt** on income related to their charitable mission, and donors can take a tax deduction for contributions. **Important exception:** income from a regularly carried-on trade or business unrelated to the charitable purpose is **unrelated business taxable income (UBTI)** and *is* taxable. Private foundations are also subject to certain excise taxes and minimum annual distribution requirements.
- **Liability:** As formal entities (usually nonprofit corporations or trusts), they provide limited liability to their trustees/directors acting in good faith.
- **Authority:** A **board of directors/trustees** or a designated **investment committee** holds authority. The custodian requires the organizing documents and a **resolution** naming authorized signers. Investment decisions for endowment funds are governed by **UPMIFA** (the Uniform Prudent Management of Institutional Funds Act), which — like the prudent investor rule — requires prudent, diversified management that balances current spending needs against preserving the fund for the future.

**Exam tip:** Tax-exempt status is not unlimited. If a question describes a charity running a business activity unrelated to its mission, that income is **UBTI** and taxable. Also remember these clients emphasize **capital preservation, income, and a long or perpetual time horizon** when suitability is tested.

**Putting it together — the three lenses recap:**

| Client type | Liability | Taxation | Authority document |
|---|---|---|---|
| Individual | N/A (owns directly) | Personal (1040) | Owner; POA if delegated |
| Sole proprietorship | Unlimited | Pass-through (Schedule C) | Owner |
| General partnership | Unlimited, joint & several | Pass-through (1065/K-1) | Partnership agreement |
| Limited partnership | GP unlimited; LP limited | Pass-through (1065/K-1) | General partner |
| LLC | Limited | Flexible (default pass-through) | Operating agreement |
| C-corporation | Limited | Double taxation (1120) | Corporate resolution |
| S-corporation | Limited | Pass-through (1120-S/K-1) | Corporate resolution |
| Revocable trust | Weak | Grantor taxed | Trust agreement (trustee) |
| Irrevocable trust | Stronger | Separate taxpayer (1041) | Trust agreement (trustee) |
| Estate | Estate assets only | Estate income tax (1041) | Letters testamentary/administration |
| Foundation/charity | Limited | Tax-exempt (UBTI taxable) | Board/committee resolution |

### B. Client Profile

Before an investment adviser representative (IAR) can recommend a single security or strategy, the adviser's fiduciary duty and professional standards require that the recommendation be **suitable** for — and in the **best interest** of — the specific client. To meet that standard you must first *know the client*. The organized collection of everything relevant about a client is called the **client profile** (sometimes "customer profile" or "investment profile"). It is the foundation of the suitability analysis: no valid recommendation exists without it.

A useful way to remember the required elements is that the profile combines **financial** facts (goals, cash flow, net worth, tax status, time horizon) with **nonfinancial** facts (risk attitude, values, experience, life stage, behavioral tendencies). Regulators expect the adviser to gather this information *before* advising, to keep it current, and to document it.

**Exam tip:** On the Series 65, the phrase "before making any recommendation, the IAR must…" almost always resolves to "**determine suitability / gather the client's financial situation, objectives, and risk tolerance**." Suitability is not a one-time event — it must be updated when the client's circumstances change.

**Common trap:** Suitability is not judged only by the client's *stated desire*. A client can *want* an aggressive growth fund, but if their situation, time horizon, or risk capacity does not support it, the recommendation is unsuitable. Wanting a product is not the same as it being appropriate.

#### 1. Gathering Client Data (Identification, Questionnaires, Interviews)

The profile is built through a **data-gathering process**. The three tested methods are:

| Method | What it is | Strengths / limits |
|---|---|---|
| **Identification** | Verifying who the client legally is — legal name, date of birth, physical residential or business address, and Social Security number or Tax ID | Required to open the account; anti-money-laundering (AML) compliance, not just suitability |
| **Questionnaire / fact-finder** | A structured written form the client completes covering assets, liabilities, income, objectives, risk tolerance, and time horizon | Efficient, consistent, creates a documented record; but relies on the client's self-reporting and can miss nuance |
| **Interview** | A face-to-face or verbal conversation with the client | Captures nuance, emotion, values, and follow-up detail a form cannot; but is less standardized and harder to document |

Best practice combines methods: the written fact-finder captures the hard data, and the interview probes attitudes, values, and inconsistencies. This is often summarized as **"Know Your Customer" (KYC)**.

**Identification** ties into two overlapping obligations you should recognize:
- A **Customer Identification Program (CIP)** under the USA PATRIOT Act / AML rules — the firm must verify the client's identity (name, DOB, physical address, and an identification number such as an SSN or TIN) and check the client against government lists.
- **Suitability/KYC**, which goes beyond identity to the client's financial and personal circumstances.

**Exam tip:** If a client *refuses* to provide financial information, the adviser may not simply guess. The recommendation must rest on the information reasonably available; if essential suitability information (objectives, financial situation, risk tolerance) is missing, the prudent answer is generally to **decline to make a specific recommendation** or limit advice to what can be justified.

**Common trap:** Collecting the information once at account opening is not enough. Profiles must be **periodically updated** — especially after major life events — because suitability is measured against the client's *current* situation.

#### 2. Financial Goals and Objectives

**Goals** are what the client wants their money to accomplish; **objectives** translate those goals into investment terms. Common objectives, in the language the exam uses:

- **Preservation of capital / safety** — protect principal; accept low return (money market, T-bills, CDs).
- **Income** — generate current cash flow (bonds, dividend stocks, annuities).
- **Growth (capital appreciation)** — increase asset value over time (equities); often paired as "growth and income."
- **Speculation / aggressive growth** — pursue high return, accept high risk of loss (options, small-cap/emerging growth).
- **Liquidity** — ability to convert to cash quickly without loss of principal (note: this is a constraint/need, not a return objective).
- **Tax minimization / tax advantages** — reduce the tax drag (municipal bonds, tax-deferred accounts).

Goals should be **specific and prioritized**, because they often conflict. The classic point is that objectives cannot all be maximized at once — for example, an investment cannot be simultaneously highest **safety**, highest **return**, and highest **liquidity**. Something must be traded off.

**Exam tip:** Watch for the **conflicting-objectives** question — e.g., a client who wants "maximum growth **and** complete safety of principal." That combination is internally inconsistent; the correct answer identifies the conflict or picks the objective the client's situation supports.

**Common trap:** "Liquidity" is not a return goal. An investment being liquid (easily sold) is different from it being safe or high-yielding. Real estate is an illiquid asset even when it grows in value.

#### 3. Time Horizon

**Time horizon** is the length of time until the client needs the money for its intended goal. It is one of the most important inputs because it drives how much risk the portfolio can prudently take: a longer horizon allows the portfolio to ride out short-term volatility, so more equity exposure is appropriate; a short horizon calls for stable, liquid, lower-volatility holdings.

| Horizon | Typical range | Appropriate emphasis |
|---|---|---|
| Short-term | 0–3 years | Preservation, liquidity — money markets, short CDs, T-bills |
| Intermediate | 3–10 years | Balanced — mix of bonds and equities |
| Long-term | 10+ years | Growth — heavier equity allocation, can absorb volatility |

Time horizon also **shortens as a goal approaches** — a retirement goal that was 25 years away at age 40 is a short horizon by age 64. This is the rationale behind target-date ("glide path") funds, which grow more conservative as the target year nears.

**Exam tip:** Longer time horizon → **greater capacity for risk/volatility**. A young investor saving for retirement can hold volatile growth assets *because* time smooths out short-term losses. Conversely, money needed in 18 months for a house down payment should **not** be in equities.

**Common trap:** Age alone does not dictate the horizon. A 75-year-old investing for grandchildren or a bequest may have a *long* horizon; a 30-year-old needing tuition next year has a *short* one. Match horizon to the **goal**, not just the client's age.

#### 4. Current and Future Financial Situation

The adviser must quantify where the client stands financially today and where they are heading. Two core financial statements are tested:

**a) Balance sheet (net worth statement).**
This is a snapshot of what the client owns versus what they owe at a point in time.

Formula (plain text):

Net Worth = Total Assets − Total Liabilities

Worked example:

| Assets |  | Liabilities |  |
|---|---|---|---|
| Home (market value) | $400,000 | Mortgage | $280,000 |
| Retirement accounts | $250,000 | Auto loan | $12,000 |
| Brokerage account | $100,000 | Credit card balances | $8,000 |
| Cash / savings | $30,000 |  |  |
| Automobile | $20,000 |  |  |
| **Total assets** | **$800,000** | **Total liabilities** | **$300,000** |

Net Worth = $800,000 − $300,000 = **$500,000**

Related liquidity concept — not every asset can be turned into cash quickly. The home ($400,000) and automobile ($20,000) are **illiquid**. The client's liquid holdings total $250,000 retirement + $100,000 brokerage + $30,000 cash = **$380,000**, but retirement accounts generally carry a 10% early-withdrawal penalty before age 59½, so truly accessible emergency reserves are closer to the **$130,000** in the brokerage account plus cash.

**b) Cash flow statement (income vs. expenses).**
This measures the client's ability to *save and invest* out of ongoing income.

Formula (plain text):

Net Cash Flow = Total Income − Total Expenses

Worked example:

- Monthly gross income: $9,000
- Monthly expenses (housing, debt service, living costs, taxes): $7,200
- Net monthly cash flow = $9,000 − $7,200 = **$1,800 surplus**
- Annualized investable surplus = $1,800 × 12 = **$21,600 per year**

A **positive** (surplus) cash flow means the client has **discretionary income** to invest or build reserves; a **negative** (deficit) cash flow signals that new investment is inappropriate until spending or debt is addressed. A high-net-worth client with poor cash flow may still be constrained.

**Emergency fund / liquidity need.** A standard planning benchmark is to hold **3 to 6 months of living expenses** in liquid, safe reserves before committing to longer-term or volatile investments.

**c) Existing investments.**
The adviser must review what the client already holds to assess **diversification, concentration, and overlap**. A recommendation is judged in the context of the *whole* portfolio, not in isolation. A classic red flag is **concentration** — e.g., a large position in the client's employer stock — which creates unsystematic (diversifiable) risk that a broader allocation could reduce.

**d) Tax situation.**
Two rates are commonly distinguished:

- **Marginal tax rate** — the rate on the *next* (highest) dollar of income; used to evaluate the tax benefit of an additional investment.
- **Effective (average) tax rate** — total tax paid divided by total income.

For evaluating a *new* investment's tax impact, use the **marginal** rate.

Two tested formulas:

After-Tax Yield = Taxable Yield × (1 − Marginal Tax Rate)

Tax-Equivalent Yield = Tax-Free (Municipal) Yield / (1 − Marginal Tax Rate)

Worked example — should a client in the **32%** bracket choose a **3.5%** municipal bond or a **5.0%** corporate bond?

- After-tax yield of the corporate = 5.0% × (1 − 0.32) = 5.0% × 0.68 = **3.40%**
- Tax-equivalent yield of the muni = 3.5% / (1 − 0.32) = 3.5% / 0.68 = **5.15%**

The municipal's after-tax return (3.5%, since its interest is federally tax-free) beats the corporate's after-tax return (3.40%), and its tax-equivalent yield (5.15%) exceeds the corporate's 5.0%. For a **high-bracket** client, the muni is superior. For a low-bracket client the taxable corporate would usually win — the break-even here is a 30% bracket — so **the client's tax bracket determines whether tax-exempt bonds make sense.**

**Exam tip:** Municipal bonds and other tax-favored products are most valuable to clients in **high** marginal brackets. Never put a tax-free muni inside a tax-deferred account like an IRA — you would waste the tax exemption (and, in a traditional account, convert tax-free interest into ordinary income at withdrawal).

**e) Social Security and pensions (future / retirement income).**
The profile must account for **guaranteed or expected future income streams**, because they reduce how much the client's own portfolio must generate and may increase the client's *capacity* to take investment risk.

| Feature | Defined benefit (pension) | Defined contribution (401(k)/403(b)) |
|---|---|---|
| Who bears investment risk | The employer | The employee |
| Payout | Guaranteed formula-based lifetime income | Depends on contributions and market performance |
| Predictability for planning | High (a reliable income floor) | Variable |

**Social Security** provides an inflation-adjusted (COLA), government-backed income floor; a traditional **defined-benefit pension** provides another. A client with a solid pension plus Social Security has a dependable income "floor" and may be able to take **more** risk with the discretionary portfolio, since day-to-day living expenses are already covered. A client relying entirely on a **defined-contribution** plan bears the market risk themselves and typically needs a more conservative, income-aware approach as retirement nears.

**Common trap:** More total wealth does not automatically mean more investment risk is appropriate. Capacity depends on the *reliability* of income sources and the *stability* of cash flow, not just the size of the balance sheet.

#### 5. Risk Tolerance

**Risk tolerance** is how much loss or volatility the client can and will accept. The exam splits it into two distinct components that must **both** be satisfied:

| Component | Also called | Nature | Driven by |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Ability to take risk** | Risk capacity | Objective / financial | Time horizon, net worth, cash flow, income stability, liquidity needs |
| **Willingness to take risk** | Risk attitude | Subjective / emotional | The client's psychological comfort with loss |

The **binding constraint is the lower of the two.** If a client has the financial *ability* to take risk but is emotionally *unwilling* (they will panic-sell in a downturn), the portfolio should respect the lower willingness. Conversely, a client eager to gamble but with no financial cushion lacks the *ability*, and the adviser should not accommodate the appetite.

**Exam tip:** When ability and willingness **conflict**, the generally accepted approach is to educate the client and then invest at the **more conservative** level — usually the lower of the two — and document the discussion. Never solve a conflict by ignoring the client's emotional limits.

**Common trap:** Risk tolerance is not the same as time horizon or net worth, though they influence it. A wealthy client can still have low willingness; a young client can still be risk-averse. Test each element separately.

#### 6. Nonfinancial Considerations

Numbers alone do not define a client. The exam explicitly tests several **nonfinancial factors** that shape a suitable recommendation.

**a) Values-based investing — ESG and religious values.**
Some clients want their portfolio to reflect their beliefs. This is generally captured under **socially responsible / ESG (Environmental, Social, Governance) investing** and **faith-based (religious) investing**. Such clients may **screen out** industries they object to (e.g., tobacco, firearms, alcohol, gambling, fossil fuels) or **screen in** companies meeting certain standards. The adviser must honor these constraints even when a screened-out security would otherwise be suitable. These preferences act as **investment constraints**, similar to a legal or liquidity constraint.

**b) Investment experience and knowledge.**
A client's familiarity with markets and complex products affects what is appropriate. A sophisticated client may understand options and margin; an inexperienced client needs simpler products and more explanation. Experience influences both the products recommended and the adviser's disclosure obligations.

**c) Life events.**
Marriage, divorce, birth or adoption of a child, a new job or job loss, a large inheritance, disability, or the death of a spouse each materially change goals, cash flow, risk capacity, and tax status. Life events are the primary trigger to **update the profile** and re-test suitability.

**d) Life stage (life-cycle investing).**
Financial needs evolve predictably across the life cycle:

| Stage | Emphasis |
|---|---|
| Accumulation (younger, working) | Growth; long horizon; higher equity |
| Consolidation (mid-career, peak earning) | Growth + income; moderate risk |
| Spending / gifting (retirement) | Income and preservation; lower risk; liquidity |

A general (not rigid) guideline is that **equity exposure declines as the client ages** and the horizon shortens, because there is less time to recover from losses.

**e) Behavioral finance biases.**
Behavioral finance recognizes that clients do not act as perfectly rational investors. The exam distinguishes **two families** of bias, and the distinction drives the "correct" adviser response:

- **Cognitive biases** — errors of *faulty reasoning or information processing*. Because they are informational errors, they can usually be **corrected through education and better data.**
- **Emotional biases** — reactions rooted in *feeling and impulse*. They are harder to fix with facts, so the adviser more often **adapts or moderates the portfolio** to accommodate them.

| Bias | Family | What it looks like |
|---|---|---|
| **Anchoring** | Cognitive | Fixating on a reference price (e.g., "I'll sell when it gets back to what I paid") |
| **Confirmation bias** | Cognitive | Seeking only information that supports an existing view |
| **Availability bias** | Cognitive | Overweighting easily recalled or recent, vivid events |
| **Recency bias** | Cognitive | Assuming recent performance will continue |
| **Framing** | Cognitive | Deciding differently based on how a choice is worded (gain vs. loss) |
| **Mental accounting** | Cognitive | Treating money differently by "bucket" (spending a bonus recklessly while hoarding salary) |
| **Overconfidence** | Emotional | Overestimating one's own knowledge/skill; excessive trading |
| **Loss aversion** | Emotional | Feeling losses far more than equivalent gains; refusing to sell losers |
| **Regret aversion** | Emotional | Avoiding action for fear of a later-regretted decision; inertia |
| **Status quo bias** | Emotional | Preferring to leave the portfolio unchanged |
| **Endowment effect** | Emotional | Overvaluing something simply because you own it (e.g., inherited stock) |
| **Herd mentality (herding)** | Emotional | Following the crowd into or out of the market |

**Exam tip:** Memorize the standard remedies. For a **cognitive** bias, the best answer is usually to **educate/inform** the client. For an **emotional** bias, the best answer is usually to **accommodate/moderate** within a suitable portfolio rather than fight the emotion head-on.

**Common trap:** Do not confuse **loss aversion** (hating losses more than valuing equal gains, leading clients to *hold* losers too long) with simple risk aversion. And distinguish the **disposition effect** — selling winners too early while holding losers too long — which is a downstream consequence of loss aversion and mental accounting.

Together, these financial and nonfinancial elements form the complete client profile. Every recommendation you make on the exam must be defensible against this profile: right objective, right time horizon, right risk level, right tax treatment, and consistent with the client's values, experience, and behavioral tendencies.

### C. Capital Market Theory

Capital market theory is the body of academic finance that answers three linked questions: *What return should I expect for taking on risk? How should I combine securities to get the best trade-off between risk and return? And how efficiently do prices already reflect information?* On the Series 65 these ideas are tested conceptually and with a handful of very predictable calculations. You will not derive anything, but you will plug numbers into the CAPM formula, identify what the efficient frontier represents, and match each form of the Efficient Market Hypothesis to the type of analysis it renders useless. Master those three tasks and this subsection is easy points.

First fix one distinction, because everything below depends on it:

- **Systematic risk** (also called *market risk* or *non-diversifiable risk*) affects the entire market — interest rates, recession, inflation, war. You cannot diversify it away. It is measured by **beta**.
- **Unsystematic risk** (also called *specific*, *business*, *diversifiable*, or *non-systematic risk*) is unique to one company or industry — a lawsuit, a product recall, a bad CEO. You *can* diversify it away by holding many securities.

Capital market theory rewards investors only for bearing *systematic* risk, because unsystematic risk is something the investor could have removed for free through diversification.

#### The Capital Asset Pricing Model (CAPM)

The **Capital Asset Pricing Model** answers the question: given how much systematic risk a security carries, what return should an investor reasonably expect? CAPM says the expected return equals the risk-free return plus an extra reward for the systematic risk taken on.

The formula, in plain text, is:

**Expected Return = Risk-Free Rate + Beta × (Market Return − Risk-Free Rate)**

Or in the shorthand you may see on the exam:

**E(R) = Rf + β × (Rm − Rf)**

Where:
- **Rf** = the risk-free rate, almost always represented by short-term U.S. Treasury bills.
- **Rm** = the expected return of the overall market (e.g., the S&P 500).
- **β (beta)** = the security's sensitivity to market movements — its measure of systematic risk.
- **(Rm − Rf)** = the **market risk premium**, the extra return investors demand for holding the market instead of a risk-free T-bill.
- **β × (Rm − Rf)** = the security's own risk premium.

**Understanding beta.** Beta is the engine of CAPM. It measures how much a security moves relative to the market:

| Beta | Meaning |
|------|---------|
| β = 1.0 | Moves in line with the market (same volatility) |
| β > 1.0 | More volatile than the market (aggressive; e.g., a tech stock at 1.5 tends to move 15% when the market moves 10%) |
| β < 1.0 | Less volatile than the market (defensive; e.g., a utility at 0.6) |
| β = 0 | No systematic risk (a risk-free asset has a beta of 0) |
| β < 0 | Moves opposite to the market (rare; e.g., certain hedging instruments) |

**Worked example.** A stock has a beta of 1.5. Treasury bills yield 3%, and the overall market is expected to return 9%. What is the stock's expected return under CAPM?

1. Market risk premium = Rm − Rf = 9% − 3% = **6%**
2. Stock's risk premium = β × market risk premium = 1.5 × 6% = **9%**
3. Expected Return = Rf + stock's risk premium = 3% + 9% = **12%**

So an investor should expect a 12% return to be fairly compensated for this stock's systematic risk. If the analyst believes the stock will actually return *more* than 12%, it is attractive (undervalued); if less, it is unattractive (overvalued).

**A second example to cement the mechanics.** Beta = 0.8, Rf = 2%, Rm = 7%: E(R) = 2% + 0.8 × (7% − 2%) = 2% + 0.8 × 5% = 2% + 4% = **6%**.

**Alpha — the natural follow-up concept.** CAPM tells you the return a security *should* earn. **Alpha** measures how much a security's *actual* (or forecast) return exceeds or falls short of that CAPM benchmark.

**Alpha = Actual Return − CAPM Expected Return**

If our first stock actually returned 14% against a CAPM expected return of 12%, its alpha is +2% — it outperformed on a risk-adjusted basis, a sign of manager skill or an undervalued security. A negative alpha means underperformance for the risk taken.

**Exam tip:** The most common CAPM question hands you Rf, Rm, and beta and asks for the expected return. Work it in the order above — find the market risk premium first, multiply by beta, then add the risk-free rate. A frequent wrong-answer trap multiplies beta by the *full market return* (1.5 × 9%) instead of by the *market risk premium* (1.5 × 6%). Beta scales only the **premium**, never the whole market return.

**Common trap:** CAPM uses **beta** (systematic risk), *not* standard deviation (total risk). Any answer implying CAPM compensates investors for total risk or for company-specific risk is wrong. CAPM rewards only non-diversifiable, systematic risk.

**The Security Market Line (SML).** Graphing the CAPM expected return (vertical axis) against beta (horizontal axis) produces a straight line called the **Security Market Line**. Every fairly priced security plots *on* the SML. A security plotting *above* the line offers more return than its beta warrants (undervalued, positive alpha); one *below* it is overvalued (negative alpha). Do not confuse the SML with the **Capital Market Line (CML)**, discussed below — the SML uses **beta** on its horizontal axis, while the CML uses **standard deviation**.

#### Modern Portfolio Theory (MPT)

**Modern Portfolio Theory**, developed by Nobel laureate **Harry Markowitz**, made a revolutionary claim: a security should be evaluated not in isolation but by what it contributes to the risk and return of the *entire portfolio*. A volatile, risky stock can actually *lower* overall portfolio risk if it zigs when the rest of the portfolio zags. MPT is a theory about building portfolios, and its two big deliverables are **the role of correlation in diversification** and **the efficient frontier**.

**Risk aversion is the starting assumption.** MPT assumes investors are rational and risk-averse: for any given expected return they prefer the least risk, and for any given level of risk they prefer the highest expected return. Here "risk" is measured by **standard deviation**, the dispersion of a portfolio's returns around its average — a measure of *total* risk (systematic plus unsystematic).

**Correlation is the key to diversification.** Combining securities reduces risk because securities do not move in perfect lockstep. This relationship is captured by the **correlation coefficient**, which always ranges from **−1 to +1**:

| Correlation | Meaning | Diversification benefit |
|-------------|---------|-------------------------|
| +1.0 | Perfectly positive — securities move exactly together | **None** — no risk reduction |
| Between 0 and +1 | Move together imperfectly (most real-world pairs) | Some risk reduction |
| 0 | Uncorrelated — no relationship in movements | Meaningful risk reduction |
| Between −1 and 0 | Tend to move opposite imperfectly | Strong risk reduction |
| −1.0 | Perfectly negative — move exactly opposite | **Maximum** — risk can theoretically be eliminated |

The lesson: **the lower (more negative) the correlation between holdings, the greater the diversification benefit.** Adding a security highly positively correlated with what you already own does almost nothing to reduce risk. This is why advisers combine asset classes that behave differently (e.g., stocks and bonds) rather than ten stocks in the same industry.

**Diversification eliminates only unsystematic risk.** As you add more, less-correlated securities, the portfolio's unsystematic risk shrinks toward zero. But no amount of diversification removes systematic (market) risk — that floor remains. This ties back to CAPM: because diversification can wipe out unsystematic risk for free, the market pays you (via beta) only for the systematic risk you cannot escape.

**The efficient frontier.** Plot every possible portfolio on a graph with **risk (standard deviation) on the horizontal axis** and **expected return on the vertical axis.** The cloud of portfolios has an upper-left boundary. The **efficient frontier** is that curved boundary: the set of portfolios offering the **highest expected return for each level of risk** (equivalently, the lowest risk for each level of return).

- A portfolio **on** the efficient frontier is **efficient** — you cannot get more return without accepting more risk.
- A portfolio **below/inside** the frontier is **inefficient** — another portfolio exists with the same risk but higher return, or the same return with lower risk. A rational investor would never hold it.
- Nothing can exist **above** the frontier; it represents the best achievable trade-offs.

Which point *on* the frontier an investor chooses depends on personal risk tolerance: a conservative investor picks a spot toward the lower-left (less risk, less return), an aggressive investor a spot toward the upper-right.

**The Capital Market Line (CML).** As an extension of MPT, introducing a risk-free asset turns the optimal set of portfolios into a straight line running from the risk-free rate up through the "market portfolio" on the efficient frontier. This line is the **Capital Market Line**, and it plots expected return against **standard deviation (total risk)**. Remember the pairing: **CML uses standard deviation; SML uses beta.**

**Exam tip:** Know the one-sentence definition cold — the efficient frontier is "the set of portfolios offering the highest expected return for a given level of risk (standard deviation)." Questions often describe a portfolio with the same return as another but higher risk and ask you to identify it as *inefficient* / *suboptimal*.

**Common trap:** Wrong answers reverse the correlation extremes. Maximum diversification benefit comes from **−1** (negative correlation), *not* from +1; a correlation of +1 gives **zero** benefit. And diversification reduces **unsystematic** risk only — an answer claiming it eliminates market/systematic risk is wrong.

#### The Efficient Market Hypothesis (EMH)

The **Efficient Market Hypothesis**, associated with economist **Eugene Fama**, argues that security prices already reflect available information, adjusting rapidly and accurately as new information arrives. The practical punchline: if markets are efficient, it is very difficult (or impossible) to consistently "beat the market" through analysis or stock-picking — the intellectual case for **passive, low-cost index investing.** EMH comes in three forms, distinguished by *how much* information is assumed to be baked into prices. The higher the form, the more information is reflected, and the more types of analysis become useless.

Keep the two forms of analysis straight:
- **Technical analysis** studies past price and volume data, charts, and trends to predict future prices.
- **Fundamental analysis** studies a company's financials, management, industry, and the economy to estimate intrinsic value from *public* information.

| Form | Information already in the price | Which analysis becomes useless | What (if anything) could still give an edge |
|------|-------------------------------|-------------------------------|---------------------------------------------|
| **Weak** | All *past price and trading* data | **Technical analysis** | Fundamental analysis and inside information |
| **Semi-strong** | All *publicly available* information (past prices + financials, news, filings) | **Technical *and* fundamental analysis** | Only material non-public (inside) information |
| **Strong** | *All* information — public *and* private (including insider) | **Technical, fundamental, *and* inside information** | Nothing — no one can consistently beat the market |

Walking up the ladder:

- **Weak form.** Prices already reflect all historical price and volume data, so studying past charts and trends (technical analysis) cannot produce excess returns — past prices tell you nothing about future prices. However, digging into fundamentals or possessing inside information could still, in theory, beat the market.

- **Semi-strong form.** Prices reflect all *publicly available* information — not just past prices but earnings reports, news, analyst research, and regulatory filings — and adjust almost instantly when new public information is released. Therefore **both technical and fundamental analysis are useless**, because by the time you read the 10-K, the price already reflects it. Only someone acting on **material non-public (inside) information** could gain an edge. The semi-strong form is often considered the most widely accepted version.

- **Strong form.** Prices reflect *absolutely all* information, public and private — even corporate insiders trading on secret information cannot consistently earn excess returns, because prices already embed that information too. Under the strong form, no analysis of any kind beats the market. (This form is the hardest to defend in reality, since insider trading demonstrably *can* be profitable — which is precisely why it is illegal.)

**How to reason about EMH questions.** Read the form named in the question, then ask "what does this form say I *cannot* profit from?" Each higher form adds another tool to the useless pile: weak kills technical, semi-strong adds fundamental, strong adds inside information.

**Exam tip:** The classic question is a matching exercise: "Under which form of the EMH is *fundamental analysis* unable to produce excess returns?" Answer: **semi-strong (and strong)** — those forms assume all public information is already in the price. If the question asks which form makes *technical* analysis useless, the answer is **all three**, since even the weak form reflects past prices.

**Common trap:** Do not mix up which analysis each form defeats. A common wrong answer pairs the *weak* form with the defeat of *fundamental* analysis. The weak form defeats **technical** analysis only; you need the **semi-strong** form to defeat **fundamental** analysis. And only the **strong** form claims that even **inside information** provides no advantage.

**Tying it together.** These three theories form one coherent worldview the Series 65 expects you to recognize: MPT says diversify so you bear only systematic risk; CAPM prices that systematic risk through beta to tell you the return you deserve; and EMH argues that because prices already reflect information, the low-cost, well-diversified, passively managed portfolio is a rational default. An adviser who accepts EMH leans toward **index funds and strategic asset allocation**; one who rejects it believes skilled active management can generate positive **alpha**.

### D. Portfolio Management Strategies, Styles and Techniques

Once you understand a client's goals, risk tolerance, and time horizon, you have to actually build and run a portfolio. NASAA tests this on three levels, and it is worth keeping the vocabulary straight from the start:

- **Strategies** answer *how the overall mix is set and adjusted* (strategic vs. tactical asset allocation).
- **Styles** answer *what kind of manager or securities you use* (active/passive; growth/value/income/capital appreciation).
- **Techniques** are the specific *tools and tactics* applied inside the portfolio (diversification, sector rotation, dollar-cost averaging, options, leverage, volatility management, inverse strategies, high-frequency trading).

Most questions in this subsection are conceptual "which best describes…" or "which is most suitable for this client" items. A handful are calculations — dollar-cost averaging, option breakevens, and leverage math — so those are worked below.

#### Strategic vs. Tactical Asset Allocation

**Asset allocation** is the decision of how to split a portfolio among asset classes (stocks, bonds, cash, and sometimes alternatives/real estate). It is generally considered the single most important driver of a portfolio's long-run risk and return — more than individual security selection.

**Strategic asset allocation (SAA)** sets a *long-term target mix* based on the client's objectives, risk tolerance, and time horizon — for example, 60% equity / 35% fixed income / 5% cash. This target is the "policy portfolio." The manager then **rebalances** periodically back to those targets. Rebalancing is inherently a *sell-high/buy-low* discipline: if stocks rally to 68% of the portfolio, you sell some stock and buy bonds to return to 60/35/5. Strategic allocation is essentially a **long-term, relatively passive** approach.

**Tactical asset allocation (TAA)** allows *short-term, deliberate deviations* from the strategic targets to exploit perceived market opportunities or mispricings — a controlled form of market timing. If a manager believes equities are temporarily undervalued, TAA might move the mix to 70% equity for a while, then return to the 60% strategic target.
