Tactical allocation is **shorter-term and active**; it is layered on top of the strategic policy, not a replacement for it.

| Feature | Strategic | Tactical |
|---|---|---|
| Time horizon | Long-term ("policy" mix) | Short-term deviations |
| Nature | Passive; rebalance to targets | Active; market timing |
| Goal | Match client's long-run objectives | Add return by exploiting opportunities |
| Discipline | Rebalance back to target weights | Overweight/underweight, then revert |

**Exam tip:** Strategic = **set the long-term target and rebalance to it**. Tactical = **temporary tilts to time the market**. If the question stresses "long-term policy," it is strategic; if it stresses "capitalize on current conditions" or "short-term," it is tactical.

**Common trap:** Rebalancing is part of *strategic* allocation, not a form of market timing. Selling appreciated assets to return to target weights is discipline, not a tactical bet.

#### Portfolio Management Styles: Active vs. Passive

This choice is about *how a manager tries to add value*.

**Active management** seeks to *outperform* a benchmark (to generate "alpha") through security selection, sector bets, and/or timing. It relies on the manager's skill and research. Trade-offs: **higher expense ratios**, **higher portfolio turnover** (more trading costs), and greater **tax inefficiency** (more realized capital gains passed through to investors).

**Passive management (indexing)** seeks to *match* a benchmark, typically by holding an index (e.g., an S&P 500 index fund or ETF). Advantages: **low cost**, **low turnover**, and **tax efficiency**. The intellectual case for passive investing rests on the **Efficient Market Hypothesis (EMH)** — in its semi-strong and strong forms, prices already reflect available information, so consistently beating the market *after costs* is very difficult.

| Feature | Active | Passive |
|---|---|---|
| Objective | Beat the benchmark (seek alpha) | Match the benchmark |
| Cost / expense ratio | Higher | Lower |
| Turnover / trading | Higher | Lower |
| Tax efficiency | Lower (more realized gains) | Higher |
| Rationale | Markets are inefficient; skill pays | EMH; can't reliably beat market net of costs |

**Exam tip:** When a question asks which style has lower costs and better tax efficiency, the answer is **passive/indexing**. When it emphasizes trying to add value through research and selection, it is **active**.

#### Equity Styles: Growth, Value, Income, and Capital Appreciation

These describe *what kinds of securities* a manager emphasizes.

- **Growth style** — Buys companies with above-average earnings/revenue growth. These stocks usually have **high P/E and high price-to-book ratios**, **reinvest earnings** (so they pay little or no dividend), and are **more volatile (higher beta)**. Return comes mainly from **price appreciation**.
- **Value style** — Buys stocks that appear **underpriced relative to fundamentals**: **low P/E, low price-to-book, higher dividend yield**. These are often mature or temporarily out-of-favor companies. Value investing emphasizes a **"margin of safety"** and is somewhat **contrarian** (associated with Graham and Buffett).
- **Income style** — Emphasizes **current income** over growth: dividend-paying stocks, bonds, preferred stock, REITs. Suits investors who need cash flow, such as **retirees**.
- **Capital appreciation** — The objective is **growth in the principal value** of the assets over time, with little concern for current income. In practice it overlaps heavily with the growth style and is the *opposite* of an income objective.

| Style | Typical P/E | Dividend yield | Return source | Suits |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Growth / Capital appreciation | High | Low or none | Price appreciation | Younger, aggressive, long horizon |
| Value | Low | Higher | Undervaluation + dividends | Patient, contrarian |
| Income | Varies | High | Current income | Retirees, income-needers |

**Common trap:** Do not mix up the ratios. **Growth = high P/E, low/no dividend.** **Value = low P/E, higher dividend yield.** Exam questions love to describe a "low P/E, low price-to-book, out-of-favor" stock and ask for the style — that is **value**, not growth.

**Exam tip:** "Capital appreciation" and "income" are usually presented as *opposite objectives*. A client who needs cash flow now is an **income** investor; a client who wants the portfolio's value to grow and does not need income is a **capital appreciation/growth** investor.

#### Diversification

**Diversification** means spreading investments across many securities (and asset classes, sectors, and geographies) so that no single holding can sink the portfolio. Its purpose is to reduce risk without necessarily reducing expected return.

Total risk has two parts:

- **Unsystematic risk** (also called *diversifiable, nonsystematic,* or *company/industry-specific* risk) — this is what diversification **can eliminate**.
- **Systematic risk** (market risk, measured by **beta**) — this affects the whole market and **cannot be diversified away**.

Diversification works best when the assets have **low or negative correlation**. The correlation coefficient ranges from **-1.0 (perfectly negatively correlated)** to **+1.0 (perfectly positively correlated)**. Combining assets whose returns do not move together lowers the portfolio's overall standard deviation (volatility). The marginal benefit shrinks as you add names — most diversifiable risk is removed by holding a moderate number of well-chosen, low-correlation securities, after which only systematic risk remains. This is a core idea behind **Modern Portfolio Theory (MPT)**.

**Exam tip:** Diversification reduces **unsystematic** risk only; **systematic (market) risk remains** no matter how many stocks you hold. Buying 50 different technology stocks is *poor* diversification because they are highly correlated.

#### Sector Rotation

**Sector rotation** is a tactical technique of shifting money among industry sectors based on where the economy sits in the **business cycle**. It rests on the idea that different sectors lead at different stages.

- **Cyclical** stocks (consumer discretionary, technology, financials, industrials, materials, energy) rise and fall *with* the economy.
- **Defensive / non-cyclical** stocks (**consumer staples, utilities, healthcare**) hold up relatively well in downturns because demand for their products is stable.

| Business-cycle phase | Sectors that tend to lead |
|---|---|
| Early expansion / recovery | Consumer discretionary, technology, financials |
| Late expansion / peak | Energy, materials, industrials |
| Contraction / recession | Defensive: consumer staples, utilities, healthcare |

**Exam tip:** In a slowing economy or recession, a sector-rotation manager moves toward **defensive sectors (utilities, staples, healthcare)**. Sector rotation is an **active/tactical** technique — recognize it as a form of market timing across industries.

#### Dollar-Cost Averaging (DCA)

**Dollar-cost averaging** means investing a **fixed dollar amount at regular intervals** (e.g., $300 every month) regardless of price. Because the dollar amount is fixed, you automatically buy **more shares when prices are low and fewer shares when prices are high**. The mathematical result is that your **average cost per share is lower than the average of the prices** you paid.

Two formulas are tested:

- Average price per share = (sum of the purchase prices) / (number of purchase periods)
- Average cost per share = (total dollars invested) / (total shares purchased)

**Worked example:** An investor puts in **$300 per month** for four months at these prices:

| Month | Price | Shares bought ($300 / price) |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | $10 | 30 |
| 2 | $15 | 20 |
| 3 | $20 | 15 |
| 4 | $12 | 25 |

- Total invested = 4 × $300 = **$1,200**
- Total shares = 30 + 20 + 15 + 25 = **90 shares**
- **Average cost per share** = $1,200 / 90 = **$13.33**
- **Average price per share** = ($10 + $15 + $20 + $12) / 4 = $57 / 4 = **$14.25**

The average **cost** ($13.33) is lower than the average **price** ($14.25) — the signature result of DCA.

**Exam tip:** If a question asks for **average cost**, divide **total dollars by total shares**. If it asks for **average price**, take the **simple mean of the prices**. Average cost is always **less than** average price when prices fluctuate (the two are equal only if the price never changes).

**Common trap:** DCA does **not** guarantee a profit and does **not** protect against loss in a steadily declining market. Its discipline also depends on the investor **continuing to buy through down periods** — which is exactly when investors are tempted to stop.

#### Buying and Selling Options

Options let a manager adjust risk and return in ways stock alone cannot. Remember that **one listed option contract covers 100 shares**, and the **premium** is the price of the option. The most-tested positions are:

- **Protective put (long stock + long put)** — Buying a put on stock you own acts like **insurance**: the put sets a floor selling price. Cost is the premium paid.
- **Covered call (long stock + short call)** — Writing a call against stock you own **generates income** (the premium) and provides a small downside cushion, but **caps your upside** because the stock can be called away at the strike.
- **Collar (long stock + long put + short call)** — Combine the two: the sold call's premium helps pay for the protective put, limiting **both** downside and upside — a low-cost hedge.
- **Buying calls** (bullish, leveraged upside) and **buying puts** (bearish or hedging) are the basic long positions.

**Worked example — covered call:** You own stock bought at **$50** and write one call with a **$55 strike** for a **$3 premium**.
- Breakeven = purchase price − premium = $50 − $3 = **$47**
- Maximum gain = (strike − purchase price) + premium = ($55 − $50) + $3 = **$8 per share ($800 per contract)**, realized if the stock is called away at $55.
- The premium cushions losses down to $47, but you give up any gain above $55.

**Worked example — protective put:** You own stock bought at **$50** and buy a put with a **$45 strike** for a **$2 premium**.
- Breakeven = purchase price + premium = $50 + $2 = **$52** (the stock must recover the cost of the insurance)
- Maximum loss = (purchase price − strike) + premium = ($50 − $45) + $2 = **$7 per share**, no matter how far the stock falls.

**Exam tip:** Match the goal to the strategy: **income → covered call**; **downside protection/insurance on a long position → protective put**; **cheap two-sided hedge → collar**.

#### Leveraging

**Leverage** means using **borrowed money** (or leveraged products) to increase market exposure beyond the cash invested. The classic vehicle is a **margin account**; under **Regulation T the initial margin requirement is 50%**, so an investor can buy up to twice the value of the cash deposited. Leverage **magnifies both gains and losses**, adds **interest cost**, and creates **margin-call risk**.

**Worked example:** An investor has **$10,000** in cash and buys **$20,000** of stock using 50% margin (borrowing $10,000). Ignore interest for simplicity.
- If the stock **rises 10%** to $22,000: repay the $10,000 loan → **$12,000 equity**. Gain of $2,000 on $10,000 invested = **+20%** (double the 10% unleveraged move).
- If the stock **falls 10%** to $18,000: repay the $10,000 loan → **$8,000 equity**. Loss of $2,000 = **−20%**.

So 2-to-1 leverage roughly **doubles the percentage return in both directions**. **Leveraged ETFs (2x, 3x)** achieve a similar effect without a margin account, but — like inverse funds below — they reset daily and are meant for short holding periods.

**Exam tip:** The key phrase is that leverage **amplifies returns and losses** and is therefore only suitable for investors who can tolerate higher risk. A margin call occurs when equity falls below the maintenance requirement.

#### Volatility Management

**Volatility management** refers to strategies aimed at **controlling the ups and downs (standard deviation) of portfolio returns** to produce smoother results and smaller drawdowns. Common tools include:

- **Diversification** and adding **low-correlation** or uncorrelated assets.
- **Low-volatility / minimum-variance** equity strategies (favoring stocks with historically smaller swings).
- **Options overlays** — protective puts and collars to cap downside.
- **Managed-volatility (target-volatility) funds** that **reduce equity exposure when market volatility rises and increase it when volatility falls**.
- Monitoring the **VIX** (the "fear index"), which measures expected S&P 500 volatility.

The trade-off is that damping volatility usually means **giving up some upside** in strong bull markets.

**Exam tip:** Volatility management targets **risk (standard deviation)**, not maximizing return. It suits **risk-averse clients** who value stability and smaller losses over maximum growth.

#### Inverse Strategies

**Inverse strategies** are designed to **profit when the underlying falls in value**. The main forms are **short selling**, **buying puts**, and **inverse ETFs** (−1x, −2x, −3x) that are built to deliver the *opposite* of an index's return.

A critical, frequently tested feature: **leveraged and inverse ETFs reset daily**, so over multiple days their results **do not simply mirror the index** — compounding and path-dependency cause **tracking error ("decay")**. They are intended for **short-term (often single-day) use**, not buy-and-hold.

**Worked example — inverse ETF decay:** An index starts at **100**; a −1x inverse ETF starts at **$100**.
- Day 1: index rises **+10%** to 110. The inverse ETF falls **10%** → $100 × 0.90 = **$90**.
- Day 2: index falls back from 110 to 100, a change of **−9.09%**. The inverse ETF rises **+9.09%** → $90 × 1.0909 = **$98.18**.

The **index is flat over the two days (back to 100)**, yet the inverse ETF is at **$98.18, down about 1.82%**. You might have expected it to be unchanged too — the gap is daily-reset decay.

**Exam tip:** Leveraged/inverse ETFs are **short-term trading tools** and are generally **unsuitable for long-term retail investors** — a favorite suitability/regulatory point. Because of daily reset, their long-horizon return can differ sharply from the stated multiple of the index's return.

#### High-Frequency Trading (HFT)

**High-frequency trading** is a form of **algorithmic trading** in which powerful computers execute enormous numbers of orders in **fractions of a second**, profiting from tiny, fleeting price discrepancies. HFT firms often use **co-location** (placing servers physically near the exchange to shave microseconds) and can act as **liquidity providers**. It is also **controversial**, associated with concerns about market fairness, front-running, and events like "flash crashes."

On the Series 65, HFT appears as a **definitional/conceptual** item, not something an individual adviser or retail client would implement. Recognize it as **institutional, ultra-fast, computer-driven trading**.

**Exam tip:** If a choice describes **millisecond, algorithm-driven institutional trading using co-located servers**, that is **high-frequency trading** — distinct from the longer-horizon strategies (strategic/tactical allocation, DCA, buy-and-hold) that individual advisers actually use for clients.

### E. Tax Considerations

Taxes are one of the few "certain" drags on an investor's return, and a suitable recommendation is always measured on an **after-tax** basis. The Series 65 does not turn you into a CPA, but it expects you to understand how the major tax rules affect a client's investments, retirement income, and estate — and to spot the traps advisers walk clients into. This subsection builds from the individual income tax, up through entity taxation, and finishes with the transfer (estate and gift) taxes.

**Exam tip:** The exam almost never asks you to compute an exact tax bill from a full bracket schedule. It asks you to *classify* items (ordinary vs. capital, qualified vs. non-qualified, marginal vs. effective) and to know the rules and thresholds. Learn the concepts and the commonly tested numbers, not a tax return.

#### Individual Income Tax: Ordinary Income, Marginal Bracket, and Effective Rate

The U.S. individual income tax is **progressive**: income is sliced into brackets, and each additional slice is taxed at a higher rate. Two rate concepts are constantly confused, and the exam loves the distinction.

- **Marginal tax bracket (marginal rate):** the rate applied to the client's *next* dollar of income — the top bracket their income reaches.
- **Effective (average) tax rate:** total tax paid divided by total taxable income.

Formula (plain text):

- Effective Tax Rate = Total Tax Paid / Taxable Income

Worked example (using simplified illustrative single-filer brackets: 10% on the first $11,000, 12% on $11,000–$44,000, 22% on $44,000–$95,000):

A client has $60,000 of taxable income.
- 10% × $11,000 = $1,100
- 12% × ($44,000 − $11,000) = 12% × $33,000 = $3,960
- 22% × ($60,000 − $44,000) = 22% × $16,000 = $3,520
- Total tax = $1,100 + $3,960 + $3,520 = **$8,580**

This client's **marginal rate is 22%** (the last dollar is taxed there), but the **effective rate is $8,580 / $60,000 = 14.3%**.

**Exam tip:** When a question asks "what rate should you use to evaluate whether a municipal bond beats a corporate bond?" the answer is the client's **marginal** rate, because the muni's tax-free advantage applies to the *next* dollars of income the client would otherwise be taxed on.

**Common trap:** Choosing the effective rate when the question is about the value of a deduction or a tax-free investment. The benefit of a deduction or exclusion is always valued at the marginal rate.

#### Capital Gains and Losses: Short-Term vs. Long-Term

A **capital gain (or loss)** arises when a capital asset (stock, bond, fund, real estate) is *sold*. It is a **realized** event — unrealized appreciation ("paper gains") is not taxed until sale.

The single most tested distinction is the **holding period**:

| Holding period | Classification | Taxed at |
|---|---|---|
| **One year or less** | Short-term | Ordinary income rates (up to 37%) |
| **More than one year** ("a year and a day") | Long-term | Preferential rates: 0%, 15%, or 20% |

Long-term rates are deliberately lower to reward longer holding. The applicable long-term rate (0/15/20%) depends on the taxpayer's income; most clients land at 15%.

**Netting rules.** Gains and losses are netted at year-end: short-term against short-term, long-term against long-term, then the two categories against each other. Key limits the exam expects you to know:

- Net capital losses can offset ordinary income only up to **$3,000 per year**.
- Any excess loss is **carried forward indefinitely** to future years (never lost, never carried back for individuals).

**Wash sale rule.** If a client sells a security at a loss and buys the *same or a substantially identical* security within **30 days before or after** the sale (a 61-day window), the loss is **disallowed**. The disallowed loss is added to the basis of the replacement shares.

**Common trap:** Buying the identical security 25 days after harvesting a loss triggers the wash sale — it applies to purchases on *either side* of the sale, not just after.

**Exam tip:** Long-term status requires **more than** 12 months. Exactly 12 months is still short-term. "A year and a day" is the memory hook.

#### Tax Basis (Cost Basis)

**Basis** is the taxpayer's investment in an asset for tax purposes — the amount that is *not* taxed again on sale. Gain or loss equals sale proceeds minus adjusted basis.

- Capital Gain/Loss = Net Sale Proceeds − Adjusted Cost Basis

Basis includes the purchase price **plus commissions/acquisition costs**, and it is **increased by reinvested dividends and capital gains distributions** (because those were already taxed when reinvested).

Worked example: A client buys 100 shares at $40 ($4,000) and pays a $50 commission → initial basis $4,050. Over the years she reinvests $600 of dividends (already taxed). Adjusted basis = $4,050 + $600 = **$4,650**. She later sells all shares for $7,000 (net of commission). Capital gain = $7,000 − $4,650 = **$2,350**.

**Common trap:** Forgetting to add reinvested dividends to basis. If the client is taxed on the dividend when reinvested and again treats it as $0 basis at sale, the gain is double-taxed. Reinvested distributions raise basis.

**Two special basis rules the exam tests:**

- **Inherited property — step-up (or step-down) in basis:** Assets passed at death receive a basis equal to **fair market value on the date of death**. Years of appreciation escape income tax entirely. Example: father bought stock at $10/share; at his death it is worth $90; the heir's basis is $90. If the heir sells at $92, the taxable gain is only $2.
- **Gifted property — carryover basis:** A gift made during life generally carries over the **donor's original cost basis** to the recipient (for computing gains). There is no step-up for lifetime gifts.

**Exam tip:** "Should the client gift the highly appreciated stock now or leave it in the estate?" Gifting carries over the low basis (built-in gain follows the asset); holding until death gives the heir a stepped-up basis that wipes out the gain. This is a classic estate-planning trade-off question.

#### Qualified Dividends

Dividends are ordinary income by default, but **qualified dividends** are taxed at the lower **long-term capital gains rates (0/15/20%)**. To qualify:

- The dividend is paid by a U.S. corporation or a qualified foreign corporation, **and**
- The investor meets a **holding period**: the stock must be held **more than 60 days during the 121-day period beginning 60 days before the ex-dividend date**.

**Non-qualified (ordinary) dividends** include REIT dividends (mostly), money-market "dividends" (really interest), and dividends on shares held too briefly.

**Common trap:** Assuming all dividends get the low rate. REIT and most MLP distributions do **not** qualify. Also, a day-trader who buys a stock just to capture the dividend and sells immediately fails the holding period — the dividend is non-qualified.

#### Alternative Minimum Tax (AMT)

The **AMT** is a parallel tax system designed to ensure that higher-income taxpayers who use many deductions and preference items still pay a minimum amount. The taxpayer computes tax two ways — regular and AMT — and **pays the higher** of the two.

AMT is calculated by starting with regular taxable income, **adding back certain "preference items"** to reach Alternative Minimum Taxable Income (AMTI), subtracting the AMT exemption, and applying the AMT rate.

Commonly tested AMT preference/add-back items:

- **Interest on private-activity (private-purpose) municipal bonds** — otherwise tax-free interest becomes taxable for AMT.
- The **bargain element on incentive stock options (ISOs)** exercised and held.
- **Accelerated depreciation**, **intangible drilling costs**, and **percentage depletion** (common in oil-and-gas and real estate limited partnerships).
- Certain deductions such as **state and local taxes**.

**Exam tip:** The bond distinction is a favorite. Interest on a **public-purpose** general obligation or standard revenue muni is *not* an AMT item; interest on a **private-activity bond** *is* an AMT preference item. A client already subject to AMT should be steered away from private-activity municipals.

**Common trap:** Thinking municipal bond interest is always completely tax-free. Public-purpose muni interest is free of regular federal tax, but private-activity muni interest can be pulled back into AMT.

#### Retirement Plan Distributions and Required Minimum Distributions (RMDs)

**Taxation of distributions.** Contributions and distributions are taxed depending on whether the account is traditional (pre-tax) or Roth (after-tax):

- **Traditional IRA / 401(k):** contributions may be deductible (pre-tax); the account grows tax-deferred; **distributions are taxed as ordinary income** (never capital gains, even if the growth came from stocks).
- **Roth IRA / Roth 401(k):** contributions are after-tax (no deduction); **qualified distributions are entirely tax-free** (account open 5 years *and* age 59½, death, or disability).
- **Premature distributions** (generally before age **59½**) are subject to a **10% penalty** on top of ordinary income tax, unless an exception applies (death, disability, first-home up to $10,000, qualified education, certain medical, substantially equal periodic payments, etc.).

**Common trap:** Answering "capital gains" for the tax on a traditional IRA withdrawal. All of it — original contributions deducted and all growth — comes out as **ordinary income**.

**Required Minimum Distributions (RMDs).** The government eventually wants its tax, so traditional retirement accounts must begin paying out.

- RMDs must begin at age **73** (under the SECURE Act 2.0; scheduled to rise to 75 in 2033).
- The **first** RMD may be delayed until **April 1 of the year *after*** the year the owner turns 73 (the "required beginning date"); every RMD after that is due by December 31.
- **Roth IRAs have no RMDs during the owner's lifetime.** (SECURE 2.0 also eliminated lifetime RMDs from Roth 401(k)s beginning 2024.)
- **Penalty for a missed RMD:** an excise tax of **25%** of the shortfall (reduced from the old 50%), dropping to **10%** if corrected promptly within the correction window.

RMD amount = Prior year-end account balance / IRS life-expectancy factor. Example: $500,000 balance and a life-expectancy factor of 25 → RMD = $500,000 / 25 = **$20,000** that must be withdrawn and taxed as ordinary income.

**Exam tip:** Two dates to memorize — **59½** (penalty-free access begins) and **73** (RMDs begin). Delaying the *first* RMD to April 1 forces *two* taxable distributions into one year (that year's and the next), possibly pushing the client into a higher bracket — a suitability point.

#### Government-Benefit Implications: IRMAA

Retirement income does not exist in a vacuum; higher income can raise a client's **Medicare** costs. The **IRMAA (Income-Related Monthly Adjustment Amount)** is a surcharge added to **Medicare Part B and Part D** premiums for higher-income beneficiaries.

- IRMAA is based on **Modified Adjusted Gross Income (MAGI) from two years prior** (there is a lookback).
- Because it is triggered by income *thresholds* (a "cliff"), a single extra dollar of income can bump a client into a higher IRMAA tier and raise premiums for the whole year.

**Exam tip:** Actions that spike a client's MAGI — a large Roth conversion, realizing big capital gains, or taking an oversized IRA distribution — can raise Medicare premiums two years later. A tax-aware adviser manages the *timing* of income around these thresholds. Roth distributions are especially valuable here because qualified Roth withdrawals do **not** count toward MAGI for IRMAA.

**Related concept — taxation of Social Security benefits:** up to **85%** of Social Security benefits can become taxable once "combined income" (AGI + nontaxable interest + one-half of benefits) exceeds set thresholds — another reason tax-free Roth income is prized in retirement.

#### Corporate Taxation: C Corporation vs. S Corporation

How a business entity is taxed drives which securities and strategies suit its owners.

| Feature | C Corporation | S Corporation |
|---|---|---|
| Taxation | **Double taxation** — taxed at corporate level, then again on dividends to shareholders | **Pass-through** — no entity-level tax; income/loss flows to shareholders |
| Corporate tax rate | Flat **21%** (post-TCJA) | None at federal level |
| Shareholder limit | Unlimited | **100 or fewer** |
| Classes of stock | Multiple allowed | **One class** of stock only |
| Owner eligibility | Anyone | U.S. citizens/residents; generally no corporate or partnership owners |
| Reports to owners | Form 1099-DIV for dividends | **Schedule K-1** |

**Double taxation** is the defining C-corp trait: the corporation pays 21% on its profits, and shareholders pay tax again when those profits are distributed as dividends.

**Dividends-received deduction (DRD):** to soften the layering of taxation when one corporation owns stock in another, a corporate shareholder can deduct **50%** of dividends received (65% if it owns 20% or more of the payer). This is why corporations sometimes prefer dividend-paying stock over bonds (interest is fully taxable to the corporation).

**Common trap:** Applying the DRD to an individual investor. The dividends-received deduction is available only to **corporate** shareholders, not individuals.

#### Trusts

A **trust** is a separate taxable entity that holds assets for beneficiaries. For the exam:

- **Simple trust:** must distribute **all** income annually and makes no principal distributions; the *beneficiaries* pay tax on the income.
- **Complex trust:** may **accumulate** income and distribute principal; the trust pays tax on what it retains.
- **Grantor trust:** the person who created it retains control, so **income is taxed to the grantor**, not the trust.

**Key exam point — compressed brackets:** Trusts (and estates) reach the **top marginal tax rate (37%) at a very low income level — around $15,000** — far faster than an individual, who does not hit the top bracket until income is in the hundreds of thousands. This creates a planning incentive to **distribute income to beneficiaries**, who are usually in lower brackets, rather than let it accumulate inside the trust.

#### Pass-Through Entities: REITs, MLPs, and LLCs

Pass-through (or "flow-through") entities avoid entity-level tax; income flows to the owners, who are taxed once.

**REIT (Real Estate Investment Trust):**
- Must distribute at least **90%** of its taxable income to shareholders to keep its pass-through status (and generally hold at least 75% of assets in real estate / derive at least 75% of income from real estate).
- Distributions are **largely taxed as ordinary income to the shareholder — not qualified dividends** (though a portion may be return of capital or capital gain, and the ordinary-income portion of REIT dividends can qualify for the QBI/199A deduction).

**MLP (Master Limited Partnership):**
- A publicly traded **partnership** — a pass-through that reports on a **Schedule K-1**, not a 1099.
- Distributions often exceed taxable income and are treated partly as **return of capital**, which **reduces the investor's basis** (deferring, not eliminating, tax until sale).
- **Common trap:** Holding an MLP inside an IRA can generate **Unrelated Business Taxable Income (UBTI)**, which is taxable even inside the tax-deferred account — MLPs are generally poor IRA holdings.

**LLC (Limited Liability Company):**
- Provides **limited liability** like a corporation but **flexible tax treatment**. By default it is a pass-through (single-member = disregarded entity; multi-member = partnership), but it may **elect** to be taxed as a C or S corporation.

**Exam tip:** The recurring theme is *how the income is reported and taxed to the owner* — **K-1 (partnership/MLP/S corp)** vs. **1099**, and **ordinary income (REIT)** vs. **qualified dividend (typical C-corp stock)**.

#### Wealth Transfer: Estate and Gift Tax Fundamentals

The federal transfer taxes catch large transfers of wealth — during life (gift tax) and at death (estate tax). They share a **unified** framework.

**The unified credit and lifetime exemption.** Gift tax and estate tax are integrated under one **unified transfer tax** with a single **unified credit**. This credit shelters a large **basic exclusion amount (lifetime exemption)** from tax — indexed for inflation (**$15 million per individual in 2026** under the One Big Beautiful Bill Act, up from $13.99 million in 2025, and indexed for inflation thereafter). Transfers above the exemption are taxed at rates up to **40%**. Because the exemption is so high, most clients owe no federal estate or gift tax — but the *rules* are still tested.

**Annual gift tax exclusion (heavily tested).** Separate from the lifetime exemption, each donor may give up to the **annual exclusion amount per recipient, per year, with no gift tax and no filing** — **$19,000 per donee in 2026** (unchanged from 2025; indexed periodically). There is no limit on the *number* of recipients.

Worked example: A grandmother gives $19,000 each to her four grandchildren in one year = $76,000 transferred out of her estate with **no** gift tax and no use of her lifetime exemption. Do it every year and the amounts compound out of the taxable estate.

**Gift splitting.** A married couple can elect to treat a gift as made half by each spouse, effectively **doubling** the annual exclusion to **$38,000 per recipient** (2026).

**Annual vs. lifetime — how they interact:** Only the amount of a gift **above** the annual exclusion counts against the donor's lifetime exemption. Example: a $119,000 gift to one child in 2026 uses $19,000 of annual exclusion and $100,000 of lifetime exemption; no tax is actually due until the lifetime exemption is exhausted.

**Other tested essentials:**

- The **donor**, not the recipient, is liable for gift tax. Gifts received are **not** taxable income to the donee.
- **Unlimited marital deduction:** transfers between U.S.-citizen spouses (during life or at death) are entirely tax-free.
- **Unlimited exclusions** also apply to **direct payments of tuition and medical bills** made to the institution/provider, and to qualified charitable gifts.
- **Basis reminder:** gifts carry **carryover basis**; bequests at death get a **stepped-up basis** (tie back to the Tax Basis section).

**Portability.** If one spouse dies without using the full exemption, the **unused amount (the DSUE — Deceased Spousal Unused Exclusion) can be transferred to the surviving spouse**, who may add it to their own exemption. Portability is **not automatic** — the executor must **elect it by filing a federal estate tax return (Form 706)**, even if no tax is owed.

**Exam tip:** Remember the three tiers — **(1) annual exclusion** (per donee, per year), **(2) lifetime exemption/unified credit** (cumulative, shared by gift and estate tax), and **(3) unlimited marital deduction and portability** (spouse-to-spouse). The **annual gift tax exclusion** is the single most likely of these to appear on your exam.

**Common trap:** Confusing the annual exclusion with the lifetime exemption. The **annual exclusion** ($19,000 in 2026) resets every year and is per recipient; the **lifetime exemption** ($15 million in 2026) is a one-time cumulative shelter that gift and estate tax both draw on.

### F. Retirement Plans

Retirement plans are one of the most heavily tested topics in Part 3 because a Series 65 candidate is expected to recommend suitable savings vehicles to clients. The single most important organizing idea is the split between **qualified** and **nonqualified** plans and how each is taxed. Learn that framework first, then fit every specific plan (IRAs, 401(k)s, 403(b)s, etc.) into it.

> **Note on numbers:** Contribution limits are indexed for inflation and change most years. The figures below reflect widely tested current values. The exam rewards you for knowing the *concepts and relationships* (which plan lets you defer more, who bears investment risk, how distributions are taxed) far more than the exact dollar figure for any given tax year. Know the round numbers, but do not panic over a $500 change.

#### The Big Picture: Qualified vs. Nonqualified Plans

A **qualified plan** meets the requirements of the Internal Revenue Code and ERISA (the Employee Retirement Income Security Act of 1974) and therefore receives favorable tax treatment. Its defining features:

- **Contributions are made with pre-tax dollars** (they are tax-deductible or made by salary reduction before tax).
- **Earnings grow tax-deferred.**
- **The entire distribution is taxed as ordinary income** at withdrawal — because none of the money was ever taxed.
- The plan **must be nondiscriminatory** (it cannot favor highly compensated employees/owners), must follow vesting and coverage rules, and generally requires IRS approval and a trust.

A **nonqualified plan** does **not** meet those Code/ERISA requirements. Its defining features:

- **Contributions are made with after-tax dollars** (not deductible).
- Earnings still grow **tax-deferred.**
- At distribution, **only the earnings (growth) are taxed** as ordinary income; the participant's own after-tax contributions (the cost basis) come back tax-free because they were already taxed.
- The plan **may discriminate** — this is exactly why employers use them to reward select executives.

**Exam tip:** The classic trap question asks how a distribution is taxed. In a **qualified** plan the *whole* payment is taxable ordinary income. In a **nonqualified** plan only the *earnings* are taxable; the basis is returned tax-free. Memorize that one sentence and you will answer several questions correctly.

#### Traditional IRAs

An **Individual Retirement Arrangement (IRA)** is a personal, self-directed retirement account — it is *not* an employer plan and is *not* covered by ERISA.

**Who can contribute and how much.** You must have **earned income** (compensation from working — wages, salary, self-employment income, tips, and taxable alimony under pre-2019 divorce decrees). Investment income, Social Security, pensions, and rental income do **not** count. The annual contribution limit is the lesser of the indexed dollar cap or 100% of earned income:

- Contribution limit: **$7,000** per year.
- **Catch-up contribution** for those **age 50 and older: an extra $1,000**, for **$8,000** total.
- Contributions may be made up until the **tax-filing deadline (April 15)** of the following year.
- Thanks to the SECURE Act, there is **no upper age limit** on making contributions as long as you have earned income.

**Spousal IRA.** A nonworking spouse may fund an IRA based on the working spouse's income, provided the couple files a **joint return** and has enough combined earned income to cover both contributions.

**Deductibility (the part people confuse).** The *contribution* is always allowed if you have earned income, but the *deduction* depends on whether you (or your spouse) are an **active participant** in an employer-sponsored plan and on your **modified adjusted gross income (MAGI)**:

- If **neither** spouse is covered by an employer plan, the contribution is **fully deductible regardless of income.**
- If you **are** an active participant, the deduction **phases out** over an income range (representative single range roughly $79,000–$89,000 MAGI; married filing jointly roughly $126,000–$146,000). Above the range, you get **no deduction** but may still make a **nondeductible contribution.**

**Taxation of a Traditional IRA.**

- Deductible contributions and all earnings are taxed as **ordinary income** when withdrawn (this is "qualified-money" treatment even though an IRA is technically not an ERISA "qualified plan").
- **Early withdrawal penalty:** distributions before age **59½** face a **10% penalty** on top of ordinary income tax, unless an exception applies (death, disability, first-time home purchase up to $10,000, qualified higher-education expenses, certain medical expenses, health insurance while unemployed, or a series of substantially equal periodic payments).
- **Required Minimum Distributions (RMDs):** the owner must begin taking RMDs at age **73** (rising to 75 in 2033 under SECURE 2.0). Failure to take an RMD triggers an excise tax of **25%** of the shortfall (reduced to 10% if corrected promptly).

**Worked example — early withdrawal cost.** A 50-year-old in the 24% marginal bracket withdraws **$10,000** of fully deductible IRA money.

- 10% penalty: 0.10 × $10,000 = **$1,000**
- Ordinary income tax: 0.24 × $10,000 = **$2,400**
- Total cost: $1,000 + $2,400 = **$3,400**; net received = **$6,600**

**Worked example — RMD.** RMD = Prior Year-End Account Balance ÷ Distribution Period (from the IRS Uniform Lifetime Table). At age 73 the table factor is about **26.5**. With a **$500,000** balance:

- RMD = $500,000 ÷ 26.5 = **$18,868** (approximately)

#### Roth IRAs

A **Roth IRA** flips the tax timing of a Traditional IRA: you pay tax now, not later.

- **Contributions are always after-tax** (never deductible).
- The annual limit is the **same $7,000 / $8,000** cap, and it is a **combined** limit — total contributions to Traditional and Roth IRAs together cannot exceed it.
- **Eligibility phases out at higher incomes** (representative single range roughly $150,000–$165,000 MAGI; married filing jointly roughly $236,000–$246,000). High earners can be barred from contributing directly to a Roth. (Contrast: there is no income limit to *contribute* to a Traditional IRA, only to *deduct* it.)
- **No age limit** to contribute; earned income still required.

**Qualified (tax-free) distributions.** A Roth distribution is completely tax-free and penalty-free if **both** tests are met:

1. The account has been open for at least **5 years** (the 5-year rule), **and**
2. The owner is at least **59½**, **or** the distribution is due to **death**, **disability**, or a **first-time home purchase** ($10,000 lifetime cap).

**Other key Roth features.**

- **Contributions (basis) can be withdrawn at any time, tax- and penalty-free**, because that money was already taxed. Only *earnings* pulled out early are subject to tax and the 10% penalty.
- **No RMDs during the owner's lifetime** — a major planning advantage over Traditional IRAs.

**Common trap:** Students confuse the two income tests. Roth IRAs have an income limit on the *ability to contribute at all*. Traditional IRAs have no contribution income limit — the income test only affects *deductibility*. Read the question stem carefully.

#### Traditional vs. Roth IRA at a Glance

| Feature | Traditional IRA | Roth IRA |
|---|---|---|
| Contribution deductible? | Sometimes (depends on active participation and MAGI) | Never (always after-tax) |
| Income limit to contribute? | No | Yes (phase-out at higher MAGI) |
| Growth | Tax-deferred | Tax-deferred |
| Qualified distributions | Taxed as ordinary income | **Tax-free** (5-year rule + qualifying event) |
| 10% early-withdrawal penalty? | Yes, before 59½ | On earnings only; basis always accessible |
| Lifetime RMDs? | Yes, at age 73 | **None** |
| Annual limit | $7,000 (+$1,000 age 50+) | Same $7,000 (+$1,000) cap, **combined** across both types |

#### Solo 401(k): Traditional and Roth

A **Solo 401(k)** (also called an Individual or One-Participant 401(k)) is designed for a **self-employed person with no employees** other than a spouse. Its power comes from letting the owner contribute in **two capacities at once**:

1. **As the employee** — an elective salary deferral up to the standard 401(k) deferral limit of **$23,500** (plus the **$7,500** catch-up if age 50+). This portion can be made as **traditional (pre-tax)** or **Roth (after-tax).**
2. **As the employer** — a profit-sharing contribution of up to **25% of compensation** (pre-tax).

The combined employee + employer total cannot exceed the overall defined-contribution limit of **$70,000** (plus any catch-up).

**Worked example.** A 45-year-old sole proprietor with $120,000 of W-2-equivalent compensation:

- Employee deferral: **$23,500**
- Employer profit-sharing: 25% × $120,000 = **$30,000**
- Total: $23,500 + $30,000 = **$53,500** (within the $70,000 cap)

**Exam tip:** The Roth Solo 401(k) applies only to the **employee deferral** piece; the employer profit-sharing portion is always pre-tax. Also note the Solo 401(k) lets a self-employed saver contribute far more than a SEP or SIMPLE at modest income levels because of the flat employee deferral on top of the percentage-based employer piece.

#### Qualified Employer Plans: Defined Benefit vs. Defined Contribution

All employer-sponsored qualified plans fall into two structural families. Knowing **who bears the investment risk** is the key distinction.

**Defined Benefit (DB) plan — a traditional pension.**

- The plan **promises a specific benefit at retirement**, usually a formula based on salary and years of service (e.g., "60% of final average salary").
- **The employer bears the investment risk**; required contributions are calculated by an **actuary** to fund the promised benefit.
- Favors older, highly paid employees who have less time to accumulate.

**Defined Contribution (DC) plan.**

- The **contribution** is defined, but the **future benefit is not guaranteed** — it depends on investment performance.
- **The employee bears the investment risk.**
- Examples: **401(k)**, profit-sharing, money-purchase, and (in the broader sense) 403(b) plans.
- Individual accounts; each participant's balance reflects contributions plus/minus investment results.

| | Defined Benefit | Defined Contribution |
|---|---|---|
| What's promised | A specific retirement benefit | A specific contribution amount |
| Who bears investment risk | **Employer** | **Employee** |
| Funding | Actuarially determined | Contributions to individual accounts |
| Typical example | Traditional pension | 401(k), profit-sharing |

**Vesting.** Employees are always **100% immediately vested in their own contributions.** **Employer** contributions may vest over time under a **cliff** (full vesting after a set number of years) or **graded** (gradual) schedule.

#### Company 401(k)

A **401(k)** is the dominant defined-contribution plan. Employees elect to defer part of their salary (a **cash-or-deferred arrangement**):

- **Elective deferral limit: $23,500**, plus a **$7,500** catch-up at age 50+, and (under SECURE 2.0) an enhanced catch-up of **$11,250** for ages 60–63.
- Employers frequently **match** contributions (e.g., 50% of the first 6% deferred) — commonly tested as "free money" the client should capture.
- A **Roth 401(k)** option allows after-tax deferrals with tax-free qualified withdrawals; note that unlike a Roth IRA, a Roth 401(k) has **no income limit** and (under SECURE 2.0) **no lifetime RMDs** starting in 2024.

#### 403(b) Plans (Tax-Sheltered Annuities / TSAs)

A **403(b)** is the nonprofit-sector cousin of the 401(k), available to employees of **public schools, tax-exempt 501(c)(3) organizations, and certain church organizations.**

- Salary-reduction deferrals with the **same $23,500 limit** (plus catch-ups).
- **Investment choices are restricted to annuities and mutual funds** (the latter held through custodial accounts). A 403(b) **cannot invest directly in individual stocks or bonds.**

**Common trap:** If a question describes a public school teacher who wants to hold **individual stocks** inside a 403(b), the answer is that it is **not permitted** — 403(b) investments are limited to annuities and mutual funds.

#### 457 Plans

A **457(b)** is a deferred-compensation plan for **state and local government employees** and certain tax-exempt organizations.

- Same **$23,500** elective deferral limit; a special "final-3-years" catch-up can allow larger contributions as retirement nears.
- **Key distinction:** a governmental 457(b) has **no 10% early-withdrawal penalty** on distributions after **separation from service**, even before age 59½. This is the most commonly tested 457 fact.
- Technically, 457 plans are **nonqualified deferred-compensation** arrangements, but the exam groups them with employer retirement plans; they still receive salary-deferral, tax-deferred treatment.

#### SIMPLE IRA

The **Savings Incentive Match Plan for Employees (SIMPLE) IRA** is for **small employers with 100 or fewer employees** that maintain no other plan.

- **Employee deferral limit: $16,500**, plus a **$3,500** catch-up at age 50+.
- The employer **must** contribute, choosing either:
  - a **dollar-for-dollar match up to 3%** of the employee's compensation, **or**
  - a **2% nonelective** contribution for all eligible employees (whether or not they defer).
- All contributions are **immediately 100% vested.**
- **Special penalty:** withdrawals during the **first 2 years** of participation incur a **25% penalty** (versus the usual 10%).

**Worked example — SIMPLE match.** An employee earning $60,000 defers 4% ($2,400). Under the 3% match, the employer matches only up to 3% of pay:

- Employer match = 3% × $60,000 = **$1,800**
- Total going into the account = $2,400 + $1,800 = **$4,200**

#### SEP IRA

The **Simplified Employee Pension (SEP) IRA** is an easy-to-administer plan popular with small businesses and the self-employed.

- **Funded entirely by the employer** — employees do not make their own salary deferrals.
- The employer may contribute up to **25% of each eligible employee's compensation**, capped at **$70,000.**
- Contributions are **discretionary** (the employer can vary the amount year to year) but must be a **uniform percentage** for all eligible employees, and are **immediately 100% vested.**

**Worked example — SEP contribution.** An employer contributes the maximum for a common-law employee earning $100,000:

- Contribution = 25% × $100,000 = **$25,000** (within the $70,000 cap)

**Common trap (self-employed):** For a **common-law employee** the rate is **25% of compensation.** For a **self-employed** owner, the contribution is figured on net earnings *after* subtracting the contribution itself and half of self-employment tax, which works out to an effective **20% of net self-employment income.** If a question gives a sole proprietor's net earnings and asks the maximum SEP contribution rate, the answer is **20%**, not 25%.

#### Nonqualified Retirement Plans

**Nonqualified plans** intentionally sidestep ERISA's qualification rules so the employer can reward **selected executives or key employees** — discrimination is allowed. Common types include **deferred compensation plans**, **payroll-deduction plans**, **executive bonus plans**, and **split-dollar life insurance.**

- **Contributions are after-tax / not deductible** to the participant (and the employer's deduction is generally deferred until the benefit is paid).
- Earnings still grow **tax-deferred.**
- At payout, **only the earnings are taxed**; the participant's after-tax basis is returned tax-free.
- Because they are not ERISA-qualified, benefits may be at risk of the employer's creditors (the promise is often unfunded).

**Worked example — nonqualified distribution taxation.** An executive contributed **$50,000** of after-tax dollars to a nonqualified plan that grew to **$80,000**, then took the full distribution:

- Return of basis (already taxed): **$50,000 tax-free**
- Taxable earnings: $80,000 − $50,000 = **$30,000** taxed as ordinary income

Contrast this with a **qualified plan**: if the entire $80,000 had been pre-tax money, the **full $80,000** would be taxable.

#### Qualified vs. Nonqualified: The Taxation Distinction (Summary)

This comparison ties the whole section together and is the most exam-relevant takeaway:

| Feature | Qualified Plan | Nonqualified Plan |
|---|---|---|
| Contributions | **Pre-tax** (deductible / salary reduction) | **After-tax** (not deductible) |
| Must satisfy ERISA / be nondiscriminatory | **Yes** | **No** — may favor executives |
| Earnings growth | Tax-deferred | Tax-deferred |
| Cost basis at distribution | **Zero** (nothing was taxed yet) | Equal to after-tax contributions |
| What is taxed at distribution | **Entire distribution** as ordinary income | **Only the earnings** as ordinary income |
| IRS approval / trust required | Yes | No |
| Examples | 401(k), 403(b), DB pension, profit-sharing, SEP, SIMPLE, IRAs | Deferred comp, executive bonus, split-dollar, payroll-deduction, 457 (technically) |

**Exam tip:** When you see a distribution-taxation question, first classify the plan as qualified or nonqualified. Qualified → the entire payout is ordinary income. Nonqualified → subtract the after-tax basis and tax only the growth. Also remember that all retirement-plan distributions of previously untaxed money are taxed as **ordinary income**, never at capital-gains rates — a frequently tested point.

**Rollovers (brief but testable).** Money can move between retirement accounts without immediate tax. A **direct (trustee-to-trustee) transfer** moves funds between custodians with no withholding and no limit on frequency — the preferred method. A **60-day (indirect) rollover** pays the money to the client, who must redeposit it within **60 days**; on IRA-to-IRA rollovers this is limited to **one per 12-month period**, and employer-plan distributions paid to the client are subject to mandatory **20% withholding.** A **Roth conversion** moves Traditional IRA money to a Roth and is a **taxable event** in the year of conversion, but future qualified Roth withdrawals are tax-free.

### G. ERISA Issues

The Employee Retirement Income Security Act of 1974 (ERISA) is the federal law governing how private-sector employer-sponsored retirement plans must be operated. On the Series 65, ERISA questions cluster around one central theme: the fiduciary standard. The people who run, advise, and invest a retirement plan owe demanding duties, and the exam tests whether you know who those fiduciaries are, what standard of care they owe, and what they are absolutely forbidden from doing. This subsection walks through the fiduciary rules (prudent investment choices, the qualified default investment alternative, and diversification), the investment policy statement that documents a prudent process, and the prohibited-transaction rules — all built on the foundational point that ERISA reaches private plans but not government plans.

#### What ERISA Covers — and What It Does Not

ERISA applies to **private-sector** employee benefit plans: corporate pension plans, profit-sharing plans, 401(k) plans, and similar arrangements sponsored by private employers. It does **not** apply to plans set up for government employees.

| Plan / arrangement | Covered by ERISA? |
| --- | --- |
| Corporate 401(k), profit-sharing, private pension | Yes |
| Private employer defined benefit plan | Yes |
| Federal, state, county, or municipal (public) employee plans — e.g., a state teachers' or city workers' plan, a 457(b) governmental plan | No |
| Church plans (generally, unless they elect in) | No |
| Individual IRAs the owner opens on their own | Not an ERISA plan (though the prohibited-transaction concepts have IRS parallels) |

The exam loves the government-plan carve-out. If a question describes a pension for city firefighters, state university employees, or a public school district, ERISA's fiduciary provisions do **not** govern it — those plans answer to state and local law instead.

**Exam tip:** The single most tested ERISA fact is the private-vs-government distinction. When you see "municipal," "state," "county," "public school," or "federal" employees, ERISA does **not** apply.

**Common trap:** Candidates assume ERISA covers "all retirement plans." It does not cover government plans, and a personal IRA is not itself an ERISA plan. Read the sponsor carefully.

#### Fiduciary Duties: The Prudent Expert Standard

A **fiduciary** under ERISA is anyone who exercises discretionary authority or control over the management of the plan or its assets, or who renders investment advice for a fee. This sweeps in the plan sponsor's investment committee, trustees, and — importantly for you — an investment adviser hired to manage or advise on plan assets. Fiduciary status is based on function, not title: if you make decisions about the plan's money, you are a fiduciary.

ERISA imposes four core fiduciary duties:

1. **Duty of loyalty (the "exclusive benefit" rule).** The fiduciary must act *solely* in the interest of plan participants and beneficiaries, for the exclusive purpose of providing benefits and defraying reasonable plan expenses. The fiduciary's own interests — and the employer's convenience — come second.
2. **Duty of prudence (the "prudent expert" rule).** The fiduciary must act with the care, skill, prudence, and diligence that a prudent person "familiar with such matters" would use. That phrase — *familiar with such matters* — raises the bar above the ordinary "prudent man" standard to what is often called a **prudent expert** standard. It governs the *process*: it asks whether decisions were made carefully and with appropriate expertise, not whether every investment happened to earn a positive return.
3. **Duty to diversify.** The fiduciary must diversify plan investments to minimize the risk of large losses, unless under the circumstances it is clearly prudent not to.
4. **Duty to follow plan documents.** The fiduciary must operate the plan according to its governing documents, to the extent those documents are consistent with ERISA.

**Exam tip:** The prudent-expert standard is about **prudent process, not perfect results.** A fiduciary who followed a careful, well-documented process is not liable simply because an investment lost money. Conversely, a lucky gain does not excuse a reckless process.

##### Diversification in more detail

Diversification exists to reduce the chance of a catastrophic loss from concentration. A fiduciary who dumps most of the plan into a single stock or sector has breached the duty even if that bet happens to pay off. The classic red flag is over-concentration in **employer (company) stock**, which exposes participants to a double risk — if the company fails, they lose both their jobs and their retirement savings. ERISA allows certain individual-account plans (such as ESOPs and some profit-sharing plans) to hold employer securities, but a prudent fiduciary still watches concentration carefully.

#### Participant-Directed Plans and ERISA Section 404(c)

Many modern plans (like 401(k)s) let each participant choose how their own account is invested. **ERISA Section 404(c)** offers the plan fiduciaries relief from liability for the *investment results* of those participant-directed choices — but only if the plan gives participants genuine control. To qualify, the plan generally must:

- Offer a **broad range of investment alternatives** — at least **three diversified options** with materially different risk-and-return characteristics.
- Allow participants to change their allocations with reasonable frequency (at least **quarterly**, and more often for volatile investments).
- Provide participants with sufficient information to make informed decisions.

If those conditions are met and a participant chooses poorly, the fiduciary is not liable for that participant's loss. The fiduciary remains responsible, however, for **prudently selecting and monitoring** the menu of options offered — 404(c) never excuses a bad lineup, only bad participant choices from a good lineup.

**Common trap:** 404(c) relief does not cover the *selection and monitoring* of the investment menu. The fiduciary is always on the hook for choosing prudent options and replacing ones that no longer belong.

#### Qualified Default Investment Alternatives (QDIA)

Participant-directed plans face a practical problem: what happens to the money of an auto-enrolled worker who never actually picks an investment? Parking their contributions in cash would be safe short-term but a poor long-term choice, and it would leave the fiduciary exposed. The **Qualified Default Investment Alternative (QDIA)** solves this. If the plan defaults a non-electing participant's money into a QDIA and meets the notice and transfer conditions, the fiduciary receives **404(c)-style relief** for that default investment — as if the participant had chosen it.

To be a QDIA, the default option generally must:

- Be **diversified** to minimize the risk of large losses.
- **Not invest primarily in employer securities.**
- Be managed by a registered investment manager, or be a diversified investment company (mutual fund) or similar product.
- Permit participants to **transfer out** to other plan options at least as often as other participants can move, with **no restrictions, fees, or penalties during the first 90 days**.
- Be preceded by an **advance notice** to participants explaining the default and their right to opt out.

Acceptable QDIA types typically include:

| QDIA type | Nature |
| --- | --- |
| Target-date / lifecycle fund | Asset mix based on the participant's expected retirement date; grows more conservative over time |
| Balanced fund | Blend of stocks and bonds set for the plan population as a whole |
| Managed account | Professionally managed allocation tailored to the individual participant |
| Capital-preservation product (money market / stable value) | Permitted **only as a short-term default**, generally limited to the first 120 days, not as a long-term QDIA |

**Exam tip:** A **target-date fund is the textbook QDIA answer.** Its self-adjusting, diversified allocation is exactly what regulators intended for a default.

**Common trap:** A money market fund is **not** a valid long-term QDIA. It is permitted only for a short window (about 120 days). And a fund that invests primarily in employer stock can never be a QDIA.

#### The Investment Policy Statement (IPS)

An **Investment Policy Statement (IPS)** is the written roadmap for how a plan's (or client's) assets will be managed. While ERISA does not literally command every plan to adopt one by statute, maintaining and following an IPS is treated as a hallmark of a **prudent process** — it is the primary evidence that fiduciaries made decisions deliberately rather than haphazardly.

A well-constructed IPS typically documents:

- **Objectives and risk tolerance** — the plan's return goals and how much risk it can bear.
- **Asset allocation guidelines** — target allocations and permitted ranges among asset classes.
- **Selection criteria** — the standards used to choose investment options or managers (e.g., track record, expenses, style consistency).
- **Monitoring and review procedures** — how performance is measured against benchmarks and how often the menu is reviewed.
- **Criteria for replacing** underperforming options and other governance procedures.

Because the fiduciary standard is process-based, an IPS is the fiduciary's best defense: it shows a reasoned framework was set in advance and followed consistently. Note that the IPS is a set of guidelines to be applied prudently — it is not a rigid contract, and fiduciaries must still exercise judgment as conditions change.

**Exam tip:** If a question asks what document demonstrates a prudent, disciplined investment process for a plan (or an individual client), the answer is the **Investment Policy Statement.**

#### Prohibited Transactions and Parties in Interest

ERISA flatly bars fiduciaries from causing the plan to engage in certain self-dealing and conflict-of-interest transactions with a **"party in interest"** (called a "disqualified person" in the parallel IRS rules). A party in interest includes the people and entities close to the plan, such as:

- Plan **fiduciaries**, trustees, and the plan's **service providers** (including the investment adviser, custodian, and accountant).
- The **employer** (plan sponsor) and its employees, officers, and directors.
- **Relatives** of the above and entities they substantially own.

The following dealings between the plan and a party in interest are **prohibited transactions**:

| Prohibited transaction | Example |
| --- | --- |
| Sale, exchange, or lease of property | The employer sells its office building to the plan |
| Lending money or extending credit | The plan loans money to the sponsoring company |
| Furnishing goods, services, or facilities | A party in interest provides services to the plan for more than reasonable compensation |
| Transferring plan assets to, or using them for the benefit of, a party in interest | Using plan cash to prop up the employer |
| Acquiring or holding employer securities or real property beyond legal limits | Over-concentrating the plan in company stock |

On top of those, ERISA specifically forbids fiduciary **self-dealing**. A fiduciary may not:

- Deal with plan assets in the fiduciary's **own interest** or for the fiduciary's own account.
- Act on behalf of a party whose interests are **adverse** to the plan or its participants.
- Receive any **consideration (a kickback)** for their own account from a third party dealing with the plan.

**Exam tip:** Think "no self-dealing, no cozy insider deals." If the plan is transacting with the employer, a fiduciary, or an adviser to the plan — buying from them, lending to them, or paying them a kickback — it is almost certainly a prohibited transaction.

The rules are not absolute. The Department of Labor can grant **Prohibited Transaction Exemptions (PTEs)** — either individual or class exemptions — when a transaction is protective of and in the interest of participants (for example, exemptions allowing certain adviser compensation or the provision of routine services at reasonable cost). The default, however, is prohibition; the exemption is the exception.

**Common trap:** Fair terms do not cure a prohibited transaction. Even if the employer sells property to the plan at an honest market price, the sale between the plan and a party in interest is still prohibited unless a specific exemption applies. The prohibition targets the **relationship**, not just the price.

Putting it together for the exam: ERISA fiduciaries owe an exclusive-benefit duty of loyalty, a prudent-expert duty of care focused on process, and a duty to diversify. They can shift responsibility for participants' own investment choices through Section 404(c) and for defaulted participants through a proper QDIA (a target-date fund being the model example), they document their prudent process in an Investment Policy Statement, and they must scrupulously avoid prohibited transactions with parties in interest — all within the world of **private-sector** plans, never government plans.

### H. Special Types of Accounts

Beyond ordinary individual and joint brokerage accounts, the Series 65 expects you to know a handful of *tax-advantaged* and *custodial* account types built for specific goals — mostly education and health care. For each, focus on the three things the exam repeatedly tests: (1) **who contributes and how much**, (2) **the tax treatment going in, growing, and coming out**, and (3) **who actually owns and controls the money**. Get those three right and most questions in this area answer themselves.

A recurring theme is the **"after-tax in, tax-free out"** structure shared by 529 plans, Coverdell ESAs, and Roth IRAs: you fund them with money that has already been taxed (no federal deduction), the account grows tax-deferred, and **qualified** withdrawals come out entirely tax-free. (The **HSA**, covered last, is the exception — its contributions are *tax-deductible* going in.) The traps almost always involve *non-qualified* withdrawals or confusion about *control* of the assets.

Note: several dollar figures below (the gift-tax annual exclusion, HSA limits, and kiddie-tax brackets) are **indexed to inflation and adjust annually**. The exam rewards knowing the *structure and the standard tested values*, not memorizing the current-year number to the dollar. Fixed figures (Coverdell's $2,000 limit and its income phase-outs) do **not** adjust and are fair game as exact numbers.

#### 529 Plans (Qualified Tuition Programs)

A **529 plan** is a state-sponsored, tax-advantaged education savings vehicle authorized under **Section 529 of the Internal Revenue Code**. There are two distinct kinds, and the exam wants you to tell them apart.

**1. College Savings Plan (the common type).** The account owner (usually a parent or grandparent) contributes after-tax dollars, which are invested in a menu of portfolios chosen by the plan — typically mutual funds or **age-based (target-date) portfolios** that automatically shift from equities toward fixed income as the beneficiary nears college age. The account value **fluctuates with the market**; there is investment risk and no guarantee. Funds can be used at virtually any eligible institution nationwide.

**2. Prepaid Tuition Plan.** The owner buys future tuition credits at **today's prices**, hedging against tuition inflation. These plans are usually limited to **in-state public colleges**, may be **state-guaranteed** (or backed by the participating institutions), and offer far less investment flexibility. Value is measured in tuition units rather than a market portfolio.

**Key features common to both types:**

- **Contributions:** After-tax (no federal deduction), but **many states offer a state income-tax deduction or credit** to residents who use their own state's plan. There are **no income limits** on who may contribute.
- **High contribution ceilings:** Set by each state and very high — commonly an aggregate account balance cap of **$300,000+ per beneficiary**.
- **Tax treatment:** Tax-deferred growth; **qualified withdrawals are federally tax-free**.
- **Qualified expenses:** Tuition, fees, books, supplies, required equipment, and **room and board** (if enrolled at least half-time). Also included: up to **$10,000 per year** for **K–12 tuition**, and up to **$10,000 lifetime** for **student-loan repayment**.
- **Control stays with the owner.** This is heavily tested — see the callout below. The owner may also **change the beneficiary** to another qualifying family member without penalty.
- **Financial aid:** A parent-owned 529 is treated as the **parent's asset** on the FAFSA, which is favorable (assessed at a low rate) compared with a student-owned custodial account.

**Non-qualified withdrawals:** The **earnings** portion is taxed as **ordinary income to the recipient plus a 10% penalty**. (Only the earnings are hit — the original after-tax contributions come out tax- and penalty-free.) The penalty is waived in limited cases such as the beneficiary's death, disability, or receipt of a scholarship (up to the scholarship amount).

**529-to-Roth IRA rollover (SECURE Act 2.0):** Leftover 529 funds may be rolled into the **beneficiary's Roth IRA**, subject to a **$35,000 lifetime cap**, provided the 529 has been open at least **15 years**. Each year's rollover is also limited to the annual IRA contribution limit. This provision softens the old "what if my child doesn't go to college?" objection.

> **Exam tip:** In a 529 plan, the **account owner — not the beneficiary — retains control** of the assets, can change the beneficiary, and can even reclaim the money (subject to tax and penalty on earnings). This is the single biggest contrast with UTMA/UGMA custodial accounts, where the assets **irrevocably belong to the minor**.

**Gift-tax treatment and 5-year "superfunding."** A contribution to a 529 is treated as a **completed gift** to the beneficiary, so it qualifies for the annual gift-tax exclusion (a representative tested figure is **$19,000 per donor, per beneficiary, per year**; it is inflation-indexed). A special election unique to 529 plans lets a donor **front-load five years of exclusions in a single year** and treat it as if spread evenly over five years.

- Formula: **Maximum 5-year front-load = Annual Exclusion × 5**
- Worked example: One grandparent contributes in a single year: **$19,000 × 5 = $95,000** free of gift tax. A married couple electing to **split the gift** can contribute **$95,000 × 2 = $190,000** at once for one beneficiary — with no gift tax and no use of the lifetime estate/gift exemption, as long as they make no further gifts to that beneficiary for five years.

> **Common trap:** Distractors will say 529 contributions are "**federally tax-deductible**." They are **not** — contributions are made with after-tax dollars. The tax benefit is **tax-free growth and qualified withdrawals** (plus a possible *state* deduction), not a federal deduction.

#### Coverdell Education Savings Account (ESA)

A **Coverdell ESA** is another after-tax-in, tax-free-out education account, but it is much smaller and more restricted than a 529 — and, importantly, it is typically **self-directed**, meaning the owner can invest in almost any securities (individual stocks, bonds, funds) rather than a fixed plan menu.

**Key rules (memorize the fixed numbers — they do not inflation-adjust):**

- **Contribution limit: $2,000 per year, per beneficiary** — an **aggregate** cap across *all* contributors, not $2,000 from each. If a parent and a grandparent both want to contribute, together they may put in no more than $2,000 for that child that year.
- **Income limits on contributors (MAGI phase-outs):** **$95,000–$110,000 for single filers** and **$190,000–$220,000 for married filing jointly**. Above the top of the range, direct contributions are disallowed.
- **Age restrictions:** Contributions must stop when the beneficiary turns **18** (special-needs beneficiaries excepted), and the funds must generally be used by age **30**. Any balance left at 30 is distributed (earnings taxed + 10% penalty) or **rolled to another family member's** Coverdell.
- **Qualified expenses:** Both **higher education and K–12** (tuition plus a broad range of elementary/secondary costs) qualify.
- **Tax treatment:** After-tax contributions, tax-deferred growth, tax-free qualified withdrawals; non-qualified withdrawal earnings are taxed plus a **10% penalty**.

> **Exam tip:** If a question stresses **wide investment choice / self-directed** for an education account, think **Coverdell**. If it stresses **very high contribution limits and no income cap**, think **529**.

#### Contrasting 529 vs. Coverdell

This side-by-side comparison is among the most testable in the whole education-accounts topic.

| Feature | 529 Plan | Coverdell ESA |
|---|---|---|
| Annual contribution limit | No fixed annual cap; high aggregate balance limit (often $300,000+, state-set) | **$2,000/year per beneficiary** (aggregate across all contributors) |
| Income limit on contributor | **None** | **Yes** — $95,000–$110,000 single; $190,000–$220,000 MFJ |
| Investment control | Limited to plan's menu (age-based/portfolio options) | **Self-directed** — nearly any security |
| Age restrictions | **None** — any age beneficiary; no use-by deadline | Contribute before **18**; use by **30** |
| Qualified expenses | Higher ed; up to $10K/yr K–12 **tuition**; $10K lifetime student loans | Higher ed **and** broad K–12 expenses |
| Tax treatment (federal) | After-tax in; tax-free qualified out | After-tax in; tax-free qualified out |
| Who controls the assets | **Account owner** (can change beneficiary) | Responsible individual/owner (can change beneficiary) |
| Special features | 5-year gift front-loading; 529→Roth rollover | Broad K–12 coverage; self-directed |

> **Common trap:** Watch for a stem claiming you **cannot** hold both. You *can* contribute to a 529 **and** a Coverdell for the same beneficiary in the same year. Also watch the K–12 distinction: 529s cover K–12 **tuition** (up to $10,000/yr), while Coverdells cover a **broader set** of K–12 expenses.

#### UTMA / UGMA Custodial Accounts

Custodial accounts let an adult hold and manage assets on behalf of a **minor** who cannot legally hold securities in their own name. Two statutes govern them:

- **UGMA — Uniform Gifts to Minors Act:** the older law; permits gifts of **financial assets** — cash, securities, and life-insurance contracts.
- **UTMA — Uniform Transfers to Minors Act:** the broader, more common modern law; permits **UGMA assets plus other property** such as **real estate, art, patents, and royalties**, and often allows the custodian to hold assets until a **later age** (up to 21, or in some states 25).

**How they work — and the ownership rule the exam loves:**

- A **gift to a custodial account is an irrevocable, completed gift.** Once transferred, the assets **belong to the minor** — not to the custodian and not to the donor. The custodian merely manages them as a **fiduciary**, acting solely in the minor's best interest.
- **Account registration:** Assets are held under the **minor's name and Social Security number**, so income is **taxed to the minor** (subject to the kiddie tax, below).
- **One custodian, one minor** per account. The custodian may be the donor, a parent, or another responsible adult, but the roles cannot be split among several people, and one account cannot serve multiple minors.
- **At the age of majority** (set by state law, commonly **18 or 21**), the account transfers outright to the (former) minor, who may then use the money for **any purpose whatsoever** — a key contrast with a 529, where the owner keeps control indefinitely.
- **No contribution limits** (the gift-tax annual exclusion still applies), and **no restriction to education** — funds may be spent for the minor's benefit at any time.
- **Death of the minor:** because the minor owns the assets, they pass to the **minor's estate**, not back to the donor.

> **Exam tip:** The custodian **cannot** use custodial assets for their own benefit, cannot pledge them for a personal loan, and generally should not buy on **margin, trade options, or sell short** — these are inconsistent with the prudent, conservative standard owed to a minor. The account is managed for the **minor's** benefit under a fiduciary duty.

**Kiddie tax.** To stop parents from shifting investment income to a low-bracket child, a child's **unearned (investment) income** above a threshold is taxed at the **parents' marginal rate**. The tested structure: a first slice of unearned income is tax-free (the child's standard deduction), the next slice is taxed at the **child's own rate**, and everything above that is taxed at the **parents' rate**.

- Illustrative (inflation-adjusted) figures: first **~$1,350** tax-free; next **~$1,350** at the child's rate; amounts over **~$2,700** at the **parents'** rate.
- Worked example: A UTMA account generates **$3,000** of dividends and interest in a year. Roughly the first $1,350 is offset by the standard deduction, the next $1,350 is taxed at the child's (low) rate, and the remaining **$3,000 − $2,700 = $300** is taxed at the **parents' marginal rate**.

> **Common trap:** Answer choices often say the custodial account is owned by the **custodian** or the **donor**. It is **owned by the minor** — the donor cannot take the gift back, and the custodian cannot spend it on themselves. Contrast this squarely with the **529 owner's retained control**.

#### Health Savings Account (HSA)

Although not an education account, the HSA appears in this section as a **tax-advantaged special account**, and it carries the strongest tax benefit of all: a **triple tax advantage**.

- **Contributions are tax-deductible** (or made pre-tax through payroll), reducing current taxable income.
- **Growth is tax-deferred** (invested balances compound tax-free).
- **Qualified-medical withdrawals are tax-free.**

**Eligibility.** To contribute, an individual must be covered by a **High-Deductible Health Plan (HDHP)**, must **not** be enrolled in **Medicare**, and must **not** be claimed as someone else's dependent. The HDHP must meet minimum-deductible and maximum-out-of-pocket thresholds set by the IRS (these adjust annually).

**Contribution limits (indexed annually; representative tested values):** **$4,400 for self-only** coverage and **$8,750 for family** coverage, with an extra **$1,000 catch-up** for those **age 55+**.
