Unlike a Flexible Spending Account (FSA), **HSA balances roll over year to year** and are **portable** — the account belongs to the individual, not the employer, with no "use it or lose it."

- Worked example: A 57-year-old with **family** HDHP coverage may contribute **$8,750 + $1,000 = $9,750** for the year (using the representative figures above).

**Withdrawals:**

- **Qualified medical expenses:** always **tax-free**, at any age.
- **Non-qualified withdrawals before age 65:** the amount is taxed as **ordinary income plus a 20% penalty** (note: **20%**, higher than the 10% on education and retirement accounts).
- **After age 65:** non-medical withdrawals are taxed as **ordinary income with no penalty** — effectively functioning like a **Traditional IRA**, while medical withdrawals remain tax-free. This makes the HSA a powerful supplemental retirement vehicle.

> **Exam tip:** Two HSA facts are disproportionately tested: (1) you must have an **HDHP** to contribute, and (2) the **penalty for non-qualified withdrawals is 20%** before age 65 — larger than the 10% penalty attached to 529s, Coverdells, and IRAs. After 65, the penalty disappears and only ordinary income tax applies to non-medical use.

> **Common trap:** Don't confuse the **HSA** (paired with an HDHP, balances roll over, individually owned, investable) with an **FSA** (employer plan, "use it or lose it," not investable). Only the HSA offers the triple tax advantage and long-term investment growth.

### I. Ownership and Estate Planning Techniques

How an account is titled determines who controls the assets, who inherits them, whether they pass through probate, and how they are taxed at death. On the Series 65, this material appears as scenario questions ("Two sisters own an account together and one dies — what happens to her share?") and as comparison questions that hinge on a single distinguishing feature: right of survivorship, spousal-only status, creditor protection, or step-up in basis. Learn each form by its distinguishing feature and you can answer almost any question in this area.

#### Forms of Property Ownership: An Overview

Property held by more than one person is held in one of several "concurrent ownership" forms. The two questions the exam always cares about are:

1. **Is there a right of survivorship?** Does a deceased owner's share pass automatically to the surviving owner(s), or does it fall into the deceased owner's estate?
2. **Does the transfer avoid probate?** A right of survivorship generally means the asset passes automatically and bypasses probate.

The forms below are simply different ways those two questions get answered.

#### Joint Tenants with Right of Survivorship (JTWROS)

Under JTWROS, two or more people own the account together, each with an **equal, undivided interest** in the whole. The defining feature is the **right of survivorship**: when one owner dies, that person's share passes **automatically** to the surviving owner(s), **outside of probate**, regardless of what the deceased's will says.

- Owners need **not** be married (a parent and child, or two business partners, may hold JTWROS).
- Because shares are equal, a two-person JTWROS is always 50/50; a three-person JTWROS is one-third each.
- Any owner can typically trade or withdraw the full account balance, so JTWROS requires mutual trust.

**Exam tip:** The phrase "right of survivorship" is your signal that the asset **avoids probate** and passes to the co-owner, not to heirs named in a will. A will cannot override survivorship.

#### Tenants in Common (TIC)

Under Tenants in Common, two or more people own the account together, but there is **NO right of survivorship**, and ownership shares may be **unequal** (e.g., 70/30). When one owner dies, that person's fractional share passes to **their own estate** and is distributed by their will (or by state intestacy law) — meaning it **goes through probate**, not to the surviving co-owner.

- TIC is the common choice when co-owners want their share to go to their own heirs rather than to each other.
- Because shares can be unequal, TIC also fits owners who contribute different amounts.

**Common trap:** Students confuse JTWROS and TIC. Memorize the split: **JTWROS = equal shares + survivorship (avoids probate); TIC = can be unequal + no survivorship (share goes to the estate, through probate).**

#### Tenancy by the Entirety (TBE)

Tenancy by the Entirety is a special joint form available **only to married spouses** (and, in some states, only for real property). It includes a **right of survivorship**, so on the death of one spouse the property passes automatically to the survivor and avoids probate.

Two features distinguish TBE from ordinary JTWROS:

- **Both spouses must consent** to sell, transfer, or encumber the property — one spouse acting alone cannot dispose of it.
- **Creditor protection:** a creditor of only **one** spouse generally **cannot** attach or force the sale of TBE property, making TBE a modest asset-protection tool. (A joint creditor of both spouses can still reach it.)

**Exam tip:** If a question stresses that neither spouse can act without the other, or that one spouse's creditors can't reach the property, the answer is **tenancy by the entirety**. TBE is **not** recognized in every state.

#### Community Property (and CPWROS)

In the nine **community property states** — Arizona, California, Idaho, Louisiana, Nevada, New Mexico, Texas, Washington, and Wisconsin — most property **acquired during the marriage** is owned **50/50** by both spouses, regardless of who earned it. (Property owned before marriage, or received by gift or inheritance, is typically **separate property**.)

Standard community property does **not** automatically include a right of survivorship; the deceased spouse's half passes by will and may go through probate. **Community Property with Right of Survivorship (CPWROS)** adds survivorship, so the deceased spouse's half passes automatically to the surviving spouse and **avoids probate**.

The heavily tested advantage of community property is the **full (double) step-up in cost basis** at the first spouse's death — see the worked example below.

#### Cost Basis Step-Up at Death — Worked Example

At death, inherited property generally receives a **step-up in basis** to its **fair market value (FMV) on the date of death**. How much of the property steps up differs by ownership form, and this is a favorite exam calculation.

Formula (plain text):

- New basis of survivor's holding = (stepped-up portion at FMV) + (any portion that keeps its original basis)

**Facts:** A married couple owns stock with an original cost basis of **$100,000**. On the first spouse's death, the stock is worth **$500,000** (FMV). The survivor sells immediately.

**Case A — JTWROS in a common-law (non-community-property) state:** Only the **deceased spouse's half** steps up. Original basis was split 50/50 = $50,000 each.
- Survivor's own half keeps its basis: **$50,000**
- Deceased half steps up to FMV (half of $500,000): **$250,000**
- New total basis = $50,000 + $250,000 = **$300,000**
- Taxable gain if sold at $500,000 = $500,000 − $300,000 = **$200,000**

**Case B — Community property (CPWROS):** The **entire** account receives a **full step-up** to FMV.
- New total basis = **$500,000**
- Taxable gain if sold at $500,000 = $500,000 − $500,000 = **$0**

**Exam tip:** The **double step-up** is the key benefit of community property versus JTWROS. Same asset, same death: community property erased the entire $200,000 of built-in gain, while JTWROS stepped up only half.

#### Comparison Table: Ownership Forms

| Form | Who can hold it | Shares | Right of survivorship? | Probate on death? | Distinctive feature |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| JTWROS | Anyone (2+) | Equal | Yes | No — passes to co-owner | Any owner can act alone |
| TIC | Anyone (2+) | May be unequal | No | Yes — passes to estate/heirs | Share goes to owner's own heirs |
| TBE | Married spouses only | Equal | Yes | No — passes to spouse | Both must consent; creditor protection |
| Community property (CPWROS) | Married spouses (9 states) | 50/50 | Only if "WROS" added | No if WROS | Full step-up in basis |

#### Transfer-on-Death (TOD) / Pay-on-Death (POD)

A **TOD** designation (used for **brokerage/securities accounts**) — and its bank-account equivalent, **POD** ("pay on death") — lets an owner name a beneficiary who receives the account **automatically at death, avoiding probate**.

- During life, the owner keeps **complete control**: the beneficiary has **no rights, no access, and no ownership** while the owner is alive. The owner can spend it all, change the beneficiary, or revoke the designation.
- TOD/POD is a simple, low-cost probate-avoidance tool that does **not** require a trust.
- The designation controls who inherits — it **overrides the will** for that account.

**Common trap:** A TOD beneficiary is not a co-owner. Compare with JTWROS, where the co-owner has full ownership and access **during life**. With TOD, control stays 100% with the owner; the beneficiary only inherits later.

#### Beneficiary Designations: Per Stirpes vs. Per Capita

Retirement accounts (IRAs, 401(k)s), TOD/POD accounts, annuities, and life insurance pass by **beneficiary designation**, which **overrides the will**. A key testable detail is what happens when a named beneficiary **dies before** the account owner. That is governed by whether the designation is **per stirpes** or **per capita**.

- **Per stirpes** ("by the branch/by the roots"): a deceased beneficiary's share passes **down to that beneficiary's own descendants** (their children).
- **Per capita** ("by the head"): the assets are divided **equally among the surviving named beneficiaries**; a deceased beneficiary's descendants get **nothing**.

**Worked example.** An IRA owner names her **three children — A, B, and C — as equal beneficiaries (one-third each)**. Child **C dies before** the owner, leaving **two children** (grandchildren of the IRA owner).

- **Per stirpes:** A = 1/3, B = 1/3, and **C's two children split C's 1/3**, so each grandchild gets **1/6**. C's branch is preserved.
- **Per capita:** the account is split only among the **surviving named beneficiaries**, A and B, so **A = 1/2 and B = 1/2**, and C's children receive **nothing**.

**Exam tip:** Remember **stirpes = descendants** (the deceased beneficiary's line inherits in their place). **Per capita = by head count of survivors**, so a predeceased beneficiary's children are cut out.

#### Wills, Intestacy, and Probate

A **will** is a legal document directing how a person's assets are distributed at death. It names an **executor** (personal representative) to carry out its terms and can name a **guardian** for minor children. Dying **without** a valid will is dying **intestate**; in that case, **state intestacy law** determines who inherits — the individual loses all say in the distribution.

**Probate** is the court-supervised legal process that:

1. Validates the will,
2. Pays the deceased's debts and taxes, and
3. Distributes the remaining assets to heirs.

Probate is generally **public, time-consuming, and costly**. That is why the probate-avoiding tools above — JTWROS/TBE survivorship, TOD/POD, beneficiary designations, and revocable trusts — are central to estate planning.

**Exam tip:** Assets that pass by **survivorship, beneficiary designation, TOD/POD, or a funded living trust bypass probate**. Assets titled in the individual's name alone, or passing under a **will**, go **through** probate. A will controls only **probate** assets — it does **not** override survivorship or beneficiary designations.

#### Trusts (Basic Concepts)

A **trust** is a legal arrangement in which a **grantor** (also called *settlor* or *trustor*) transfers assets to a **trustee**, who holds and manages them for the benefit of one or more **beneficiaries** according to the trust document. The three parties to know are **grantor, trustee, and beneficiary** (one person can occupy more than one role).

Key classifications:

| Trust type | Can it be changed? | In the grantor's taxable estate? | Probate at death? | Notes |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **Revocable (living) trust** | Yes — grantor can amend/revoke | **Yes** — still the grantor's assets | **No** — avoids probate | Grantor is usually trustee; primary purpose is probate avoidance and incapacity planning, **not** tax savings |
| **Irrevocable trust** | No — generally cannot be changed | Generally **No** — removed from estate | No | Used for estate-tax reduction and **asset protection**; grantor gives up control |
| **Testamentary trust** | Created by the will | Yes | **Yes** — created *through* probate | Comes into existence only at death, via the will |
| **Living / inter vivos trust** | Created during life | Depends (revocable vs. irrevocable) | No | Any trust established while the grantor is alive |

**Exam tips:**
- A **revocable living trust avoids probate but does NOT reduce estate taxes** — the grantor still controls the assets, so they remain in the estate. Students often wrongly assume a revocable trust saves estate tax; it doesn't.
- An **irrevocable trust** trades away control in exchange for removing assets from the estate and providing creditor/asset protection.
- A **testamentary trust is created by the will**, so it **does not avoid probate** — it is born out of the probate process.

#### Qualified Domestic Relations Orders (QDROs)

A **QDRO** is a court order, issued in a **divorce or legal separation**, that divides the benefits of an **ERISA-qualified employer retirement plan** (such as a **401(k) or pension**) between the plan participant and an **alternate payee** (typically a former spouse, but it may also be a child or other dependent).

Critical, frequently tested points:

- A QDRO applies to **employer-sponsored qualified plans**, **not IRAs**. Dividing an IRA in a divorce is done through a "**transfer incident to divorce**," which is not a QDRO.
- When funds are paid to a **former spouse** under a QDRO, the **former spouse (alternate payee) is responsible for the income tax** on distributions they take — the tax burden shifts to the recipient.
- A distribution made to an alternate payee under a QDRO is **exempt from the 10% early-withdrawal penalty**, even if the payee is under age 59½. This QDRO exception is a classic exam point.

**Exam tip:** If a question involves splitting a **401(k) or pension in a divorce**, the answer is **QDRO** — and remember the **10% penalty does not apply** to the QDRO distribution.

**Common trap:** Do not apply QDRO rules to an **IRA** — IRAs are divided by a transfer incident to divorce, not a QDRO.

#### Donor-Advised Funds (DAFs)

A **donor-advised fund** is a charitable-giving vehicle sponsored by a public charity (often the charitable arm of a brokerage or fund company). The donor makes an **irrevocable contribution** to an account within the fund, receives an **immediate income-tax charitable deduction** in the year of the gift, and then retains **advisory privileges** — the donor can recommend how the assets are invested and which qualified charities receive grants, and when, over future years.

Why DAFs are attractive and how they're tested:

- **Immediate deduction, deferred giving:** the donor deducts in the contribution year but can spread grants to charities across many later years.
- **Appreciated securities:** contributing long-term appreciated stock lets the donor **avoid capital gains tax** on the appreciation and generally **deduct the full fair market value** — a common exam advantage.
- **No annual payout requirement:** unlike a **private foundation** (which must distribute roughly 5% of assets annually), a DAF has **no mandated minimum annual distribution**.
- **Lower cost and administrative burden** than establishing a private foundation, plus **anonymity** if the donor wants it.

**Exam tip:** Key DAF facts are (1) the contribution is **irrevocable**, (2) the donor gets the **deduction now** but keeps only **advisory (not legal) control** over grants, and (3) it is **simpler and cheaper than a private foundation**, with **no 5% annual payout rule**.

### J. Trading Securities

Once an investment adviser has recommended a security or strategy, the trade has to be routed through the market and executed. The Series 65 expects you to understand the mechanics of how orders are priced, the different order types, the accounts trades settle in, who the players in the execution chain are, and what a trade costs. You are not expected to be a trading-desk professional, but you must know the vocabulary cold and be able to reason about the conflicts of interest and the fiduciary duty of best execution.

#### The Quote: Bids, Offers/Asks, and the Spread

Every actively traded security has a two-sided quote. Learn these definitions from the perspective of the dealer or market maker who is publishing the quote — this is the single most common source of confusion on the exam.

- **Bid** — the price at which the dealer is willing to **buy** the security. Therefore the bid is the price at which the **public investor sells**.
- **Offer** (also called the **ask**) — the price at which the dealer is willing to **sell** the security. Therefore the offer/ask is the price at which the **public investor buys**.

The bid is always lower than the ask. The difference between them is the **spread**.

Spread = Ask − Bid

**Worked example.** A market maker quotes ABC stock "20.00 – 20.25." An investor who wants to *sell* receives the bid, 20.00. An investor who wants to *buy* pays the ask, 20.25. The spread is 20.25 − 20.00 = 0.25 (25 cents per share). If the investor bought at 20.25 and immediately sold at 20.00, the round-trip cost from the spread alone would be 0.25 per share.

**Exam tip:** Remember the mnemonic "buyer pays the ask, seller hits the bid." When a question says a customer "buys at the market," they pay the **ask**; when they "sell at the market," they receive the **bid**.

**Common trap:** Students reverse the definitions because they think of "bid" from their own point of view (as if *they* are bidding to buy). On the exam, the quote is always from the **dealer's** side. The dealer bids to buy from you and offers to sell to you.

A **narrow** spread signals a liquid, actively traded security; a **wide** spread signals an illiquid security (thinly traded stock, some municipal or corporate bonds) and a higher implicit cost of trading.

#### Types of Orders

An order tells the broker-dealer how and when to fill the trade. The four you must know are market, limit, stop, and stop-limit.

| Order type | What it guarantees | What it does NOT guarantee | Typical use |
|---|---|---|---|
| Market | Immediate execution | Price | Get in/out right now |
| Limit | Price (or better) | Execution | Control the price paid/received |
| Stop | Triggers, then becomes a market order | Price after trigger | Protect a position / limit a loss |
| Stop-limit | Triggers, then becomes a limit order | Execution after trigger | Trigger + price control |

**Market order.** Executes immediately at the best available price. It guarantees that the trade *will* happen but not at what price. In a fast-moving or illiquid market the fill can be worse than the last quote.

**Limit order.** Sets the worst acceptable price. A **buy limit** executes at the limit price *or lower*; a **sell limit** executes at the limit price *or higher*. It guarantees the price will be as good as (or better than) specified but does not guarantee the order fills at all — the market may never reach the limit.

- Example: A buy limit at 50 fills at 50 or less. A sell limit at 50 fills at 50 or more.

**Stop order (stop-loss).** A resting order that is dormant until the market trades at or through the **stop (trigger) price**. Once triggered, it becomes a **market order** and executes at the next available price.

- A **sell stop** is placed *below* the current market. It protects a long position (or an unrealized gain) by getting the investor out if the price falls to the stop.
- A **buy stop** is placed *above* the current market. It is most often used to protect a **short seller**, limiting the loss if the price rises.

**Stop-limit order.** Identical trigger mechanics, but once the stop is hit the order becomes a **limit order** rather than a market order. This adds price protection but reintroduces the risk of non-execution — if the price gaps past the limit, the order may not fill.

**Exam tip:** The key contrast is "limit guarantees price but not execution; market guarantees execution but not price." A stop *becomes* a market order when triggered; a stop-limit *becomes* a limit order when triggered.

**Common trap:** A "sell stop" is placed **below** the market and a "buy stop" **above** the market — the opposite of where beginners expect. Also, a stop order is *not* the same as a limit order: the stop price is a *trigger*, not the execution price.

#### Short Sales

A **short sale** is the sale of a security the investor does not own. The mechanics: the investor **borrows** the shares (through the broker-dealer), sells them at the current price, and hopes to **buy them back later at a lower price** to return to the lender, keeping the difference. It is a bet that the price will **fall**.

Key facts tested:

- **Unlimited loss potential.** A stock's price can rise without limit, so the short seller's potential loss is theoretically infinite (unlike a long buyer, whose maximum loss is the amount invested).
- **Must be done in a margin account.** Short selling requires borrowing securities and posting collateral, so it cannot be done in a cash account.
- **Locate/borrow requirement.** Under Regulation SHO the broker-dealer must reasonably locate and arrange to borrow the shares before executing the short sale (this prevents "naked" short selling).
- The short seller is responsible for any **dividends** paid on the borrowed stock while the position is open.

**Exam tip:** Contrast the risk profiles — a long position has *limited* loss (price can only fall to zero) and *unlimited* gain; a short position has *limited* gain (price can only fall to zero) and *unlimited* loss. Short selling is generally suitable only for sophisticated, risk-tolerant clients.

#### Cash Accounts vs. Margin Accounts

The account type determines how a purchase is funded.

| Feature | Cash account | Margin account |
|---|---|---|
| Payment | Pay in full for all purchases | Borrow part of the purchase price from the BD |
| Leverage | None | Yes (amplifies gains and losses) |
| Short selling | Not permitted | Permitted |
| Governing rule | — | Reg T initial requirement (currently 50%) |
| Documents | Standard new-account form | Also requires a margin (credit) agreement and hypothecation agreement |

**Cash account.** The customer pays the full purchase price. This is the default account and the only type suitable for many retail clients, retirement accounts, and fiduciary accounts.

**Margin account.** The customer deposits a portion of the price and **borrows the remainder** from the broker-dealer, pledging the securities as collateral. Margin creates **leverage**, magnifying both gains and losses, and the customer pays **interest** on the borrowed (debit) balance.

- The Federal Reserve's **Regulation T** sets the **initial margin** requirement — currently **50%** of the purchase price for marginable equity securities. (Reg T is a Federal Reserve rule; you need to know the 50% figure, not perform detailed margin math on the Series 65.)
- A margin account exposes the client to a **margin call** — a demand to deposit additional funds if the account's equity falls below the maintenance level.

**Exam tip:** Margin and short selling are frequently flagged as **unsuitable** for conservative clients, retirees living on the account, or fiduciary/trust accounts because of the leverage and open-ended loss potential.

#### Principal vs. Agency Trades, and the Costs of Trading

How a broker-dealer participates in a trade determines how it is compensated. This is one of the most heavily tested ideas in the trading section.

**Agency (brokerage) trade.** The firm acts as a **broker** — an agent — matching the customer with the other side of the trade. It does not take ownership of the security. Its compensation is a **commission**, which is disclosed as a separate line item on the confirmation.

**Principal (dealer) trade.** The firm acts as a **dealer** — a principal — trading **from its own inventory**. When it sells to the customer out of inventory it adds a **markup**; when it buys from the customer into inventory it subtracts a **markdown**. The markup/markdown is built into the net price the customer pays or receives.

| | Agency trade | Principal trade |
|---|---|---|
| Firm's role | Broker (agent) | Dealer (principal) |
| Source of security | Another party | Firm's own inventory |
| Compensation | Commission | Markup / markdown |
| How shown on confirmation | Separately stated | Embedded in the net price |

**The three costs of trading:**

1. **Commission** — the agency charge, disclosed separately.
2. **Markup / markdown** — the principal charge, embedded in the price.
3. **Spread** — the difference between bid and ask; when a market maker fills a customer as principal, the spread is effectively the firm's revenue and the customer's implicit cost.

**FINRA 5% Policy.** As a guideline, FINRA expects markups, markdowns, and commissions to be **fair and reasonable**. The "5%" is a **guideline, not a hard rule** — the reasonableness of a charge depends on the security's price, availability, the amount of work involved, and other factors. A charge above 5% can be fair, and one below 5% can be excessive.

**Worked markup example.** A dealer buys a security into its inventory at 20.00 and shortly after sells it to a customer as principal at 21.00. Because the dealer's contemporaneous cost (20.00) establishes the prevailing market price, the markup is:

Markup % = (Customer Price − Prevailing Market Price) / Prevailing Market Price × 100
Markup % = (21.00 − 20.00) / 20.00 × 100 = 5.0%

A markup at 5% sits right at the FINRA guideline and would draw scrutiny; whether it is actually fair still depends on the security and the circumstances.

**Common trap:** A markup is **not** a commission. If the firm acts as **principal**, it charges a markup/markdown (buried in the price); if it acts as **agent**, it charges a commission (shown separately). The exam often gives you a fact pattern (from inventory vs. matching a buyer and seller) and asks which form of compensation applies.

#### Market Participants: Introducing BDs, Clearing BDs/Custodians, Market Makers, and Exchanges

An order passes through several firms before it settles. Know each role and, crucially, **which firm holds the customer's cash and securities**.

- **Introducing broker-dealer.** The firm with the **customer relationship**. It opens accounts, takes orders, and makes recommendations, but it typically **does not hold customer funds or securities** — it "introduces" the business to a clearing firm. Introducing firms operate this way to avoid the capital and operational burden of custody.
- **Clearing broker-dealer / custodian.** The firm that **carries the account**: it holds (has custody of) the customer's cash and securities, executes the clearance and **settlement** of trades, sends confirmations and statements, and handles margin lending. In advisory relationships, a **qualified custodian** (often a clearing broker-dealer or a bank) is the entity that physically holds client assets — a critical concept tied to the custody rule.
- **Market maker.** A **dealer** that stands ready to buy and sell a particular security by continuously publishing a **two-sided quote** (bid and ask). Market makers provide **liquidity** and trade as **principal**, earning the **spread** (plus any markup/markdown). Nasdaq is a **dealer/quote-driven** market built around competing market makers.
- **Exchange.** A centralized, regulated **marketplace** that brings buyers and sellers together (e.g., the NYSE, an auction/order-driven market). Exchanges provide price transparency, standardized rules, and a mechanism for continuous trading.

**Exam tip:** If a question hinges on "who holds the customer's securities and settles the trade," the answer is the **clearing/carrying firm (custodian)**, not the introducing firm. This distinction also matters for the **custody rule** under state and federal adviser regulation: an adviser that uses an independent **qualified custodian** to hold client assets faces lighter requirements than one deemed to have custody itself.

#### Payment for Order Flow

**Payment for order flow (PFOF)** is compensation a broker-dealer receives for **routing its customers' orders to a particular market maker or trading venue** for execution. The market maker pays the broker for the order because it profits from executing the flow (chiefly from the spread).

Why the exam cares:

- **Conflict of interest.** PFOF creates an incentive to route orders to whoever pays the broker the most, rather than to the venue offering the **best execution** for the customer. The two can conflict.
- **Disclosure requirement.** PFOF must be **disclosed** to customers, and firms must periodically disclose their order-routing practices. Receiving PFOF does **not** relieve the firm of its **best-execution** obligation.

**Common trap:** PFOF is not inherently prohibited, and it does not by itself mean the customer got a bad price. The tested point is that it is a **disclosed conflict of interest** that must be managed so it does not compromise best execution.

#### Best Execution

**Best execution** is the obligation to seek the **most favorable terms reasonably available** for a customer's order. It is a duty owed both by broker-dealers executing orders and, as a component of the **fiduciary duty**, by investment advisers who direct client trades.

"Best execution" is broader than simply the lowest quoted price. Firms must regularly and rigorously evaluate factors such as:

- the **price** obtained (net of costs),
- the **speed** and likelihood of execution and settlement,
- the **size** of the order and available liquidity,
- **transaction costs** (commissions, markups, fees), and
- the **overall value** delivered to the client.

For an **investment adviser**, best execution is inseparable from the **duty of loyalty and care**. When an adviser has **discretion** to select broker-dealers, it must periodically assess the quality of executions and cannot let its own interests — for example, **soft-dollar** arrangements or directed-brokerage benefits — override the client's interest in favorable execution. The adviser must be able to **document** that its broker-selection process seeks best execution over time.

**Exam tip:** Best execution ≠ lowest commission. An adviser that routes trades to a broker offering research (soft dollars) or that receives PFOF still owes best execution and must show that the *total* result serves the client's interest. Failing to periodically review execution quality is a compliance and fiduciary breach.

**Common trap:** Best execution is an ongoing, **process-based** obligation evaluated across many trades — not a guarantee that every single order got the single best possible price. The exam rewards recognizing it as a **fiduciary/regulatory duty** rooted in acting in the client's best interest.

### K. Portfolio Performance Measures

Once an adviser builds a portfolio, both the adviser and the client need a way to answer a simple-sounding question: "How did we do?" The Series 65 expects you to know that there is no single answer — a return can be measured many ways, and each is designed to isolate a different thing: the security's income, the manager's skill, the investor's actual dollar experience, the effect of taxes, the effect of inflation, or the amount of risk taken to earn the return. This subsection walks through each measure, gives the formulas you must know cold, and flags the comparisons the exam loves to test.

Keep one distinction in mind throughout: a **return** describes what actually happened over a period, while a **yield** describes an ongoing income rate at a point in time. The exam frequently pairs a return figure with a "which is larger?" or "which measure is appropriate here?" question rather than asking for a raw calculation.

#### Total Return and Holding-Period Return (HPR)

**Total return** captures *everything* an investment earned: income (dividends or interest) plus capital appreciation (or minus depreciation). It answers "what was my total gain relative to what I put in?"

Total Return = (Income + (Ending Value − Beginning Value)) / Beginning Value

The **holding-period return (HPR)** applies that same idea over the entire time the investor actually held the position — whether one month, one year, or five years. The formula is identical:

Holding-Period Return = (Income Received + (Ending Value − Beginning Value)) / Beginning Value

**Worked example.** An investor buys a stock for $50, holds it for one year, collects a $2 dividend, and sells it for $55.

- Income = $2
- Capital gain = $55 − $50 = $5
- HPR = ($2 + $5) / $50 = $7 / $50 = **14%**

HPR blends income and price change into one number. Had the same stock paid no dividend and simply risen to $55, the return would be only $5 / $50 = 10% — the $2 dividend is what lifts the total to 14%.

**Exam tip:** When a question gives you a purchase price, a sale price, *and* a dividend or coupon, it is almost always testing total/holding-period return — you must include the income, not just the price change. Candidates who compute only the capital gain get the trap answer.

**Common trap:** HPR is *not* annualized. A 14% holding-period return earned over three years is very different from 14% per year. To compare investments held for different lengths of time, you must annualize (next topic).

#### Current Yield

**Current yield** is not a total return — it measures only the income a security throws off *right now* relative to its current market price. It ignores capital gains, capital losses, and a bond's pull to par at maturity.

Current Yield = Annual Income / Current Market Price

For a bond, "annual income" is the annual coupon in dollars; for a stock, it is the annual dividend.

**Worked example (bond).** A bond has a 6% coupon (paying $60 per year on its $1,000 par value) and currently trades at $900.

Current Yield = $60 / $900 = **6.67%**

**Worked example (stock).** A stock pays a $2.40 annual dividend and trades at $60.

Current Yield = $2.40 / $60 = **4.0%**

The bond example reveals a relationship the exam tests constantly. Because the bond trades at a *discount* ($900 < $1,000 par), its current yield (6.67%) is higher than its coupon rate (6%). The full ranking for a discount bond is:

**Nominal (coupon) yield < Current yield < Yield to maturity (YTM) < Yield to call (YTC)**

For a *premium* bond (price above par), the ranking reverses:

**Nominal (coupon) yield > Current yield > Yield to maturity > Yield to call**

**Exam tip:** Memorize these two "yield ladders." A common question shows a premium bond and asks you to rank the four yields — for a premium bond the coupon is always highest and YTC lowest, because calling a premium bond forces the premium to be recovered faster and returns less than holding to maturity.

**Common trap:** Current yield uses *current market price*, never par value and never original cost. Dividing the coupon by $1,000 par just gives you the coupon rate back, not the current yield.

#### Annualized Return

An **annualized return** restates a return as an equivalent per-year rate so that investments held for different periods can be compared on equal footing. On a compounded basis:

Annualized Return = (1 + Holding-Period Return)^(1 / n) − 1, where n = number of years

**Worked example.** The 14% holding-period return above was earned over exactly one year, so it is already annualized at 14%. But suppose a different position produced a 33.1% total return over three years:

Annualized Return = (1 + 0.331)^(1/3) − 1 = (1.331)^(0.333) − 1 = 1.10 − 1 = **10% per year**

For sub-annual periods you scale up: a 3% return over six months compounds to (1.03)^2 − 1 = 6.09% annualized (or roughly 6% using simple annualization).

**Exam tip:** The point tested is usually conceptual — you cannot directly compare a 20% return earned over four years with a 12% return earned over one year until both are put on an annual basis. Annualizing makes the comparison apples-to-apples.

#### Time-Weighted vs. Dollar-Weighted Return

This is one of the most heavily tested distinctions in the entire performance section. Both measure return, but they answer different questions.

- **Time-weighted return (TWR)** measures the performance of the *portfolio/manager* by removing the distorting effect of client cash flows (deposits and withdrawals). It breaks the total period into sub-periods at each cash flow, computes each sub-period return, and links them geometrically: TWR = (1 + r1) × (1 + r2) × ... − 1. Because a manager does not control *when* a client adds or pulls money, TWR is the fair way to judge manager skill. This is why the GIPS standards and mutual fund performance reporting use time-weighted returns.

- **Dollar-weighted return (DWR)** measures the *investor's actual experience* in dollar terms. It is the internal rate of return (IRR) of the portfolio's cash flows, so it is sensitive to *how much* was invested and *when*. If a client adds a large sum right before a downturn, the DWR is dragged down even if the manager performed well.

**Worked example illustrating the difference.**

- Year 1: The account starts at $10,000 and grows to $12,000 (a +20% year).
- End of Year 1: The client contributes an additional $12,000, bringing the balance to $24,000.
- Year 2: The market falls and the account declines to $21,600 (a −10% year, since $24,000 × 0.90 = $21,600).

*Time-weighted return* links the two annual returns: (1.20) × (0.90) − 1 = 1.08 − 1 = **+8% cumulative** (about +3.9% annualized). The manager earned a positive result.

*Dollar-weighted return* solves for the single rate that equates all cash flows (−$10,000 at the start, −$12,000 at the end of Year 1, +$21,600 at the end): the IRR works out to roughly **−1.3% per year**. The client's *dollar* result was negative because most of the money was invested right before the losing year.

| Feature | Time-Weighted Return | Dollar-Weighted Return |
|---|---|---|
| What it measures | Manager/portfolio performance | Investor's actual dollar experience |
| Effect of cash-flow timing | Removed (neutralized) | Included (drives the result) |
| Equivalent to | Geometric linking of sub-period returns | Internal rate of return (IRR) |
| Best used to | Compare managers / funds | Judge whether *this investor* did well |
| Required by GIPS / fund reporting | Yes | No |

**Exam tip:** Remember the mnemonic — **Time-weighted = Talent** (measures the manager) and **Dollar-weighted = Dollars/Decisions** (measures the investor's contribution timing). If a question asks which method fairly evaluates a portfolio manager whose clients make large, irregular deposits, the answer is time-weighted.

**Common trap:** Dollar-weighted return and IRR are the *same thing*; do not treat them as two separate measures on the exam.

#### Internal Rate of Return (IRR)

The **internal rate of return** is the discount rate that makes the net present value (NPV) of all of an investment's cash flows equal to zero — the single interest rate at which the present value of everything you put in equals the present value of everything you take out. As noted above, IRR *is* the dollar-weighted return.

**Worked example.** An investor puts in $1,000 today and receives $1,210 two years later with no interim cash flows.

$1,000 × (1 + r)^2 = $1,210
(1 + r)^2 = 1.21
1 + r = 1.10
r = **10% (the IRR)**

Two applications the exam expects:

- **A bond's yield to maturity (YTM) is its IRR** — the single rate that discounts all future coupons and the par repayment back to the bond's current price. YTM assumes coupons are reinvested at the YTM, an assumption that rarely holds perfectly (a commonly tested limitation).
- **IRR assumes reinvestment of all interim cash flows at the IRR itself.** This reinvestment assumption is the classic criticism of IRR on the exam.

**Exam tip:** If a question describes "the rate that sets the present value of cash inflows equal to the present value of cash outflows," it is defining IRR — and, by extension, the dollar-weighted return.

#### Expected Return

**Expected return** is a forward-looking, probability-weighted average of an investment's possible outcomes. It is the number you *anticipate* earning, not a historical result.

Expected Return = Sum of (Probability of each outcome × Return in that outcome)

**Worked example.** An analyst estimates three scenarios for a stock:

| Scenario | Probability | Return |
|---|---|---|
| Strong economy | 30% | +20% |
| Normal economy | 40% | +10% |
| Weak economy | 30% | −5% |

Expected Return = (0.30 × 20%) + (0.40 × 10%) + (0.30 × −5%) = 6% + 4% − 1.5% = **8.5%**

For a *portfolio*, the expected return is the weighted average of the expected returns of its holdings, weighted by each holding's percentage of the portfolio.

**Exam tip:** Expected return is an input to risk models like CAPM and is contrasted with *actual* (realized) return. The difference between an investment's actual return and the return CAPM said to expect (given its beta) is its **alpha** (see below).

#### Risk-Adjusted Return

Two portfolios can post the same 10% return, but if one took far more risk to get there, its *risk-adjusted* return is inferior. Risk-adjusted measures relate return to the amount of risk taken. Three appear on the Series 65:

| Measure | Risk used | Formula (conceptual) | Reward per unit of… |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Sharpe ratio** | Total risk (standard deviation) | (Portfolio Return − Risk-Free Rate) / Standard Deviation | Total volatility |
| **Treynor ratio** | Systematic risk (beta) | (Portfolio Return − Risk-Free Rate) / Beta | Market risk |
| **Alpha (Jensen's)** | Systematic risk (beta) | Actual Return − CAPM Expected Return | Skill above/below the market |

The "excess return" numerator in Sharpe and Treynor is the return *above the risk-free rate* (commonly proxied by the 90-day Treasury bill).

**Worked example (Sharpe ratio).** A portfolio returns 10%, the risk-free rate is 2%, and the portfolio's standard deviation is 12%.

Sharpe Ratio = (10% − 2%) / 12% = 8 / 12 = **0.67**

A higher Sharpe ratio is better — more return earned per unit of total risk. Sharpe is the most commonly tested of the three.

**Alpha** measures value added by the manager: a *positive* alpha means the portfolio beat the return its risk level (beta) predicted, and a *negative* alpha means it underperformed for the risk taken. Alpha is often described as the manager's contribution or "skill."

**Exam tip:** Know the risk each ratio uses. **Sharpe uses standard deviation (total risk); Treynor and alpha use beta (systematic risk).** Use Sharpe when a portfolio is *not* well diversified (total risk matters); use Treynor/alpha when the portfolio is well diversified (only market risk remains).

**Common trap:** Do not confuse standard deviation with beta. Standard deviation measures *total* variability of returns; beta measures only *systematic* (market-related) risk relative to a benchmark.

#### Inflation-Adjusted (Real) Return

The **real return** strips out inflation to reveal the true increase in purchasing power. A 6% return when prices are rising 4% leaves an investor only slightly better off in what they can actually buy.

Approximate Real Return = Nominal Return − Inflation Rate

The exact (Fisher) formula compounds them:

Real Return = ((1 + Nominal Return) / (1 + Inflation Rate)) − 1

**Worked example.** A portfolio earns a nominal 8% while inflation runs 3%.

- Approximate: 8% − 3% = **5%**
- Exact: (1.08 / 1.03) − 1 = 1.0485 − 1 = **4.85%**

For exam purposes, the approximation (subtract inflation) is usually sufficient unless the question specifically asks for the precise figure.

**Exam tip:** Real return is central to retirement and purchasing-power discussions. Fixed-income investors face **purchasing-power (inflation) risk** because a fixed coupon buys less over time — a key reason TIPS and equities are recommended for long-horizon, inflation-sensitive goals.

#### After-Tax Return

The **after-tax return** is what the investor actually keeps once federal (and, where relevant, state) taxes are paid. It is what matters most to a taxable client.

After-Tax Return = Pretax Return × (1 − Tax Rate)

**Worked example.** An investment produces a 10% pretax return, and the applicable tax rate is 25%.

After-Tax Return = 10% × (1 − 0.25) = 10% × 0.75 = **7.5%**

A closely related, frequently tested calculation is the **tax-equivalent yield**, used to compare a tax-free municipal bond with a taxable bond. It grosses the muni yield *up* to the taxable yield a client would need in order to earn the same after-tax income:

Tax-Equivalent Yield = Tax-Free (Municipal) Yield / (1 − Tax Rate)

**Worked example.** A municipal bond yields 4% and the investor is in the 32% federal bracket.

Tax-Equivalent Yield = 4% / (1 − 0.32) = 4% / 0.68 = **5.88%**

So this investor should prefer the 4% muni over any comparable taxable bond yielding *less than* 5.88%.

**Exam tip:** The higher the investor's tax bracket, the more attractive tax-free municipal bonds become — the tax-equivalent yield rises with the bracket. Match the strategy to the client: tax-exempt bonds suit high-bracket clients in taxable accounts, and the *type* of return matters, because long-term capital gains and qualified dividends are taxed at lower rates than ordinary income and interest.

**Common trap:** Tax-deferred or tax-exempt vehicles change the analysis. Municipal bond interest, or gains inside a Roth IRA or 529 plan, are not reduced by the ordinary after-tax formula — check the account type before applying a tax haircut.

#### Relevant Benchmarks

A return means little in isolation; it must be compared against an appropriate **benchmark** — a passive index representing the same asset class, style, and risk as the portfolio. Beating a fair benchmark shows the manager added value.

| Benchmark | Represents | Notable feature |
|---|---|---|
| **S&P 500** | Large-cap U.S. equities | Market-cap weighted; broad large-cap gauge |
| **Dow Jones Industrial Average (DJIA)** | 30 large U.S. "blue-chip" stocks | Price-weighted (higher-priced stocks have more influence) |
| **NASDAQ Composite** | Tech-heavy / growth stocks | Market-cap weighted |
| **Russell 2000** | Small-cap U.S. equities | The standard small-cap benchmark |
| **MSCI EAFE** | Developed international equities (Europe, Australasia, Far East) | Excludes U.S. and Canada |
| **Bloomberg U.S. Aggregate Bond Index** | Investment-grade U.S. bonds | Standard broad fixed-income benchmark |

**Exam tip:** The benchmark must *match the portfolio's mandate*. Judging a small-cap growth fund against the large-cap S&P 500, or a bond fund against a stock index, is inappropriate and produces misleading conclusions. If a question offers an "inappropriate benchmark" answer choice, it usually involves a style or asset-class mismatch.

**Common trap:** The DJIA is **price-weighted**, not market-cap weighted — a distinction the exam likes to test. In a price-weighted index, a $300 stock moves the average more than a $30 stock regardless of company size, whereas the S&P 500 and most other indexes weight by market capitalization.

#### Putting It Together

| Measure | Answers the question… | Key formula / idea |
|---|---|---|
| Total / Holding-period return | What did I earn in total? | (Income + Price Change) / Beginning Value |
| Current yield | What income does it pay now? | Annual Income / Current Price |
| Annualized return | What is that per year? | (1 + HPR)^(1/n) − 1 |
| Time-weighted return | How good is the manager? | Geometric link of sub-period returns |
| Dollar-weighted return / IRR | How did *this investor* do? | Rate where NPV of cash flows = 0 |
| Expected return | What do we anticipate? | Sum of (Probability × Return) |
| Risk-adjusted (Sharpe/Treynor/Alpha) | Was the return worth the risk? | Excess return per unit of risk |
| Real (inflation-adjusted) return | Did purchasing power grow? | Nominal − Inflation |
| After-tax return | What did I keep? | Pretax × (1 − Tax Rate) |
| Benchmark comparison | Compared to what? | Match index to portfolio style |

**Exam tip:** Most performance questions are not brute calculations — they ask you to pick the *right measure for the situation* (manager evaluation → time-weighted; investor's dollar experience → dollar-weighted/IRR; concentrated vs. well-diversified → Sharpe vs. Treynor; high-bracket client → tax-equivalent yield) or to rank yields correctly for premium and discount bonds. Master the distinctions and the handful of formulas you can be asked to compute (HPR, current yield, Sharpe, tax-equivalent yield), and this subsection becomes a reliable source of points.

---

## Part IV — Laws, Regulations, and Guidelines Including Prohibition on Unethical Business Practices

*30% of the exam · approximately 39 questions.*

### A. Regulation of Investment Advisers (State-Registered and Federal Covered)

Investment adviser (IA) regulation sits at the center of the Series 65 and is the single most heavily tested cluster of material on the exam. To master it, keep two questions separate at all times: **(1) Is this person an investment adviser at all?** (the definition), and **(2) If so, who regulates them — the SEC or the states?** (the division of authority). This subsection walks through both, then covers how advisers register, stay registered, and supervise their people.

#### The Two Governing Statutes and the Division of Authority

Two laws work in tandem:

- The **Investment Advisers Act of 1940** — the federal statute. It defines an investment adviser at the federal level and governs advisers who register with the **SEC** ("federal covered" advisers).
- The **Uniform Securities Act (USA)** — the model state law (as interpreted through NASAA model rules) adopted in some form by each state. It governs advisers who register with a **state Administrator** ("state-registered" advisers).

The line between them was drawn by the **National Securities Markets Improvement Act of 1996 (NSMIA)**, which eliminated dual registration. For registration purposes, an adviser is regulated by the SEC **or** the state(s) — not both at once. States retain **antifraud authority** over everyone, but only the appropriate regulator handles registration.

**Exam tip:** When a question describes a person doing something with securities, run the definition first. Only after you have decided the person *is* an IA do you ask which regulator applies.

#### Defining an "Investment Adviser": The Three-Prong ABC Test

A person is an investment adviser only if **all three** prongs are met. Memorize them as **A-B-C**:

| Prong | Name | Meaning |
|---|---|---|
| **A** | **Advice** | Gives advice, issues reports, or renders analyses about **securities** (their value or the advisability of buying/selling them). |
| **B** | **Business** | Is **in the business** of doing so — holds itself out as an adviser, provides advice with regularity, or the advisory activity is a normal part of its operations. |
| **C** | **Compensation** | Receives **compensation** — any economic benefit — for the advice. |

Key nuances the exam loves:

- **A (Advice about securities specifically.)** Advice about fixed annuities, rare coins, commodities, real estate, or life insurance is **not** securities advice. But advice about the *asset allocation* of a securities portfolio, or telling a client to sell securities to buy something else, generally **is** securities advice.
- **B (Business.)** The advisory service does **not** have to be the person's *primary* business. Doing it regularly, or holding out as an adviser, satisfies this prong.
- **C (Compensation.)** Compensation need **not** come directly from the client and need **not** be a separate fee. It can be a commission, a 12b-1 fee, or any economic benefit from any source. **Common trap:** "The planner charged no fee for the plan." If the planner earns commissions on the products recommended, the compensation prong is still met.

**Exam tip:** If any **one** prong is missing, the person is not an IA. All three are required.

#### Exclusions From the Definition (the "LATE" Professionals and Others)

Certain persons are **excluded from the definition** even though they might seem to satisfy the ABC test. Under the Advisers Act, the main exclusions are:

- **Banks** and bank holding companies (not savings and loans).
- **L**awyers, **A**ccountants, **T**eachers, and **E**ngineers (**"LATE"**) whose advice is **solely incidental** to their profession and who receive no special compensation for it.
- **Broker-dealers** whose advice is **solely incidental** to their brokerage business and who receive **no special compensation** for the advice. **Common trap:** the moment a BD charges a **separate advisory fee** (special compensation) or offers wrap-fee/managed accounts, this exclusion is lost and it must register as an IA.
- **Publishers** of bona fide newspapers or magazines of **general and regular circulation** (not market letters timed to specific events or promotional in nature).
- Persons whose advice relates **only to U.S. government securities**.

Under state law, the definition also **excludes** investment adviser representatives, federal covered advisers, and certain other persons, since they are regulated elsewhere.

#### Exempt Reporting Advisers (ERAs)

An **Exempt Reporting Adviser** is an adviser that is **exempt from registration** with the SEC but is still required to **file reports** — specific items of **Form ADV Part 1A** through the IARD system. ERAs are "reporting, not registering." Two categories qualify:

1. The **Private Fund Adviser Exemption** — advisers who advise **solely private funds** and have **less than $150 million** in private-fund assets under management in the U.S.
2. The **Venture Capital Fund Adviser Exemption** — advisers who advise **solely one or more venture capital funds**, regardless of AUM.

ERAs remain subject to the **antifraud provisions** and to SEC examination even though they are not fully registered.

**Exam tip:** Watch the **$150 million** figure and the word "**solely**" ("solely private funds" or "solely venture capital funds"). Adding a single separate-account managed client can blow the exemption.

#### Federal Covered vs. State-Registered: The AUM Dividing Line

For a typical adviser, the split turns on **Regulatory Assets Under Management (RAUM)** — reported on Form ADV, calculated on a **gross** basis (leverage included) for securities portfolios that receive **continuous and regular** supervisory or management services. The commonly tested categories:

| Size category | RAUM | Where to register |
|---|---|---|
| **Small adviser** | Under **$25 million** | **State** (register with the state Administrator). |
| **Mid-sized adviser** | **$25M to $100M** | **State**, *unless* an exception forces the SEC (see below). |
| **Buffer zone** | **$100M to $110M** | Adviser's **choice** — may register with SEC or remain state-registered. |
| **Large adviser** | **$110 million or more** | **SEC** (federal covered) — **mandatory**. |

**Mid-sized adviser rule.** A mid-sized adviser ($25M–$100M) generally must register with the **states** and is **prohibited** from SEC registration **unless**:

- It is **not required to register** in the state where it maintains its **principal office** (e.g., that state has no IA registration requirement), **or**
- Its home state does **not subject the adviser to examination** (New York is the classic tested example — it does not examine mid-sized advisers, so a mid-sized adviser headquartered in NY must register with the **SEC**), **or**
- It would otherwise be required to register in **15 or more states** (the multi-state exemption), which makes it **eligible** for SEC registration.

**The 90/100/110 buffers.** To spare advisers from flipping registration back and forth over small market swings:

- A mid-sized adviser becomes **eligible** for the SEC at **$100 million** and is **required** at **$110 million**. Between **$100M and $110M**, registration is optional.
- Once SEC-registered, an adviser need not withdraw and move to the states until its RAUM drops **below $90 million**.

**Worked example.** An adviser reports RAUM of $103 million on its annual updating amendment.
- Rule: If RAUM ≥ $110M → SEC required; if $100M ≤ RAUM < $110M → adviser's choice; if RAUM < $90M (once SEC-registered) → withdraw to states.
- $103M falls in the **$100M–$110M buffer**, so the adviser **may** choose to register with the SEC or remain state-registered. Neither is forced.

**Other federal covered advisers (outside the standard RAUM thresholds).** Some advisers qualify for or must use SEC registration regardless of their own RAUM:

- Advisers to a **registered investment company** (a mutual fund or other RIC) — SEC registration is **mandatory** regardless of size.
- **Pension consultants** whose advisory services cover employee benefit plan assets of at least **$200 million** (eligible/optional). Note this test looks to the **plan assets advised**, not the adviser's own RAUM.
- **Internet investment advisers** that provide advice exclusively through an interactive website.
- Advisers **eligible for the multi-state (15+ states) exemption** (optional).
- **Newly formed** advisers that reasonably expect to be eligible for SEC registration within **120 days** of registering.

**Common trap:** "An adviser manages $40 million but advises a mutual fund." Do not be fooled by the small AUM — advising a **registered investment company** makes it **federal covered**.

#### Notice Filing for Federal Covered Advisers

A federal covered adviser does **not register** with the states, but a state may still require it to make a **notice filing**. Notice filing means the adviser:

- Files **copies of the documents it filed with the SEC** (i.e., its Form ADV),
- Pays the **state filing fees**, and
- Files a **consent to service of process**.

Notice filing lets states collect revenue and monitor advisers doing business there **without** imposing substantive registration requirements (which NSMIA preempts). The state **retains antifraud jurisdiction** and can still bring enforcement actions.

**Exam tip:** Federal covered advisers **notice file**; state-covered advisers **register**. If a question asks what a state can require of a federal covered adviser, the answer is a **notice filing (documents + fees + consent to service of process)** — not full registration.

#### Registration and Form ADV (Parts 1, 2, and 3)

Registration for both SEC and state advisers is filed electronically through the **IARD** (Investment Adviser Registration Depository). The vehicle is **Form ADV**, which has three parts:

| Form ADV part | What it is | Contains |
|---|---|---|
| **Part 1** | Administrative/"check-the-box" information about the firm | Business form, ownership, AUM, types of clients, disciplinary history, custody. Part **1A** = SEC; Part **1B** = additional state-required items. |
| **Part 2A** | The **firm brochure** (plain-English narrative) | Services, **fee schedule**, types of clients, methods of analysis, **conflicts of interest**, disciplinary disclosures. |
| **Part 2B** | The **brochure supplement** | Information about the **specific IARs** who serve the client — education, business experience, disciplinary history. |
| **Part 3** | **Form CRS** (Client Relationship Summary) | A short (max **2 pages** for a standalone adviser) relationship summary for **retail investors**; required of SEC-registered advisers. |

**State registration effectiveness.** Under the Uniform Securities Act, a complete application becomes **effective at noon on the 30th day** after filing, absent a pending proceeding or a stop order.

#### Post-Registration Requirements

**Brochure delivery (the "two-day free look").** The adviser must deliver the **brochure (Part 2A) and supplement (Part 2B)**:

- **State/NASAA rule:** Deliver **at least 48 hours before** entering into the advisory contract, **OR** at the time of entering the contract **if** the client is given a **5-business-day right to rescind** the contract without penalty. **Exam tip:** remember it as "**48 hours in advance, or a 5-day free look.**"
- **Annual delivery:** Within **120 days** of the end of the adviser's fiscal year, deliver either the updated brochure **or** a **summary of material changes** with an **offer** to provide the full brochure, **free of charge**.

**Form ADV amendments.** File an **annual updating amendment within 90 days** of the fiscal year-end, and file **prompt** amendments whenever the information becomes **materially inaccurate**.

**Books and records.** Both SEC (Rule 204-2) and the NASAA model require records be kept for **5 years**, with the most recent **2 years** in an **easily accessible** place (typically the principal office). Corporate/organizational documents (charter, articles, minute books) are kept for the **life of the firm** plus a period after dissolution. SEC-registered advisers follow SEC record rules, and states **cannot impose stricter** recordkeeping on them.

**Registration maintenance.** State registration is effective for **one year, expiring December 31**, and is **renewed annually** with a fee; the adviser must keep all filings current through amendments.

#### Financial Requirements and Commonly Tested State Thresholds

State-registered advisers face **net worth**, **bonding**, and **disclosure** thresholds that appear repeatedly on the exam:

| Situation | Minimum net worth (state) | Notes |
|---|---|---|
| Adviser has **custody** of client funds/securities | **$35,000** | Also triggers qualified custodian, account statements, and an annual **surprise exam** by an independent accountant. |
| Adviser has **discretionary authority** but **no custody** | **$10,000** | Discretion = adviser decides securities/amount/timing without prior client approval. |
| Neither custody nor discretion | No net-worth minimum (may need a bond) | — |

- **Net worth deficiency:** If net worth falls below the minimum, the adviser must **notify the Administrator by the close of the next business day** and then file a **financial report** (typically by the close of business the following day).
- **Bonding:** A state may require a **surety bond** instead of (or in addition to) the net worth requirement; the adviser may deposit **cash or securities** in lieu of the bond.

**Substantial prepayment of fees (balance sheet trigger).**

- **State rule:** If an adviser collects **more than $500 in prepaid fees, 6 months or more in advance**, it must include an **audited balance sheet** in its brochure.
- **Federal (SEC) rule:** The threshold is **more than $1,200, 6 months or more in advance**.

**Worked example.** A state-registered adviser bills a client **$900 up front covering the next 12 months**. Is a balance sheet required? The prepayment ($900) is **more than $500** and covers a period of **6 months or more** (12 months). **Yes** — the adviser must include an **audited balance sheet** in Part 2A.

**Common trap:** Do not confuse the **$500 (state)** and **$1,200 (federal)** prepayment thresholds — the exam frequently swaps them.

#### IAR Continuing Education

An **investment adviser representative (IAR)** is an **individual** associated with an IA who makes recommendations or gives advice, manages accounts, solicits or sells advisory services, or **supervises** those who do. IARs register at the **state** level where they have a **place of business** — this is true even for IARs of a **federal covered** adviser.

Under the **NASAA Model Rule on IAR Continuing Education** (states began adopting it effective January 1, 2022), a registered IAR must complete **12 CE credits per year**, split as:

- **6 credits — Products and Practice**, and
- **6 credits — Ethics and Professional Responsibility**.

**Worked example.** 6 + 6 = **12 total credits annually**. An IAR who completes 6 Ethics credits but only 4 Products-and-Practice credits has **10 of 12** and is deficient.

Failing to complete CE renders the IAR's status **"CE inactive"** — the IAR **cannot renew** the registration until the credits are made up, though this does not by itself revoke the registration. **Exam tip:** CE is a **renewal** requirement; it does **not** waive the qualification exam, and it applies only in states that have **adopted** the model rule.

#### IAR Supervision

An investment adviser must **establish, maintain, and enforce written supervisory procedures** reasonably designed to prevent and detect violations of the securities laws by the firm and its IARs. Core points tested:

- The firm must designate **supervisors** with responsibility over the activities of IARs and other associated persons.
- **Failure to supervise** is itself a prohibited business practice and a basis for **disciplinary action** against the firm, even where the underlying violation was committed by an employee.
- Reasonable supervision includes review of recommendations, correspondence, advertising, and handling of client funds, plus follow-up on **red flags**.

**Exam tip:** The firm can be held liable for an IAR's misconduct if it **failed to supervise**, but it has a defense if it had **reasonable procedures in place and was following them** — supervision must be both written **and** actually enforced.

#### Quick-Reference: Commonly Tested Thresholds

| Item | Threshold |
|---|---|
| Small adviser (state) | Under **$25M** AUM |
| Mid-sized adviser (state, with exceptions) | **$25M–$100M** AUM |
| SEC registration optional (buffer) | **$100M–$110M** AUM |
| SEC registration mandatory | **$110M+** AUM |
| Withdraw from SEC back to states | RAUM below **$90M** |
| Exempt Reporting Adviser (private fund) | Under **$150M** in private-fund AUM |
| Multi-state SEC eligibility | Required to register in **15+ states** |
| Pension consultant SEC eligibility | **$200M+** in plan assets advised |
| Net worth — custody (state) | **$35,000** |
| Net worth — discretion, no custody (state) | **$10,000** |
| Substantial prepayment balance sheet — state | Over **$500**, 6+ months in advance |
| Substantial prepayment balance sheet — federal | Over **$1,200**, 6+ months in advance |
| Brochure delivery | **48 hours** before contract, or with a **5-day** rescission right |
| Annual brochure delivery | Within **120 days** of fiscal year-end |
| Form ADV annual updating amendment | Within **90 days** of fiscal year-end |
| Books and records retention | **5 years** (first **2** easily accessible) |
| State registration effectiveness | Noon on the **30th day** after filing |
| IAR continuing education | **12 credits/year** (6 Products & Practice + 6 Ethics) |

### B. Regulation of Investment Adviser Representatives

An investment adviser (IA) is a **firm** — a legal entity. But the actual advice is delivered by **people**: the humans who sit across from clients, manage portfolios, and pitch the firm's services. Those individuals are **investment adviser representatives (IARs)**, and they are regulated separately from the firm that employs them. This subsection covers who is an IAR, when registration is required, who does the registering, and what an IAR must do to stay in good standing after registration.

**Exam tip:** Keep three roles cleanly separated in your mind — the **IA** (the firm/entity), the **IAR** (the individual giving advice), and the **BD agent** (an individual on the brokerage side). The exam constantly tests whether you can tell which rules attach to the entity versus the individual.

#### What Is an Investment Adviser Representative?

An IAR is any **individual (a natural person)** who is a partner, officer, or director of — or is otherwise employed by or associated with — an investment adviser (or a federal covered adviser) and who does **one or more** of the following:

- **Makes recommendations or renders advice** regarding securities;
- **Manages the accounts or portfolios** of clients;
- **Determines** which securities recommendation or advice should be given;
- **Solicits, offers, or negotiates for the sale of, or sells, investment advisory services**; or
- **Supervises** employees who perform any of the above.

Two words in that definition are load-bearing. First, **individual** — an IAR is always a natural person; a firm can never be an IAR (a firm is an IA). Second, the fifth bullet — **supervisors of advisory personnel are themselves IARs**, even if they never personally speak to a client. A branch manager who oversees ten advice-givers must register as an IAR.

Notice that **soliciting or selling advisory services** counts. This means a salesperson who never actually gives investment advice — someone whose entire job is to bring in advisory clients — is still an IAR and must register.

**Common trap:** Students assume "IAR" only means someone who picks stocks. It also includes the person who *sells the advisory relationship* and the person who *supervises* the advice-givers. Both must register.

#### Activities That Trigger IAR Registration

Because the five functions above define the role, doing **any one of them** on behalf of an adviser is the activity that triggers registration. The exam likes fact patterns where you must decide whether a given employee is "in" or "out":

| Employee's activity | IAR? |
|---|---|
| Meets clients and recommends a portfolio allocation | Yes |
| Cold-calls prospects to sell the firm's wrap-fee advisory program | Yes (solicits/sells advisory services) |
| Decides which model portfolio a client is placed in | Yes (determines the advice) |
| Manages a supervisory team of advice-givers | Yes (supervises IAR functions) |
| Answers the phone, schedules appointments, files paperwork | No (purely clerical/ministerial) |
| Processes trade confirmations and mails statements | No (ministerial) |

#### Exclusions from the Definition

Not everyone at an advisory firm is an IAR. The exclusions are the mirror image of the activities above.

**Clerical and ministerial personnel are excluded.** Receptionists, IT staff, back-office processors, and administrative assistants are not IARs because they render no advice and solicit no advisory business. If a person's function is purely secretarial or administrative, they do not register — no matter that they work at an advisory firm.

**Federal covered adviser personnel who fall below the federal thresholds.** For an individual who works for a **federal covered adviser** (an IA registered with the SEC), SEC Rule 203A-3 defines which employees the states may require to register as IARs. Under that rule, a *supervised person* of a federal covered adviser is an IAR (subject to state registration) **only if** the individual has a **place of business in the state** and:

- has **more than five** clients who are natural persons (excluding certain high-net-worth "qualified" clients); **and**
- **more than 10%** of the individual's clients are such natural persons.

Additionally, a supervised person is **not** an IAR if they either:

- do **not**, on a regular basis, solicit, meet with, or otherwise communicate with clients; **or**
- provide **only impersonal investment advice** (advice not tailored to individual clients — e.g., a general market newsletter or statistical/model output distributed to everyone).

**Exam tip:** The "more than 5 natural-person clients / more than 10%" screen is a **federal** test used to decide which employees of a *federal covered adviser* must register at the state level. Do not apply it to employees of a *state-registered* adviser — for a state adviser, the five-function definition (plus the clerical exclusion) governs.

#### Where an IAR Must Register: Registration Authority, Place of Business, and the De Minimis Exemption

**IARs register at the STATE level — always.** There is no such thing as SEC registration of an individual IAR. The **SEC registers firms** (advisers); the **state Administrator registers individuals** (IARs). This holds even when the IAR works for a federal covered adviser: the *firm* is SEC-registered, but its *representatives* still register with the states. The **registration authority for every IAR is the state Administrator.**

The trigger for *which* states an IAR registers in turns on **place of business** and, secondarily, on client counts. A **"place of business"** is any office where the IAR regularly provides advisory services or meets with clients, or any location held out to the public as such a place.

| The IAR works for a… | Must register in a state when… |
|---|---|
| **State-registered IA** | The IAR has a **place of business** in the state, **or** has clients in the state and does not qualify for the de minimis exemption |
| **Federal covered adviser** | The IAR has a **place of business** in the state — this is the **only** trigger (client count in that state is irrelevant under NSMIA) |

The key takeaway for federal covered adviser IARs: **no place of business in a state means no registration in that state, even if the IAR has many clients there.** The National Securities Markets Improvement Act (NSMIA) prohibits states from requiring registration of a federal covered adviser's representative absent a place of business in the state.

**The IAR de minimis exemption.** An IAR who has **no place of business** in a state is **not required to register** in that state if, during the preceding 12 months, the IAR has had **no more than five (5) clients** who are residents of that state. In counting terms:

- Clients in the state within 12 months = **5 or fewer**, **and** no place of business → **exempt** (no registration).
- A **6th** resident client (with no place of business) → registration is now required.

*Worked example.* An IAR maintains her only office in Illinois. Over the past 12 months she has picked up **4** advisory clients who reside in Indiana, where she has no office and never meets clients (all contact is by phone). Count = 4, which is 5 or fewer, and she has no place of business → she is **exempt** in Indiana. If her Indiana clientele later grows to **6** residents, she exceeds the de minimis of 5 and must register in Indiana.

**Common trap:** The de minimis counts **clients**, not solicitations or prospects, and the client cap only helps you when there is **no place of business** in the state. The moment the IAR opens or holds out an office in a state, she must register there even with a single client — the de minimis is off the table.

**Exam tip:** An IAR is **not** required to meet any **financial/net-worth or surety-bond** requirement. Minimum net worth, net capital, and bonding obligations attach to the **adviser (the firm)** — typically when it has custody or discretion — never to the individual IAR. If a question asks whether an IAR must post a bond, the answer is no.

#### Registration Process and Effectiveness

An IAR registers by filing **Form U4** (Uniform Application for Securities Industry Registration or Transfer) electronically through the CRD/IARD system, together with the applicable fee. (The firm, by contrast, files **Form ADV**; do not confuse the two.)

An IAR's registration **cannot stand alone** — it is effective only while the individual is **associated with a registered investment adviser or a federal covered adviser.** If the employing adviser's registration is suspended or revoked, the IAR cannot function. Absent a specified date or a request for more information, an application generally becomes effective at **noon on the 30th day** after a complete filing, consistent with the general registration-effectiveness rule of the Uniform Securities Act.

Because registration is tied to the employing firm, **moving between firms requires re-registration** — an IAR's registration does not automatically travel from one adviser to another.

**Termination and beginning of employment — who must notify.** This is a heavily tested distinction that hinges on whether the employer is state-registered or federal covered:

| IAR's employer | Who notifies the Administrator when the IAR begins/terminates? |
|---|---|
| **State-registered IA** | The **investment adviser (the firm)** notifies |
| **Federal covered adviser** | The **IAR (the individual)** notifies |
| *(Compare: BD agent)* | **Both** the agent **and** the broker-dealer notify |

The logic: for a federal covered adviser, the **firm is not registered with the state**, so the state can only look to the individual to report the change. Termination is documented on **Form U5**, filed promptly (generally within 30 days).

**Common trap:** For **BD agents**, both the agent *and* the broker-dealer must notify. For **IARs of a state adviser**, only the **firm** notifies; for **IARs of a federal covered adviser**, only the **individual** notifies. Match the notifier to the fact pattern.

#### Post-Registration Maintenance Requirements

Registration is not "set and forget." An IAR has three ongoing obligations: keep the uniform forms current, disclose reportable events, and complete continuing education.

**1. Updating uniform forms.** The IAR must keep **Form U4 accurate and current** by filing **amendments promptly** whenever the information becomes materially inaccurate — generally **within 30 days** of the IAR learning of the change. Changes that require an amendment include name and residential/office address, employment and outside business activities, and any of the disclosure (reportable) events below.

**2. Disclosure of reportable events.** Form U4 contains disclosure questions that must be updated as events occur. Commonly tested reportable events include:

- **Criminal** charges or convictions (felonies and certain misdemeanors, including those involving money, securities, or fraud);
- **Regulatory or self-regulatory actions** (denials, suspensions, revocations, fines, bars, orders);
- **Civil judicial** actions involving investment-related conduct;
- **Customer complaints, arbitrations, and settlements** meeting reporting thresholds;
- **Financial disclosures** — personal **bankruptcy** within the past 10 years, and unsatisfied **judgments or liens**;
- **Termination for cause** (being discharged after allegations of violating rules, fraud, or failure to supervise).

**Exam tip:** A disclosure event does not automatically bar or revoke registration, but **failing to update the U4 to disclose it is itself a violation** and grounds for administrative action. The obligation is affirmative and continuous, not something that waits for the annual renewal.

**3. Continuing education (CE).** Under the **NASAA Model Rule on Investment Adviser Representative Continuing Education**, a registered IAR must complete **12 CE credits every year**, allocated as:

CE credits per year = **6 credits "Ethics and Professional Responsibility" + 6 credits "Products and Practice" = 12 total**

- **6 Ethics and Professional Responsibility** credits (at least 3 of which must cover ethics), **and**
