- **6 Products and Practice** credits (covering securities products, investment strategies, and applicable regulatory requirements).

*Worked example.* An IAR completes 6 Products-and-Practice credits and only 4 Ethics-and-Professional-Responsibility credits in a given year: 6 + 4 = 10 credits, which is short of the required 12 and also short of the 6 required in the Ethics-and-Professional-Responsibility bucket. The IAR has **not** satisfied CE for the year, and her registration becomes **"CE inactive."** A CE-inactive IAR remains registered but **cannot renew** the registration until the deficiency is cured; if the shortfall persists, the individual will be unable to continue acting as an IAR once the annual renewal period closes.

An individual who holds **both** IAR and BD-agent registrations and who satisfies FINRA's continuing education for the agent side may be credited toward the corresponding NASAA IAR CE requirement, avoiding fully duplicative coursework.

**Common trap:** IAR CE is an **annual** requirement (12 credits every year), not a one-time or every-other-year obligation, and the credits are **split into two fixed buckets** — you cannot satisfy the requirement by loading up on one category. Also note that the consequence of noncompliance is a **CE-inactive status that blocks renewal**, not an immediate revocation.

**Renewal.** Like all registrations under the Uniform Securities Act, an IAR's registration **expires December 31** each year and must be renewed (with the renewal fee) to continue into the next calendar year — assuming CE and all disclosure obligations are current.

### C. Regulation of Broker-Dealers

Broker-dealers (BDs) are the firms that execute securities transactions — the "plumbing" through which investors buy and sell securities. Unlike investment advisers, where the National Securities Markets Improvement Act of 1996 (NSMIA) split regulatory authority between the SEC and the states, broker-dealers remain subject to **dual regulation**. A firm typically registers with the SEC, becomes a member of FINRA, *and* registers in each state where it does business — unless a specific exclusion applies. There is no "federal covered broker-dealer" that escapes state registration the way a federal covered adviser escapes state adviser registration. For the Series 65, this subsection tests four things precisely: what a BD *is*, who is *excluded*, *when* registration in a state is triggered, and the *snowbird* exemption for existing customers.

#### Definition of a Broker-Dealer Under the Uniform Securities Act

The Uniform Securities Act (USA) defines a **broker-dealer** as *any person engaged in the business of effecting transactions in securities for the account of others or for its own account.* Break the definition into its parts:

- **"Person"** — under the USA this is broad: individuals, corporations, partnerships, associations, trusts, and other legal entities. (In practice a BD is an entity/firm, while the individuals working for it are "agents.")
- **"Engaged in the business"** — the activity must be conducted as a business, i.e., with regularity and for compensation. A person who executes an occasional, isolated transaction for its own account is not "in the business" of effecting transactions.
- **"For the account of others"** — acting as an agent/broker (executing trades on behalf of clients for a commission).
- **"For its own account"** — acting as a principal/dealer (buying and selling from its own inventory, earning a markup or markdown).

Notice that the state term "broker-dealer" combines two roles that federal law (the Securities Exchange Act of 1934) defines separately — "broker" (agent) and "dealer" (principal). At the state level, both roles are captured under the single term **broker-dealer**.

**Exam tip:** The two elements the exam keys on are "engaged in the business" and "transactions in securities for the account of others or its own account." If a scenario describes someone regularly buying and selling securities for clients (or trading securities as a business from its own inventory), it is a broker-dealer unless an exclusion removes it.

#### Exclusions From the Definition of Broker-Dealer

Certain persons are carved out of the definition — meaning they are **not** broker-dealers and therefore do not register as BDs in the state. The USA excludes the following:

1. **An agent.** An agent is an *individual* who represents a broker-dealer or an issuer in effecting securities transactions. The salesperson is not the firm; the individual registers as an "agent," not as a "broker-dealer."
2. **An issuer.** A company selling its *own* securities is transacting in its own securities, not effecting transactions for others as a business.
3. **A bank, savings institution, or trust company.** These institutions are excluded from the *state* definition of broker-dealer. (Federal treatment of banks under the '34 Act is more nuanced, but the Series 65 tests the state exclusion.)
4. **A person with no place of business in the state who deals only with excluded (institutional) clients.** The firm has no place of business in the state and effects transactions exclusively with or through: the *issuers* of the securities involved, *other broker-dealers*, and *institutional clients* — banks, savings institutions, trust companies, insurance companies, investment companies, pension or profit-sharing trusts, and other financial institutions or institutional buyers.
5. **A person qualifying for the snowbird exemption** (existing out-of-state customers) — covered in detail below.

| Person / situation | Excluded from BD definition? | Why |
|---|---|---|
| Individual sales rep at a firm | Yes — this is an **agent** | Individuals are agents, not BDs |
| Company selling its own stock | Yes — this is an **issuer** | Transacting in its own securities |
| Bank / savings institution / trust company | Yes | Statutory institutional exclusion |
| Firm, no office in state, deals only with banks, insurers, investment companies, other BDs, issuers | Yes | No place of business + institutional-only clients |
| Firm with an office in the state | **No** — must register | Place of business triggers registration |

**Common trap:** The exclusions in items 4 and 5 both depend on the firm having **no place of business** in the state. If the firm maintains a place of business in the state, it must register as a broker-dealer there **even if it deals only with institutions or only with out-of-state customers.** Test writers love to place an office in the state and then describe an otherwise "exempt" client list — the office defeats the exclusion.

**Exam tip:** Memorize the three "pure" exclusions that apply regardless of place of business: **agent, issuer, and bank/savings institution/trust company.** The other two exclusions are conditional on having *no place of business* in the state.

#### When a Broker-Dealer Must Register in a State

A person that meets the definition of broker-dealer and does not qualify for an exclusion **must register in the state** before transacting business there. Registration is triggered by either of two things:

- **Having a place of business in the state.** A "place of business" is an office at which the firm conducts securities business or holds itself out as doing so. Any place of business in the state requires registration.
- **Doing business with (non-exempt) persons in the state.** Even with no in-state office, a firm that solicits or transacts securities business with *retail* (non-institutional, resident) customers in the state must register. The state has jurisdiction where an offer is made *in* the state, *from* the state, or *accepted* in the state.

Mechanically, a broker-dealer registers by filing **Form BD** through the **Central Registration Depository (CRD)** system operated by FINRA, which routes the filing to the SEC, FINRA, and the relevant states. Registration in a state generally becomes effective at **noon on the 30th day** after a complete application is filed, absent a stop order or a request for additional information. The Administrator may also require the BD to meet **minimum net capital** and post a **surety bond** (no bond may be required of a firm that meets the applicable net capital requirement), and may require a **qualification examination**.

**Common trap:** Because broker-dealers are dually regulated, there is **no NSMIA-style preemption** of state registration for BDs. A firm registered with the SEC and FINRA still must register in each state where it has a place of business or non-exempt customers. Do not confuse the "federal covered adviser" preemption — which removes *state* registration for large investment advisers — with broker-dealers; no such preemption exists for BDs.

#### The De Minimis / No-Place-of-Business "Snowbird" Exemption for Existing Customers

The most heavily tested exclusion is the **snowbird exemption**. It solves a practical problem: a customer who lives in one state but travels to another (classically, a retiree wintering in Florida — a "snowbird") should be able to keep dealing with their existing broker-dealer without forcing that firm to register in every state the customer happens to visit.

A broker-dealer is **excluded** from the definition — and thus need **not** register in the visited state — when **all** of the following are true:

- The firm has **no place of business in that state**; and
- Its only business in that state is with **existing customers** (an established relationship already exists); and
- Those customers are **not residents of the state** — their principal place of residence is in another state; they are only temporarily present.

(The firm is presumed to remain properly registered where it does maintain a place of business, typically the customer's home state.) Put simply: *no office in the state + existing customer + customer is a non-resident just passing through.* If those conditions hold, the firm can continue servicing that customer without registering in the state the customer is visiting.

Because no dollar amounts or offer counts change the outcome once these conditions are met, there is **no calculation** to perform here — the exemption is a *status test*, not a *threshold test*. The label "de minimis" simply reflects that the firm's contact with the state is minimal (existing, transient, out-of-state customers); unlike the numeric de minimis test for investment advisers, it imposes no numeric client limit.

**Common trap — new customers.** The exemption covers **existing** customers only. If the traveling snowbird refers a friend who lives in the visited state, or the firm otherwise **solicits a new customer** in that state, the exemption is lost and registration is required. The relationship must have pre-existed the customer's presence in the state.

**Common trap — the customer becomes a resident.** If the snowbird stops being a visitor and **establishes residency** (principal place of residence) in the new state, the customer is now a resident and the exemption no longer applies — the broker-dealer must register in that state to continue serving them.

**Exam tip:** Watch two facts in any snowbird question — (1) is there a **place of business** in the state, and (2) is the customer a **pre-existing, non-resident** customer? If the firm has any in-state office, or the customer is new or a resident, the exemption fails and registration is required.

| Snowbird scenario (firm has NO in-state office) | Registration required in the visited state? |
|---|---|
| Existing customer of the firm, resides out of state, vacationing in the state | No — snowbird exemption applies |
| New prospect solicited while in the state | Yes — not an existing customer |
| Existing customer who moves and becomes a resident of the state | Yes — customer is now a resident |
| Existing out-of-state customer, but firm opens an office in the state | Yes — place of business defeats the exemption |

### D. Regulation of Agents of Broker-Dealers

An "agent" is the human being on the front line of the brokerage business — the person who actually talks to customers and enters their buy and sell orders. The Uniform Securities Act (USA) defines who qualifies as an agent, dictates when that person must register with the state, and carves out the narrow situations in which an individual can effect securities transactions without registering. Because this material overlaps heavily with the Series 63/66 world, the Series 65 tests it through precise definitions and "gotcha" fact patterns rather than math. Master the exact wording and you will get every one of these points.

#### What Is an Agent? (The Statutory Definition)

Under the Uniform Securities Act, an **agent** means *any individual, other than a broker-dealer, who represents a broker-dealer or issuer in effecting or attempting to effect purchases or sales of securities.*

Break that definition into its load-bearing pieces:

- **"Any individual"** — An agent is *always a natural person*. A corporation, partnership, LLC, or any other entity can never be an agent. Firms register as broker-dealers; the people who work for them register as agents.
- **"Other than a broker-dealer"** — The statute deliberately separates the firm (broker-dealer) from the human (agent). The same actor is not both under a single transaction.
- **"Represents a broker-dealer or issuer"** — An agent works on behalf of either a brokerage firm or an issuer of securities. Which of these two the individual represents is the single most important fact for deciding whether registration is required (covered below).
- **"In effecting or attempting to effect purchases or sales of securities"** — The individual must be involved in the *securities transaction itself*: soliciting, taking, or executing orders. "Attempting" matters — merely trying to make a sale (cold-calling, soliciting) triggers agent status even if no trade ever closes.

**Exam tip:** If a question describes an entity (a firm, a bank, a corporation) and asks whether it is an "agent," the answer is almost always **no** — an agent is by definition an individual. The classic distractor is calling a broker-dealer firm an "agent."

**Common trap:** Partners, officers, and directors of a broker-dealer or issuer are **agents only if they otherwise meet the definition** — that is, only if they personally effect or attempt to effect securities transactions. A CFO who signs regulatory filings but never solicits or takes customer orders is not acting as an agent.

#### Agent vs. Broker-Dealer: A Critical Distinction

Candidates constantly blur these two terms. Keep them cleanly separated:

| Feature | Agent | Broker-Dealer |
|---|---|---|
| Legal nature | Always an **individual** (natural person) | A **"person"** — almost always a firm/entity (corporation, partnership, LLC); can be a sole proprietor |
| Function | *Represents* a BD or issuer in effecting trades | Is *in the business* of effecting securities trades for others' accounts (broker) or its own account (dealer) |
| Registration form | Form U4 | Form BD |
| Relationship | Must be associated with a registered BD (or issuer) to be registered | Registers on its own behalf; employs and supervises agents |
| Compensation clue | Transaction-based pay (commissions) for effecting trades | Earns commissions, markups, and markdowns as a firm |

The mental model: the **broker-dealer is the firm**, and its **agents are the licensed individuals** who transact with the public on the firm's behalf. A single BD may have thousands of agents; each agent generally represents one BD at a time (dual registration with a second BD is possible only with the consent of the firms and the Administrator).

**Exam tip:** Watch the word "person." In securities law a **person includes entities**, but an **agent is specifically an *individual***. That narrowing is intentional and heavily tested.

#### When Is Registration Required?

The governing rule from the USA is simple to state:

> It is unlawful for any individual to **transact business as an agent** in a state unless the individual is **registered** in that state or is **exempt/excluded** from the definition.

Practical consequences the exam tests:

- **Register in every state where you do business.** An agent must be registered in each state where they have customers or solicit business — determined by where the *client* is located and where the offer is *directed and received*, not merely where the agent sits. Soliciting a customer in a state where the agent is not registered is a violation even if the customer declines.
- **No registration link, no agent registration.** An agent's registration is *tethered* to their employing broker-dealer (or issuer). If the broker-dealer's registration is not effective — or is suspended or revoked — the agent's registration is **inoperative** for that period. An agent's registration is not effective during any time the agent is not associated with a registered broker-dealer or issuer.
- **Changing firms is a three-party notice event.** When an agent moves from one broker-dealer to another, **the agent, the former firm, and the new firm must all promptly notify the Administrator.** (Under the USA, whenever an agent begins or terminates a connection with a broker-dealer or issuer, the agent *and* the firm involved must notify the Administrator — so a move triggers notice by all three.)
- **Mechanics.** Registration is filed on **Form U4** through the CRD system, generally becomes effective at **noon on the 30th day** after a complete filing (absent a stop order or a request for additional information), and requires **consent to service of process** appointing the Administrator as attorney to receive legal papers. The Administrator may also require exams, minimum standards, and fees.

**Common trap:** A newly hired individual **may not solicit or accept orders before their registration is effective**. "The client called *me* first" does not cure an unregistered agent's status — the unsolicited-order exemption is an *exempt transaction* concept that relieves the *firm/issuer* from certain obligations, not a license for an unregistered person to transact business.

#### Exclusions: When an Individual Is NOT an Agent

The most heavily tested part of this subsection is the set of situations in which an individual effects securities transactions yet is **excluded from the definition of agent** and therefore **need not register**. Two ideas dominate.

**1. Purely clerical and ministerial employees.** An individual who performs only administrative, clerical, or back-office functions and who does **not** effect (or attempt to effect) securities transactions is not an agent — such a person simply never meets the definition. Answering phones, mailing statements, or processing paperwork is not "effecting transactions."

**2. Certain individuals who represent an *issuer*.** This is the heart of the topic and the source of most trick questions. An individual who represents an **issuer** is *excluded from the definition of agent* (no registration) when effecting transactions in the following categories:

- Transactions in the specific enumerated **exempt securities** that trigger the exclusion (see list below);
- **Exempt transactions** (see list below);
- Transactions in certain **federal covered securities**; and
- Transactions with the issuer's **own existing employees, partners, or directors** where **no commission or other remuneration is paid, directly or indirectly, for soliciting** any person in the state (e.g., an employee-stock-plan enrollment handled by an unpaid company officer).

The unifying principle: representing an *issuer* in one of these "clean" contexts is activity the states chose not to license.

##### The exclusion applies to ISSUER representation only — not broker-dealer representation

This is the point NASAA loves to test. The exempt-securities and exempt-transactions carve-outs **only** apply to an individual representing an **issuer**. There is **no** parallel carve-out for an individual representing a **broker-dealer**.

| The individual represents… | …in a transaction involving… | Agent? Register? |
|---|---|---|
| An **issuer** | A qualifying **exempt security** (e.g., U.S. Treasury bonds, muni bonds) | **No** — excluded, no registration |
| An **issuer** | An **exempt transaction** | **No** — excluded, no registration |
| An **issuer** | A **non-exempt** security in a **non-exempt** transaction | **Yes** — must register |
| A **broker-dealer** | An **exempt security** (e.g., U.S. Treasury bonds) | **Yes** — must register |
| A **broker-dealer** | **Anything** involving securities transactions | **Yes** — must register |

**Common trap:** "An individual sells **U.S. government securities** on behalf of a **broker-dealer**. Must the individual register as an agent?" The tempting wrong answer is "no, because government securities are exempt." The correct answer is **YES** — the exempt-security exclusion protects only *issuer* representatives. Because this person represents a *broker-dealer*, the exempt status of the product is irrelevant.

**Exam tip:** Flip the same facts and the answer flips. "An individual represents the **issuer** in selling that issuer's own **U.S. government securities**" → **not an agent**, no registration required.

##### Reference: Which exempt securities trigger the issuer-agent exclusion

The issuer-agent exclusion does not reach every security the USA exempts; it is tied to specific enumerated categories. An individual representing an **issuer** is not an agent when effecting transactions in:

- U.S. government and municipal (state/local) securities
- Securities of foreign national governments with which the U.S. maintains diplomatic relations
- Depository institution securities (banks, savings institutions, credit unions)
- Certain short-term commercial paper (top-tier rated, $50,000 or greater denomination, maturity of nine months or less)
- Investment contracts issued in connection with employee benefit (pension/profit-sharing) plans
- Certain federal covered securities (e.g., exchange-listed and investment-company securities)

**Note:** Other securities the USA generally exempts — for example, insurance company securities, regulated public utility and common carrier securities, and nonprofit (religious/educational/charitable) securities — do **not** by themselves extend the issuer-agent exclusion. And **variable annuities/variable life are not exempt at all** — they are securities requiring registration and a licensed agent.

##### Reference: Exempt transactions (issuer's representative is not an agent)

- **Isolated non-issuer transactions** (occasional, one-off resales)
- **Unsolicited non-issuer orders** (the customer initiates; the BD did not solicit)
- **Underwriter transactions** (between the issuer and an underwriter, or among underwriters)
- **Institutional transactions** — sales to banks, insurance companies, investment companies, other broker-dealers, pension plans, and similar financial institutions
- **Private placements / limited offerings** (offers to a limited number of non-institutional buyers, meeting the statutory conditions)
- **Fiduciary transactions** by an executor, administrator, sheriff, marshal, receiver, trustee in bankruptcy, guardian, or conservator
- **Transactions with existing security holders** (preemptive rights, warrants, conversions) where no commission is paid for soliciting

**Exam tip:** Notice the built-in shortcut — the biggest categories of *institutional* and *unsolicited* business are exempt *transactions*. That is why an individual dealing only with those situations on behalf of an issuer often escapes agent registration, while a retail-facing individual almost never does.

#### A Note on Snowbirds and No Place of Business

An agent's registration obligation follows the broker-dealer's. If the BD is **not required to register** in a state — for example, it has **no place of business** there and deals only with an **existing customer who is merely temporarily present** in the state (a client on vacation) — then an agent representing that BD there is likewise not transacting business in a way that requires new registration. The relationship pre-existed and the customer's presence is transient. Do not over-extend this: soliciting *new* residents of the state, or maintaining any office there, defeats it.

**Common trap:** This relief depends on the client being *temporarily* in the state. If the customer **moves** and becomes a resident of the new state, the agent (and the firm) must become registered there to continue doing business with that person.

#### Rapid-Fire Exam Summary

- An **agent is always an individual**; a broker-dealer is (almost always) a **firm**.
- "Agent" = an individual representing a **BD or issuer** in **effecting or attempting to effect** securities transactions.
- **Purely clerical** staff are **not** agents.
- The **exempt-security / exempt-transaction exclusions apply only to issuer representatives** — an individual representing a **BD** is an agent regardless of how exempt the product is.
- An individual selling an issuer's securities to the issuer's **own employees for no commission** is **not** an agent.
- Registration is required in **every state where the agent transacts business**, is filed on **Form U4**, and is **inoperative whenever the associated BD's registration is not in effect**.
- When an agent **changes firms**, the **agent, the former BD, and the new BD** all notify the Administrator.

### E. Regulation of Securities and Issuers

This subsection covers the "product and issuer" side of state securities law: what counts as a security in the first place, the three ways securities get registered at the state level, the long list of things that escape registration (either because they are exempt or because they were never securities at all), who an "issuer" is and when the people selling for an issuer must register, and finally the enforcement and antifraud powers the state Administrator holds over everyone. On the Series 65 this material is a heavy source of questions, and the traps are predictable once you understand the underlying logic.

#### 1. What Is a "Security"? — The Definition and the Howey Test

The Uniform Securities Act (USA) does not define a security with a single sentence; it gives a long **list**. If an instrument appears on that list, it is a security. The commonly tested items include:

- Stock, preferred stock, and treasury stock
- Bonds, debentures, notes, and other evidence of indebtedness
- Transferable shares and certificates of interest in a profit-sharing agreement
- **Investment contracts**
- Certificates of interest in oil, gas, or mining title or lease
- Warrants, rights, options on securities, and interests in a voting-trust certificate
- Certificates of deposit **for a security** (a receipt for a security, not a bank CD)

The catch-all is the **investment contract**, and that is where the **Howey test** comes in. In *SEC v. W.J. Howey Co.* (1946), the Supreme Court held that an investment contract — and therefore a security — exists when there is:

1. An **investment of money**,
2. In a **common enterprise**,
3. With an **expectation of profits**,
4. Resulting **solely (or primarily) from the efforts of others** — a promoter or a party other than the investor.

All four prongs must be met. The classic Howey facts: investors bought rows of orange trees and simultaneously signed a service contract letting Howey's company cultivate and sell the fruit and remit profits. The land sale alone was not a security, but bundled with the management contract it became an investment contract because the buyers expected profits from *someone else's* work.

**Exam tip:** The fourth prong is the decider. If the investor is expected to run the enterprise personally (e.g., a general partnership interest where every partner actively manages), it usually fails Howey and is not a security. A **limited** partnership interest, where limited partners are passive, generally *is* a security.

**What is NOT a security (exclusions from the definition).** These never become securities, so registration questions do not even arise:

- Insurance and fixed annuity contracts, whole life, term life, and endowment policies with a **guaranteed fixed benefit** (note: a **variable** annuity or variable life policy *is* a security because the investment risk is on the holder)
- Commodities themselves and commodity **futures** contracts
- Collectibles, precious metals, gold/silver bullion, real estate held for personal use, and condominiums (unless sold with a mandatory rental-pool/management arrangement, which can re-create a Howey investment contract)
- Retirement plans and accounts as vehicles — an IRA or Keogh is *not itself* a security (although it holds securities)

**Common trap:** A **fixed** annuity is not a security; a **variable** annuity is. A bank **CD** is not a security; a **certificate of deposit for a security** is. The exam swaps these constantly.

#### 2. State Registration Methods

Any security that is not exempt and is not a federal covered security must be **registered** in a state before it can be offered or sold there. The USA provides three methods. All three, once effective, are good for **one year**.

| Method | Who uses it | How it becomes effective | Key facts |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Notification / Filing** (Sec. 302) | Seasoned, established issuers with a track record (often already registered federally) | Automatically, on a short streamlined basis, absent a stop order | Fastest/least burdensome; reserved for issuers that meet operating-history and financial-condition tests |
| **Coordination** (Sec. 303) | Issuers registering with the **SEC** under the Securities Act of 1933 **at the same time** (typical IPO) | Automatically, at the **same moment** the federal registration becomes effective, provided the documents have been on file with the Administrator for the required minimum period | Most **common** method for interstate offerings; state and federal registrations "coordinate" |
| **Qualification** (Sec. 304) | Securities sold only within one state (**intrastate**) and not registered federally; anything that doesn't fit the other two | When the **Administrator so orders** — the Administrator has discretion over the effective date | Most **thorough/stringent**; requires the most disclosure |

**Registration by Coordination** is the workhorse for companies going public across state lines: the issuer files essentially the same registration statement/prospectus it filed with the SEC, and state effectiveness is pegged to federal effectiveness (so long as the statement has sat on file with the Administrator for the minimum period — commonly cited as 10 days).

**Registration by Qualification** is the only method available when there is no federal registration to coordinate with — the classic case is a purely **intrastate** offering. Because there is no SEC review to lean on, the Administrator demands the fullest disclosure and controls the effective date.

**Exam tip:** Remember the effective-date logic: coordination = effective *with the SEC*; qualification = effective *when the Administrator orders*. Any of the three methods can require the issuer to file a **consent to service of process** and to pay filing fees.

**Note on federal covered securities:** Under the National Securities Markets Improvement Act (NSMIA), states may **not** require registration of federal covered securities (e.g., investment company/mutual fund shares, and securities listed on the NYSE, Nasdaq, or an exchange of equal standing). States may still require a **notice filing** (documents plus a fee) and always retain **antifraud** authority.

#### 3. Exemptions From Registration

The USA relieves many offerings from registration. Distinguish two very different categories:

- **Exempt securities** — the *security itself* is exempt, no matter how many times or to whom it is sold. The exemption follows the security.
- **Exempt transactions** — a *particular transaction* is exempt because of how or with whom it occurs. The exemption follows the deal, not the paper.

In **both** cases, the exemption is only from **registration** (and from filing of advertising/sales literature). It is **never** an exemption from the **antifraud** provisions.

**Common exempt securities:**

- U.S. government and **municipal** securities (any state, city, or political subdivision)
- Securities of the Canadian government (and any Canadian province) and of a foreign national government the U.S. maintains diplomatic relations with
- Securities of **banks, savings institutions, and trust companies**
- Securities of **insurance companies** authorized to do business in the state
- Securities of federally regulated common carriers and **public utilities**
- Securities issued by nonprofit, religious, charitable, or fraternal organizations
- Securities of federal covered issuers, including investment companies registered under the Investment Company Act of 1940
- Securities issued in connection with an **employee benefit plan**
- **Commercial paper** (promissory notes) with a maturity of **9 months (270 days) or less**, in denominations of at least **$50,000**, rated in one of the **top three** rating categories

**Common exempt transactions** (these come up more often on the exam than exempt securities):

- **Isolated non-issuer transactions** — an occasional, one-off resale not by the issuer
- **Unsolicited non-issuer transactions** — a customer contacts the agent to buy or sell without solicitation. This is the **most common** exempt transaction; the Administrator may require the customer to acknowledge in writing that the order was unsolicited
- Transactions between an issuer and an **underwriter**, or among underwriters
- **Transactions with institutional/financial investors** — banks, insurance companies, investment companies, broker-dealers, pension/profit-sharing trusts, and other financial institutions
- **Fiduciary transactions** — sales by an executor, administrator, sheriff, marshal, receiver, trustee in bankruptcy, guardian, or conservator (note: a **custodian** or **ordinary trustee** is *not* on this list)
- **Private placements** — offers to **no more than 10 non-institutional (retail) persons** in a **12-month** period, where the seller reasonably believes buyers purchase **for investment** (not resale), and **no commission** is paid for soliciting retail buyers. Offers to institutional buyers are **unlimited** and do not count toward the 10
- **Preorganization certificates** — no more than **10 subscribers**, no commission paid, and no payment made by any subscriber
- Transactions with **existing security holders** (rights, warrants, conversions), if no commission is paid for soliciting
- **Manual exemption** — certain non-issuer transactions in securities listed in a recognized securities manual

**Worked example — counting private-placement offers.** An issuer wants the private-placement exemption. In a 12-month window it makes offers to 8 pension funds, 3 banks, and 9 individual retail investors. Do the institutional offers count?

- Institutional offers (8 pension funds + 3 banks = 11) are **unlimited** → they do not count.
- Retail offers = **9**, which is **≤ 10** → still within the limit.
- Result: the exemption is **preserved**. If the issuer had made offers to 11 retail investors, the exemption would be lost — and note it is the number of **offers**, not sales, that matters.

**Exam tip:** The private-placement ceiling is on **non-institutional offers made**, not on purchasers or dollars. **Common trap:** A security being *exempt* does not exempt the *seller* from registering as a broker-dealer or agent, and it never exempts anyone from antifraud liability.

#### 4. Issuers, Issuer Agents, and Finders

**Definition of an issuer.** An **issuer** is any person who **issues or proposes to issue a security**. The company raising capital is the issuer. A **non-issuer** transaction is one in which the proceeds do not go to the issuer — i.e., ordinary secondary-market trading among investors. (Special rule: for fractional undivided interests in oil, gas, or mining, there is deemed to be *no* issuer.)

**Registration of an issuer's agents.** An **agent** is an *individual* (a natural person) who represents a broker-dealer or an issuer in effecting or attempting to effect securities transactions. Two rules matter:

- An individual representing a **broker-dealer** in securities transactions is **always** an agent and must register (unless the role is purely **clerical/ministerial**).
- An individual representing an **issuer** is **excluded** from the definition of agent (and need **not** register) when the transactions are in specified **exempt securities** (such as U.S. government, municipal, and certain financial-institution securities), or are **exempt transactions**, or involve certain employee-benefit-plan sales with no commission. Representing an issuer in the sale of **non-exempt** securities to the **public** *does* require registration as an agent.

**Common trap:** "An individual sells only U.S. Treasury securities for the issuing entity, so she must register." False — representing an issuer in exempt securities places her *outside* the agent definition. But note that a person representing a **broker-dealer** in those very same Treasuries **is** an agent and must register. The exclusion is for **issuer** representatives, not broker-dealer representatives.

**Finders.** A **finder** is a person who, for compensation, brings together parties to a securities transaction — for example, introducing an issuer to potential investors or a firm to potential clients. When a finder receives **transaction-based compensation** tied to securities business, states increasingly require the finder to **register** (as an agent or broker-dealer). Many states have adopted specific finder-registration requirements. On the exam, treat referral-for-a-fee arrangements that touch securities transactions as generally triggering a registration requirement.

#### 5. State Enforcement and Antifraud Authority

**Jurisdiction — when does the Administrator have authority?** An offer or sale is subject to a state Administrator if the offer **originates in** that state, is **directed into** that state, or is **accepted in** that state. This means a single transaction can fall under more than one Administrator's jurisdiction. Two exceptions: offers made through a **bona fide newspaper** published out of state, or via a **radio/TV broadcast** originating outside the state, are **not** considered offers made in the state.

**Powers of the Administrator.** The Administrator may:

- Make, amend, and rescind **rules and orders**, and require **registration**
- Conduct **investigations** (inside or outside the state), **administer oaths**, and **subpoena** people and records
- Issue **cease-and-desist orders** — with or without a prior hearing
- **Deny, suspend, revoke, cancel, or withdraw** registrations (for cause, generally after notice and opportunity for a hearing)
- Require the filing of advertising and sales literature for non-exempt securities

The Administrator **cannot**:

- **Issue an injunction** directly — the Administrator must **ask a court** to do so
- **Arrest** anyone or bring criminal charges — those are referred to the **attorney general / district attorney**
- **Award damages** to injured investors — that is for the **civil courts**

**Antifraud authority reaches everyone.** This is the single most tested point in the subsection: the antifraud provisions apply to **all securities and all persons**, including **exempt securities**, **exempt transactions**, and **federal covered securities**. A security can be exempt from *registration* yet its seller can still be prosecuted for fraud. The Administrator never loses antifraud jurisdiction.

**Criminal penalties (the "5 and 3" and "5-year" rules).** A **willful** violation of the USA is a **criminal offense**. The maximum criminal penalty is a fine of up to **$5,000** and/or imprisonment of up to **3 years**. The **statute of limitations for criminal** prosecution is **5 years** from the alleged violation. (A defendant who proves he had no knowledge of the rule or order violated cannot be imprisoned.)

**Civil liability and rescission (the "2 and 3" rule).** A purchaser who bought a security sold in violation of the Act may sue to recover. The **statute of limitations** is the **sooner of 2 years after discovery or 3 years after the sale/purchase**. The buyer recovers via **rescission** (tendering the security back) or, if it has already been sold, damages.

The recovery formula is:

**Recovery = Purchase Price + Interest (at the state rate) − Income Received on the security + Reasonable Attorney's Fees and Court Costs**

**Worked example — civil rescission.** An investor paid **$10,000** for a security sold in violation of the Act. Two years later she still holds it. The state legal interest rate is **6%** per year, she has collected **$400** in dividends over the two years, and reasonable attorney's fees and court costs total **$500**. She sues for rescission.

- Purchase price = **$10,000**
- Interest = 6% × $10,000 × 2 years = **$1,200**
- Less income received (dividends) = **−$400**
- Plus attorney's fees and court costs = **+$500**
- **Recovery = $10,000 + $1,200 − $400 + $500 = $11,300**, and the investor tenders the security back to the seller.

Notice that **income the buyer received is subtracted** (she cannot keep both the dividends and full rescission), while interest at the state rate is **added**.

**The rescission offer.** Rather than face a lawsuit, a seller who made a violative sale may send the buyer a **letter of rescission**, offering to buy the security back for the amount above. The buyer generally has **30 days** to accept or reject. If the buyer **does not respond within 30 days**, or accepts the offer, the buyer **loses the right to later sue** over that transaction.

**Exam tip:** Memorize the two limitation pairs — **criminal: 5-year statute, 5,000 dollar fine, 3 years prison ("5/5/3")**; **civil: sooner of 2 years after discovery or 3 years after sale ("2 and 3")**. **Common trap:** The Administrator does not put anyone in jail, does not issue injunctions on its own, and does not hand investors a check for damages — courts do those. The Administrator investigates, orders, and refers.

### F. Remedies and Administrative Provisions

This subsection covers what happens when the securities laws are broken: who enforces them, what tools that enforcer has, and what penalties a violator faces. On the Series 65 these questions reward precise knowledge of who can do what (the *Administrator* versus the *courts*), the exact standards that must be met before a registration is denied or revoked, and a small set of memorizable numbers (criminal penalties and statutes of limitations). Master the vocabulary and the numbers and this becomes one of the more predictable topics on the exam.

Throughout, "the Act" means the **Uniform Securities Act (USA)** — the model state law each state adapts — and "the Administrator" means the state securities official (or agency) charged with enforcing it.

#### The Administrator's Authority: Rulemaking, Orders, Investigations, and Subpoenas

The Administrator is an *administrative* official, not a legislator and not a judge. Understanding those boundaries is the key to the whole subsection.

**Rulemaking and orders.** The state legislature passes the statute (the Act itself). The Administrator may then **make, amend, and rescind rules, forms, and orders** necessary to carry the Act out. The distinction matters:

| Instrument | Who issues it | Scope |
|---|---|---|
| **Statute (the Act)** | State legislature | The law itself |
| **Rule** | Administrator | General application to everyone (like a mini-law) |
| **Order** | Administrator | Applies to a specific person or specific situation |

Rules and orders carry the force of law, but they may **not conflict with the Act** and may not conflict with federal securities law. The Administrator cannot rewrite the statute — only the legislature can do that.

**Exam tip:** A *rule* applies to all persons; an *order* is directed at a specific applicant, registrant, or transaction. If a question describes something that affects everyone (e.g., a new recordkeeping requirement for all advisers), that is a rule.

**Good-faith reliance protection.** No liability provision of the Act applies to anyone who acted in **good-faith reliance** on a rule, form, or order — even if that rule or order is later amended, rescinded, or held invalid by a court. This protects registrants who follow the Administrator's guidance.

**Investigations.** The Administrator may conduct investigations — **public or private, inside or outside the state** — to determine whether any person **has violated, is violating, or is about to violate** the Act. Note the breadth: the Administrator does **not** have to wait for an actual violation, and does not have to prove one before investigating. In the course of an investigation the Administrator may require written statements under oath.

**Subpoena power.** To conduct investigations, the Administrator may:
- Administer oaths and affirmations,
- **Subpoena witnesses** and compel their attendance,
- Take evidence, and
- Require the **production of books, records, correspondence, and other documents.**

This subpoena power reaches across state lines: an Administrator can compel testimony and records from persons located in other states, and one state's Administrator will assist another's.

**Common trap:** The Administrator **cannot** issue an injunction and **cannot** imprison anyone — those are judicial (court) powers, and criminal fines and imprisonment are imposed only by the courts. If a subpoenaed person refuses to cooperate (contumacy), the Administrator must go to **court**, and the court can hold that person in **contempt**. Likewise, to stop ongoing conduct with an order enforceable by contempt, the Administrator must obtain an **injunction from a court.** The Administrator investigates and issues administrative orders; the courts punish and enjoin.

#### Administrative Actions Against Registrations: Deny, Suspend, Revoke, Cancel, Bar

These actions concern a person's *registration* as an investment adviser (IA), investment adviser representative (IAR), broker-dealer, or agent — or the registration of a security. Distinguish carefully between **punitive** actions (which require wrongdoing) and **non-punitive** actions (which do not).

**The two-part test for punitive action.** Before the Administrator may **deny, suspend, or revoke** a registration (or **bar/censure** a person), *both* of the following must be true:

1. The action is **in the public interest**, **AND**
2. A **specific cause** enumerated in the Act exists.

**Common trap:** "Public interest" **alone is never sufficient.** The exam loves an answer choice that says the Administrator revoked a registration "because it was in the public interest." That is wrong — a stated statutory cause must also exist. Both prongs are always required.

**Enumerated causes** include that the applicant or registrant:
- Filed an application that was **false or misleading** in a material way;
- **Willfully violated** the Act (or another securities law);
- Was **convicted of a securities-related misdemeanor, or any felony, within the past 10 years**;
- Is **enjoined by a court** from engaging in the securities business;
- Is the subject of an order by another state's Administrator or the SEC (within the past 10 years);
- Engaged in **dishonest or unethical practices** in the securities business;
- Is **insolvent**;
- Is **not qualified** on the basis of training, experience, or knowledge; or
- **Failed to pay** the required filing fee (a fee-based denial is cured when the fee is paid).

**Exam tip:** Memorize the **10-year window** for convictions and prior orders. A felony conviction 11 years ago is *not*, by itself, a cause. Also note the qualification standard: the Administrator **may not** base an order **solely on an IAR's lack of experience** — lack of experience must be combined with lack of knowledge or training. And the Administrator may not use a fact already known at the time registration was granted as grounds to later revoke.

**Cancellation — the non-punitive termination.** The Administrator **cancels** (not "revokes") a registration when a registrant:
- **No longer exists**,
- **Cannot be located** (e.g., mail is returned undeliverable),
- Has **ceased doing business**, or
- Is **declared mentally incompetent.**

Cancellation involves **no finding of wrongdoing** and requires no public-interest/cause test. It is purely administrative housekeeping.

**Common trap:** Distinguish *cancel* from *revoke*. Revocation is **punitive** (a penalty for cause). Cancellation is **non-punitive** (the registrant simply cannot be located, no longer exists, or is incompetent). A firm whose owner dies is *canceled*, not *revoked*.

**Withdrawal.** A registrant may voluntarily **withdraw** its registration. Withdrawal becomes effective **30 days** after filing (unless a proceeding is pending). Importantly, the Administrator retains **jurisdiction for one year** after a withdrawal becomes effective — a registrant cannot escape a pending or forthcoming enforcement action simply by withdrawing.

| Action | Punitive? | Initiated by | Requires cause + public interest? |
|---|---|---|---|
| Deny / Suspend / Revoke / Bar | Yes | Administrator | Yes (both prongs) |
| Cease-and-desist | Yes (enforcement) | Administrator | Cause; may issue with or without hearing |
| Cancel | No | Administrator | No (person gone/incompetent) |
| Withdraw | No | Registrant | No (voluntary; 1-yr retained jurisdiction) |

#### Cease-and-Desist Orders and Injunctions

A **cease-and-desist (C&D) order** is the Administrator's tool to tell a person to **stop** a violation. Key features:
- The Administrator may issue a C&D **with or without a prior hearing** (it can be summary — issued immediately when needed).
- A C&D **carries no fine or penalty by itself** and the Administrator **cannot itself enforce it.** It is simply a directive to stop.
- If the person **ignores** the C&D, the Administrator must apply to a **court for an injunction.** Violating the court's injunction can then result in contempt, fines, or imprisonment imposed by the **court** — not the Administrator.

**Exam tip:** A cease-and-desist order is administrative and unenforceable on its own; an **injunction** is judicial and enforceable through contempt. The Administrator issues the first; only a **court** issues the second.

#### Hearings, Summary Orders, and Appeals

Due process runs through all of this. A person facing an adverse order is generally entitled to **notice** and an **opportunity for a hearing**, with the Administrator issuing **written findings of fact and conclusions of law.**

- **Right to a hearing:** If a person requests a hearing in writing, the Administrator must hold it within **15 days** of receiving the request.
- **Summary (interim) orders:** The Administrator may, **by order**, **summarily postpone or suspend** a registration *pending final determination* of a proceeding — i.e., before a full hearing — but must **promptly notify** the affected person and give an opportunity to be heard.
- **Appeal to the courts:** A person aggrieved by a final order may seek judicial review by filing a petition in the appropriate court **within 60 days** of the order. Filing an appeal does **not automatically stay** (halt) the order unless the court so directs.

**Exam tip:** Two numbers to keep straight — **15 days** for the Administrator to hold a requested hearing, and **60 days** to appeal a final order to court.

#### Criminal Penalties

Criminal liability under the Act is reserved for **willful** violations. The standard Uniform Securities Act criminal penalty — the number the Series 65 expects — is:

> **Fine of up to $5,000 and/or imprisonment for up to 3 years** per willful violation.

A memory aid: **"3 / 5 / 5"** — up to **3** years' imprisonment, up to a **$5,000** fine, and a **5**-year criminal statute of limitations (covered below).

Three important qualifiers:
- **"Willful" does not require intent to break the law.** It only means the person intended to do the act; the person need not have known the act was illegal.
- **No-knowledge defense for rules/orders:** A person may **not be imprisoned** for violating a *rule or order* if they prove they had **no knowledge** of that rule or order. (This defense applies to rules and orders, not to violating the Act's core provisions.)
- Criminal cases are brought and penalties are imposed by the **courts** (typically referred to the state Attorney General for prosecution), never by the Administrator directly.

**Comparison for context** (state vs. federal acts — the state USA figure is the one most heavily tested on a state-law exam):

| Law | Max criminal fine | Max imprisonment |
|---|---|---|
| **Uniform Securities Act (state)** | **$5,000** | **3 years** |
| Investment Advisers Act of 1940 | $10,000 | 5 years |
| Securities Act of 1933 | $10,000 | 5 years |

**Common trap:** Don't confuse the state USA numbers ($5,000 / 3 years) with the federal Investment Advisers Act numbers ($10,000 / 5 years). The question stem will tell you which law applies.

#### Civil Liability and Rescission

Civil liability is the **private remedy**: a wronged **purchaser sues the seller** (or a client sues the adviser) to get their money back. It arises when a security or advisory service is sold/rendered in **violation** of the Act — for example, sale of an **unregistered non-exempt security**, sale by an **unregistered agent**, or a sale made through a **material misstatement or omission.**

**What the buyer can recover (rescission).** If the buyer **still owns** the security, they may **tender it back** and recover:

> **Recovery = Consideration paid + Interest at the state's legal rate − Income already received (dividends/interest) + Court costs + Reasonable attorney's fees**

**Worked example:**
- An investor buys a security for **$20,000** in a transaction that violated the Act.
- She holds it for **2 years** and receives **$1,000** in total dividends.
- The state's legal interest rate is **8%.**

Step 1 — Interest: $20,000 × 8% × 2 years = **$3,200**
Step 2 — Subtract income received: −**$1,000**
Step 3 — Recovery = $20,000 + $3,200 − $1,000 = **$22,200**, plus court costs and reasonable attorney's fees, **upon tendering the security back** to the seller.

**If the buyer has already sold** the security (no longer owns it), they recover **damages** instead of full rescission: essentially the amount they would have recovered on tender **minus** what they received when they disposed of the security (so both income received and the sale proceeds reduce the claim, while interest is still added).

**Advisory clients** have a parallel remedy: a client who received services in violation may recover the **advisory fees paid plus any loss caused by the advice, plus interest, minus any income received,** together with costs and attorney's fees.

**Right (offer) of rescission.** A seller who discovers they made a sale in violation may head off a lawsuit by sending a **written offer of rescission** — an offer to buy the security back for the price paid plus interest, less income received. The buyer has **30 days** to accept. **If the buyer does not accept a valid offer within 30 days, the buyer loses the right to sue.**

**Exam tip:** In a rescission calculation, you **add interest** and **subtract income (dividends/interest) received.** A common wrong answer forgets to subtract the income or forgets to add interest. Also remember: the buyer must **tender the security back** to collect full rescission.

#### Statutes of Limitations

A statute of limitations sets the deadline for bringing an action. The USA sets separate clocks for criminal and civil matters — memorize both.

| Type of action | Statute of limitations |
|---|---|
| **Criminal** prosecution | Within **5 years** of the alleged violation |
| **Civil** action (rescission/damages) | The **sooner** of **2 years after discovery** of the violation **or 3 years after** the sale/contract |

For the civil clock, think **"2 and 3, whichever comes first."** If the fraud is discovered quickly, the 2-year-from-discovery clock usually controls; if it stays hidden, the 3-year-from-sale cap can bar the claim even before discovery.

**Worked example (civil SOL):** A security is sold in violation on **January 1, 2024.** If the buyer discovers the violation on **June 1, 2025**, they have until **June 1, 2027** (2 years after discovery) — *but* the 3-year outer limit expires **January 1, 2027**, so the **earlier** date, **January 1, 2027**, is the true deadline.

**Exam tip:** The single most tested numbers in this subsection are **criminal SOL = 5 years**, **civil SOL = sooner of 2 years (discovery) or 3 years (sale)**, and **criminal penalty = $5,000 / 3 years.** Lock these in and you'll capture most of the points this topic offers.

### G. Communication with Clients and Prospects

Everything an investment adviser (IA) says to a client or prospect — from the disclosure documents it hands over, to the contract it signs, to a tweet or a homepage banner — is regulated. The unifying idea is the adviser's **fiduciary duty**: the obligation to act in the client's best interest, which requires **full and fair disclosure of all material facts**, especially conflicts of interest. This subsection walks through the required disclosures, the things an adviser may never say, the rules governing advisory contracts, and the standards for advertising and electronic communications.

#### Required Disclosures: The Brochure and Brochure Delivery

The core disclosure document is the adviser's **brochure**, which is **Form ADV Part 2**. It must be written in **plain English** and delivered to clients. Part 2 has two pieces:

| Document | Common name | What it discloses |
|---|---|---|
| **Form ADV Part 2A** | The "firm brochure" | Services, fee schedule, types of clients, methods of analysis, conflicts of interest, disciplinary history, business practices |
| **Form ADV Part 2B** | The "brochure supplement" | Information about the specific **individuals** (supervised persons) who actually give advice to that client — their education, business background, and disciplinary events |

**The brochure delivery rule (the "48-hour rule").** The adviser must deliver the brochure to a client or prospective client under one of two timing options:

- **At least 48 hours before** entering into the advisory contract — this 48-hour gap serves as the "cooling-off" period, so **no separate termination right is required**; **or**
- **At the time of entering into** the contract, **but only if** the client is given the right to **terminate without penalty within 5 business days**.

Think of it as "48 hours before, or at signing with a 5-day free look."

**Annual delivery.** Each year, within **120 days of the end of the adviser's fiscal year**, the adviser must deliver, free of charge, **either** (1) an updated brochure that includes a **summary of material changes**, **or** (2) just the summary of material changes together with an **offer** to provide the full updated brochure.

- **Worked example (annual delivery deadline):** If the adviser's fiscal year ends December 31, the deadline is 120 days later. Counting the days after December 31: 31 (Jan) + 28 (Feb) + 31 (Mar) + 30 (Apr) = 120 days → **April 30**. The exam loves this "120 days" figure. (Do not confuse it with the **90-day** deadline for *filing* the annual updating amendment to Form ADV — that is a separate requirement.)

**Exam tip:** The brochure need **not** be delivered to clients who are **registered investment companies** (mutual funds) or to clients receiving only **impersonal advice costing less than $500 per year**. Watch for those two carve-outs.

**Common trap:** Candidates confuse "48 hours" with "5 days." Remember: **48 hours before** signing = no withdrawal right needed; **at signing** = client gets the **5-business-day** penalty-free termination window.

#### Disclosure of Material Conflicts of Interest

Because the adviser is a fiduciary, it must disclose — clearly and in advance — any **material conflict of interest** that could affect its advice or that might cause it to render advice that is not disinterested. Disclosure lets the client give **informed consent**; hiding the conflict is a violation regardless of whether the client was actually harmed.

Commonly tested examples of conflicts that must be disclosed:

- The adviser or its reps earn **commissions** on products they recommend (dual registration as an agent/IAR).
- **Soft-dollar** arrangements or use of an affiliated broker-dealer.
- Acting as **principal or agent** in a transaction with a client (which also requires written disclosure **and** client consent **before** the completion of that specific trade).
- **Compensation for referrals**, or a financial interest in a recommended security.
- Charging **higher-than-typical fees**, or having **custody** of client assets.

**Exam tip:** The tested standard is "disclose, then obtain informed consent." A conflict is **not** automatically prohibited — an **undisclosed** conflict is what gets an adviser in trouble.

#### Unlawful Representations Concerning Registration

It is a prohibited and unethical practice to state or imply that registration means the government has **approved, endorsed, or passed on the merits** of the adviser or its services. The correct, legally required understanding is:

> **Registration does not imply approval.** Being registered with the SEC or a state means the required forms were filed — it says **nothing** about the quality, honesty, or ability of the adviser.

Related unlawful statements:

- Claiming the SEC or a state administrator has **"approved"** or **"sponsored"** the firm.
- Misrepresenting the meaning of designations, or of terms like **"Registered Investment Adviser (RIA)"** as if it were a badge of merit.
- Misstating **qualifications** (education, experience, credentials).

**Common trap:** An adviser may **truthfully state that it is registered** — that is permitted. The violation is suggesting registration equals a government **seal of approval**. The exam phrase to memorize is "registration does not imply approval or endorsement."

#### Prohibition on Performance Guarantees

An adviser may **not guarantee** a client that a specific result will be achieved — for example, promising that a security will not lose value, that gains are assured, or that the account will hit a set return. Markets carry risk, and any guarantee against loss or of a specific profit is inherently misleading. This is a straightforward prohibited practice.

**Exam tip:** Distinguish this from lawful, factual statements. Describing a bond's **stated (fixed) coupon**, or noting that an **FDIC-insured** instrument protects principal up to limits, is not a prohibited "guarantee" — those are contractual/statutory features. The prohibition targets the **adviser guaranteeing investment performance or against loss**.

**Common trap:** "You can't lose money in this account" from an IAR is a guarantee-against-loss violation, even if said informally or verbally.

#### Investment Advisory Contracts

Under the NASAA Model Rule, a state-registered adviser's contract must be **in writing** and must address several specific points. Federal covered advisers are governed by parallel provisions of the Investment Advisers Act of 1940 (Section 205), which impose the same substantive terms but do not themselves require a written contract. Required/prohibited contract terms include:

| Contract requirement | Rule |
|---|---|
| **No assignment without consent** | The contract may not be **assigned** to another party without the **client's consent**. |
| **Notice of partnership change** | If the adviser is a **partnership**, the contract must provide that the adviser will **notify the client of any change in the membership** of the partnership within a **reasonable time**. |
| **No performance-based fees** | Compensation may **not** be based on a share of capital gains or capital appreciation, **except as permitted** (see below). |
| **Written terms** (state level) | The contract discloses services, term, the **fee or fee formula**, and the amount to be **refunded** on prepaid fees if the contract is terminated. |

**Assignment explained.** "Assignment" includes an outright transfer of the contract and also a change in **controlling ownership** of the adviser. For a **partnership**, assignment is triggered by a change involving a **majority** of the partners (a "material" change in membership). A **minority** change in partners does **not** count as an assignment — but the client must still be **notified** of it within a reasonable time. Because advisory services are personal, the client's consent is required so they are never silently handed to a different firm.

**Refund of prepaid fees.** If a client prepays and later terminates, the adviser refunds the **unearned** portion.

- **Worked example (prepaid fee refund):** A client prepays a **$2,400** annual fee on January 1. The client terminates at the end of March, having received **3 of 12 months** of service.
  - Refund = Total prepaid × (Unused months ÷ Total months)
  - Refund = $2,400 × (9 ÷ 12) = **$1,800**
  - The adviser keeps $600 (the 3 months earned) and refunds $1,800.

**Exam tip:** Requiring **substantial prepayment** of fees (for a **state-registered** adviser: **more than $500, six or more months in advance**; the SEC threshold is **$1,200 or more**, six or more months in advance) triggers a **balance-sheet** disclosure requirement in Form ADV Part 2 — a favorite detail.

##### The Performance-Fee Exception (Qualified Clients)

The default rule bans fees calculated as a share of gains, because they can tempt an adviser to take excessive risk (heads the adviser wins, tails the client loses). The main exception allows performance fees **only** when the client is a **qualified client** — a client who is financially sophisticated enough to fend for themselves. Under SEC Rule 205-3, a qualified client is one who has **at least $1.1 million in assets under management with the adviser**, **or** a **net worth of more than $2.2 million** (these dollar figures are inflation-adjusted; older exam materials may cite $1 million / $2 million).

- Net worth for a natural person **excludes** the value of the **primary residence**.
- The fee arrangement must still be **fair** and disclosed.

**Exam tip:** Another permitted structure is a **fulcrum fee**, which adjusts symmetrically up **and** down relative to a benchmark (used with registered investment companies). Straight "we keep 20% of the profits" is only allowed with **qualified clients**.

**Common trap:** Even for a qualified client, the adviser must still meet all other fiduciary and disclosure duties — qualifying for the exception does not waive suitability or conflict disclosure.

#### Correspondence and Advertising

All communications to the public — **letters, emails, texts, digital messaging, websites, blogs, and social media** — are treated as advertising when they promote advisory services, and they may **not be false or misleading**. The federal standard is the **SEC Marketing Rule (Rule 206(4)-1)**, which consolidated the old advertising and cash-solicitation rules. Under it, an advertisement may **not**:

- Make an **untrue statement** of material fact or omit a material fact.
- Make a **material statement the adviser cannot substantiate** on demand.
- Include **untrue or misleading implications or inferences**.
- Discuss **benefits without fair and balanced treatment** of associated **material risks or limitations**.
- Reference **specific investment advice** in a way that is not fair and balanced (**cherry-picking** winners).
- Present **performance results** in a misleading way.

**Performance advertising specifics** the exam may touch: net-of-fee performance generally must accompany gross performance, and certain time-period and disclosure requirements apply. The overarching theme is **fair, balanced, and not misleading**.

##### Social Media, Email/Digital Messaging, and Websites

- **Static vs. interactive content.** A firm's **website content, a fixed "profile," or a pinned bio** is generally treated as **static** content the firm creates and must supervise/approve. Real-time, back-and-forth **interactive** posts are treated more like conversation but are still subject to recordkeeping and anti-fraud rules.
- **Entanglement and adoption.** A firm can become responsible for **third-party** content if it was **entangled** in creating it or later **adopts/endorses** it (e.g., "liking," sharing, or linking to a favorable third-party post can make that content the firm's own advertising).
- **Recordkeeping.** Business-related electronic communications — including texts and social-media messages — must be **retained** as required records. Advisers commonly prohibit using unmonitored personal channels for business.

##### Testimonials and Endorsements

Historically, testimonials were **flatly prohibited** in adviser advertising. The **SEC Marketing Rule now permits testimonials and endorsements**, but only with strict conditions. Know both the old default (still a tempting wrong answer) and the modern conditions.

| Old rule (pre-2021) | Current Marketing Rule |
|---|---|
| Testimonials **prohibited** in adviser ads | Testimonials/endorsements **permitted with conditions** |

Conditions for a permitted testimonial/endorsement:

- **Clear and prominent disclosure** of (1) whether the speaker is a **client** or non-client, (2) whether the person is **compensated**, and (3) any **material conflicts of interest**.
- **Adviser oversight** — the adviser must supervise the compliance of the testimonial/endorsement.
- **Written agreement** with the promoter if compensation exceeds a **de minimis** amount (**more than $1,000 in a 12-month period**).
- **Disqualification** — certain **"bad actors"** (persons with disqualifying regulatory or criminal events) may not be paid more than the de minimis amount to give endorsements.

**Worked example (de minimis threshold):** A firm pays an influencer **$75 per referral**. After the influencer sends 20 referrals in a year, total compensation is 20 × $75 = **$1,500**, which **exceeds $1,000** — so a **written promoter agreement** and the full disclosure conditions are required. Had it been a **$40 gift card** (under $1,000/year), the written-agreement condition would not be triggered, though disclosure and oversight still apply.

**Exam tip:** If a question is framed around the **traditional NASAA/Uniform Securities Act** unethical-practices list, "using **testimonials**" may be presented as **prohibited**. If the question references the **current SEC Marketing Rule**, testimonials are **allowed with disclosure and oversight**. Read which framework the question invokes.

**Common trap:** A **paid** endorsement that is not disclosed as paid is a violation under either framework — the compensation and conflict **must** be disclosed clearly and prominently. Silence about payment is the classic wrong practice.

### H. Ethical Practices and Fiduciary Obligations

This subsection ties together the ethical backbone of the entire exam. Nearly every "prohibited practices" question flows from one root principle: an investment adviser (IA) is a **fiduciary**. Master the fiduciary standard and the specific rules below become logical applications of it rather than a list to memorize.

#### The Fiduciary Duty of an Investment Adviser

A fiduciary must place the client's interests ahead of its own at all times. This standard arises under the Investment Advisers Act of 1940 and was reinforced by the Supreme Court in *SEC v. Capital Gains Research Bureau* (1963), which held that Section 206 imposes a duty to disclose all material conflicts of interest. The fiduciary duty has two components:

- **Duty of loyalty** — Act in the client's best interest, avoid conflicts of interest, and where a conflict cannot be avoided, **fully and fairly disclose** it so the client can give informed consent. Loyalty means not favoring the adviser's own compensation, affiliates, or proprietary products.
- **Duty of care** — Provide advice that is suitable and in the client's best interest, seek best execution of trades, and monitor the account over the course of the relationship.

Contrast this with the standard historically applied to broker-dealer agents. The IA fiduciary standard is higher than the older "suitability only" floor, and disclosure plus informed consent is the mechanism that legitimizes an otherwise conflicted transaction. (Broker-dealers now operate under a "best interest" standard of their own, but the adviser's fiduciary duty remains the more demanding benchmark.)

**Exam tip:** The single most tested fiduciary concept is **disclosure**. When a question describes a conflict (the adviser earns a commission on a recommended product, has a financial interest in a security, or acts as principal), the correct answer is almost always "disclose the conflict in writing and obtain the client's consent," not "the practice is automatically prohibited." Disclosure cures most conflicts; concealment is the violation.

**Common trap:** A fiduciary cannot disclaim its fiduciary duty by contract. A "hedge clause" or waiver purporting to limit the adviser's liability for its own misconduct is prohibited, even if the client signs it.

#### Compensation: Fees, Commissions, and Performance-Based Fees

An adviser's compensation is a built-in conflict, so its form and disclosure are heavily regulated.

**Permitted fee structures.** IAs may charge a percentage of assets under management (AUM), a flat/fixed fee, an hourly fee, or a subscription/retainer. All fees must be **reasonable** relative to services rendered. Fees need not match the industry average, but grossly excessive fees violate the fiduciary duty. The fee schedule and how fees are calculated and billed must be disclosed in **Form ADV Part 2A (the brochure)**.

**Commissions and dual registration.** An adviser (or its associated persons) who also acts as a broker-dealer agent may earn commissions on securities it recommends. This creates a conflict, permitted only if **disclosed**. If the adviser wants to act as **principal** (selling a security out of its own inventory to a client) or as **agent for the other side of a client trade**, Section 206(3) requires **written disclosure to, and consent obtained from, the client before completion of each such transaction** — transaction-by-transaction consent, not a blanket authorization.

**Prepayment of fees.** Under NASAA rules, an adviser that collects **more than $500 in fees, six or more months in advance**, is deemed to have a "substantial prepayment" and must include an audited **balance sheet** in its brochure. This threshold is a favorite exam number.

**Performance-based fees.** A performance-based fee is compensation based on capital gains or appreciation of the client's account (for example, "20% of profits"). These are **generally prohibited** under Section 205 of the Advisers Act because they tempt the adviser to take excessive risk with the client's money. Rule 205-3 provides an exception — a performance fee is permitted only for a **"qualified client,"** defined as a client who meets one of these tests:

| Qualified client test | Threshold (current SEC-adjusted figure) |
|---|---|
| Assets under management with the adviser | at least **$1,100,000** |
| Net worth (individual, excluding primary residence) | more than **$2,200,000** |
| Alternatively | a "qualified purchaser" (≥ $5 million in investments) or a knowledgeable employee of the adviser |

**Worked example (performance-fee eligibility):** A prospective client has $600,000 invested with the adviser and a net worth of $2.4 million (excluding home). She fails the AUM test ($600,000 < $1.1 million) but **passes** the net worth test ($2.4 million > $2.2 million). Because the tests are alternatives, she **is** a qualified client and a performance fee is permitted.

**Exam tip:** Some older materials list $1,000,000 AUM / $2,100,000 net worth. The SEC adjusts these for inflation; the current values are **$1.1 million / $2.2 million**. Either way, the exam concept is that performance fees are barred for ordinary retail clients and allowed only for sophisticated, wealthy investors after disclosure.

**Common trap:** A normal AUM fee (the same percentage whether the account rises or falls) is **not** a performance fee and is allowed for any client. Only a fee that shares in gains or appreciation triggers the qualified-client rule.

#### Soft Dollars and the Section 28(e) Safe Harbor

"Soft dollars" arise when an adviser directs client brokerage (commissions) to a broker-dealer and, in return, receives **research or brokerage services** rather than cash. Because the client pays the commission but the adviser receives the benefit, this is a conflict of interest. **Section 28(e)** of the Securities Exchange Act creates a **safe harbor**: an adviser does not breach its fiduciary duty by paying more than the lowest available commission, provided the products or services received provide **lawful and appropriate assistance in the investment decision-making process** and the adviser makes a good-faith determination that the commissions are reasonable relative to the value received.

| Inside the safe harbor (permitted) | Outside the safe harbor (prohibited use of soft dollars) |
|---|---|
| Research reports, financial analysis | Rent, office furniture, and overhead |
| Software that analyzes securities/portfolios | Travel, entertainment, meals |
| Attendance at research seminars | Salaries and personal expenses |
| Trade execution and clearance services | Computer hardware for general office use |

**Exam tip:** The dividing line is "does it help me make investment decisions or execute trades?" If yes, it likely qualifies. Anything that primarily benefits the **adviser's own operations** (overhead, personal items) falls outside 28(e) and must be paid with the adviser's hard dollars. Soft-dollar arrangements must also be **disclosed** in Form ADV.

#### Custody of Client Funds and Securities

**Custody** means holding, directly or indirectly, client funds or securities, or having **any authority to obtain possession** of them. An adviser has custody if it:

- Physically holds client cash or certificates;
- Has authority to **withdraw funds or securities** from the client's account (including the ability to **deduct advisory fees directly** — this alone is deemed custody);
- Acts as **general partner** of a limited partnership, **trustee** of a trust, or in a comparable capacity giving it access to client assets;
- Receives client checks made payable to the adviser (as opposed to third-party checks it forwards promptly).

Under the NASAA custody rule (mirroring Advisers Act Rule 206(4)-2), an adviser with custody must:
