1. **Use a qualified custodian** — a bank or a registered broker-dealer that holds the assets in a separate account under the client's name (or in the adviser's name as agent or trustee for clients).
2. **Notify the client in writing** of the qualified custodian's name and address and how the assets are held.
3. Have a **reasonable belief that the qualified custodian sends account statements at least quarterly** directly to the client.
4. Undergo an **annual surprise examination** by an **independent public accountant** who verifies the client funds and securities without advance notice.
5. If the adviser (or an affiliate) is itself the custodian, obtain an annual **internal control report** (e.g., SOC-1/SAS 70) from an independent accountant.

**Minimum financial requirements (NASAA, state-registered IAs):**

| Situation | Minimum net worth (or bonding alternative) |
|---|---|
| IA has **custody** of client funds/securities | **$35,000** |
| IA has **discretion** but no custody | **$10,000** |
| Net worth falls below the required minimum | Notify the Administrator **by the close of the next business day**, then file a financial report by the close of business the following day |

**Exam tip:** Deducting advisory fees straight from the client account **is custody** under the rule. However, many states grant relief from the **surprise examination** requirement when custody arises *solely* from fee deduction and safeguards are met (fees billed with client authorization, statements sent by the qualified custodian). Watch for questions distinguishing "custody exists" (yes, fee deduction counts) from "surprise exam required" (often excused for fee-deduction-only custody).

**Common trap:** Simply having **discretionary authority** to place trades is **not** custody — the adviser decides trades but never possesses the assets. Custody requires the ability to *withdraw or possess* funds or securities. Distinguish the $10,000 (discretion) versus $35,000 (custody) net-worth figures.

#### Discretion and Trading Authorization

**Discretionary authority** is the power to decide, without contacting the client first, at least one of the **three A's**: the **Asset** (which security), the **Action** (buy or sell), or the **Amount** (how many shares). Deciding only the **time or price** of a trade the client has already ordered ("buy 100 XYZ, you pick a good moment today") is **not** discretion.

- **State (NASAA) rule:** The IA must have **written** discretionary authorization. It may exercise discretion on **oral authorization for up to the first 10 business days** following the initial discretionary transaction, provided the written authority is obtained within that period.
- **Trading authorization** may also be granted via a **limited power of attorney** (trading only) versus a **full power of attorney** (which permits withdrawals and generally creates custody).

**Common trap:** Time-and-price-only discretion does **not** require written authorization and does not make the account "discretionary." Only choosing the security, the action, or the amount triggers the written-authorization requirement.

#### Standard of Care, Suitability, and Anti-Money Laundering (AML)

**Suitability and best interest.** Consistent with the duty of care, every recommendation must fit the client's financial situation, objectives, risk tolerance, time horizon, and needs — gathered through a documented client profile. For advisers, suitability is elevated to a **best-interest** obligation rather than a mere "not unsuitable" floor.

**Anti-Money Laundering (AML).** AML obligations flow from the **Bank Secrecy Act** and the **USA PATRIOT Act**, administered by **FinCEN**. Key components tested:

- **Customer Identification Program (CIP)** — Verify the identity of each customer (name, date of birth, address, taxpayer ID) and check names against government (OFAC) lists.
- **Currency Transaction Report (CTR)** — Filed for cash transactions **exceeding $10,000** in a single business day.
- **Suspicious Activity Report (SAR)** — Filed for suspicious transactions (generally **$5,000 or more** for broker-dealers) where money laundering or fraud is suspected; the firm may **not** "tip off" the customer that a SAR was filed.
- **Structuring** — Deliberately breaking a large cash transaction into smaller amounts (e.g., several $9,500 deposits) to evade the CTR threshold is itself a crime.

**Exam tip:** Memorize the two numbers together — **CTR = cash over $10,000**, **SAR = suspicious activity (BD threshold $5,000)**. Also remember that structuring to avoid reporting is illegal regardless of the source of funds.

#### Conflicts of Interest and Prohibited Business Practices

The following practices are core "unethical business practices" that appear repeatedly. Each is an application of the fiduciary duty of loyalty or of the anti-fraud provisions (Section 206 and the NASAA Model Rule on Unethical Business Practices).

**Loans to or from clients.** An IA generally may **not borrow money or securities from, or lend to, a client.** Exception: the client is in the business of lending (a bank, broker-dealer, or affiliate) or is an affiliate of the adviser. A broker-dealer agent likewise may not lend to or borrow from a customer unless the customer is a lending institution or an immediate family member (firm-specific rules apply).

**Sharing in profits/losses.** An IA may **not** share directly in the profits or losses of a client's account (other than a permitted performance fee for a qualified client). For **broker-dealer agents**, sharing is permitted only if the agent receives **written approval from the firm**, shares **in direct proportion to the agent's own financial contribution**, and the account is a **joint account** — but for an IA the sharing arrangement is barred outright.

**Client confidentiality.** Nonpublic client information may not be disclosed to third parties without the client's consent, except as required by law (subpoena, regulatory request). This connects to the privacy rules below.

**Insider trading.** Trading on, or tipping, **material nonpublic information** is prohibited under the **Insider Trading and Securities Fraud Enforcement Act of 1988 (ITSFEA)**. Penalties (as amended by later securities legislation):
- **Civil penalty of up to three times (treble) the profit gained or loss avoided**, plus disgorgement.
- **Criminal fines up to $5 million** for individuals ($25 million for entities) and **up to 20 years imprisonment**.
- **Controlling persons** (the firm) can be liable up to the **greater of $1 million or three times** the profit or loss from the controlled person's violation if they failed to maintain adequate supervisory procedures.

**Worked example (treble damages):** An analyst learns of an unannounced merger and buys stock, making a **$50,000** profit. The SEC may seek disgorgement of the $50,000 **plus** a civil penalty of up to **3 × $50,000 = $150,000**, for total exposure of **$200,000**, in addition to possible criminal prosecution.

**Selling away.** An agent effecting **private securities transactions outside the firm** without the firm's prior written knowledge and approval. Prohibited because the firm cannot supervise it.

**Market manipulation.** Creating a false or misleading appearance of trading activity — examples include **matched orders**, **wash trades** (buying and selling to create fake volume), **painting the tape**, and **pump-and-dump** schemes. All are prohibited.

**Excessive trading / churning.** Trading in a client's account primarily to generate commissions rather than to benefit the client. In a fee-based advisory account the analog is **reverse churning** — charging an ongoing wrap/advisory fee on an account that rarely trades, so the client would be better off in a commission account. Both violate the best-interest duty.

**Front running, misrepresentation, and unauthorized trading** round out the anti-fraud list: trading ahead of a client's large order, making false statements about qualifications or returns, and executing trades the client never authorized.

**Common trap:** Guaranteeing a client against loss, or promising a specific return, is **always prohibited** for both IAs and agents. Only certain securities (e.g., a bond guaranteed by a third party as to principal and interest) may use the word "guaranteed," and even then it refers to the issuer's or guarantor's obligation, not a promise from the adviser.

#### Personal Securities Transactions and Holdings Reports

Because an adviser's employees can front-run or trade against clients, Advisers Act **Rule 204A-1** requires a written **Code of Ethics**. **"Access persons"** (employees with access to nonpublic recommendations or client holdings) must report their personal and outside securities accounts and holdings:

| Report | Deadline |
|---|---|
| **Initial holdings report** | Within **10 days** of becoming an access person; information current as of a date **no more than 45 days** before the report |
| **Annual holdings report** | At least once every **12 months**; information current within **45 days** |
| **Quarterly transaction report** | Within **30 days** after the end of each calendar quarter |

**Outside securities accounts.** An associated person who opens a brokerage account away from the firm must give **written notice to the employing firm**, and the executing firm must, on request, provide **duplicate confirmations and statements** to the employer so the personal trading can be supervised.

**Exam tip:** Anchor the numbers — **10 days initial, 45 days current, 30 days quarterly, annual**. Reportable securities generally **exclude** direct-obligation U.S. Treasuries, money market funds, and open-end mutual funds not advised by the firm, because those pose little front-running risk.

#### Political Contributions and Pay-to-Play

To stop advisers from "buying" government advisory business through campaign donations, Advisers Act **Rule 206(4)-5** imposes a **two-year "time out."** If the adviser (or a covered associate) makes a political contribution to an official who can **influence the award of advisory business** to a government client (e.g., a public pension fund), the adviser is **barred from receiving compensation** for advisory services to that government entity for **two years**.

**De minimis exceptions** (contributions small enough to be exempt):

| Contribution | De minimis limit per election |
|---|---|
| To a candidate the person **is entitled to vote for** | **$350** |
| To a candidate the person **cannot vote for** | **$150** |

**Worked example:** A portfolio manager contributes **$400** to a state treasurer for whom she is eligible to vote. Because $400 exceeds the **$350** limit, the exception does not apply and the firm faces a **two-year ban** on compensated advisory work for that state's pension plan.

**Common trap:** The pay-to-play penalty is a **ban on compensation**, not a ban on providing the service and not a fine paid to the client. Note also that the limits are "per election," so a primary and a general election count separately.

#### Exploitation of Vulnerable Adults

The **NASAA Model Act to Protect Vulnerable Adults from Financial Exploitation** addresses suspected exploitation of **"eligible adults"** — clients **age 65 or older**, or adults who qualify for protective services under state law. When a **"qualified individual"** (an agent, IA representative, or supervisor) reasonably believes financial exploitation is occurring or has been attempted, the model act allows the firm to:

- **Notify** the state securities regulator and **Adult Protective Services (APS)**;
- **Delay a disbursement or transaction** for up to **15 business days**, extendable by an additional **10 business days** if requested by a regulator or agency, or as ordered by a court;
- **Notify** third parties previously designated by the client;
- Receive **immunity** from liability for good-faith reporting and delays.

**Exam tip:** Remember the age (**65+**) and the initial hold period (**15 business days**). The purpose is protective — the firm is permitted, not required, to pause a suspicious transaction while it investigates.

#### Cybersecurity, Privacy, and Data Protection

Protecting client data is both a fiduciary and a regulatory duty.

- **Regulation S-P (Privacy Rule)** requires delivery of a **privacy notice at account opening (initial)** and **annually** thereafter, describing what nonpublic personal information is collected and shared. Before sharing information with **nonaffiliated third parties**, the firm must give clients an **opt-out** opportunity. (Sharing with affiliates and sharing to service the account are generally exempt from the opt-out.) The **Safeguards Rule** requires written policies to protect client records and information.
- **Regulation S-ID (Identity Theft Red Flags Rule)** requires a written **identity theft prevention program** to detect, prevent, and respond to "red flags."
- **Cybersecurity programs** should address access controls, encryption, employee training, vendor/third-party risk, and an **incident response plan** for breaches. Firms are expected to conduct risk assessments and to maintain procedures for detecting and reporting intrusions.

**Exam tip:** Two triggers for a privacy notice — **initial (account opening)** and **annual** — plus the **opt-out** right for sharing with **nonaffiliated** third parties. Reg S-P = **privacy**; Reg S-ID = **identity theft red flags**. Don't confuse the two.

#### Business Continuity and Succession Planning

The **NASAA Model Rule on Business Continuity and Succession Planning** requires every state-registered adviser to maintain a written **Business Continuity Plan (BCP)** proportionate to its size and complexity. Because clients rely on the adviser as a fiduciary, an interruption in service is itself a client-protection issue. A BCP should address:

- **Protection, backup, and recovery of books, records, and data** (including electronic/cloud data);
- **Alternate means of communication** with clients, employees, custodians, and regulators;
- **Office relocation** in the event of a disaster (disaster recovery);
- **Assignment of duties** to qualified personnel during a business interruption;
- **Succession planning** — designating who will manage or wind down the business if a key person (often a sole proprietor) dies, becomes disabled, or otherwise cannot continue, so client accounts are not orphaned.

**Exam tip:** Distinguish the two prongs. **Disaster recovery** keeps the firm operating through short-term disruptions (fire, storm, system outage). **Succession planning** addresses the long-term loss of a key individual. A common question tests why a **sole proprietor adviser** especially needs a succession plan — so clients retain access to their assets and advice if that one person is suddenly unavailable.

---

## Appendix A — Key Formulas

This appendix collects every calculation the Series 65 exam is likely to test. Each entry gives the formula in plain text, defines each variable, and closes with a one-line worked example. Formulas are grouped by theme.

#### Yields & Returns

**Current Yield**

Current Yield = Annual Coupon Interest / Current Market Price

- *Annual Coupon Interest* = the dollar interest a bond pays per year (coupon rate x par value)
- *Current Market Price* = the bond's present market price

Example: An 8% bond ($80 annual coupon) trading at $1,000 has a current yield of 80 / 1,000 = 8.0%.

**Tax-Equivalent Yield (TEY)**

Tax-Equivalent Yield = Municipal (Tax-Free) Yield / (1 - Marginal Tax Rate)

- *Municipal Yield* = the tax-free yield offered by a municipal bond
- *Marginal Tax Rate* = the investor's highest bracket, expressed as a decimal

Example: A 4% muni for an investor in the 25% bracket has a TEY of 0.04 / (1 - 0.25) = 5.33%.

**Holding-Period Return / Total Return**

Total Return = (Ending Value - Beginning Value + Income Received) / Beginning Value

- *Ending Value* = the investment's value at the end of the period
- *Beginning Value* = the value at the start (typically purchase price)
- *Income Received* = dividends or interest collected during the period

Example: Buy at $50, sell at $55, collect $2 in dividends: (55 - 50 + 2) / 50 = 14%.

**Real (Inflation-Adjusted) Return**

Real Return = Nominal Return - Inflation Rate

- *Nominal Return* = the stated (unadjusted) return on the investment
- *Inflation Rate* = the rate at which prices rose over the period (often the CPI)

Example: A 7% nominal return during 3% inflation gives a real return of 7% - 3% = 4%.

**After-Tax Return**

After-Tax Return = Pre-Tax Return x (1 - Tax Rate)

- *Pre-Tax Return* = the return before taxes are applied
- *Tax Rate* = the applicable tax rate on the gain or income, as a decimal

Example: A 10% taxable return for an investor in the 30% bracket nets 0.10 x (1 - 0.30) = 7%.

**Net Asset Value (NAV)**

NAV per Share = (Total Assets - Total Liabilities) / Shares Outstanding

- *Total Assets* = market value of all securities and cash the fund holds
- *Total Liabilities* = amounts the fund owes
- *Shares Outstanding* = number of fund shares held by investors

Example: A fund with $100M assets, $10M liabilities, and 9M shares has an NAV of (100M - 10M) / 9M = $10.00.

**Dividend Yield**

Dividend Yield = Annual Dividends per Share / Price per Share

- *Annual Dividends per Share* = total dividends paid on one share over a year
- *Price per Share* = the stock's current market price

Example: A stock paying $1.50 in dividends at a $30 price yields 1.50 / 30 = 5.0%.

#### Ratios & Valuation

**Current Ratio**

Current Ratio = Current Assets / Current Liabilities

- *Current Assets* = cash and assets convertible to cash within one year
- *Current Liabilities* = obligations due within one year

Example: $600,000 current assets against $300,000 current liabilities gives a current ratio of 2.0.

**Quick Ratio (Acid-Test)**

Quick Ratio = (Current Assets - Inventory) / Current Liabilities

- *Current Assets* = current assets as above
- *Inventory* = goods held for sale (excluded because it is the least liquid current asset)
- *Current Liabilities* = obligations due within one year

Example: ($600,000 - $200,000 inventory) / $300,000 = a quick ratio of 1.33.

**Debt-to-Equity Ratio**

Debt-to-Equity = Total Debt / Total Shareholders' Equity

- *Total Debt* = the company's interest-bearing borrowings (often long-term debt)
- *Total Shareholders' Equity* = assets minus liabilities; the owners' stake

Example: $4M in debt against $8M in equity gives a debt-to-equity ratio of 0.5.

**Price-to-Earnings (P/E) Ratio**

P/E = Market Price per Share / Earnings per Share (EPS)

- *Market Price per Share* = the stock's current price
- *Earnings per Share* = net income available to common shareholders divided by shares outstanding

Example: A $40 stock with $2 EPS has a P/E of 40 / 2 = 20.

**Price-to-Book (P/B) Ratio**

P/B = Market Price per Share / Book Value per Share

- *Market Price per Share* = the stock's current price
- *Book Value per Share* = (Total Assets - Total Liabilities - Preferred Equity) / Common Shares Outstanding

Example: A $30 stock with a $15 book value per share has a P/B of 30 / 15 = 2.0.

**Market Capitalization**

Market Capitalization = Current Share Price x Shares Outstanding

- *Current Share Price* = the stock's market price
- *Shares Outstanding* = total common shares held by investors

Example: A $50 stock with 20 million shares outstanding has a market cap of 50 x 20M = $1 billion.

#### Risk & Statistics

**CAPM Expected Return**

Expected Return = Risk-Free Rate + Beta x (Market Return - Risk-Free Rate)

- *Risk-Free Rate* = yield on a risk-free asset, typically short-term Treasuries
- *Beta* = the asset's sensitivity to market movements (systematic risk)
- *Market Return* = the expected return of the overall market; the term in parentheses is the market risk premium

Example: With a 2% risk-free rate, beta of 1.2, and 10% market return: 2% + 1.2 x (10% - 2%) = 11.6%.

**Alpha**

Alpha = Actual Return - CAPM Expected Return

- *Actual Return* = the return the portfolio actually earned
- *CAPM Expected Return* = the return predicted by CAPM for the portfolio's beta

Example: A portfolio that returned 13% when CAPM predicted 11.6% has an alpha of +1.4% (outperformance).

**Beta**

Beta = Covariance(Asset Return, Market Return) / Variance(Market Return)

- *Covariance* = how the asset's returns move together with the market's
- *Variance(Market Return)* = the dispersion of the market's returns
- The market itself has a beta of 1.0; beta above 1 is more volatile than the market, below 1 is less

Example: A stock with a beta of 1.5 tends to rise (or fall) about 15% when the market moves 10%.

**Standard Deviation (Concept)**

Standard Deviation = the square root of the average squared deviation of returns from their mean

- *Concept* = a measure of total risk (volatility); the dispersion of an investment's returns around its average return
- Larger standard deviation means wider swings and greater uncertainty; roughly 68% of returns fall within one standard deviation of the mean

Example: A fund averaging 8% with a standard deviation of 10% typically returns between -2% and 18% in a given year.

**Sharpe Ratio**

Sharpe Ratio = (Portfolio Return - Risk-Free Rate) / Standard Deviation

- *Portfolio Return* = the return the portfolio earned
- *Risk-Free Rate* = return on a risk-free asset
- *Standard Deviation* = the portfolio's total risk (volatility)

Example: A portfolio returning 12% with a 2% risk-free rate and 10% standard deviation has a Sharpe ratio of (12 - 2) / 10 = 1.0.

#### Time Value of Money

**Future Value (FV)**

Future Value = Present Value x (1 + r)^n

- *Present Value* = the amount invested today
- *r* = the periodic interest (growth) rate, as a decimal
- *n* = the number of compounding periods

Example: $1,000 growing at 6% for 2 years: 1,000 x (1.06)^2 = $1,123.60.

**Present Value (PV)**

Present Value = Future Value / (1 + r)^n

- *Future Value* = the amount to be received in the future
- *r* = the discount rate per period, as a decimal
- *n* = the number of periods until the amount is received

Example: $1,000 needed in 2 years, discounted at 6%: 1,000 / (1.06)^2 = $890.00.

**Net Present Value (NPV)**

NPV = (Sum of Present Values of Future Cash Flows) - Initial Investment

- *Present Values of Future Cash Flows* = each expected cash inflow discounted back to today
- *Initial Investment* = the up-front cost paid today
- A positive NPV means the investment's discounted return exceeds its cost; NPV > 0 implies a yield above the discount rate

Example: An investment costing $900 that returns a single $1,000 in one year, discounted at 6%, has NPV = (1,000 / 1.06) - 900 = +$43.40.

**Rule of 72**

Years to Double = 72 / Interest Rate (in whole-number percent)

- *72* = a fixed approximation constant
- *Interest Rate* = the annual compounding rate expressed as a whole number (e.g., 8, not 0.08)
- Rearranged, Rate to Double = 72 / Years, giving the return needed to double money in a set time

Example: At an 8% annual return, money doubles in about 72 / 8 = 9 years.

---

## Appendix B — Glossary

**Accredited investor** — Under Regulation D, an individual with $200,000 income ($300,000 joint) for the past two years or $1 million net worth excluding primary residence, or specified institutions; such investors may buy unregistered private-placement securities.

**Accretion** — The annual upward adjustment of a bond's cost basis from its discount purchase price toward par; on a taxable original-issue-discount bond the accreted amount is taxed as interest each year.

**ADR (American Depositary Receipt)** — A negotiable receipt issued by a U.S. bank representing shares of a foreign company, letting Americans trade foreign equity in dollars on U.S. markets; holders bear currency risk and, while they receive dividends and can generally vote the underlying shares, lack preemptive rights.

**Agent** — Under the Uniform Securities Act, an individual who represents a broker-dealer or issuer in effecting securities transactions; agents must register in each state where they solicit or transact.

**Alpha** — The portion of a portfolio's return above or below what its beta (market risk) would predict; positive alpha indicates value added by the manager relative to expected risk-adjusted return.

**AML (Anti-Money Laundering)** — Programs required under the Bank Secrecy Act and USA PATRIOT Act to detect and prevent laundering, including customer identification, SAR filings, and CTRs for cash transactions over $10,000.

**Annuity** — An insurance contract that accumulates on a tax-deferred basis and can pay out income for a set period or life; fixed annuities pay guaranteed amounts while variable annuities fluctuate with subaccount performance.

**Basis point** — One one-hundredth of one percent (0.01%); 100 basis points equal 1%, a standard unit for quoting yield and interest-rate changes.

**Beta** — A measure of a security's volatility relative to the overall market; a beta of 1.0 moves with the market, above 1.0 is more volatile, and below 1.0 is less volatile.

**Breakpoint** — A dollar level of mutual fund purchase at which the sales charge is reduced; selling just below a breakpoint to earn a higher commission ("breakpoint sale") is a prohibited practice.

**Brochure Rule** — SEC Rule 204-3 requiring a registered investment adviser to deliver its Form ADV Part 2 (or equivalent brochure) and brochure supplement to clients at or before entering an advisory contract, with annual updates.

**Callable bond** — A bond the issuer may redeem before maturity, usually when rates fall; the call feature benefits the issuer and exposes the holder to reinvestment risk, so such bonds carry higher yields.

**Capital Asset Pricing Model (CAPM)** — A model that estimates an asset's required return as the risk-free rate plus beta times the market risk premium; it prices only systematic (non-diversifiable) risk.

**Convertible bond** — A bond that the holder may exchange for a fixed number of the issuer's common shares; the conversion feature lets bondholders participate in equity upside, so these bonds typically pay lower coupons.

**Correlation** — A statistic from -1 to +1 measuring how two assets' returns move together; combining assets with low or negative correlation reduces portfolio risk through diversification.

**Coupon (nominal yield)** — The fixed annual interest rate a bond pays on its par value, stated as a percentage; a 5% coupon on a $1,000 bond pays $50 per year regardless of market price.

**Current yield** — A bond's annual coupon interest divided by its current market price; it rises when the bond trades at a discount and falls when it trades at a premium.

**Custody** — Holding or having access to a client's funds or securities, or authority to withdraw them; advisers with custody face extra requirements such as surprise audits, qualified custodians, and account statements.

**De minimis exemption** — A provision letting an adviser or agent avoid state registration if it has no place of business in the state and serves few clients—generally five or fewer clients in any 12-month period for advisers.

**Discount rate** — The interest rate the Federal Reserve charges member banks for short-term loans through the discount window; also, more broadly, the rate used to compute the present value of future cash flows.

**Discretion** — Authority granted to an adviser or agent to decide the security, quantity, or whether to buy or sell without prior client approval for each trade; it requires prior written authorization (though time and price alone are not discretion).

**Diversification** — Spreading investments across different securities, sectors, or asset classes to reduce unsystematic (company-specific) risk; it cannot eliminate systematic market risk.

**Dollar cost averaging** — Investing a fixed dollar amount at regular intervals regardless of price, which buys more shares when prices are low and fewer when high, lowering average cost per share over time.

**Duration** — A measure of a bond's price sensitivity to interest-rate changes, expressed in years; the higher the duration, the more the bond's price moves for a given change in rates.

**Efficient Market Hypothesis (EMH)** — The theory that security prices already reflect available information, so consistently beating the market is difficult; its weak, semi-strong, and strong forms differ on which information is already priced in.

**ERISA (Employee Retirement Income Security Act of 1974)** — Federal law setting fiduciary, funding, vesting, and reporting standards for private-sector employer retirement plans; Section 404(c) shifts investment liability to participants who direct their own accounts.

**ETF (Exchange-Traded Fund)** — An investment fund, usually index-based, whose shares trade intraday on an exchange like a stock; ETFs typically offer low expenses and tax efficiency and can be bought on margin or sold short.

**Expected return** — The probability-weighted average of an investment's possible returns; it represents the mean outcome an investor anticipates, not a guaranteed result.

**Federal covered adviser** — An investment adviser registered with the SEC rather than the states—generally those managing $110 million or more, advising registered investment companies, or otherwise exempt from state registration; states may still require notice filing.

**Fiduciary** — A person legally obligated to act in a client's best interest, placing the client's interests above their own; investment advisers owe clients a fiduciary duty of care and loyalty.

**Form ADV** — The uniform registration form investment advisers file; Part 1 covers business and disciplinary information, Part 2 is the client-facing brochure, and Part 3 (Form CRS) is a relationship summary for retail clients.

**Form U4** — The Uniform Application for Securities Industry Registration used to register agents and investment adviser representatives, disclosing employment, disciplinary, and financial background.

**529 plan** — A state-sponsored, tax-advantaged education savings plan whose earnings grow tax-deferred and are tax-free when used for qualified education expenses; contributions may qualify for state tax benefits.

**GDP (Gross Domestic Product)** — The total market value of all final goods and services produced within a country in a period; two consecutive quarters of declining real GDP signal a recession.

**GO bond (General Obligation bond)** — A municipal bond backed by the full faith, credit, and taxing power of the issuer; because repayment relies on taxes, issuing GO debt often requires voter approval.

**Hedge fund** — A lightly regulated private pooled fund open mainly to accredited and institutional investors that uses leverage, short selling, and derivatives to pursue high returns, typically charging a performance-based fee.

**IAR (Investment Adviser Representative)** — An individual associated with an investment adviser who gives advice, manages accounts, solicits clients, or supervises those who do; IARs register at the state level.

**Index** — A statistical benchmark tracking the performance of a group of securities, such as the S&P 500; indexes are used to gauge markets and as the basis for index funds and ETFs.

**Inflation** — A sustained rise in the general price level that erodes purchasing power; it is commonly measured by the Consumer Price Index (CPI) and is a key form of purchasing-power risk for bondholders.

**Investment adviser** — Any person who, for compensation, is in the business of advising others about securities or issuing analyses; the definition turns on the three-part "advice, business, compensation" test.

**Investment company** — A firm that pools investor money to invest in securities, classified as a face-amount certificate company, unit investment trust, or management company (open-end mutual funds and closed-end funds).

**Leading indicators** — Economic data series such as building permits, new orders, and stock prices that tend to change before the overall economy does, used to forecast turns in the business cycle.

**Liquidity** — The ease with which an asset can be converted to cash at a fair price without significant loss; money market instruments are highly liquid, while real estate and thin securities are not.

**Margin** — Borrowing from a broker-dealer to buy securities, using the account as collateral under Regulation T; margin magnifies both gains and losses and requires maintaining minimum equity.

**Modern Portfolio Theory (MPT)** — A framework holding that investors should evaluate securities by their contribution to a portfolio's overall risk and return, building an "efficient frontier" that maximizes return for each level of risk.

**Money market** — The market for short-term, high-quality debt instruments maturing in one year or less, such as T-bills, commercial paper, and CDs, valued for safety and liquidity.

**Municipal bond** — Debt issued by state or local governments whose interest is generally exempt from federal income tax (and often state tax for residents), making it attractive to investors in high tax brackets.

**NAV (Net Asset Value)** — The per-share value of a mutual fund, equal to total assets minus liabilities divided by shares outstanding, calculated at least once daily; it is the price at which open-end fund shares are redeemed.

**Nonsystematic (unsystematic) risk** — Risk specific to a single company or industry—such as business, financial, or regulatory risk—that can be reduced through diversification.

**Open-end company (mutual fund)** — A management investment company that continuously issues and redeems shares at NAV; investors buy from and sell back to the fund rather than trading on an exchange.

**Preferred stock** — An equity security paying a fixed dividend with priority over common stock in dividends and liquidation but usually no voting rights; its price behaves much like a bond, moving inversely with interest rates.

**Present value** — The current worth of a future sum discounted at a given interest rate; it reflects the time value of money, the principle that a dollar today is worth more than a dollar later.

**Prospectus** — The disclosure document delivered to investors in a registered public offering, summarizing the issuer, the securities, risks, and use of proceeds; it is the abbreviated form of the registration statement's Part I.

**QDIA (Qualified Default Investment Alternative)** — A diversified default investment—such as a target-date or balanced fund—into which a participant's 401(k) contributions are placed when no election is made, giving plan fiduciaries safe-harbor protection under ERISA.

**Qualified plan** — An employer retirement plan meeting ERISA and IRS rules, offering pre-tax contributions, tax-deferred growth, and taxation upon withdrawal; examples include 401(k)s and defined-benefit pensions.

**REIT (Real Estate Investment Trust)** — A company that owns or finances income-producing real estate and trades like a stock; it avoids corporate tax by distributing at least 90% of taxable income to shareholders as dividends.

**Revenue bond** — A municipal bond repaid solely from the income of a specific project or facility, such as a toll road or airport, rather than from general taxes; it carries no voter approval requirement and higher risk than a GO bond.

**Roth IRA** — An individual retirement account funded with after-tax dollars whose qualified withdrawals, including earnings, are tax-free; it has income eligibility limits and no required minimum distributions during the owner's life.

**Rule 144** — The SEC rule governing the resale of restricted and control (affiliate) securities into the public market, imposing holding periods, volume limits, and Form 144 filing to allow sales without full registration.

**Sharpe ratio** — A measure of risk-adjusted return equal to a portfolio's return above the risk-free rate divided by its standard deviation; a higher ratio indicates better reward per unit of total risk.

**Soft dollars** — Research or brokerage services an adviser receives from a broker-dealer in exchange for directing client trades there; the Section 28(e) safe harbor permits this only if the benefits are eligible research or execution services.

**Standard deviation** — A statistic measuring how widely an investment's returns vary around their average, used as a proxy for total risk; a higher standard deviation means greater volatility.

**Systematic risk** — Market-wide risk—such as interest-rate, inflation, and market risk—that affects all securities and cannot be diversified away; it is the risk measured by beta.

**TIPS (Treasury Inflation-Protected Securities)** — U.S. Treasury bonds whose principal adjusts with the CPI, so both interest payments and the amount repaid at maturity keep pace with inflation, protecting purchasing power.

**Total return** — An investment's complete return combining income (interest or dividends) and capital appreciation, expressed as a percentage of the amount invested; it gives a fuller performance picture than yield alone.

**Uniform Securities Act (USA)** — The model state securities law (blue-sky law) that states adapt to register securities, advisers, agents, and IARs, and to police fraud; it is the foundation of the NASAA-administered state regulatory framework.

**Variable annuity** — An annuity whose value and payout depend on the performance of investor-selected subaccounts; because it carries investment risk, it is a security requiring both a securities and an insurance license to sell.

**Wrap fee (wrap account)** — An arrangement bundling advisory services, execution, and administrative costs into a single asset-based fee; sponsors must deliver a specialized wrap-fee brochure (Form ADV Part 2A, Appendix 1).

**Yield curve** — A graph plotting yields of similar bonds across maturities; a normal upward-sloping curve reflects higher long-term yields, while an inverted curve (long-term below short-term) often signals a coming recession.

**Yield to maturity (YTM)** — The total annualized return an investor earns holding a bond to maturity, accounting for coupon income plus any gain or loss between purchase price and par; it is the market's true measure of a bond's yield.

**Zero-coupon bond** — A bond issued at a deep discount that pays no periodic interest, returning full par value at maturity; the accreted discount is taxed annually as "phantom income" even though no cash is received.
